# Nava-vraja-mahima. Vol. 3

## Dedication

Although I have written Nava-vraja-mahima for the pleasure of all Vaisnavas who either physically or mentally reside in Vrndavana, I dedicate this book to those whose hearts are devoted to Radha-Syamasundara and New Vraja-dhama. It is my hope that they will pass their time in this way:

*iha vatsan samacarayad*  
*iha nah svami jagau vamsim*  
*iti sasram gadato me*  
*yamuna-tire dinam yayat*

**“Here our Lord herded the calves, and here He played the flute. I pray that I may pass my days shedding tears as I speak these words on the Yamuna’s shore.” (Sri Raghupati Upadhyaya)**

## Preface

The title of the book you are now reading, Nava-vraja-mahima, when rendered in English becomes “The Glories of New Vraja-dhama.” I originally conceived of it as a booklet, meant to guide devotees visiting the holy places of New Vraja-dhama, iskcon’s rural community in Hungary.

As you must have noticed, the book has grown in size since its inception and would now likely require a small handcart for eager pilgrims to be able to transport it from one pastime place to another! Nonetheless, it still serves its original purpose of extolling New Vraja-dhama by praising its presiding Deities, Radha-Syamasundara, and by narrating the Supreme Lord Krsna’s pastimes in His eternal abode, Sri Vrndavana-dhama.

Texts that glorify the holy abodes (dhamas) of the Lord are called dhama-mahatmyas. Two prime examples of this genre of devotional literature are Rupa Gosvami’s Sri Mathura-mahatmya and Bhaktivinoda Thakura’s Sri Navadvipa-dhama-mahatmya. Both of these books establish the sanctity of the dhamas they depict — Vrndavana and Navadvipa respectively — by citing Puranic praise of the dhamas themselves as well as the pastime places within their boundaries. There is, however, an interesting difference between the two. Sri Mathura-mahatmya is an unordered compendium of scriptural references to the pastime places it describes, while Sri Navadvipa-dhama-mahatmya chronicles the Lord’s pastimes and the places at which they occurred by following the sequence in which pilgrims visit those places as they circumambulate the dhama. Thakura Bhaktivinoda also recounts the personal spiritual reflections he gleans from circumambulating Navadvipa in an appendix to Sri Navadvipa-dhama-mahatmya that he calls Sri Navadvipa-bhava-taranga.

Despite their stylistic differences, the transcendental benefit readers derive from these two types of mahatmyas is the same. They act as practical guidebooks to the dhama, and at the same time they aid pilgrims in immersing their thoughts in the Lord’s glories. For that reason, devotees who do not travel to the dhama can also derive transcendental benefit by circumambulating the holy places in their minds as they meditate on both the Lord’s pastimes and the dhama's glories as they are recounted in sacred texts of this nature.

Nava-vraja-mahima is a mahatmya in the style of Sri Navadvipa- dhama-mahatmya in that it includes an account of my own personal circumambulation, or parikrama, of New Vraja-dhama and the realisations that came to me as a result. In fact, as I visit the pastime places in New Vraja-dhama — more commonly known to the people of Hungary as Krsna Valley — I sometimes reflect on similar parikramas I have undertaken in Bhauma Vrndavana, recount histories of how New Vraja-dhama was established and developed, or express my own devotional aspirations. This personal approach is primarily an attempt to please the devotees of New Vraja-dhama, who requested me to describe the parikrama not as a passive observer but as a participant who shares his adventure with the reader.

Amongst many possible readers, this personal adventure is especially intended to be shared with the residents of and visitors to New Vraja-dhama, for whom this book will naturally be of greatest interest. Those readers who are not a part of that particular audience may rightly question what New Vraja-dhama is and how its glorification can be relevant to persons who live outside Hungary or who may never visit.

As its name suggests, New Vraja-dhama is modelled after the original Vraja-dhama, Bhauma Vrndavana, Krsna’s eternal home as it appears on earth. The inspiration to establish New Vraja-dhama came from my spiritual master, His Divine Grace A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada, the founder-acarya of the International Society for Krishna Consciousness. In the 1960s in America, Srila Prabhupada established New Vrndavana and envisioned it to be a self-sufficient community, dependent upon agriculture and cow protection, as well as a place of pilgrimage in the West. New Vraja-dhama is therefore our humble attempt to fulfil the desire of His Divine Grace that such places of pilgrimage be established throughout the world.

In the introduction to this book and in the essay entitled “The Truths of the Dhama,” the reader will find a detailed history of how and why New Vraja-dhama came to be, along with a philosophical study of the spiritual principles that make it a holy place. I cannot overstate how important it is for the reader to carefully go through these parts of the book in order to clearly understand the spiritual foundations upon which New Vraja-dhama and Nava-vraja-mahima rest.

What benefit will those who do not visit New Vraja-dhama gain from reading this book? The answer is threefold.

Nava-vraja-mahima contains extensive and very detailed narrations of Krsna’s pastimes at all the major places in Vrndavana, as well as at many lesser-known places. Extensive research went into compiling — from the Vedas, Upanisads, Puranas, Samhitas, and other Vedic histories, as well as from the commentaries of our previous acaryas and other sources — the information it contains. Altogether, the text and the accompanying references offer the reader a unique opportunity to delve deeply into Krsna’s pastimes in Bhauma and Goloka Vrndavana and better get to know the Supreme Lord and His loving associates.

Second, although the topographical orientation, distances, and occasionally the sequence of New Vraja-dhama’s parikrama differ from the same in Bhauma Vrndavana, the book also serves as an in-depth guide for pilgrims to use in visiting the pastime places of Sri Krsna’s original earthly abode.

Third, through the glorification of New Vraja-dhama, this book testifies to the greatness of Srila Prabhupada’s achievement in spreading the potency of Vrndavana throughout the world.

Now, allow me to clarify a few points about certain expressions and literary devices used in the text, about the structure of the volumes, and about the actual parikrama route of New Vraja-dhama.

The expression “Krsna’s pastimes in New Vraja-dhama” is not meant to imply that Krsna manifests His eternal pastimes here in the way that He does, creation after creation, in Bhauma Vrndavana. Rather it means that the pastime places in New Vraja-dhama correspond to the original places in Bhauma Vrndavana or Goloka Vrndavana. Largely due to the uncommon format of the text (which I will discuss elsewhere in this preface) and because of the correspondence between the pastime places in both dhamas, it was sometimes necessary to refer to the pastimes associated with those places in a way that may make it sound as if they happened in New Vraja-dhama.

If I say, for example, “At this place, called Aghavana, Krsna killed Aghasura,” it means “Krsna killed Aghasura in the Aghavana of Bhauma Vrndavana, which is commemorated by the place of the same name here in New Vraja-dhama.” Similarly, phrases such as “At this place Radha and Krsna enact Their rasa dance” should be taken to mean “At the Rasa-sthali of Goloka Vrndavana, the divine couple enact Their eternal rasa dance. The place called Rasa-sthali in New Vraja-dhama commemorates that original place.”

The reason I use such expressions, aside from literary necessity, is to emphasise the importance of New Vraja-dhama and its stature as a place of pilgrimage. Again, the reader will find the philosophical and spiritual reasoning and supporting scriptural evidence for employing this convention in the introduction and the essay on dhama-tattva that follow this preface.

Another literary device found in this book — as well as in other books describing the holy dhama — is that I sometimes speak about New Vraja-dhama as if I were directly seeing the unmanifest (aprakata) abode and pastimes of the Lord. These expressions are not declarations of my existential position; rather, they are poetic expressions of my deep feelings for the subject matter and the places themselves. I qualify such statements here to avoid causing misunderstandings that might blur the line between the manifest and unmanifest features of the dhama, merging them in a way that could be taken as contrary to authorised dhama-tattva.

When, in describing the dhama, authors weave between manifest and unmanifest realities in this way, it often causes the features of the aprakata realm or the pastimes Krsna is relishing there to seem as if they are part of the world visible to conditioned souls. But devotees well versed in the principles of dhama-tattva will have no difficulty following these transitions between the manifest and unmanifest realms, whereas others may either not notice them or find them confusing.

Our great acaryas may express themselves in such ways due to the overwhelming bliss they feel at seeing the aprakata-dhama and the eternal pastimes going on there. Or, in the case of those who are not on the fully realised platform of our great acaryas, authors may be making a conscious endeavour to emphasise the divinity that underlies the features of the dhama that conditioned souls are able to perceive.

In writing Nava-vraja-mahima, poetic inspiration would sometimes impel me to describe the features of the dhama not in terms of what I was seeing with my eyes — for I cannot see that which is unmanifest—but in terms of the aprakata counter-whole as I have heard about it from scripture and as I contemplate it in relation to New Vraja-dhama. Readers who notice such expressions in the pages of this book should take them in this light, not as my personal realisation of the unmanifest dhama in the way of the great acaryas.

**\* \* \* \***

Paricrama is the traditional way of visiting Vrndavana.When Krsna sent Uddhava to Nandagrama with messages for the Vraja-vasis, He directed His cousin along a specific route and pointed out places to note along the journey. Thus Uddhava relished parikrama by travelling from Mathura to Nandagrama, as did Krsna by describing the journey to him.

On His only visit to Vrndavana, Caitanya Mahaprabhu spent the first month of His two-month stay touring through Vraja’s twelve forests. And even though Lord Caitanya is Krsna Himself, He requested the help of a guide and followed the established route of His time.

With the rediscovery of Krsna’s pastime places, the Vraja-mandala parikrama has evolved into what is currently called caurasi-krosa parikrama, or circumambulation of the one hundred and sixty-eight-mile dhama, the longest version of which takes almost two months to complete. Today, often during the holy month of Karttika, hundreds of devotees relish walking barefoot from place to place on iskcon’s Vraja-mandala parikrama, living in the meadows or fields of Vraja, and absorbed in hearing about Krsna and chanting His names.

For Hungarian devotees who cannot go to Bhauma Vrndavana, who cannot stay there for thirty days or more, or who would like to go on parikrama every year but cannot afford to go to India so often, the Nava-vraja-mandala parikrama is for them. Annually, members of the Hungarian yatra visit selected places of New Vraja-dhama over the course of several days, and as the dhama is further developed and parikrama paths established, the guided tours will become more extensive. In addition, by using the separate book of maps provided with Nava-vraja-mahima, devotees may go on parikrama at any time they want and thus relish the nectar of hearing Krsna’s pastimes and serving His dhama.

In this way, Nava-vraja-mahima seeks to serve those devotees keen to visit Krsna’s abode, both in Hungary and in India. And for devotees who are neither residents of nor pilgrims to one dhama or the other, this work gives them the chance to visit Krsna’s holy dhama in their minds and thus bring the atmosphere of Vrndavana to wherever they reside.

Circumambulating the dhama is a means of offering respect. It includes visiting pastimes places, remembering pastimes, bowing down, taking the dust from pastime places on one’s head, bathing in sacred waters, and performing nama-sankirtana. Many major places within the dhama — Govardhana Hill, Vrndavana town, and Varsana amongst them — have their own parikrama routes. As far as possible, these places have been or will be represented in New Vraja-dhama. There is never a dearth of transcendental diversion for the devout pilgrim!

At the time of writing this book, the main pastime places of New Vraja-dhama have been established, whereas many parikrama paths and other secondary pastime places have been mapped out but not yet developed. These places — like the construction of a magnificent new temple for Radha-Syamasundara— remain part of future plans to develop New Vraja-dhama.

Let me now describe the book’s structure.

As already mentioned, this book is written in the form of my own parikrama, which is actually a compilation of multiple parikramas I made of New Vraja-dhama, some encompassing the entirety of the dhama, others only of places especially dear to me.

In the course of describing my parikrama, there were times at which deep philosophical topics or lengthy pastimes came up that required explanations so extensive that to describe them properly would have broken the flow of the narration. These explanations became essays that are now appendices at the end of each volume. I request my reader not to neglect these appendices — nor the endnotes that accompany each chapter, for that matter — as readers are sometimes wont to do. They comprise roughly one third of the book’s total content and are instrumental in bringing the reader to a balanced understanding of its message. Designated stanzas in the running text direct the reader to these appendices at the proper times.

In addition to the parikrama and appendices, the ten final chapters of Nava-vraja-mahima are dedicated to directly glorifying Radha- Syamasundara, the presiding Deities of New Vraja-dhama. In these chapters I exalt Their Lordships through the meditations that arise as I chant the Gayatri mantras and the maha-mantra and from my direct service to Them on the altar. This last section of the book therefore contains in-depth analyses of the Gaudiya Vaisnava diksa-mantras as well as the Hare Krsna maha-mantra. In this way the book has three parts: parikrama, appendices, and direct glorification of Radha-Syamasundara.

The running text that forms the bulk of the book, comprised of the parikrama and the praises of Radha-Syama, is written in three-line prose stanzas. It is pleasant to look at and easy to read, but it was a challenge to write, edit, and lay out on the page. This challenge led us to adopt certain conventions, such as mixing Sanskrit verses with English translations within a single stanza, or having longer verses straddle more than one stanza. However, we feel confident that the book’s format will enable you to relish the topic all the more.

From the outset I wanted Nava-vraja-mahima to be well illustrated to give windows into Krsna’s pastimes and to contribute to the overall purpose of the work — to increase the reader’s attachment to the Lord. I hope this has been achieved to your satisfaction. In Sri Krsna-samhita (9.16), Bhaktivinoda Thakura writes, “By seeing pictures of Krsna or by repeatedly hearing descriptions of Krsna from persons who have seen Him, one’s greed for attaining Krsna increases unlimitedly.”

To assist you in navigating through the unfamiliar names of pastime places and the variances in the locations of these places in New Vraja-dhama and Bhauma Vrndavana, each chapter also includes a map or maps of the area it describes.

Altogether there are eight volumes of text. The set is complete with a ninth volume solely dedicated to maps of the various holy places of both New Vraja-dhama and Bhauma Vrndavana, along with short descriptions of each pastime place. Devotees can take this map book with them to follow the parikrama routes in both dhamas and have a reminder of Krsna’s activities there.

Nava-vraja-mahima is meant to serve as a companion for devotees who want to take full advantage of the presence of Vrndavana in Hungary and to develop their relationship with its presiding Deities, Radha-Syamasundara. Furthermore, it is meant to encourage devotees anywhere in the world to worship their local Deities with devotion and recognise the Vaikuntha nature of those Deities’ abodes. It is my hope as the author that this work will serve these purposes in some small way and thus bring pleasure to devotees, for my own access to the dhama will be possible only by the good wishes of the Vaisnavas.

In conclusion, I request the reader, wherever he or she may reside, to consider that in its essence this book praises the same dhama and the same Krsna that are there in their own hearts, and which are thus very near and dear to them. It is in this way that our journey around New Vraja-dhama through the pages of this book is synonymous with our own inner journey back to Godhead.

The journey through Nava-vraja-mahima is a long one. The book spans over five thousand pages and took five years to write. Therein I have tried my best to portray the truths of Vrndavana as they are recorded in scriptures and narrated by perfect devotees like Srila Prabhupada. Sadly, I remain a conditioned soul and thus am prone to make mistakes. If my readers find mistakes within the pages of this book, I ask them to tolerate such faults in the way they do the pebbles or thorns that prick their feet while on parikrama of the dhama.

Hare Krsna.  
Sivarama Swami  
New Vraja-dhama  
Gopastami, 2 November 2011

## Chapter Nine - The Festival and Lifting of Govardhana Hill

In New Vraja-dhama, the western portion of the Govardhana parikrama-marga runs along the Govardhana-Sonkh road, turning north where the sacred forest of Rahela and Yamuna-mata intersect.

In Bhauma Vrndavana, a tributary of the Yamuna once flowed here, as it did through many places in Vraja. Today that sacred river is barely visible, and in only one part of the holy dhama.

South of Rahela is Mukharai, the residence of Radha’s talkative maternal grandmother, Mukhara-devi, who breastfed Yasoda with the same affection with which she suckled her own daughter Kirtida.

And Mukhara loved Yasoda’s son Krsna no less than she loved her own granddaughter Radha. While living in Gokula, she and Mother Yasoda would share the pleasure of seeing Krsna’s childhood pranks.

Once, while the two women watched from a hidden place, they saw child Krsna step stealthily out of the bushes. Intending to steal the gopis’ butter, His eyes darted craftily here and there.

When Krsna’s family moved to nearby Nandagrama, Mukhara’s role in the exchanges between Krsna and the gopis would sometimes seem contradictory, especially when she dealt with Srimati Radharani.

After Radha’s marriage, for example, when Jatila would accuse Krsna of harassing her daughter-in-law, Mukhara would often take Krsna’s side and angrily rebuke Jatila for falsely blaming Him.

But from time to time Mukhara would also call into question the nature of Radha and Krsna’s relationship, if, for example, she saw Radha wearing Krsna’s yellow wrap instead of Her own blue veil.

But Mukhara’s disapproval of Radha’s and Krsna’s attraction for one another was simply an external show — social decorum — for secretly she nurtured Their love by helping to arrange Their meetings.

Let us offer our obeisances to the residence of Mukhara-devi, the place where Radharani, on the plea of visiting Her grandmother, would meet a certain dark-complexioned boy.

Beside a lake in Mukharai is a temple with deities of Radha, Her mother Kirtida, and grandmother Mukhara. A bhajana-sila is found here, a magical stone that makes a ringing sound when struck.

South of Mukharai is Deravali, a clearing in which Nanda Maharaja’s clan encamped on their way from Chatikara to Nandagrama. The caravan of the Vraja-vasis’ carts stretched all the way to Khelanavana.

From where I stood on the parikrama path, midway between Deravali and Khelanavana, I could see New Vraja-dhama’s Giri Govardhana, the epitome of the transcendental form of the king of mountains.

My next step on this circumambulation would thus take me to my favourite place. Here was Giriraja — the best servant of Radha and Syama — the final destination I aspire for, who is everything to me. Nanda Maharaja’s younger brother Sannanda once praised Giri Govardhana in this way, namo vrndavanankaya tubhyam goloka-mauline / purna-brahmatapatraya namo govardhanaya ca:

“My respectful obeisances to you, who are the crown of Goloka and who sits on Vrndavana’s lap! My respectful obeisances to Govardhana, the parasol of the Supreme Personality of Godhead!”

Repeating this verse over and over, I contemplated the pastimes of which it reminded me: how Giriraja became pre-eminent in the spiritual sky, how he appeared on earth, and how Krsna lifted him.

Before we visit the sacred sites around Govardhana Hill, let us hear these three pastimes with faithful hearts. After all, we receive blessings on parikrama in proportion to our faith.

May hearing these pastimes give faith in service to Giriraja to those who have not yet heard them, and may devotion to the divine couple’s best servant increase in those already familiar with them.

Now let us hear these three pastimes, along with the glory and opulences of Giri Govardhana, and the process by which to worship the king of mountains on the festival day known as Annakuta.

**\* \* \* \***

The first pastime describes how Govardhana Hill became the crest jewel of Vraja by appearing from the heart of Lord Krsna in Goloka Vrndavana, where the Lord and His associates live eternally.

How eternal objects and persons appear and disappear in the land where there is neither birth nor death is summarised in the appendix “Apparent Transience In the Realm of Absolute Permanence.”

Dear Vaisnavas! Contemplate this carefully: By a serious and scrutinising study of this matter, your understanding of Goloka, Bhauma Vrndavana, and New Vraja-dhama will surely mature.

Long, long ago in Mithila, Narada Muni told the story of Govardhana’s birth to King Bahulasva, who wanted to know how this great hill first appeared from Lord Krsna’s chest.

Narada began by describing how Lord Krsna, in His own abode and from His own spiritual form, manifested His countless associates, beginning with Sesa, who has gigantic white coils.

Then from the Lord’s feet came the river Ganges, from His left shoulder the river Yamuna, from His anklets the arena of the rasa dance, and from His legs His favourite forest grove.

From His knees came Vrndavana forest, from His hips the Yogapitha, from the line of hairs on His abdomen many flowering vines, and from His navel the varieties of lotus flowers in Goloka’s lakes.

From the folds of skin on His abdomen appeared a gentle breeze, from His two collarbones Mathura and Dvaraka, from His arms eight friends led by Sridama, and from His wrist His father Nanda.

From the hairs on His body came the cowherd boys, from His mind the cows and bulls, from His intelligence the grass, plants, and shrubs, and from His left shoulder His most beloved Srimati Radharani.

Then from Sri Radha’s arms emerged Lalita and Visakha, and from Her bodily hairs the other gopis; in this way, Krsna and Radha manifested the unlimited wonders and residents of Goloka.

Once, Krsna and the gopis were resting in a grove decorated with parasols, flower-awnings, and strands of pearls. The air still resonated with the sounds of the flute, mrdangas, and singing of a rasa dance.

Surrounded by beautiful young girls, Radha cast a sidelong glance at Her beloved Syamasundara — who was more charming than any Cupid could ever be — and She spoke to Him affectionately.

Srimati Radharani said, “O My Lord! If You are pleased with Me, with My love for You, and with Our festival of dance, then please fulfil a desire that has long resided within the core of My heart.”

Without a moment’s hesitation Krsna replied, “O beautiful one! Although I am surprised that there is still a desire within You that I have not yet fulfilled, I will happily grant whatever You may want.”

Radha acknowledged Krsna’s kindness with a smile and whispered, “Somewhere in Vrndavana forest, by the bank of the Yamuna, please create a very beautiful, and very secluded, dance arena.”

Eyes half-closed, Krsna sat in meditation, searching His heart for the most perfect fulfilment of Radha’s desire. Then, as the gopis looked on in amazement, His love for Radha took form before them.

At first Krsna’s love appeared in a form that resembled water and fire, but was neither, and it came out of His heart in the way that a sprout shoots up from the ground, although it was not a creeper.

When that indescribable droplet of love fell to the ground it transformed itself into a mountain with palatial caves, crystalline streams, flowering trees, networks of vines, and graceful birds.

In a single moment the mountain expanded in length, breadth, and height. It assumed a form that possessed one hundred golden peaks and defeated the beauty of Lord Narayana’s palace in Vaikuntha.

Some Vrndavana-vasis called the mountain Govardhana because it was the infallible shelter for wish-fulfilling cows; others called it Satasringa because it had one hundred majestic peaks.

The essence of Govardhana is prema, and because the nature of prema is to always expand, Govardhana expanded in every direction without limit, following the dictates of his own heart.

Soon there was no space in Goloka for anything other than Govardhana Hill, and thus the inhabitants of Goloka Vrndavana, filled with apprehension, created a great uproar.

When Krsna saw the disturbance His latest creation had caused, the Lord stood up and slapped the mountain with His hand, making Govardhana assume a size proportionate to his surroundings.

Then the Lord reprimanded Govardhana, saying, “Why did you expand to such an extent, without considering the feelings and needs of others who reside here? Stop this nonsense at once!”

Once Giriraja had been corrected, Radha surveyed Krsna’s handiwork. Pleased by what She saw, She took Her beloved to a secluded place on that mountain to enjoy loving pastimes.

In brief, this was how Krsna manifested that best of mountains, multicoloured like a rainbow, reservoir of all holy places, and very, very dear to the divine couple, the Vraja-vasis, and their cows.

Govardhana learned a great lesson that day: Tasting the greatest ecstasies of pure love — even in the presence of the divine couple — is no excuse for inconveniencing others, especially the Lord’s associates.

When Giriraja appeared on earth with Goloka’s other holy places, Krsna widely broadcast that lesson by causing Giriraja to shrink day by day, due to a brahmana's curse, until he would be visible no more.

Let us now hear the history of this curse as it relates to Giriraja’s birth to the wife of Mount Drona in Salmali-dvipa, and his journey, by the power of Pulastya Muni, eastward to Bharata-varsa.

**\* \* \* \***

In response to Brahma’s request that He remove the burden of the earth, Krsna was preparing to leave Goloka. At that time He turned to Radha and said, “O beloved! Will you not come with Me?”

Sri Radha hesitantly replied, “Wherever You go, I must also be there. But how can I be happy in a place where there is no Vrndavana forest, no Yamuna River, and no Govardhana Hill?”

Krsna understood Radha’s concern, and so He put Her at case by transporting to earth an eighty-four-*krosa* Goloka, which included Govardhana Hill, the Yamuna River, and Goloka’s twelve forests.

Even though Govardhana is eternally transcendentally present in Gokula, according to the Garga-samhita, he first took birth from the wife of Mount Drona in Salmali-dvipa, a land west of Bharata-varsa.

When Govardhana was born, Mount Drona and his wife were overjoyed, the demigods showered flowers, and the Himalayas, Mount Sumeru, and other mountains came to offer their respects.

The great mountains of the universe circumambulated, worshipped, bowed down before, and then glorified Govardhana with these words: “Our respectful obeisances to Govardhana, king of mountains.

“In the divine abode of Goloka Vrndavana, which is filled with spiritually perfected gopas, gopis, and cows, you serve Lord Krsna as His personal mountain-resort and playground.

“We repeatedly bow before you, and offer you our obeisances. ? crown of Goloka, Govardhana Hill! You sit on the lap of Vrndavana and are known as the parasol of the Supreme Lord Sri Krsna.”

After speaking these prayers, the mountains departed. Because they had expressed fealty to Govardhana Hill, he is known not merely as the best of mountains but also as their king.

After some time the great sage Pulastya was one day touring the holy places of the universe. As he flew over Salmali-dvipa, he was drawn to the peerless beauty of Govardhana Hill.

The sage alighted upon Giriraja, who was luxuriant with flowering madhavi vines, heavy with abundant fruits, resplendent with precious jewels, and decorated with many colourful minerals.

That king of mountains gurgled with the sounds of gushing streams. He was alive with birds, deer, and trees, which were fragrant with countless blossoming flowers and vibrant with the calls of peacocks.

The sage reflected on Govardhana’s naturally sattvika atmosphere and peaceful caves and concluded that this mountain was a fit place not only for performing austerities but also for attaining liberation.

Pulastya approached Mount Drona and revealed his desire to take Drona’s son to Kasi. “O Drona! You are filled with transcendental medicinal herbs and thus you always restore the peoples’ lives.

“I am Pulastya of Kasi and I have come to you with a request: Please give me your son Govardhana. I want to take him to Kasi and place him beside the Ganges so that I can perform austerities there.”

King Drona almost fainted at the thought of losing his son, but he was fearful of being cursed by the sage. After due reflection, Drona agreed and requested his son Govardhana to accompany the sage.

Govardhana Hill was gigantic: eight yojanas long, five yojanas wide, and two yojanas tall. When he enquired from Pulastya how he would be transported, the sage replied, “I will carry you.”

But Giriraja had one stipulation: “You will not be able to pick me up a second time. I will stay permanently wherever you first put me down.” After bowing to Drona, he then rose to sit upon the sage’s hand.

The sage carried Govardhana upon his right hand. As they both flew towards Kasi, onlookers marvelled at the great power of the sage and wondered what he intended to do with a mountain.

As the two were passing over Vraja-mandala, Govardhana could recognise his eternal home, the land upon which Lord Krsna would soon descend to enjoy herding cows and teasing gopis.

The best of Krsna’s servants saw his own resting place beside the Yamuna and thought, “Here is the site at which Krsna will extract tolls from the gopis, and over there He will make Radha angry.

“If I stay in this area, I will be able to see Radha’s and Krsna’s pastimes every day!” With total resolve, Giriraja made Pulastya feel the full weight of his body, forcing the sage to forget their agreement.

Assailed by weariness, Pulastya was forced to land in Vraja-mandala. Forgetful of Govardhana’s stipulation, he placed the hill in his eternal resting place, pointing north to south.

The sage rested beneath a desire tree and bathed in the cool Yamuna. Purified and refreshed, he extended his hand and ordered Govardhana, “Stand up! We have yet to reach our destination.”

When Giriraja would not stand up, the sage summoned all his mystic strength, but still he could not lift the hill. He even tried to employ sweet words to entice Govardhana, but the hill would not budge.

In response to Pulastya Muni’s efforts, Govardhana said, “O sage! Do not be angry with me. You have broken your own vow. You agreed not to set me down anywhere but at my final resting place.

“Since you have set me down here, I shall be true to my promise and move no further.” Pulastya became red with anger. His lips trembled as he cursed Govardhana Hill with hurtful words.

“O arrogant mountain! You have frustrated my desire to take you to Kasi. If you are so fond of where you stand, then sink into that earth by the measurement of one sesame seed every day.”

At that, Pulastya departed, and Govardhana began to sink into the ground of Gokula. Still, so long as Giriraja remains even slightly visible, he will forestall the evil effects of Kali-yuga.

Elderly Vaisnavas profess that in their youth Govardhana was taller than he is now, and it is said that at the time of Krsna’s lila, Giriraja’s afternoon shadow reached all the way to Mathura.

Some Vaisnavas assert that this incident took place during a previous day of Brahma, and although Pulastya’s curse still prevails from that previous kalpa, in this age Giriraja appeared by different means.

Scriptural evidence suggests, they say, that Giriraja appeared in Vrndavana during the pastimes of Lord Ramacandra while His monkey devotees were building a stone bridge to Lanka.

The monkeys were collecting boulders from everywhere. But Hanuman was so strong that he was collecting whole mountains, an undertaking that had taken him as far as the Himalayas.

When Hanuman saw the opulences of Giriraja, he thought “This mountain will make a superb addition to the Lord’s stone bridge.” But as hard as Hanuman tried, he could not lift Govardhana.

After Hanuman’s repeated attempts to lift Govardhana had failed, he concluded that the mountain was a divine being with supernatural powers. Thus Hanuman bowed before the mountain to offer prayers.

Hanuman explained Lord Rama’s need for a bridge, and when Govardhana understood that he was to be engaged in the Lord’s service, he at once flew onto Hanuman’s hand, eager to go to Lanka.

As divine fate would have it, however, as Hanuman was flying over Vrndavana, the loud sounds of conch shells blowing announced the completion of Lord Rama’s bridge. No more stones were needed.

Eager to lead the monkeys to war, Hanuman impulsively dropped Govardhana from a great height. When the mountain hit the ground, his outer surface broke into rocks of all sizes, from pebbles to boulders.

Govardhana Hill was very angry that Hanuman would not take him to serve Lord Ramacandra, but the great monkey-sage pacified his newfound devotee-friend by making a prediction.

He said, “Because you are so eager for divine service, when the Lord appears in the next age as a cowherd boy in Vraja, you will become an umbrella to protect Him and His kinsmen from Indra’s wrath.’’

These tidings pacified Govardhana, and as Hanuman flew south to Lanka, Giriraja settled into his new residence in Vrndavana to patiently await Krsna’s future appearance and boyhood pastimes.

When Hanumanji finally returned to Ramacandra and narrated the story of his meeting with Govardhana Hill, the Lord further elaborated on the glories of His foremost servant.

Lord Ramacandra said, “Not only will I lift Govardhana Hill with My hand, but I shall make him the playground of My youth.” Pointing to the stone bridge before Him the Lord continued:

“The inscription of My name and impressions of My feet will liberate these stones, but My pastimes of herding cows, playing with friends, and dancing with gopis will make Giriraja taste ecstasies of prema."

Thus the Adi-varaha Purana says that by Hanumanji’s intervention and the blessing of Lord Ramacandra, Govardhana Hill became a resident of Vraja and Krsna’s indispensable pleasure playground.

These sacred stories of Govardhana’s appearance in this world and his coming to Vraja, when heard with faith, purify a person of the most heinous sins and bestow the most illustrious liberation.

? revered Vaisnavas! Humbly I beg you to make Govardhana Hill your guru, your worshipable deity, object of meditation, only shelter, and ultimate destination. Your life will then know divine perfection.

As promised, let us now hear the third pastime mentioned, which is a great favourite of devotees — the story of how Krsna held Giriraja aloft to protect the Vraja-vasis from a deluge sent by Indra.

One of the reasons Krsna provoked Indra’s wrath was to inaugurate an annual festival known as Govardhana-puja, a festival so important that the Lord ordered all devotees to observe it with care every year.

To depend on the cows and Govardhana Hill, just as the Vraja-vasis in Vrndavana did five thousand years ago, is one of the goals of New Vraja-dhama. For inspiration, then, let us hear this pastime in detail.

May the residents of New Vraja-dhama learn this pastime carefully and teach it to their children. We should know the story of Krsna’s lifting Giriraja as well we know the story of Krsna’s appearance.

Please attentively study and follow the rituals involved in Govardhana- puja, so that the observance of this festival may be ingrained in the traditions of Krsna Valley and the Hungarian yatra.

Here is a mere glimpse of the glories of Govardhana-puja. Those who observe it are blessed with wealth, auspiciousness, and happiness; those who neglect it are cursed with poverty, blindness, and old age.

Let this narration of the sacrifice performed by Krsna’s greatest servants begin. And may the Vaisnavas be pleased with my attempt to pour the nectar of our acaryas’ teachings into their hearts.

**\* \* \* \***

When Krsna was well into His sixth year, Nanda Maharaja moved his family, animals, and subjects from Vrndavana to Nandagrama, drawn by spontaneous attachment to their eternal home.

Late in the month of Asvina, after Krsna had entered His seventh year, the Vraja-vasis began to make preparations for the Indra-yajna. They even left their homes temporarily in order to purify them.

Then, on the dark moon day of Karttika, also known as Dipavali, the ritualistic brahmanas of Bhatrola, who were conducting a fire sacrifice, refused to give the young gopas the food for which they had begged.

The devotee-wives of these brahmanas, however, gave the boys a nice reception and fed them sumptuously with such pure devotion that Krsna blessed the women with confidential attachment to Him.

Stomachs full, the blissful boys gathered their cows together and struck out for home. As they were passing Bhatrola, Krsna saw His father and relatives making arrangements for some kind of sacrifice.

Krsna knew why His father was making elaborate arrangements in the village of the ritualistic brahmanas, because He had previously participated in the sacrifice meant for pleasing Indra.

But Indra had become proud of his limited power, and so Krsna, unhappy that the Vraja-vasis were squandering their devotion on a paltry demigod, requested His father to revise his plans.

Gopala respectfully addressed Nanda Maharaja — that abode of kindness, simplicity, and joy — “O honoured one! What is this great endeavour you are undertaking? What is it meant to accomplish?

“If this is a ritual sacrifice, for which demigod is it meant? And is there scriptural evidence to justify performing it? Please do not think Me unqualified to understand these things just because I am young.

“People who do not know why they are working cannot enjoy success from their efforts, but those who know the purpose behind their work easily obtain success from their labours.”

Disarmed by Krsna’s unexpected eloquence, Nanda Maharaja was silent for a moment. He wondered if he should change the subject to avoid wasting time explaining a complex ritual to such a young boy.

Nanda then remembered Garga Muni’s prediction that Krsna would be “in His qualities equal to Narayana.” If the Muni’s prophecy were true, would any topic be too difficult for Krsna to understand?

Nanda Maharaja lovingly embraced his son and sat down with Him on a small carpet. Nanda’s face glowed with fatherly affection, and his white teeth peeked through his lips as he spoke.

“My dear son! This ritual is a popular custom observed by our family for a very long time. Our cows depend on rain for green grasses, and so we perform the ritual to preserve our wealth—the cows.

“Rain comes from the clouds, which are controlled by the demigod Indra. When he is pleased by our sacrifice, he sends abundant rainfall. In this way, we prosper by his blessings.

“Scriptures say that if we neglect demigods like Indra out of lust or enmity, then we will be ruined. Now I have answered Your questions and described our most important duty. Are You satisfied?”

It pained Krsna to hear His father express such devotion to a demigod, not to mention one who had become so full of himself. But to hide His feelings, Krsna mildly smiled as He thought:

“Why are My eternal companions — My father, relatives, and friends — so caught up in the worship of Indra when they are living with Me, the Supreme Truth, the power behind all the demigods!”

Krsna thus decided to enact a pastime by which He would not only humble Indra but also remind His devotees that they need not divert their attention to Indra or to any other so-called gods.

In His reply to Nanda, Krsna refuted the reasoning His father used to justify the Indra-yajna. And He did so with such eloquence that the Vraja-vasis were not only pleasantly surprised but also mesmerised.

Krsna based His arguments on two philosophies: karma-mimamsa and sankhya. Thus, for the purpose of His pastimes, He appeared to contradict the Vedic conclusions and instead favour atheism.

Finally, Krsna offered an alternative to Indra-puja— Govardhana-puja. He said, “The cows live on grass, and grass is supplied to us by Govardhana Hill. Therefore let us worship the cows and Giriraja.

“And since our family always takes shelter of pure-hearted brahmanas, let the paraphernalia collected for worshipping Indra be utilised in a sacrifice for pleasing the brahmanas as well.”

Nanda Maharaja lovingly placed Krsna upon his lap and replied, “Since You have asked, I will arrange for a separate sacrifice, but just now let me perform this sacrifice for King Indra.”

But Krsna would not be mollified. He did not want an alternate sacrifice. He wanted to break that demigod’s false pride by rededicating the ingredients already collected.

Krsna looked into His father’s eyes and spoke loud enough for all the cowherd men could hear His reply, “My dear father! Don’t delay! To prepare another sacrifice will take too much time.

“Better take the paraphernalia you have collected and the arrangements you have made for the Indra-yajna and engage them at once to satisfy Govardhana Hill and the local brahmanas ."

By this time, a crowd had gathered around Nanda and his son. Some were kinsmen and others not, but all voiced their unanimous opinion after hearing Krsna’s pleasing words and seeing His beautiful face.

The Vraja-vasis said, “Whatever a dearly beloved person requests with pleasing and reasonable words should be accepted by all. We are of one mind that Krsna’s request should be accepted.”

When Nandaraja heard the voice of the people, and when he saw the determined look on Krsna’s moonlike face, he finally relented and enquired from his son how he wanted the yajna performed.

Krsna then instructed the assembly, “Whatever offerings have already been gathered for Indra should be divided into three and offered to the cows, the brahmanas, and Govardhana Hill.

“If a fire sacrifice is needed, it must be conducted by learned brahmanas and offered to Agni, the fire god, for he is the shelter of everyone. And we can see his flames with our own eyes.

“From the grains, milk, and ghee collected for the sacrifice, prepare sumptuous dishes of all descriptions, such as rice, dhal, vegetable soups, halava, pakora, puri, and assorted cooked vegetables.

“You should also make plenty of milk preparations, such as sweet rice, rabri, sweetballs, sandesa, rasagulla, laddu, and a variety of fancy cakes, both baked and fried and soaked in milk or sugar water.

“Set out leaf bowls full of ghee, honey, and nectar drinks, larger containers full of yogurt, buttermilk, and fresh butter, and a mountain of rock candy surrounded by sikharini-rasala.”

Krsna paused to see if the cowherd men were enthusiastic for His new festival. When He saw from the expressions on their faces that they were, He continued to disclose more details.

“After the brahmanas have poured oblations onto the sacrificial fire, feed them sumptuously until they laugh and they cannot eat another bite. Then, give them cows, grains, and money in charity.

“Everyone, including the common people and insignificant animals — like dogs and monkeys — should be sumptuously fed, and the cows should be nicely decorated and well fed with sweet, succulent grasses.

“Through the brahmanas and cows you will worship Govardhana Hill indirectly, and then by offering the hill a beautifully decorated mound made from a variety of foods, you will worship him directly.

“After eating the feast and decorating yourselves with your best clothes, ornaments, and fragrant ointments, you may circumambulate the brahmanas, sacred fire, cows, and finally Govardhana Hill.”

Krsna smiled gently, glanced at His brother Balarama’s peaceful face, and concluded, “I am only a child, yet I request you to do this. The cows, brahmanas, and Govardhana Hill are all very dear to Me.

“Please therefore reject worship of King Indra, which will bring only troubles, and instead, if you are so inclined, perform the sacrifice I have just outlined, which is all-auspicious.

“Persons like you always act with your bodies, words, and minds only to please Me, so in order to please Me now, I humbly request you to conduct this all-important Govardhana-puja.”

Nanda Maharaja beamed with pride to see his son’s wisdom. And when he saw his subjects so favourably disposed towards Krsna’s proposal, he ordered that preparations for the puja begin at once.

That evening, the Vraja-vasis camped by the banks of Manasa-ganga and observed the sacred festival of Dipavali. But all they could talk about was the new sacrifice scheduled for the following day.

Overwhelmed by their love for Krsna, the gopas and gopis were like intoxicated persons, blissfully unaware of the consequences of their actions. They paid no mind to the possible results of neglecting Indra.

Their only desire was to please Krsna. Anything that interfered with that desire had no place in their lives, even if it was an age-old tradition of worshipping the universal controllers, the demigods.

**\* \* \* \***

Early the next morning the Vraja-vasis bathed, dressed and ornamented themselves. Then they decorated their cows with silver bells, pearl necklaces, silk cloths, and plated their horns with gold.

The excited calves ran around and jumped up and down to see their mothers so richly decorated, and the great bulls bellowed in the morning mist as if they were great sages chanting morning prayers.

Nanda Maharaja and Mother Yasoda looked like Indra and his consort as they shepherded their two sons before them, leading an endless train of servants carrying the wealth of the autumn harvest.

Behind Vraja’s monarchs, carrying the ingredients for the sacrifice, came the nine Nandas, the nine Upanandas, and the six Vrsabhanus, all accompanied by their wives, children, and grandchildren.

Next came the cowherd men, adolescents, and hoys, all dressed in colourful garments. Decorated with necklaces and boasting forest garlands, they wore turbans and carried multi-coloured sticks.

Then came the beautiful gopis, whose glories were beyond compare. And the most glorious amongst them was Sri Radha, who wore splendid garments and glistening ornaments and rode in a palanquin.

Radha repeatedly peeked out of Her carrier like a cakora bird, eager to get a glimpse of the Krsna-moon up ahead, a moon that liberally distributed the soothing rays of His smile in all directions.

Lalita and Visakha walked beside the palanquin, followed closely by other gopis faithful to Radha. Behind them were still other gopis, not so closely connected to Radha and all inferior to Her.

Unseen by the Vraja-vasis, covered in ashes, decorated with snakes, intoxicated by affection for Krsna, carrying the Ganges in his matted locks, and riding his carrier Nandi, came Lord Siva.

Having heard of the scheduled worship of Govardhana, rajarsis, yogis, siddhas, and paramahamsas joined the procession one by one, as did the personified forms of many great mountains, like Meru and Drona.

The entire community walked south along Giriraja, singing of Krsna’s exploits, driving their herds in front. No one even noticed as they passed the hillock upon which they would usually hoist Indra’s flag.

Instead of erecting a flagpole at Indra-dhvaja-tirtha, Nanda Maharaja had raised colourful gold-trimmed flags and banners along the entire length of the hundred majestic peaks of Govardhana Hill.

That morning Govardhana Hill — with his jewel stones, golden peaks, palatial caves, nectarean streams, and heavenly grasses — looked especially beautiful, like a young bride on her wedding day.

The valleys of Govardhana resounded with the auspicious sounds of instruments, singing, and Vedic hymns. The forest creatures felt jolly because they revelled in the bliss radiating from the Vraja-vasis.

In the pastures that lay between two lakes, the gopas grazed their cows and bulls, unloaded the festival paraphernalia, and waited for orders from the yajna's sponsor, Nanda, and Nanda’s acarya — Krsna.

The entire scene resembled a lotus waiting to bloom in the warmth of the Krsna sun — the whorl being grave Nanda Maharaja, the inner petals the eager Vraja-vasis, the outer petals the grazing cows.

The reflected rays of the rising sun danced upon Krsna’s tranquil face, bowing repeatedly to the bliss radiating from His reddish eyes, a signal that the lotus of this new pastime was about to blossom.

Nanda Maharaja invited Vedic brahmanas to officiate over the ceremonies. Some of the ingredients had been collected for the Indra- yajna, and the balance was gathered according to Krsna’s orders.

The brahmanas selected a large stone-outcropping to act as proxy for the entire hill. Then they began the ceremony, accompanied by musical instruments, conch shells, and cries of “Jaya! Jaya!”

While chanting auspicious mantras, the brahmanas first worshipped Govardhana Hill with aromatic incense, then a selection of wild flowers, a five-pronged ghee lamp, and finally a camphor lamp.

After completing the arati, the priests anointed Giriraja with cow dung, offered him a soft sitting place, cool refreshing arghya, water to wash his feet, a drink to rinse his mouth, and madhu-parka.

After they had offered him a second mouthwash, the brahmanas chanted the Purusa-sukta. A full abhiseka of Giriraja then began; the Vraja-vasis marvelled and the cows lowed at the sight.

This full bathing was conducted with the purest substances like water from the Yamuna, milk from the best cows, earth from holy places, honey from forest hives, and the richest pancamrta.

The bathing complete, Govardhana was dried with soft towels. Then the brahmanas decorated him with colourfully embroidered cloth, a variety of precious necklaces, and a regal jewel-studded gold crown.

The Vraja-vasis surged forward, holding fine flower garlands they had fashioned for Giriraja, and the brahmanas offered these gifts to him with devotion equal to that with which the garlands were strung.

Once again, articles of arati — especially various ghee lamps — were offered. Meanwhile, the Vraja-vasis made a tumult with their drums and cymbals great enough to give Indra in heaven a terrible earache.

The Vraja-vasis gathered around Govardhana, flowers in hand, to offer puspanjali. As they recited the mantra that begins with vedaham, they showered the hill with grains. Then came the offerings of food:

From the rolling hills of grains, vegetables, sugar, and fruit, and the bottomless lakes of ghee, yogurt, milk, and honey that had filled Nanda’s storehouse, countless preparations had been cooked.

Endless varieties of taste, aroma, texture, and colour graced the multitude of preparations served in leaf-plates, leaf-cups, and clay jugs placed on silk tapestries laid end to end on the ground.

Neither the kings on earth nor the demigods in heaven had ever offered so many succulent preparations in sacrifice, not even to the greatest of demigods, or to the Supreme Being, Lord Narayana.

The soups, breads, vegetables, sweets, cakes, drinks, deep-fried things, and other preparations were grouped according to taste: sour, sweet, salty, bitter, pungent, and astringent.

The area beside the hill, all the way from Dana-nivartana-kunda to Sankarsana-kunda, was so filled with offerings that the cowherds had to shift their animals to the side of the road away from the hill.

The Vraja-vasis were struck with wonder at Nanda Maharaja’s prosperity. They thought, “Only one who is favoured by the wife of Lord Narayana can offer such wealth and still have a full treasury.”

The last offering of food was in the form of a grand hill with peaks, lakes, pastures, and waterfalls made entirely from grains, sweets, and nectar-drinks. Indeed, it appeared to be a second Govardhana Hill.

Crowned with a central peak of camphor, glazed with a golden coating of ghee, scented with cloves, camphor, and cardamom, it appeared exactly like Lord Siva’s Mount Kailasa.

Seeing this delectable mountain of food, called an annakuta, the cowherd men and women offered their obeisances to Govardhana Hill and glorified him with fitting prayers and mantras.

The gopas had placed their full faith in Krsna, and subsequently in Govardhana Hill, yet none of them were prepared for the way in which Krsna and Giriraja would reciprocate that faith.

While still prostrate on the ground, the Vraja-vasis heard a great rumbling like the sound of a hundred thunderclaps. They looked up and saw a beautiful blackish giant standing atop Govardhana Hill.

**\* \* \* \***

Krsna had manifested a colossal second form to authenticate the Vraja-vasis’ faith in Govardhana Hill, a form that was not different from Krsna and therefore irresistibly attractive to behold.

The Vraja-vasis did not know what to make of the giant form before them — resplendent as He was with swarthy complexion, golden garments, glittering ornaments, and a huge flower crown.

The Deity then sat down upon a peak of Govardhana Hill as if it were a throne, directly facing the cowherd men, His eyes rolling in ecstatic bliss, His arms swaying like vines in the wind.

Adding further wonder as to His identity, the beautiful giant could simultaneously be seen seated in one place and spread throughout the length of the entire mountain, like the Lord’s universal form.

Still on their knees, some gopas guessed that this giant was a mirage caused by the overlapping of shadow and light, others that it was the mountain personified, still others that it was Krsna Himself.

The cows and bulls gazed motionless at the towering figure on the hill, grass hanging from their mouths. And the little calves hid under the stomachs of their mothers for protection.

The deer, rabbits, peacocks, parrots, and other creatures living around Govardhana Hill were dumbstruck. The sight of the great form before them made their fur, feathers, and hair stand on end.

Because all the Vraja-vasis were fearful, they dared not say anything, and so Krsna stood up with joined palms and respectfully addressed His own expansion: “O Lord! Who are You?”

At first the divine form again rumbled like thunderclouds and the fearful gopas thought, “Now we are all finished.” But then the giant replied in a hushed voice full of compassion.

“? little boy with graceful limbs! I am Govardhana Hill.” The proclamation resounded throughout Vraja. And all those who heard it were empowered to understand its inner meaning:

“O residents of Vrndavana! I am Govardhana Hill, the presiding Deity of this place. I have appeared because I am pleased with your devotion. Please ask from Me anything that your hearts desire.”

At the sound of these words, the Vraja-vasis lost all fear. Convinced that their faith in Krsna’s instructions had been well placed, an explosion of joy burst from their lips — the sound of “Jaya! Jaya!”

Now that Govardhana had appeared to them in person, the residents of Vraja began to chatter amongst themselves, looking upon the hill with love-filled eyes, as He affectionately gazed upon them in return.

Most of the Vraja-vasis did not think that the gigantic form on Govardhana Hill was Krsna Himself. Indeed, even Radha, who can always detect Krsna’s presence, was fooled by His clever disguise.

To silence His kinsmen, Krsna raised His arms and gravely said, “O residents of Vraja! Let us offer respectful obeisances to this invincible Deity who has manifested His divine form before our very eyes.”

Krsna then fell to the ground and offered prostrated obeisances to Himself in the form of the Deity atop Govardhana Hill. All-knowing Balarama and the carefree cowherd boys immediately followed suit.

Their hearts filled with wonder at the great Deity before them, the cowherd people also offered obeisances, and when they rose with joined palms, Govardhana filled their hearts with bliss.

Giriraja said, “O people of Vraja! I am not like other deities who remain aloof from their worshippers. I reciprocated your faith by appearing before you, and now I will personally eat your offerings.”

Speaking thus the personified form of Govardhana Hill extended His right hand and began to eat all the food that had been offered to Him: rice, vegetables, sweets, savouries, and drinks.

Having forgotten their fear, the Vraja-vasis ran to whatever the Deity was eating and stood in the shadow of His hand, gazing affectionately at His face and cheering Him on in a good-natured way.

The spontaneous devotion of the Vraja-vasis encouraged the Deity of Govardhana Hill to eat more and more; with each mouthful He ate, He grew larger and larger, and more and more blissful.

Govardhana would eat with His right hand while motioning with His left and saying, “Bring me more! Bring me more!” (Ani aur! Ani aur !) Thus that place eventually came to be known as Aniyora.

Nanda Maharaja’s followers saw Govardhana eating only the offerings they had placed at the foot of the hill. They could not see that He was also eating offerings from as far away as Nandagrama.

With thousands and millions of arms, both short and long, reaching out in all directions, the Deity of Govardhana Hill ate all the offerings made to Him, whether offered directly or in meditation.

That great Deity filled all of Vraja with joy. He inspired devoted saints, wonderstruck adults, smiling youths, and frightened children alike with His love, as between bites He sometimes laughed at Indra.

The Vraja-vasis from all the villages in the area emptied their stocks of fruits, grains, vegetables, and milk products to feed the gigantic Deity of Govardhana Hill, but He just kept demanding more.

Finally, when they had nothing else to cook and offer — out of fear of offending the Deity— they looked to Balarama for help. The Lord then offered a tulasi leaf to the giant Deity, who was finally satisfied.

When He had finished eating everything, the Deity of Govardhana rinsed His mouth with water from a nearby lake and spit it out behind Him, causing a lush savannah to magically appear.

Finally, He picked His teeth with bamboo canes and sweetened His mouth by chewing a few cartloads of betel nuts — His beautiful face glowing with satisfaction and the touch of the morning sun.

The meal complete, it was time to offer arati, but the brahmanas and Vraja-vasis were at a loss as they scrambled to find a lamp large enough to befit the size of their massive worshipable Deity.

They discussed possible alternatives amongst themselves without reaching a conclusion. Finally Krsna manifested a gigantic tray with thousands of flames, which the gopas lifted and offered en masse.

**\* \* \* \***

The prescribed worship of Giriraja now complete, the cowherd men and women danced and sang in ecstasy, filled with bliss at having seen and served the personal form of Govardhana Hill.

Giriraja was also very satisfied with the devotional sacrifice prepared by the Vraja-vasis. He offered them benediction after benediction, fulfilling all their pure-hearted desires for service to Krsna.

Then, under the watchful eyes of the Deity of Govardhana, the blissful people of Vrndavana began to worship the cows and bulls, whom they dearly loved, and who dearly loved them.

The cows were nicely ornamented, set free to mingle with their calves, and offered the best grasses, but strangely enough they would not eat. One wise cowherd then called out to Krsna.

“O Gopala!” said the cowherd, “Our cows will not eat unless You sweeten these grasses with the touch of Your lotus hands. Nothing else will satisfy them. Please come fill each cow with delight.”

Krsna at once responded by expanding Himself into as many forms as there were cows. While speaking sweet words or making soft sounds, He fed them all and scratched their heads with His own hands.

When the cows saw Krsna, He entered their hearts. They were filled with such bliss that that they seemed indifferent to their calves, in much the same way that prema-yogis become indifferent to this world.

To conclude their worship of the cows, the Vraja-vasis were supposed to circumambulate them all, but that presented a problem: there were so many cows that to go around them all was impossible.

Krsna therefore suggested that they circumambulate only one cow, and this would yield the same auspicious benefit as circumambulating them all. In the same way, they worshipped the bulls.

Krsna argued, “Just as one nourishes all the leaves and branches of a tree by watering its roots, so we can simultaneously adore all our cows and bulls by circumambulating just one cow and one bull.”

After Krsna had thus gratified all the cows and hulls, He and His friends worshipped the calves by decorating them with cloth, painting pictures on their bodies, and then blissfully frolicking with them.

Krsna made the frisky calves lay down to rest by making special sounds with His transcendental flute. Then the gopas circumambulated the calves and offered them respectful obeisances.

Krsna rose to His feet and looked at the Vraja-vasis as He explained why obeisances should be offered to the cows and bulls: “At the heart of devotion to the cows is the act of offering obeisances to them.”

The people of Vraja reasoned that worship of the cows included worship of the cowherds, and so they elaborately bathed, dressed, ornamented, and garlanded Krsna, Balarama, and Their friends.

After the cows, calves, and cowherds had been duly worshipped, Krsna, freshly attired, led the Vraja-vasis in circumambulating the golden sacrificial arena, keeping the fire on their right side.

During the course of the sacrifice the brahmanas had been offering such huge quantities of ghee into the sacrificial fire that people feared the fire god would be unable to digest the oblations.

Finally the last oblation was poured and the agni-hotra-yajna was complete. Nanda Maharaja then graciously offered the brahmanas gifts of cows, silk, grains, land, and gold, saying, “Please accept this.”

Nanda Maharaja’s son was the husband of the goddess of fortune, therefore no matter how much wealth the king distributed in charity, his treasury simply grew larger and larger.

Even though the brahmanas were completely satisfied with their gifts, Krsna and Nandaraja directed the cowherd men to make all the arrangements needed to feed these best of the twice-born.

And eat they did! The brahmanas ate until they laughed in ecstasy and perspired, until the wealth of the demigods seemed insignificant to them, until they could not eat even one more grain of rice.

While the brahmanas rested in the shade of trees or awnings, Nanda Maharaja fed his guests to their full satisfaction, regardless of whether they were king or untouchable, buffalo or dog, tiger or deer.

The great king of the cowherds occasionally cast a glance at the blackish Deity of Govardhana, who watched the proceedings with great satisfaction, nodding in approval from time to time.

Finally Nanda Maharaja’s clan, with Krsna and Balarama in the centre, sat beneath the woodland canopy of a small forest and honoured the remnants of the feast amidst much joking and bliss.

As always the Vraja-vasis observed their meal not to satisfy their senses, but as a means to keep company with Krsna; partaking in Krsna’s pastimes of love is what truly nourishes the residents of Vraja.

After finishing their meal, which of course included Madhumangala’s antics and Mother Yasoda’s maternal fussing over Krsna’s appetite, the Vraja-vasis rested. Then they all donned their finest attire.

Krsna pointed to the Deity of Govardhana who was sitting casually on the Hill. Joining His palms, Krsna reverently said, “This hill has appeared in person and bestowed His mercy upon us.

**“Govardhana Hill can assume any form He likes in order to punish those who neglect Him. Therefore, let us pay our obeisances to Him for the safety of our cows and ourselves.”**

The Vraja-vasis obeyed Krsna’s command and bowed to Govardhana Hill. When they stood up, the Deity offered to fulfil all their unspoken desires and then disappeared, just as He had appeared.

Again everyone fell down like sticks, offering respects to Giriraja, and for a few moments there was complete silence — even the leaves on the trees would not rustle, nor the birds on their branches sing.

The Vraja-vasis finally rose to their feet, sad that the personality of Govardhana was no longer visible. Still, Krsna spiritedly encouraged them to circumambulate the hill, and they again became jolly.

At the start of the parikrama, Nandaraja again rewarded the brahmanas with untold wealth. And once again he gave gifts in charity to all his guests, including the singers, dancers, and beggars.

King Nanda gazed into Krsna’s eyes and saw a reflection of his own form, bathed by Krsna’s tears of ecstasy. Nandaraja felt as though he could single-handedly conquer the kingdom of heaven.

Nanda Maharaja turned towards the cowherd men and signalled them to begin circling Govardhana Hill. As he did so, a clamour — rattling carts, bellowing cows, and cheering gopas — rose to the skies.

But some of the brahmanas were exhausted, having eaten to excess. As a way of expressing their ecstatic love for Krsna, those brahmanas declined to circumambulate Govardhana Hill.

Nanda Maharaja was careful not to displease his son or the Deity of Govardhana. Courteously requested by Nanda to rise to the occasion and perform their prescribed duty, the saints replied:

“O great king! We would prefer to offer benedictions, rather than circumambulate the hill. But if it is your order, then somehow or other we shall move our feet in the direction you desire.”

Dasa Gosvami describes the worship of Giriraja,

*vrajendra-varyarpita-bhogam uccair dhrtva brhat kayam agharir utkah*  
*varena radham chalayan vibhunkte yatranna-kutam tad aham prapadye*

**“When Vraja’s king offered a great quantity of food to Govardhana Hill, Krsna, the enemy of Aghasura, assumed a gigantic form, proclaiming that He was actually Govardhana Hill.**

**“Thus He fooled even Srimati Radharani. He then invited everyone to ask boons from Him and ate all the offered food. Let me take shelter of that festival in which Lord Krsna enjoyed these pastimes.”**

**\* \* \* \***

The assembled Vraja-vasis proceeded in a certain order: first came the white cows, then the other cows, then the brahmanas, then the community leaders, and then the leaders’ assistants.

After the assistants came the wives of the leaders, then the young gopis, the maidservants, artisans of various guilds, and finally a host of others, which included the forest animals, who also tagged along.

In front of the cows — indeed leading the entire procession — were skilled percussionists expertly playing their drums. They succeeded in animating the joyous atmosphere with their pleasing rhythms.

Some of the cowherd boys, the ones who had decorated each other with kunkuma designs, deftly wielded sticks with which they fearlessly drove the surging waves of white and coloured cows.

Behind those boys followed another group of talented musicians playing flutes and vinas, behind them were honey-voiced singers, and following the singers were row upon row of beautiful dancing girls.

Amongst the women who sat in elaborately decorated and ornamented bullock carts, were young cowherd girls whose winsome faces glowed with happiness as they sang of Krsna’s pastimes.

May all the residents of Krsna Valley learn and sing the lyrics sung by these gopis. The song they sang is greatly sanctified prose that purifies the hearts of the sadhakas and gives immense joy to the siddhas.

Towards the front of the procession, behind an opulently decorated herd of cows, were Krsna and Balarama, swaggering with the gait of young elephants as Their friends swarmed around Them like bees.

Krsna appeared like a transcendental rainbow resting on an autumn cloud. His limbs were dusky, His garments golden, His body paintings vermilion, His ornaments white, green, and red.

He carried a rope on His shoulder and a flute tucked in His belt. And even as He chased stray cows, He twirled an effulgent stick that He repeatedly tossed in the air and then caught before it hit the ground.

Extremely pleased with the worship of Govardhana Hill, Krsna smiled at His parents, laughed with His friends, clapped His hands, and played sublimely beautiful melodies on His bamboo flute.

Those meandering flute-songs wove through the throng of pilgrims, entered their ears, and polished their hearts with the varnish of loving attachment to Nanda Maharaja’s son.

After some time Krsna displayed restlessness by darting here and there, drawing the eyes of the Vraja-vasis to Him like a puppet-master moves his puppets by tugging on their strings.

Radha was in the close vicinity of elders, and so She had to keep Her face covered, but an occasional peek through Her veil found Krsna’s flying baton. In this way She kept track of where He was.

Nanda Maharaja tried to stay close to his restless son. In his efforts, the colourful garland on his chest wilted and jewel-like beads of perspiration glistened on his handsome brow.

The great king of Vraja smiled broadly from the satisfaction of having worshipped Giriraja, and from the joy of having Krsna as his son. The leading men of Vraja walking beside Nanda shared his fatherly bliss.

At different places along the parikrama path Nanda Maharaja gave charity to brahmanas, and in return he received from his son the invaluable benediction of Krsna’s sidelong loving glance.

Govardhana on their right, Krsna in their midst, and the nectar-mist of flute music everywhere, the Vraja-vasis were immersed in wave after wave of bliss. They lost all awareness of time’s passing.

To describe the bliss the Vraja-vasis felt is impossible, but devotees who sincerely worship Govardhana Hill will know that bliss, according to their faith in the eternal residents of Vraja.

Walking and walking, the cowherds returned to the place of the sacrifice, where they enjoyed hearing songs and music, watched graceful dance performances, and then happily lay down to rest.

In their sleep the Vraja-vasis dreamed of Govardhana Hill and its beautiful personal form, and of Krsna’s inimitable pastimes during the Annakuta festival and subsequent parikrama.

Little did the Vraja-vasis know that as they slept comfortably in their tents, Radha and Krsna strolled through Govardhana’s secluded gardens, sharing secret pastimes known only to Their intimate friends.

The day after Govardhana-puja was the Bhratr-dvitiya festival, in which brothers and sisters show their love for each other, following the footsteps of Yamaraja and his sister Yamuna.

To observe this festival, the Vraja-vasis came to the shore of the Kalindi, heard her glories, offered her obeisances, and bathed in her sacred waters. Then all the brothers received sweets from their sisters.

Sunanda, the daughter of Upananda, dabbed sandalwood on the foreheads of Krsna and Balarama and fed Them a variety of tasty sweets. In return, the boys offered her new clothes and ornaments.

After seeing this affectionate exchange, Srimati Radharani also placed a dot of sandalwood on Sridama’s forehead, offered him a garland and sweets, then bowed down before him with great respect.

Sridama gifted Radha with new silks and jewellery, and then, to the accompaniment of drum music, all the brothers and sisters enacted the same rite on the bank of the Yamuna with affectionate happiness.

At the completion of this exchange of gifts in love, Nandaraja drove his entourage in bullock carts to Sahar, where Upananda had arranged an opulent feast in honour of the day’s festival.

Sunanda-gopi again arranged for Krsna and His friends to be served many tasty dishes, while Madhumangala, between bites, entertained everyone with his jokes and shenanigans, for example:

“O Krsna! What a foolish creator we have. Had he made every day of the year Bhratr-dvitiya, and had he given You more sisters like Sunanda, we could then constantly enjoy delectable feasts like this!”

When the gopas, even Madhumangala, had been silenced by a great quantity of the four kinds of delicious foods, served by Sunanda and her assistants, the brothers and sisters again exchanged valuable gifts.

Then, on the plea of walking through the gardens of Upananda’s kingdom, Krsna left the resting Vraja-vasis to meet Radharani and Her friends in a secluded forest garden some call Yoga-pitha.

Krsna absorbed the gopas and gopis of all ages in worshipping Govardhana Hill in so many wonderful ways that everyone completely forgot that there had ever been an Indra-puja.

Meanwhile, in heaven, Narada Muni informed Indra that—precipitated by Krsna’s disparaging remarks about him — the Indra-yajna had been replaced by a festival for Govardhana Hill.

Indra was seated in an assembly of powerful devas and voluptuous Apsaras. Publicly humiliated, his face marred by dejection, he thought, “How could they simply cancel my sacrifice!”

The complete neglect shown by the Vraja-vasis and the insulting words of Krsna so agitated Indra that he developed a terrible headache for which even the Asvini physicians had no cure.

Indra foolishly concluded that he could alleviate his pain by taking revenge against Nanda Maharaja and the villagers of Vraja, especially the talkative boy who had deluded His elders.

Indra’s lips trembled in anger as he called for the Samvartaka clouds, the clouds responsible for universal destruction. He then outlined to them a wicked plan to destroy Vrndavana, its residents, and its cows.

**\* \* \* \***

King Indra said, “O my faithful servants! ? powerful clouds, able to destroy the fourteen planetary systems. If you are truly loyal to me, then hear what I have to say and act accordingly.

“The cowherd men of Vrndavana, headed by their king, Nanda, have stopped worshipping me at the instigation of a little boy called Krsna, whom they have accepted as their only shelter and support.

“Overtaken by false pride, they have abandoned worship of me in favour of worshipping a mountain called Govardhana. Thus they have offended me, whom the goddess of learning constantly praises.

“I therefore order you to let loose incessant rains upon Vrndavana and destroy its cows. The milk those cows produce is the sole means of livelihood for that king Nanda and his followers.

“The torrential rains and hurricane-force winds you dispatch, combined with the thunderbolts and lightning I hurl alongside you, will kill all the cows and residents of Vrndavana.

“In this way, the brief reign of talkative, arrogant Krsna will come to an end, and the world and the heavens will realise the futility of taking shelter of Him, an ordinary human child.”

Indra burned with rage ignited by his frustrated pride, by his plan to persecute the innocent, and by his offences to the Supreme Lord. He stood upon his throne before the assembly in his court and shouted:

“Mischievous cowherds! You have listened to the false words of a talkative child. You foolishly ignored my worship and brought ruin upon your own heads. Let us see this Krsna give you shelter now.”

His eyes red with fury, Indra stared expectantly at the clouds assembled before him in personified forms. Their leader, Samvartaka said, “As always, we will carry out your order without fail.”

The clouds of destruction made their way towards Vrndavana, committed to their task and vainly thinking that they would be able to punish the Vraja-vasis, who were securely protected by Krsna.

Gradually, from the north, towering black clouds charged across the sky accompanied by thunder, lightning, and icy winds. Then many ominously-coloured clouds converged on Vraja from all directions.

Light rains quickly grew into an incessant downpour. Massive columns of water fell to the earth, forming turbulent rivers and lakes in Vrndavana’s pasturelands, hills, and forests.

As the clouds became increasingly thicker, dropping hailstones the size of boulders, the entire atmosphere became horrifyingly dark, like the intense blackness created by the universal shell.

Winds tore at mountains and uprooted trees. Loosed from their moorings, the twelve directions scattered like dry leaves, making it impossible to discern what was north, south, up, or down.

Rumbling thunder reverberated like the sound of a continuous avalanche, intensifying the chaos. Intense lighting and oppressive darkness alternated in such quick succession as to blind all creatures.

The earth became waterlogged as the lakes and rivers overflowed. Their waters continued to rise, and rise, and rise until high ground could no longer be distinguished from low.

The distressed birds were being repeatedly thrown to the ground by high winds, and the forest animals were miserable because they could find no refuge from the pounding rains that pummelled their bodies.

The calves shivered in the cold rain and lowed piteously as they ran for shelter underneath their mothers’ bellies, while the bulls shook their humps and horns as if to fight off the deluge, all to no avail.

Unable to move, the cows appeared like statues soon to be adrift in the steadily rising floodwaters. Their hair stood on end, their jaws remained immobile, and their milk bags and stomachs contracted.

The gopas and gopis were terrified by this unseasonable and unprecedented tempest, thinking that it surely signalled the end of Nandaraja’s dynasty, indeed, of the entire cowherd community.

Such adversity made Maharaja Nanda despondent. Summoning his wife and her attendants he said, “O dear one! You must take the children and other women and at once travel to a distant place.
