“The hale and hearty will remain here, leaving only when faced with certain death.” After issuing this command, Nanda Maharaja began to invoke the names of Hari to protect his sons and people.

No sooner had the king of the cowherds spoken these words than he was forced to retract them. The rapidly intensifying storm made his inner palace the only haven that his dependents could reach safely.

The Vraja-vasis could understand that they were being punished for having stopped the long-standing tradition of worshipping the king of heaven. Naturally, they turned to their king, Nanda, for protection.

But Nanda Maharaja was absorbed in trying to placate Indra by chanting a prayer from the Kauthumi-sakha. Finally, the Vraja-vasis and their cows turned to their only sanctuary and solace — Krsna.

“O Lord of Gokula!” said the Vraja-vasis. “Please save the cows, now on the verge of death, from the wrath of Indra. And since You are affectionate to Your devotees, please save us as well.”

The young calves looked to Krsna with saddened eyes, as if to humbly say, “O Lord! Please protect us.” And Gopala, His eyes brimming with tears, glanced at them in reply as if to say, “Do not fear!”

As the Supreme Soul in everyone’s heart, Krsna was well aware of Indra’s misguided plan to destroy Vrndavana. To calm and reassure His kinsmen, Krsna then spoke these words:

“Dear friends! No doubt this calamitous situation is the work of King Indra, but such adversity becomes insignificant and, by My grace, is easily overcome for one who is surrendered to Me.”

As Krsna was speaking, He overheard His father reciting the forty-six names of Indra like Sacisa and Vajrahasta. Upset by this continued mood of dependence on the demigods, Krsna exclaimed:

“Father! Are we such cowards that we must pray to this Indra, whom I can pulverise into dust in a moment? Put aside your fear and watch My pastimes unfold as I crush this paltry insect’s mountainous pride.”

The Vraja-vasis were incredulous when they heard Krsna’s challenge. But despite their scepticism and the painful onslaught of hail and wind, Krsna’s beautiful smiling face made them forget their misery.

Externally Krsna remained calm, but internally He raged, thinking, “This tempest is Indra’s revenge upon us for having stopped his sacrifice, a sacrifice which is rightfully meant to please Me.

“Because he has become proud of his opulence, instead of thinking of himself as My servant, Indra, along with some of his demigod colleagues, falsely thinks himself to be the lord of the universe.

“Now I will destroy this ignorance and give Indra relief from his false prestige. And simultaneously I will protect My family and My home — the cowherd community and the land of Vrndavana.”

His mind made up, Krsna again addressed the gopas and gopis, “Earlier we worshipped Govardhana Hill. And he appeared in person to accept our offerings. Let us now turn again to that infallible Deity.

“O cowherd men of Vraja! We have not a moment to waste. At once gather your dependents, cows, and valuables and hurry to Govardhana Hill. He will certainly save us from Indra’s fury.”

Protected by their exclusive faith in Krsna, the Vraja-vasis followed His orders without a second thought. They at once left the shelter of their homes for the exposed and flooded pastures of Govardhana.

The residents of Vraja trudged through the wind and rain to Annakuta-ksetra at the foot of Govardhana Hill, sought shelter in the caves of the great mountain, and waited for Krsna to arrive.

**\* \* \* \***

Meanwhile, Krsna had stopped beneath a large kadamba tree a few kilometres southeast of Annakuta-ksetra to consult some intimate friends, like Stokakrsna, who had come along with Him.

Initially Krsna had thought to destroy Indra’s kingdom, but after deeply pondering the situation, He decided that rather than simply overpower Indra, He would showcase His compassion for His kin.

It was then that Krsna formulated the idea of using Govardhana Hill as an umbrella to shelter the Vraja-vasis. Beneath the rain-drenched boughs of that kadamba tree, He exuberantly revealed His plan.

His friends, however, thought that trying to lift a mountain was too risky a venture. They would not give their permission, despite Krsna’s past triumphs over one mighty demon after another.

To convince the gopas that He was up to the task, Krsna joined two branches of the tree to form a circle and in the process twisted its trunk. Duly impressed, the gopas and Madhu blessed Him for the task.

Krsna’s superhuman feat and the gopas’ reactions to it are described in amusing detail in the appendix entitled “Krsna Convinces the Gopas That He Can Lift Govardhana Hill.”

Krsna finally met up with the Vraja-vasis, and they at once noticed that His turban, hair, limbs, and garments were untouched by the rain and wind. Yogamaya had shielded Him as He ran to Giriraja.

The Vraja-vasis ventured out from the caves, crevasses, and forests of Govardhana and surrounded their beloved Krsna, like harassed citizens taking shelter of their heroic king.

When Krsna saw the pitiable condition of His kinsmen and their domestic animals, His heart was pulled in two directions as He felt empathy for the Vraja-vasis and outrage at Indra.

Without taking the time even to tighten His belt — as was His habit before confronting a challenge — Krsna approached the tail end of Govardhana Hill, touched it with respect, and offered pranamas.

As the Vraja-vasis looked on, unable to understand what Krsna had in mind, Hari cast a love-laden glance at His parents to assuage their fears and then disappeared within a deep cave of the mountain.

Krsna knew that the Vraja-vasis were horrified and confused at losing sight of Him, their beloved treasure, and so He moved at lightning speed into the heart of the cave beneath the centre of the hill.

Before any of the Vraja-vasis had time to follow Him, faint, or give up their lives out of shock, Krsna executed His plan and gently, as if picking up a baby calf, began to lift Govardhana Hill.

The Vraja-vasis watched in awe as the mountain rose into the air. Directly beneath Govardhana they saw Krsna standing in a threefold bending pose, left arm upraised, nonchalantly holding the hill aloft.

As the Vraja-vasis beheld butter-soft Krsna standing beneath the gigantic mountain, their bodily hairs stood on end. Though at first stunned, they soon began to swoon in loving concern.

To allay their fear, Krsna at once addressed them in a very sweet and affectionate voice. Somehow He spoke to each person individually, cutting through the terrible sounds of thunder and fury.

He began by saying, “O Mother! ? Father!” with a long dramatic pause between each word. Then He continued with a smile: “O saintly people! Bring all your possessions and come here.”

Krsna pulled Balarama to His side, as if to encourage His kinsmen. And even though He had not finished raising the hill, He persisted: “Father! Mother! Please, don’t worry about Me in the slightest.”

Verbally, Krsna had not asked His brother for permission to lift the hill, but in a subtle, sublime way the two had communicated, and Lord Balarama had given His blessings. Krsna continued:

“This king of mountains is enjoying the bliss of floating in the air. He feels to Me no heavier than a puff of cotton! I am not exerting Myself in the least, so please stay calm and peaceful.”

Krsna held Govardhana as effortlessly as a child holds a mushroom, but because the mountain was trembling in ecstasy at His touch, Krsna asked the cowherd boys to steady the hill with their sticks.

Mother Yasoda saw Govardhana Hill shake and was overcome by concern for Her son’s safety. She wanted to run and embrace Him, but she was stunned with a sort of fear only parents can understand.

This fear brought tears to her eyes, tears that completely obstructed her view of Krsna in danger. Then her throat choked up. She could not even call out and help Him to avert this dangerous calamity.

Only the reassuring words of friends like Rohini kept Yasoda from sinking into a dark sea of despair, in whose murky depths lay certain death. Luckily, they raised her again to the surface of consciousness.

Govardhana’s many peaks, decorated by jewel-like stones, cloaked by a green blanket of trees, and garlanded with billowing clouds, truly made Giriraja appear like Vrndavana’s magnificent crown.

Krsna saw that the floodwaters had begun to stream into the depression under Govardhana Hill, and so He mentally called upon Lord Sesa and the Sudarsana cakra to help Him.

Searing with the heat of ten million suns, the cakra hovered around the base of Govardhana and dried up the rain streaming down the hill, not unlike Agastya Muni once dried up the ocean by drinking it.

Then Ananta Sesa coiled Himself around the lip of the depression beneath Govardhana and held back the incoming floodwaters like the shoreline stops the ocean from coming onto dry land.

Neither Sesa nor the cakra were visible to the Vraja-vasis, who remained stunned at the sight of the great mountain hovering before them simply by the mystical touch of their beloved Krsna.

Eager that His clan be sheltered from the elements, Krsna urged them, “O residents of Vraja! You can now come beneath Govardhana Hill, along with your cows and possessions.

“Beneath the hill you will be safe from this terrible storm, as no rain or mud can enter. In fact, this place is like a subterranean heavenly kingdom you can easily access by those broad stairways.”

Some people began to cautiously advance towards the cavernous basin where Krsna stood, but then turned back when Mother Yasoda insisted that her son would not be able to continue holding the hill.

Slightly irritated, Krsna continued, “Don’t listen to My mother, who mistakenly thinks that Govardhana Hill can slip from My hand; rather, enter beneath its shelter at once.”

Just as Krsna spoke these words, Srimati Radharani caught His eye. He briefly faltered and the Hill shook on His hand. The Vraja-vasis took this as evidence that perhaps Krsna’s mother was right.

Meanwhile, Radha turned and whispered to Visakha, “Look at how Krsna keeps His right hand on His hip and beams an enchanting smile even while holding the brother of Mount Mandara like a ball.

“The restless bumblebee of His sidelong glance causes the lotus flower of My heart to tremble.” With great effort Krsna tore His eyes from those of His beloved in order to fully concentrate on the job at hand.

To encourage His townsfolk, Krsna fully extended His arm like an elephant holds out its trunk to show off a lotus. Then to mock Indra further, He slid Govardhana Hill onto the very tip of His little finger.

The motion caused by Krsna raising the hill higher created a tumultuous sound that echoed in every direction, as if Govardhana Hill were laughing at Indra’s insignificant war machines.

Again Krsna invited the Vraja-vasis to join Him. This time the subjects of Vraja’s king gathered at what had been the foot of Govardhana Hill and peered into the cavernous basin below.

**\* \* \* \***

The Vraja-vasis were shocked at what they saw: rows of elegant, ornate staircases, beyond them an effulgent valley whose opulence shamed the celestial gardens that surround Mount Meru.

A divine, Vaikuntha-like serenity pervaded the realm beneath the hill. The effects of the storm, still raging just outside that safe haven, were conspicuous by their complete and total absence.

The sides of that subterranean valley were studded with glowing, glistening jewels that illuminated the entire area with the same soft, comforting glow that lights the palace of Lord Narayana.

The floor was a mosaic of uncommon gems, gems not merely effulgent but also pleasingly soft and velvety to the touch. The entire area was divided to separately accommodate men, women, and cows.

Sitting places, couches, and groves for resting abounded, offering their comforts to the Vraja-vasis, while verdant pastures of soft sweet grass and pools of clear water offered their services to the cows.

A soft breeze bearing the scents of kadamba flowers and sandalwood blew gently through the vale beneath Govardhana, refreshing both the bodies and minds of everyone, weary from their torturous ordeal.

The Vraja-vasis saw that both they and their possessions — like carts, household goods, and animals — could easily be accommodated in comfort and opulence unmatched even on the planet Sutala.

Moreover, they noted with pleasant surprise many things gratifying to the eyes, ears, and nose — creature comforts far surpassing those of their own homes, which were now submerged in Indra’s liquid envy.

Gazing up at Govardhana’s underside, the people of Vraja saw it to be like a gigantic peacock in flight, expanding and contracting to show that it could easily accommodate the residents in all three worlds.

Once again Krsna’s sweet voice touched the Vraja-vasis’ ears and hearts. “O friends! You saw how Giriraja appeared in person and easily consumed the mountains of food you offered to him.

“Giriraja only appears to be a mountain; in reality he is a great transcendentalist. Why, then, should it be difficult for him to become weightless and sit upon My finger like a butterfly?

“How else could a child pick him up so easily? Govardhana Hill is completely independent. He does whatever he wants. I am just an instrument, a puppet moved by the strings of his will.

“Please, therefore, collect your property and at once come under the mountain. Here you will be more comfortable and more peaceful than you could be in your own homes in Vrndavana.”

Upon hearing these reasonable words, the Vraja-vasis entered the valley under Govardhana Hill, not so much because they sought Krsna’s protection, but because they desired, as always, to serve Him.

Their exuberant conversations, the bellowing of the cows, and the rumbling of clouds punctuated the cowherds’ descent beneath Govardhana Hill, causing a grand commotion.The Vraja-vasis’ parked bullock carts loaded with valuables, house-hold items, and cooking utensils beneath an area near the centre of the hill and stationed armed watchmen all around.

Then they divided their cows into herds and brought them to graze on the luscious green grasses in the pastures that were located mostly along the perimeter of their newfound home.

Once beneath Giri Govardhana no one could even recall the raging tempest outside and the suffering it had so recently caused. Now the minds of the people of Vraja were absorbed completely in Krsna.

Krsna stood upon a large crystal boulder, which placed His feet at the shoulder level of an average gopa. He was of medium stature, but the base of the hill resting in Krsna’s hand was at a height of one tala.

From His left lotus foot, which was planted squarely upon this dais, to the extended little finger of His left hand, Krsna was as straight as a sapphire column, but His right side was as curved as a snake.

The tips of His right toes, crossing over His left leg, barely touched His perch, and His right hand rested upon His hip, making that elbow seem to compete with the knee below — which could bend the most?

The colour of Krsna’s body was the same as the rain clouds outside, yet there was a gulf of difference in their effects: His bodily hue was soothing to the eyes, whereas the sight of the clouds was distressful.

The sound of Krsna’s voice and the sound of the thunder outside were the same, but again they were not the same: the tenor of His voice was pleasing, whereas the rumble of the clouds was terrifying.

The bright yellow flashes of Krsna’s dhoti were the same as the flashes of lightning outside, but His dazzling cloth assured safety to the people of Vraja, whereas the lightning signalled imminent danger.

For the people of Vrndavana, Krsna was always their shelter from peril. Now that He had rescued them once again, they crowded around to gaze at Him with a renewed and special affection.

Free from all anxiety, the Vraja-vasis stood peacefully in separate groups: the cowherd men mixed with the brahmanas, while the elderly married ladies stayed with their daughters.

Radhika and the other young married gopis stayed with their inlaws, friends like Madhumangala stood by Krsna’s side, and Nanda Maharaja, Mother Yasoda, and Balarama stood right in front of Him.

Eyes anointed with intense love, the cowherd men and women thought, “Until now we have never fully appreciated the magnificent elegance of Krsna’s body or the perfect beauty of His face.”

The smallest features of Krsna’s appearance and attire were like milestones on their journey to such full appreciation. They talked, for instance, about how casually Krsna’s hand rested upon His hip.

They said, “Look at how His right thumb is tucked into the blazing red cloth around His handsome hips, made more opulent by the three beautiful lines of His belly and His favourite instrument, the flute.”

And when lightning from beyond the shelter of Govardhana created a flickering shadow of Krsna upon the ground, one Vraja-vasi added, “Just see how the beauty of His right side decorates the earth.”

Krsna felt as much love for the Vraja-vasis as they felt for Him, and never before had all of Vrndavana’s residents — human or otherwise— felt such total dependence upon Him all at one time.

As Krsna’s eyes roamed here and there, His glance, which was dusted with pollen from the blue lotuses over His ears and sweetened by the smile on His camphor-scented lips, fell lovingly upon His kinsmen.

The Vraja-vasis surrounded Krsna on all sides, but they could all see His beautiful face as if they were standing directly in front of Him. And each of them thought He was returning his or her gaze alone.

Such were the exchanges between lover and beloved that one is left to wonder whether prema had personally orchestrated this pastime simply to give both subjects and object a fresh taste of divine love.

Outside Govardhana’s shelter, torrential rains, roaring thunder, icy winds, and dense darkness raged unabated, but inside, only dry comfort, sweet talks, pleasing breezes, and charming light prevailed.

All this was due to the presence of wonderful, glorious, and handsome Krsna, who initiated all varieties of loving exchange with His purehearted devotees and thus made everything pleasing and auspicious.

In this way the underbelly of Govardhana Hill became a wonderful palace of colourful jewels in which Krsna, His arm raised, was the central sapphire pillar, glistening with the radiance of love.

And the roof of that palace, spread in all directions, was Govardhana Hill, who was like a great umbrella, with the falling rain appearing as strings of pearls hanging from the umbrella’s sides.

At times, to ease their anxiety, Krsna playfully spoke to the people; at other times, He played His bamboo flute with His free hand; and at still others, He rested that hand on the shoulder of a friend.

Yasoda and Rohini would take turns stroking His lotus face as only mothers can, while Nandaraja and his brothers gazed at Krsna with their heads held high and their hearts swelling with pride.

Balarama spoke pleasing words and made artful gestures to encourage His brother, while entertainers danced and sang, the wealthy gave charity, brahmanas recited mantras, and children prayed for Him.

Unable to reciprocate in the ways that He normally would, Krsna let His expressive eyes, smile, and tears offer respect to His superiors, enchant His peers, and show affection to His subordinates.

Krsna glanced briefly at each person in a way perfectly befitting his or her relationship with Him, and finally He cast restless, playful glances at the gopis and piercing, angry glances in Indra’s direction.

Through His eyes, the holder of Govardhana Hill flooded the residents of Vrndavana with the nectarean light flowing from His face, and thus they were fully satisfied on all levels.

The cows and bulls, also content, felt no cold, thirst, hunger, or fatigue. What then of their masters, who were filled with varieties of divine love for Krsna, inconceivable even to the greatest demigods?

**\* \* \* \***

Yet no gestures Krsna or anyone else made could check the loving worry His parents, gopis, and friends felt as they considered the enormous weight and size of the mountain little Krsna was holding.

Krsna, noting the symptoms of His devotees’ concern for Him, would reassure them in some way, and they would again feel happiness by being near Him and take pride in His astonishing feats for some time.

Thus the seven days Krsna held Govardhana aloft were peppered with regular interludes of anxiety, adding spice to the Vraja-vasis’ cakora-like absorption in the mild moon-rays of Krsna’s beauty.

On the first day Krsna looked dishevelled. His hair was scattered over His shoulders and He appeared to be perspiring from fatigue. Mother Yasoda trembled to see her son is such a state.

Wide-eyed Yasoda stared at the scene in front of her, not wanting to believe it. When a gopa drew Krsna’s attention to Yasoda’s dismay, Krsna tried to put His mother at ease by making funny faces at her.

Krsna’s facial contortions worked magic; they convinced Mother Yasoda that holding up a mountain was mere child’s play for her son. She again became happy and smiled warmly.

But from time to time she would calculate how long Krsna had been holding Govardhana and once again become overwhelmed by an anxiety that soaked her clothes in perspiration.

As Mother Yasoda’s ecstasies ran the gamut from dread to laughter, compassion to wonder and beyond, one of her friends expressed her own astonishment at what she was witnessing.

That elderly gopi grasped the hand of Vraja’s queen and said, “Yasoda, just see the fun! On the one hand, there is your child, who is always captivated by sucking the milk from your breast.

“On the other hand, there is the great Govardhana Hill, who can obstruct the passing of the clouds, defy the thunderbolt of heavenly King Indra, and challenge the pride of Mount Sumeru.

“Just see! How wonderful it is that this great Govardhana Hill is resting on the finger of your child’s left hand as though it were an insignificant plaything. Is this not an awesome mystery?”

Shortly thereafter, Paurnamasi-devi also observed how two contradictory emotions tossed the gopis of Vrndavana back and forth between the waves of anxiety and happiness.

The gopis’ hearts were burning with pain at the thought of Krsna carrying the burden of Govardhana, but at the same time they were filled with a great festival of bliss by seeing Him day and night.

Waves of anxiety and happiness such as these, continuing over seven days of extraordinary circumstances, elevated the intensity of the Vraja-vasis’ love for Krsna to unprecedented heights.

This is Krsna’s constant objective: to magnify His devotees’ love for Him — and His love for them — an objective He realises with the help of His extraordinary pastimes and inconceivable potencies.

At different times before and after this govardhana-lila, Krsna elicits feelings of ecstatic distress from His devotees, captivating their minds by separating Himself from them for parts of each day.

Then, later in the day, when the effect of not seeing Krsna pushes the Vraja-vasis to the brink of transcendental madness, He again comes into view, plunging them into an ocean of bliss.

But the lifting of Govardhana Hill brought them all together at the same time and gave them the unprecedented opportunity to be able to see Krsna continuously, day and night for an entire week.

Such a fortunate opportunity — to spend all day, every day, for a whole week, looking at Hari without restriction — had never happened to the Vraja-vasis before and would never happen again.

Krsna’s friends, girlfriends, parents, family members, cows, and other creatures of Vraja had always lamented when they were separated from Him. During those times they all cherished the same longing.

That common longing amongst the Vraja-vasis was often expressed in their day-to-day conversations: “I wish I could be with Krsna and see Him all day, every day, without cessation.”

As the all-cognisant Supreme Being, Krsna knew what was in the hearts of the Vraja-vasis. And as their eternal well-wisher, He was eager to utilise His countless energies in fulfilling their every desire.

Thus when He noticed that Indra’s arrogance had become intolerable to the residents of heaven, Krsna inaugurated Govardhana-puja knowing that He would have to lift Govardhana to deal with Indra.

“When the Vraja-vasis take shelter beneath Govardhana,” Krsna thought, “I will give them the chance to drink from the chalice of the nectarean beauty of My blissful form and satisfy their thirsty eyes.

“For seven days, and during all eight phases of the day, the Vraja-vasis will be able to associate with Me without cessation. They will see Me without obstruction, and thus I will have granted their desire.”

But even though Krsna granted their desire, the Vraja-vasis still felt unfulfilled because they were unable to physically engage with Krsna in those ways unique to their individual relationships.

The cowherd boys, for instance, wanted to play with Krsna, but He was unable to move from where He stood. Beyond that, the presence of His parents inhibited their normal carefree behaviour.

And if the boys felt constrained by the presence of their elders, then what can be said of the poor cowherd girls, whose desire — kept secret because it was forbidden — was to have Krsna as their lover?

The cowherd damsels could neither interact with nor serve Krsna as they wished, and what limited exchanges they could have with Him had to be concealed behind a facade of propriety.

Of all the Vraja-vasis, Mothers Yasoda and Rohini were the most satisfied, for their maternal loving mood was most appropriate to the time and place, which allowed them to constantly pamper Krsna.

Even though all the Vraja-vasis could see Krsna constantly, they were not able to serve Him in the diverse variety of ways they were otherwise accustomed to. In that sense they were restricted.

While there was no sight the Vraja-vasis wanted to see more than Krsna, there were many things they would rather do than stand helplessly around Him. What they really wanted was to serve Him.

They wanted to serve Him unrestrictedly, each according to his or her own loving mellow, but prema wanted to further intensify the Vraja-vasis’ love for Krsna and therefore withheld that opportunity.

Once again, the Vraja-vasis were faced with the pain of separation from Krsna, but this time that separation was not from His physical presence—for He stood before them — but from their service to Him.

In the meantime, Krsna was also intensifying the Vraja-vasis’ loving bliss by — from time to time and in various ways — inspiring loving anxiety for His well-being within their minds.

Thus, to intensify their love for Krsna while He held Govardhana aloft, the threat of separation from Krsna was replaced by the Vraja-vasis’ concern for Him and their restricted ability to serve Him.

These feelings of separation while in Krsna’s presence are reminiscent of how Srimati Radharani entered the state of prema-vaicittya while sitting beside Krsna at Prema-sarovara.

All glories to the loving exchanges Krsna and His dearmost friends and family shared in the shelter of Giri Govardhana, which became a cauldron of transcendental love after the Vraja-vasis neglected Indra.

## Chapter Ten - Living Beneath Govardhana Hill and Krsna’s Abhiseka by the Demigods

Living beneath Govardhana Hill enabled the Vraja-vasis to see and to serve Krsna in extraordinary ways. It also let them witness an untold variety of loving exchanges between the Lord and His devotees.

This display of loving exchange between Krsna and His devotees not only heightened their affection for one another, but also infused the entire cowherd community with an even greater esprit de corps.

While everyone gazed at Krsna’s handsome face, Mother Yasoda continuously fed her son tasty dishes prepared with her own hands, and Nandaraja affectionately smelled his son’s head again and again.

Mother Rohini satisfied her urge to embrace Krsna by repeatedly kissing and fondling her own son, Balarama. Both boys rewarded all this loving care with tender smiles and heartening words.

Submissive gopis like Candra, and contrary ones like Radha — accustomed to sporting amorously with Krsna in secret — were unable to fully express their loving feelings for Him in such a public forum.

But they ambitiously compensated for their loss by constantly gazing at Krsna’s cloud-coloured limbs and face as if their eyes were cataka birds dying of thirst, eager to drink the sublime rainfall.

Since everyone was gazing at their hero, Krsna, with ever-increasing love and affection, how could anyone object to the gopis’ looking at Him with wide-open, unblinking, tear-filled eyes?

Yet under the cover of silence, the gopis — those masters of mystic meditation — drew Krsna through their eyes into their hearts and in that secluded place enjoyed intimate pastimes with Him.

To relieve Krsna of the intensity of having to share loving exchanges with so many devotees at once, Madhumangala thought to lighten Hari’s heart by speaking some humorous words. He said, “O friend!

“When You have me as an ally, saturated with immeasurable brahma-tejas, why take the trouble to hold up this puny hill? Here, let me have it. I can effortlessly balance it on the tip of my stick!”

From time to time, Madhu’s prattle would stir Krsna’s parents from their meditation and stoke the smouldering fire of anxiety, causing the smoke of loving quarrel to bring tears to everyone’s eyes.

Yasoda scolded Krsna, “Son! Due to uncontrolled senses, You always act impudently. First You provoked the demons by killing their leaders, and now You have angered the demigods by offending Indra.”

Krsna’s mother reached up to take her son’s butter-soft hand and continued, “Your audacity has forced You to hold up this mountain in desperation. And so You suffer. O Lord! Please save us.”

Madhu cast a wry smile at Krsna and said to Yasoda, “Mother! Why chastise Krsna unfairly? If angry Indra had not attacked us, then how could we relish the sweetness of seeing Krsna lift Govardhana Hill?”

Yasoda turned to face Madhu and replied, “Foolish imp! What is so sweet about a frail young boy struggling to hold up a gigantic mountain? ? Siva! The very thought of it makes me light-headed.”

Krsna’s mother looked up at Govardhana Hill, put her palms together, and with tears streaming from her eyes said, “O Giriraja! Please be compassionate and grant me a boon.

“If you are in fact a worshipful deity, then please do something to become soft and weightless so that my little boy, Krsna, will not feel any discomfort. ? honourable one! Such is my request.”

Krsna tried to reassure Yasoda: “Mother! Why are you worrying? I’ve already told you. Giriraja is floating by his own power. Nothing could be more delightful for Me than acting as an instrument of his will.”

Yasoda replied, “My dear son! You may be enjoying Yourself, but You must be getting tired by now. I will only believe what You say when Govardhana leaves Your hand and hovers in the sky by himself.”

Madhu interjected, “O Queen! Don’t you know that this mountain rests weightless on Krsna’s finger by the strength of my mantras and austerities? And they are also soothing His upright arm.”

Irate Yasoda snapped, “Impertinent boy! I am burning in anxiety over my headstrong son, who is suffering because He never listens to good counsel, and all you do is make jokes and play the fool.”

Nanda Maharaja saw that his wife was losing her temper, and so he spoke up in Madhu’s defence, “Why scold Batu? Experts in diplomacy often use amusing words to ease the tension of trying situations.”

Nanda Maharaja continued, “Madhumangala’s speech fits the necessity of the moment. Besides, our son very much appreciates the boy’s humour. You should reward him, not chastise him.”

Chastened by her husband’s words, Yasoda smiled apologetically at Madhu. Meanwhile, the Vraja-vasis, intoxicated by indulging their eyes in the sight of Krsna, began to talk loudly amongst themselves.

They transgressed all etiquette by analysing the glories of Krsna’s ankle bells in great detail. “Look! Since Krsna stands motionless, His ankle bells have become silent like dumb persons.

“The sound of these ornaments crushes all pride, solves all problems, and defuses all dangers. Although awake, these anklets appear to be a pair of white swans sleeping before a host of blue lotus flowers.

“These ankle bells must now be absorbed, like self-realised yogis, in silent meditation upon Krsna’s lotus feet. But we did see them move slightly, their meditation broken, as Krsna lifted Govardhana Hill.

“Hearing that soft tinkling sound, the demigods came to praise and worship Krsna’s anklets, but the bells again became motionless and fell silent, embarrassed by all the attention.”

Some other Vraja-vasis began to compliment Krsna, who was then playing His flute. They said, “Look at Him! While nonchalantly holding Giriraja with one hand, He plays His flute with the other.

“He presses it to His lips and plays sweet tunes that fill us with exhilaration.” Suddenly, Madhu lunged forward and grabbed Krsna’s ankle, crying out, “Friend, stop! Stop! Do not play Your flute!

“The melodies of Your flute change the course of nature. They cause mountains to melt into rivers and rivers to turn into stone. If Govardhana Hill melts away, our shelter will be gone!”

Another dismayed cowherd boy added, “If Govardhana Hill melts into a river, he will do more than just leave us without shelter, he will engulf us with water and cause us all to drown!”

A more sober, senior gopa reassured his friends, “Govardhana Hill is most grave. Even though he thrills with ecstasy at the sound of the flute, quickly he will regain his composure and stay solid.”

Undistracted, other Vraja-vasis criticised Indra — as they would continue to do for the duration of their stay beneath Govardhana Hill. They declared, “Just see the foolishness of King Indra.

“Supposedly the well-wisher of all, he shows anger toward Krsna simply due to his envy of this innocent little boy. What a scandal! The demigods must be hanging their heads in shame.”

An elder gopa added, “Just see this Indra! We worship him for untold generations, and he is so ungrateful that he tries to destroy the race of cowherds. He even dares to threaten our sacred cows and bulls.”

Another group of cowherds was trying to understand how they could be insulated from the terrible calamity raining down all around, beyond the shelter of Giriraja. They asked, “How is this happening?

“With the clouds of devastation circling above, and their waters falling everywhere, it appears that the universe is coming to an end. Our situation is that precarious, yet we are mysteriously untouched.”

Another cowherd said, “Not only are we safe and sound in the middle of this perilous disaster, but we are free from all anxiety. Rather, we delight in joking, laughter, and pleasant talks. It is inconceivable!”

**\* \* \* \***

While the men of Vrndavana were absorbed in discussing the wonderful circumstances, the young gopis broke into isolated groups and began to speak about the topic dearest to their hearts.

Syamala teased Radha, saying, “Radhika! If You continue to cast sidelong glances at Krsna from beneath Your dancing eyebrows, You will disturb His mind so much that He will drop this mountain on us.”

Radha returned the joke, “Syamala! It is your uncontrolled love for Krsna that makes My heart, eyes, and eyebrows dance. If you don’t want Krsna to drop the mountain, then control your own passion.”

In response to this explicit exchange between Radha and Syamala, one gopi said, “Krsna turned the king of mountains into a toy ball. Just see how patient He must be to stand in one position for so long!”

Another gopi challenged her girlfriend’s eulogy of Krsna. “O devi! I’ll be convinced that patience is one of Hari’s virtues when I see Him retain His composure before the sight of your splendid breasts.”

A third gopi said, “Friends! Krsna’s greatness resides not in His patience, but in His ability to satisfy everyone with His unrivalled sweetness. And is that sweetness ever as sweet as when He teases us?”

A fourth gopi, transfixed by Krsna’s expertise, said, “Just see how Krsna plays the flute with His right hand, and how sometimes, while listening to His friends, He charmingly shakes His head.”

The gopis’ gossip attracted Krsna’s attention. And without being detected by the other Vraja-vasis, He singled out Radha from amongst Her girlfriends as the sole object of His affections.

One young girl who caught Krsna’s roving eyes lingering on Radha whispered to her mistress, “Listen carefully! While the Vraja-vasis are captivated by looking at Krsna, He repeatedly glances Your way.”

Radha looked through the veil covering Her face as Her maidservant continued, “Look at how His body erupts with signs of ecstasy like tears and trembling, which the Vraja-vasis mistake for weariness.”

As Krsna admired Radha’s infinite loveliness, His trembling limbs perspired. The cowherds mistook Krsna’s ecstasies for overexertion and worriedly raised their sticks in the air to help Him hold the hill.

Radha covered Her face with Her veil and looked shyly at the ground, embarrassed at having alarmed the Vraja-vasis. But then, for only an instant, She offered Krsna a gentle smile, unseen by anyone else.

The Vraja-vasis were nearing hysteria, but Krsna was oblivious to anything but Sri Radha. Then He stole a peek at Her bodice-covered breasts and His hand shook, unnerving the cowherds even further.

At that very moment, Govardhana Hill let out a tremendous roar of ecstasy. Afraid that Krsna was about to drop the mountain, the people of Vrndavana desperately prayed to the Lord for help.

The prayers of His devotees diverted Krsna’s mind from its absorption in Radha. As He surveyed the Vraja-vasis, His gaze met the eyes of His elder brother, who was grinning at the gopas’ needless panic.

Krsna mistook Balarama’s smile to be His brother’s way of saying, “Look! I caught You gaping at Srimati Radharani.” Ashamed, Krsna at once tried to hide His flushed face from His brother.

In the midst of all this pandemonium, Madhumangala somehow managed to raise his voice above the din, crying out, “O residents of Vrndavana! Do not be afraid! You are completely safe!

“Krsna is not the least bit tired. He doesn’t need you to hold up Govardhana Hill! You think you’re helping Him, but in reality you are doing nothing but scratching Giriraja’s belly with your sticks.”

The Vraja-vasis paused to hear Madhu’s words but then turned to Krsna for comfort, which He afforded them by filling their eyes with His beauty and their ears with the comforting sound of His voice.

The Vraja-vasis bathed in the ocean of Krsna’s soothing features: His face as fresh as a blue lotus bathed in moonlight, His smile adorned with teeth sparkling like stars in a cloudless sky.

They saw the radiant pearl necklace adorning His chest. And they could understand His heart through the gentle movement of His lips, which said, “O gopas, revered even by Mahadeva himself.

“Why demote yourself to the status of common men with all this wearisome anxiety? I am here to personally protect you. Can’t you tell that I am not the least bit tired? Just look and see for yourselves.”

And once again, like night-blooming lotuses, the Vraja-vasis lost themselves in Krsna’s moon-like countenance. Their fears dissolved, leaving them forgetful of anything except Hari.

Just as a cow affectionately nuzzles her calf, Yasoda wrapped her arm around Krsna. With a doting voice, she spoke to her son: “Another day is coming to an end. Your belly is still flat, and Your belt loose.

“The cows see your face gaunt from fasting and refuse to graze in the nearby fields, rich with grass and grains. I have brought You warm soft cakes and creamy yogurt with thick skin on top. Just see!

“Please put Your flute away and enjoy these preparations with Rama and Your friends. Once You have eaten, then You may play Your flute again, with renewed energy and a full stomach.”

Madhu added, “O friend! Your mother is quite right. Besides, one should never disobey the order of His parents. Moreover, I am afflicted by hunger and will most likely die if I don’t eat soon.”

Krsna glanced lovingly at Mother Yasoda and Madhumangala and said, “I don’t feel hungry or fatigued, but because one should never transgress the orders of a superior, I will eat from My mother’s hand.”

**\* \* \* \***

Unable to find shelter when envious King Indra’s fury first struck Vrndavana, the animals and birds as well as the trees and creepers on Govardhana Hill were all put into great distress.

So pained by the wind, rain, and hail were creatures like the deer, tigers, and rabbits, that they crammed together into the caves of the mountain, shivering side by side in the cold.

Nonetheless, the water coursing down the slopes of Govardhana Hill soon flooded many of those caves and forced all the creatures, including the snakes that lived there, out into the tempest.

The animals then gathered beneath great trees, whose branches bore peacocks and doves who were desperately clinging for safety. But soon the winds uprooted the trees, leaving their wards without shelter.

The birds trying to fly away were struck to the ground by the downpour. Floodwaters raging along the mountainside would then sweep the birds away in their roaring, foamy currents.

Lesser creatures like bees hid in their hives, and butterflies hung to the underside of leaves. But they too were cast into the merciless elements, their refuges unable to withstand the rage of Indra.

These animals appeared to be ignorant creatures, but they were also residents of Vraja. They had seen and even participated in Krsna’s pastimes as He herded His cows on the slopes of Govardhana Hill.

Naturally, then, in distress they thought of Krsna and prayed to Him in their own ways. Thus they took complete shelter of the Lord, as do all great devotees when faced with utter calamity.

And although the roar of the wind drowned out the animals’ pathetic cries, those cries did not go unnoticed, for Krsna, who never forgets the service of even the tiniest insect of Vraja, heard them.

The lashing rain and gusty winds also afflicted Govardhana himself, but, unlike the Vraja-vasis eagerly taking shelter of him, he was already aware of the higher purpose of this pastime.

Giriraja recalled Hanumanji’s prophecy and weathered the storm, anticipating the unprecedented service that he would render when Krsna had finally had enough of Indra’s foolish arrogance.

Giri Govardhana wouldn’t have to wait for long. Just as Lord Nrsimha quickly came to rescue little, helpless Prahlada, Krsna soon came to protect Vrndavana’s defenceless inhabitants.

When Krsna first touched Govardhana Hill with His left hand, a current of divine ecstasy surged through the mountain, electrifying the distressed insects, plants, birds, and animals as well.

That spiritual force not only caused rapture in Giriraja and the living things that called him home but also granted an extraordinary boon that made the missiles of Indra feel like offerings of flower petals.

In fact, from the very moment Krsna raised Govardhana Hill until the very moment He put him back in place, Krsna’s enormous umbrella felt not the slightest inconvenience.

Neither Govardhana Hill nor his wards suffered in the least as they all sat atop Krsna’s little finger, just as the Vraja-vasis beneath the mountain felt no hunger, thirst, cold, or weariness.

And because Giriraja had expanded himself, part of him was above the clouds of destruction. The birds, deer, wild boar, and other animals could climb to his peaks and feel the heat of the sun.

Other fortunate animals crawled into the now dry caves of Govardhana and ventured to the base of the hill, from where they could observe the Vraja-vasi community surrounding heroic Krsna.

On the occasions that Govardhana wavered atop Krsna’s hand, touching the ground at Puncari, a few animals were even able to jump down from their perches and join Krsna and the Vraja-vasis.

None of these animals felt the slightest distress; rather, they tasted the highest ecstasy. Indra was unable to ruffle even the feathers of a baby sparrow, under Krsna’s care, in its nest.

Krsna was true to His word. By potencies incomprehensible even to great minds like Lord Brahma, every living thing in and around Govardhana Hill became immune to Indra’s barbarous onslaught.

Therefore, when Indra sent his scouts to assess the damage to Vrndavana, he was shocked to hear that his greatest efforts had not displaced even a single leaf from the most insignificant creeper.

Again and again, day after day, for seven consecutive days Indra redoubled the fury of his attack on Vraja, and after each assault he received the same report, “Our mission was unsuccessful.”

Blinded by anger, Indra could not recognise the futility of his actions. Time and time again, he released fiercer winds and more devastating clouds, determined to succeed in his offensive campaign.

On the third day, frustrated that his onslaught had again failed, Indra struck his elephant-carrier angrily with a goad and descended towards Vraja, along the way tossing lightning bolts at will.

Suddenly he saw before him — jutting out above the Samvartaka clouds—the glorious peaks of Govardhana Hill, upon which deer happily frolicked and birds sang in concert.

Beneath him, Indra could see that his powerful emissaries — the clouds, rain, and lightning — had only managed to ornament Govardhana, just as pearls, diamonds, and gold decorate an umbrella.

Rather than admit that Giri Govardhana had defined the limit of his power, cruel-hearted Indra continued to demand that his reluctant servants inflict their worst upon Vrndavana’s residents.

Out of fear of their king, the clouds followed Indra’s directions in the mood of dutiful soldiers. Their winds blew at the speed of the mind as they tried to dislodge Govardhana Hill from Krsna’s hand.

Beset by lightning bolts, hailstones, torrential rain, and piercing winds, Govardhana felt no inconvenience at all because Krsna’s touch and breath relieved him of all pain and fatigue.

Indeed, the sight of Krsna’s sapphire form and ruby smile continually refreshed Govardhana. Meanwhile, Indra’s emissaries were becoming more and more fatigued and dispirited by their futile efforts.

But foolish Indra, confident that the Vraja-vasis and their mountain resort would soon be destroyed, returned to his abode triumphant, only to be shamed yet again by the report of a messenger.

“O king of the gods! I have seen a great wonder. By consuming the offerings meant for you, that hill has become very powerful. He stands tall and proud despite our best efforts to destroy him.”

“But what about the people of Vraja?” Indra enquired. The demigod messenger replied, “We did not see people or animals anywhere. They must have been incinerated by the flames of your thunderbolts.”

This news heartened Indra. But not fully confident, he instructed his servants, “O demigods! Live long! Your news brings joy to my heart. Still, look again to assure that none of our enemies have survived.”

Due to his offences, Indra could not conceive of the blissful pastimes taking place beneath Govardhana Hill. But Indra’s lightning-messenger had a quick peek — by Yogamaya’s grace.

Just at that moment, as all the blissful Vraja-vasis looked on, Yasoda was pleading with Krsna to stop playing His flute and to accept some tambula in order to settle His stomach and calm His nerves.

Yasoda said, “Oh my darling! Please stop playing Your murali flute for a few moments and pacify my heart. Your flute music cannot fill Your stomach, but this tambula can. And it will also enchant Your mind.”

Krsna paused for a moment to smile at His mother but then persisted in playing His flute, which distressed His mother to no end. Yasoda pleaded, “O Damodara! You have not eaten for days.

“Even if You are not hungry, please keep in mind that Balarama and Your friends will not eat without You. The hungry growling of their stomachs is drowning out the noise of the thunder outside.”

Yasoda could see that her words had fallen on deaf ears, so she appealed to Subala. He took the flute from Krsna’s hand without hesitation and comically pushed the tambula into his friend’s mouth.

Krsna’s lips turned red and His cheeks puffed out from chewing the tambula. Mother Yasoda smiled broadly in great happiness, her cloth wet with tears and the milk flowing from her breasts.

Simply by witnessing these exchanges of love, the messenger of Indra, his heart melted, was drawn spontaneously to that beautiful cowherd boy. Krsna, however, pretended not to notice him.

Bewildered that his duty should pit him as an adversary of this boy of indescribable beauty and sweetness, Indra’s messenger dallied in Vraja for some time before returning reluctantly to heaven.

Back in Indra’s court, he hung his head low, ashamed of his mission, but reported to his master, “O king of Devaloka! We mistakenly thought that the cowherds perished in our attack, but they did not.

“Sheltered beneath Govardhana, in a cavern that is as opulent as the subterranean heaven Patala, the subjects of Nanda revel in a blissful festival, while their prince balances the mountain on His left pinkie.”

Indra seethed in anger at the thought of being defied by Krsna for a second time. He was even more perturbed by the conciliatory tone in his messenger’s voice. “No one can be trusted!” thought Indra.

In a vain attempt to retain his royal prestige, Indra bellowed at his courtiers, “Long ago I cut off the wings of every mountain, but it seems that this Govardhana’s wings sprout anew.

“He has become so arrogant that he foolishly intervenes between me and Vraja’s discourteous cowherds, who must be punished. But I will teach him. With a flaming thunderbolt I will destroy them all!”

Indra picked up an invincible thunderbolt and hastily threw it at Vraja. But again he was told that his assault had failed. His heart boiled with anger as he threw one thunderbolt after another.

Again Indra sent his scouts to Vraja, but again the news they brought back simply inflamed his rage. “O master of the demigods!” they said. “We watched as your insuperable lightning bolts struck Vrndavana.

“But their cumulative effect could not cause one leaf to fall to the ground or give trouble to a single ant on Govardhana Hill. The creatures on the mountainside remain completely undisturbed.

“As for the people staying beneath Govardhana? We have witnessed them being tossed by the playful glistening waves of bliss emanating from Sri Krsna. The word ‘suffering’ means nothing to them.”

The blood drained from Indra’s face. Dejected, he held his head in his hands. How could mere cowherds usurp his dominion? How could a mere hill foil his infallible weapons? Was he not the king of heaven?

Thus defeated day after day, bereft of the mercy of pure devotees, Indra simply could not understand that the real king—of whom he was but an insignificant servant — was lifting Govardhana Hill.

**\* \* \* \***

Indra felt comfort as he reached for his ultimate weapon — a lightning bolt made from the bones of Dadhici Muni. This bolt would destroy that mountain and everything beneath it, just as it had Vrtrasura.

Once again Indra stormed from his palace towards Vrndavana, lightning bolt upraised, but as he released his terrible vajra weapon at Govardhana Hill, something astonishing happened:

Krsna let out a laugh that charmed the Vraja-vasis; He decided that He’d had enough of Indra’s foolishness. Simply by the power of His will, Krsna paralysed Indra’s arm and froze the vajra in mid-air.

At the same time, Krsna immobilised the clouds and winds circling Vrndavana and froze their presiding demigods, making them appear like marionettes, harmlessly dangling by invisible wires in the sky.

But paralysis was not humbling enough to enable Indra to see the error of his ways, so Krsna forced him into a trance, in which — freed of the mode of passion — Indra could see the Lord more clearly.

In that dreamlike state, unknown to great yogis, Indra saw Krsna seated upon a thousand-petalled lotus, dressed as a cowherd boy, ornamented with jewels and garlands, holding a flute in His hand.

That vision of Krsna’s cloud-black form dispelled the ignorance in Indra’s heart, and by the mercy of the Lord he was able to remember a prayer he had received from his spiritual master, Brhaspati.

Indra fell to his knees before the Lord. He finally recognised that Krsna was the same Lord Narayana who had appointed him to his position. Indra then began to pray to that original Lord.

His voice quivered as he spoke, “O my Lord! ? indestructible and eternal Brahman. ? form of pure goodness, always free of the material modes of nature, I offer my respectful obeisances unto You.

“In Satya-yuga You appear with a white complexion, which stands for purity and truth. In Treta-yuga Your complexion is reddish like saffron. In Kali-yuga it is golden, the colour of love for You.

“And in this Dvapara age, Your bluish-black complexion reflects the loving feelings for You that the *gopis* have concealed in their hearts. Thus I bow to You, my Lord, Krsna, the son of Nanda and Yasoda.

“With the artistic sound of Your flute’s melodies and Your indescribable beauty, You steal away the hearts of the gopis and make Yourself dearer to Radha than many millions of Her own lives.

“You are self-satisfied, yet You delight in reciprocating the love of Your devotees by indulging in unique, human-like pastimes in Your original abode of Vrndavana, where many white cows reside.

“In these pastimes You sometimes comb Srimati Radharani’s hair, lacquer Her feet, chew the betel already chewed by Her, and sometimes You rest with Her in a secluded grove.

“Sometimes You play water sports with Sri Radha, stare at Her as She casts sidelong glances Your way, and sometimes You decorate Her with flower garlands You Yourself have strung.

“Sometimes You stroll through the gardens of Vrndavana with the gopis, accept food offered to You by the wives of the brahmanas, and occasionally eat tala fruit with Your friends.

“Sometimes You steal the garments of the unmarried cowherd girls, call the cows and cowherd boys on Your flute, and You regularly sing beautiful songs in the company of Your friends.

“O Supreme Personality of Godhead! ? You who engage in these and many other pastimes throughout eternity, I, Your humble servant, offer You my respectful obeisances, again and again.”

As Indra bowed, the vision of Krsna sitting on a lotus disappeared. The king of heaven returned to external consciousness and was again able to move. Embarrassed and fearful he thought:

“My master, the Supreme Lord Visnu, has descended to earth as the cowherd boy Krsna and is holding up that mountain! No one, therefore, can harm Govardhana Hill or the Vraja-vasis!”

Filled with remorse, divested of his pride, and weighed down by a hundred sorrows, Indra boarded a celestial airplane and headed back to his abode, hoping to find relief in a good night’s sleep.

Alone on his regal bed, Indra tossed and turned. His restless mind, raging out of control, refused to give him even a moment’s rest. The king of heaven thought, “My home has become like hell.”

Indra stared at the canopy over his bed and thought, “I wanted to make Vrndavana suffer, but I only brought suffering upon myself. I tried to drown Nanda’s cows, but now I am drowning in my own sins.

“I hurled thunderbolts at Govardhana Hill, but I may as well have hurled them at my own head. Whoever troubles Krsna’s devotees in any way finds his future replete with miseries.”

As Indra remembered the torrential rains and blasting winds that still lingered around Govardhana Hill, he suddenly sat up in his bed and withdrew the Samvartaka clouds by the power of his thoughts.

Overhearing other demigods preparing to travel to Vraja and propitiate Krsna for their hand in the affair, Indra also resolved to apologise to Krsna for the damage he had tried to inflict upon Vraja.

At the dawn of the eighth day, the skies of Vrndavana began to clear. The rays of the sun once again painted a coat of golden luminescence on Vrndavana’s forests and pastures.

For seven days and nights — days and nights as dark as the nights of Brahma — the torrential rains and cyclone winds of the Samvartaka clouds had continually bombarded Vrndavana, with no effect.

Those mighty clouds and their subordinates had nearly died from the toil of carrying out the orders of Indra, who had been dragged, step by step, from the platform of false pride into the mire of ignobility.

Indra had felt those days as seven painful millennia, the people of the world as seven days of bitter suffering. The Vraja-vasis had felt them as seven joy-filled hours, and Giriraja, as seven blissful moments.

The pummelling tempests could do nothing to disturb the peace and serenity of Govardhana, who, bathed by rains and anointed by Krsna’s fragrant touch, now appeared stunningly beautiful.

**\* \* \* \***

The fury outside had abated, but the Vraja-vasis, completely preoccupied by seeing, serving, and thinking about Krsna, were oblivious to the favourable turn of events.

Eventually, Krsna pointed His flute towards the rising sun and said, “My dear cowherd men! The wind and rain have stopped. You may now return to your homes with your wives, children, and possessions.”

As the Vraja-vasis observed the sun’s rays peek beneath their mountain canopy, they began to talk excitedly amongst themselves about Krsna’s effortless victory over Indra and his weapons.

To reinforce the Vraja-vasis’ faith in Giriraja, Krsna raised His voice above the tumult of the cowherds’ talking and cows’ lowing and said, “All glories to our saviour, Govardhana Hill!

“The thunderous roar of this king of mountains made the clouds tremble in fear. His radiant effulgence scattered those clouds and dried up the deluge they had brought upon our heads.

“He hosted us in the lofty palace of his pure and magnanimous heart, but we should now leave this great place and return to our own homes, confident that Giriraja is ever our protector and well-wisher.”

The Vraja-vasis repeatedly shouted “Giriraja ki jaya!” and cast grateful glances in the direction of their homes in the distance, which were glistening attractively after the cleansing rains.

As the sun’s rays fell upon the plants around Vraja whose leaves and flowers had been stripped by the storm, fresh leaves unfurled and new flowers blossomed, filling the crisp air with their bouquet.

Animals left their shelters and happily frolicked about, bent trees righted themselves, rivers no longer in spate flowed peacefully, and the wind, now free of its destructive mood, blew gently.

The muddy roads and pastures quickly dried, and the air filled with the sounds of singing birds and buzzing bees. The Vraja-vasis were relieved to find Vrndavana restored to its usual beauty.

Once again, Krsna addressed His kith and kin, “The fierce winds and rain have ceased. The sky is free of devastating clouds. ? residents of Vraja, now we should go out from beneath this mountain.”

At Krsna’s request, everyone happily prepared to leave what had been a safe haven during the storm. The cowherd boys were the first to go, gathering their cows and then calling for the herds to follow.

The cows, however, were reluctant to leave Krsna. For seven days they had continuously gazed on Krsna’s sweet face. They had lost interest in the things that had previously brought them pleasure.

With coaxing from the cowherd boys outside and some help from the cowherd men still under the hill, the cows shot out into the open air like a volley of arrows. Running about, they jumped and kicked in joy.

But when they looked back and saw that Krsna was still beneath the mountain they ran back to Him. Nudging the cowherds aside, the cows surrounded Krsna, licked His body, and lowed adoringly.

Krsna drove the cows out again with compassionate glances and familiar flute melodies, which sent the beautiful herds streaming out from under Govardhana Hill in all directions at once.

The Vraja-vasis were glad to see their cows frolicking in the meadows around Govardhana. As they departed from Giriraja’s shelter, they took up musical instruments and sang of Krsna’s heroic feats.

The Vraja-vasis’ sankirtana resonated throughout the length and breadth of the cavernous valley beneath Govardhana. So powerful was the resonance that it made Giriraja shiver in ecstasy.

The Vraja-vasis radiated love and exaltation — remembering how Krsna had saved them from imminent destruction — as they filed past Giridhari to have one last look at Him holding Govardhana aloft.

As Radhika and Her friends passed by Krsna, they cast veiled glances at Him. The gilded effulgence of the gopis’ bodies appeared to transform Krsna’s blackish complexion into the colour of molten gold.

This metamorphosis confused the Vraja-vasis, but after the gopis had gone, Krsna’s dark complexion returned. The elders could only conclude that their eyes had played tricks on them.

The gopis and older gopas all congregated around Govardhana Hill, watching with unblinking eyes, waiting for Krsna to come out. But He was still energetically talking with His intimate friends.

Some boys said, “Dear friend! We feel so distressed that You have been inconvenienced by having to hold up this mountain for so long. Why didn’t You let Sudama hold it for a while?”

Krsna reassured them, “This whole adventure has not been the slightest inconvenience for Me.” But the boys persisted, “At least You could have changed hands and let us massage Your left hand.”

Krsna considered these words very thoughtfully and then shook His head. After a while He said, “Now you all go out from under here. I want to put this mountain down so that we can play.”

Thinking themselves equal to Krsna, the boys stayed where they were. Bent on displaying their superior strength and driven by fraternal ecstasy, they insisted, “No, You go first!

“Give us the hill, and we’ll hold it while You leave.” As the hoys lifted their sticks in the air, Krsna, boasting a waggish smile, shifted the tiniest fraction of the hill’s weight to the arms of His boastful friends.

The great weight of Govardhana Hill made the boys collapse in a faint. Then Krsna quickly revived them as He had done when a forest fire had caused the boys to lose consciousness.

And so that they would not feel humiliated in the presence of the entire cowherd community, Krsna made the gopas forget the entire incident. With youthful gusto, they charged out into the open.

Alone under Govardhana Hill, Krsna briefly lingered to enjoy the last few moments of one of His inimitable pastimes, as well as to double-check that no one had stayed behind.

That subterranean valley was quiet, save for the muffled sound of the Vraja-vasis’ kirtana in the distance. Its pastoral shelter was as pristine and inviting as it had been when Krsna first raised the hill.

Krsna thought, “Who else but Govardhana Hill can render such uninterrupted service with his body? Even My mother would stop breastfeeding Me to attend to milk boiling on the stove.

“At night’s end, even the cowherd girls abandon our nocturnal pastimes when their husbands start to stir in their beds. Fearful that our love will be discovered, they leave Me alone in the kunja.

“But Giriraja did not care for the concern of his relatives or the scorn of the demigods; he stayed upon My finger despite untold difficulty, and therefore he shall always be known as the best of My servants.”

Satisfied that no one and nothing had been left behind, Krsna set Govardhana Hill down in his previous location, playfully tossing the mountain off His hand as nonchalantly as a child drops a toy ball.

Then Krsna came out from the same cave He had entered before lifting Giri Govardhana and greeted the Vraja-vasis with a cheerful smile and a meaningful wave of His left hand.

When the Vraja-vasis saw that Krsna had indeed returned to them, they were filled with bliss, and although they had watched Him replace Govardhana, they could not understand how He had done so.

A profound silence engulfed the people of Vraja as they relished the sight of Krsna’s beguiling features, unlimitedly enhanced by the physical testimony of His unconditional love for them.

Because of His love for the Vraja-vasis, Krsna, who had grown accustomed to the affection poured upon Him by the men and women of Vrndavana, had carried the burden of Giriraja for seven days.

Now that He had emerged safely from that precarious situation, the Vraja-vasis’ love erupted. Casting aside every social norm, they ran to Him, calling out His names, like cows running to their calves.

Men, women, and children, servants, friends, girlfriends, parents, subordinates, superiors — everyone ran towards Krsna, His blurred image now fixed in their tear-filled eyes.

Arms outstretched, Mother Yasoda and Nanda Maharaja stumbled towards their son, not because of obstacles in their path, but because their overwhelming love hampered the movements of their legs.

The relief of finally seeing Krsna safe burst through the dam of social constraint that had held back the mighty waves of the Vraja-vasis’ love, waves now crashing at the lotus feet of their very life — Krsna.

**\* \* \* \***

The youthful speed of Krsna’s gopa friends enabled them to reach Him first. And true to His promise, He was eager to cavort and frolic with them through the forests and groves of Vrndavana.

Krsna had to delay that plan, however, because the tidal waves of affection surging from the hearts of the Vraja-vasis washed over Him, according to the various relationships they enjoyed with Him.

Krsna’s superiors kissed Him, lovingly smelled His head, massaged His left arm, cracked the fingers of His left hand, offered Him auspicious benedictions, and repeatedly enquired about His welfare.

After the housewives of Vraja blessed Krsna, they worshipped Him with yogurt, rice paddy, and fresh grass. Meanwhile, the brahmanas and their wives offered boons and words of praise.

These great souls gave blessings to Krsna: “May You subdue the wicked, protect the decent, give pleasure to Your parents, and may You be enriched with all wealth and opulence.”

Mother Yasoda embraced her son again and again. She lamented over Krsna’s fatigue while massaging His left arm with her lotus palms and repeatedly kissing His left hand with her bimba-coloured lips.

“Oh my dear boy!” she cried. “The tremendous pressure of that great mountain’s weight has made Your body as soft as freshly churned butter. What has happened to You? What will become of You?”

Yasoda-devi thought that if she hugged Krsna, His bodily suffering would disappear. So she hugged Him a hundred times. Still not satisfied, she then offered Him a hundred blessings.

Yasoda held Krsna’s lotus face between her palms and repeated His name until her eyes were so filled with tears that she was unable to see anything at all. Such is the topmost expression of maternal love.

Then Rohini-devi, whose parental love is second only to Yasoda’s, offered arati to Krsna and Balarama with a jewelled ghee lamp. The arati complete, she first embraced Krsna and then her own son.

Nanda Maharaja, while his wife was fawning over Krsna, had been stroking his son’s soft wavy hair, occasionally managing to embrace Him and wet Krsna’s transcendental form with his tears.

Tears of love poured from Balarama’s eyes as He warmly embraced His younger brother. Baladeva, the source of spiritual strength, was overpowered by love; what then of the other residents of Vraja?

Krsna’s equals — His cowherd friends — laughed and joked with Him while they massaged His limbs. Those younger than Krsna fell at His feet and massaged them. Some even kissed His glistening toenails.

A drop of the cowherd boys’ fraternal love for their wonderful friend was exemplified by Raktaka’s entreaty to Rasada. Grasping Rasada’s hands, Raktaka said, “O my friend! What can I say!

“Just hear my one and only humble request! Please bless me so that I may always be engaged in the service of Krsna, who will now certainly become famous as the lifter of Govardhana Hill.”

Out of shyness the young cowherd girls kept their distance, but they too worshipped Krsna and Balarama with their doe-eyed glances and lotus faces blossoming with desire.

The gopis could not publicly embrace Krsna with their arms, but they could embrace Him within the privacy of their minds. And their emotion was evident in the alluring smiles decorating their lips.

From a distance, absorbed in the sight of their beloved, the gopis recalled and actually relived many episodes that took place while Krsna was holding Govardhana Hill above His head.

They knew Krsna’s secret purpose for lifting Giriraja: to disclose just how much they all — especially Srimati Radharani and Her friends — had yearned to gaze continuously at His moonlike face.

They recalled how Krsna had been making elaborate plans for the Govardhana-yajna, and how they had decorated themselves with costly clothes and valuable ornaments reserved for special occasions.

They relived how that evening the Vraja-vasis observed Dipavali by setting out rows of lamps on their rooftops, and how Krsna and they had found secret places from which they could gaze at one another.

The next day all of Vraja had celebrated the festival of Govardhana Hill, and the gopis were enthusiastic to join in the celebration, knowing that they would have many opportunities to see Krsna there.

One such opportunity came while the people of Vraja were absorbed in beholding the Deity of Giriraja, when the gopis had released the doves of their eyes to soar through the bluish sky of Krsna’s beauty.

Better yet were the times at which they could converse with Him. On the pretext of enquiring exactly how their cows should be worshipped during go-puja, the beautiful gopis had asked Krsna many questions.

Their talks with Krsna were about the cows, but the looks in the gopis’ eyes, the movements of their eyebrows, and the faint trembling of their chins revealed the passionate longing in their hearts.

But the gopis’ desires were most nearly satisfied when everyone had worshipped the gopas. Only a few of the girls were able to worship Krsna directly, but all of them had worshipped Him in their hearts.

And due to the intimate nature of the worship the gopis performed within themselves, none of them had envied those who could worship Krsna directly. This truth was clearly visible on their pretty faces.

The gopis recalled how when the time had come for the young girls to adorn the gopas’ backs with auspicious marks, they had pressed forward eagerly with the hope of being able to decorate Krsna.

But again, only a few of them had been allowed to do that service. They relived how they had controlled the itching their palms had felt to touch Krsna — if even slightly and just for a moment.

As the different events of the day had progressed, the gopis were at times very distant from their beloved. At these times they could only visualise what Krsna was doing, relying on news of Him from others.

The gopis painfully relived how at the time of offering homa, when Krsna was surrounded by brahmanas and cowherd royalty, they could only offer their hearts into the sacrificial fire of separation.

During that sacred fire, during parikrama of Govardhana Hill, and at other periods when the gopis’ eyes were deprived of the sight of Krsna, each moment was like a mountain towering in the gopis’ path.

Krsna was conscious of the gopis’ sorrow. He knew that Radha’s mascara streamed down Her cheeks. And so to let Her and the gopis see where He was, He had repeatedly tossed His stick into the air.

But a stick could hardly replace Krsna. From time to time, a cowherd boy would inform Krsna of the gopis’ spiralling sorrow, and on the pretext of walking behind the cows He had walked amongst the girls.

On such occasions He had sometimes touched a gopi while walking past her, only to be reprimanded with a lash from the whip-like vines of her moving eyebrows, which had left Krsna briefly stunned.

And when He had come close enough to Sri Radha to see Her face, Krsna forgot Govardhana and the parikrama, moving forward like a sleepwalker, rapt in meditation on Her stunning beauty.

That night the Vraja-vasis had slept under the stars. As the bards sang of the day’s events, the gopis yearned to hear only of Krsna, and so they had listened attentively to the songs that dealt with Him.

The next morning Krsna had enjoyed bathing in the Yamuna. The gopis also had gone to bathe downstream from Krsna, in the hopes that the same water that had washed His body might touch theirs.

And when Krsna and His friends had gone to feast at His cousin- sister’s house in Sahar, the gopis also went on the pretext of striking up a friendship with Krsna’s cousins, helping them cook and serve.

Alas! The happiness of seeing Krsna joke with friends while eating had been short lived. When everyone returned to their homes for the night, the gopis had felt as if they were being condemned to prison.

At night’s end, the Samvartaka clouds had begun to pound Vrndavana with rain and hail. The gopis’ immediate response was to find Krsna and shield Him from the elements in any way they could.

Full of anxiety for Krsna’s welfare, the gopis had hurriedly dressed and run to His home, along with the rest of the Vraja-vasis, only to find a dry, resplendent Krsna beneath an unusual gigantic umbrella.

They recalled how relieved they had felt that Krsna was shielded from the storm, and how they had praised the harsh rains as heavenly nectar that had enabled them to see Krsna at such an odd, early hour!

As the people of Vraja flocked to Govardhana Hill and took their respective places around Krsna, the gopis had felt as though they were safe at home, delighting in the gift of a priceless treasure.

As did the other Vraja-vasis, the gopis had gazed at Krsna for seven days with unblinking eyes — like catakas stalking clouds, demigods imbibing soma-rasa, or cakora birds drinking moonbeams.

Because the Vraja-vasis, completely rapt in meditation, had forgotten everything else, the gopis had been free to look at Krsna without fear of censure from their husbands, parents, or in-laws.

Radha remembered standing a short distance from Krsna — Her eyes glistening with a pearl-string of tears, Her heart resting on the highest pinnacle of happiness. She then recalled Her thoughts:

“O foolish Providence! You are such a poor judge of time and circumstance! Is this the only time you can arrange for Me to gaze at My beloved without interruption? When He holds up a mountain?”

Krsna had been just as eager to see the gopis as they had been to see Him. As He had held Govardhana, at times Krsna could be seen looking all around, again and again, without any apparent purpose.

But actually Krsna had been thinking, “If I can just catch a glimpse of Srimati Radharani’s face, even for a moment, then the great labour of holding this hill will have born its sweet fruit.”

Some time later, Radha had been suffering from the pain and sorrow caused by Her unrequited love for Krsna. Having no one to turn to, other than Herself, She had mused, “O heart! Please be peaceful!

“Krsna has lifted this hill to protect all the Vraja-vasis from Indra’s torrential downpour. Surely He’ll protect You from the fearful monsoon of tears that now inundates this body.”

When the rains had stopped and Krsna had asked everyone to leave their place of refuge, the gopis were the first to follow His order, so that Krsna could at once be free from holding up such a great weight.

And Krsna had been just as eager to replace Giriraja, not because of the excessive weight, but because He was now separated from the gopis after a full week of being able to see them constantly.

Finally, after Krsna had replaced Giriraja, the gopis felt powerful emotions out of separation from that shelter. They thought, “Has Krsna emerged from under the hill, or come out from our hearts?”

These were the pastimes the gopis had shared with Krsna, pastimes in which they were deeply absorbed as they stood on the periphery of the great crowd of Vraja-vasis thronging noisily around their beloved.

At the heart of that crowd, Krsna was reciprocating with the Vraja-vasis’ unlimited gestures of love, all the while casting wistful glances here and there in search of the slightest glimpse of His beloveds.

In time, the fervour of the Vraja-vasis’ love receded, and Krsna was able to see the gopis standing beneath a large banyan tree, their eyes closed in meditation, their bodies inert like dolls.

Krsna perceived what the cowherd girls were seeing within their hearts. And the depth of their transcendental love so moved Him that diamond teardrops formed at the corners of His lotus eyes.

At Yogamaya’s suggestion, the gopis opened their eyes and saw He who was in their hearts looking at them lovingly from a distance. They spoke not a word, but their exchange of glances said everything.

Radhika noticed the jewel-like tears in Krsna’s eyes and accepted His offering of love, locking those tears within the vault of Her heart, much like a wealthy man carefully hoards his treasure.

Without Radha’s grace, by which one may gain access to that locked vault and bathe in those tears, no one can properly understand these pastimes and the mercy Krsna distributed by lifting Govardhana Hill.

**\* \* \* \***

As the men and women of Vrndavana celebrated Krsna’s successful exploits and showed how thankful they were that He was now safe, Indra sat on his throne and revealed his mind to the demigods.

“O my associates! Please hear me! This cowherd boy, Krsna, is in truth the ultimate shelter of the demigods and the unconquerable enemy of the demons; therefore, you should show Him all respect.”

This happy turn of events was a great relief to the demigods, for many amongst them had known from the outset that King Indra’s war on Krsna and the Vraja-vasis was doomed to fail.

In the heavenly planets, Tumburu led the demigods — including the Siddhas, Sadhyas, Gandharvas, and Caranas — in jubilantly singing Krsna’s praises, blowing conch shells, and playing dundubhi drums.

Meanwhile, heavenly damsels like the Apsaras joyfully rained flower-petals down on Vraja. Thus flowers and songs about Krsna filled the abode of the gods, the heavens below it, and the earth.

This concert of song, prayer, and instrumental music filled the skies. The demigods’ calls of “Jaya!” echoed everywhere, and the Vraja- vasis, their chests swelling with pride for their hero, joined in.

The gopis sang the song beginning with the words *giri-pujeyam vihita kena*, and the jubilant cowherd people walked along with Vraja’s prince as He made His way towards His home in Nandagrama.

From a distance, Krsna turned and lovingly looked back at the king of mountains. Then, surrounded by His boyfriends and Balarama, Krsna again took His cows to pasture in the area near Bhatrola.

As the gopis returned to their homes, they composed new songs about Krsna. They were fully immersed in the transcendental bliss with which Krsna had so deeply touched their hearts.
