The Vraja-vasis returned to their villages to find their barns, houses, gardens, furnishings — all their property — undamaged. In fact, everything glowed with greater splendour than ever before.

The cowherd men and women concluded that by following Krsna’s advice and worshipping Govardhana Hill they had become enriched not only by His pastimes and love but also by a newfound affluence.

That afternoon the heavens reverberated with celestial sounds and angelic voices. The Vraja-vasis only heard the nondescript singing of deities, but Krsna could discern the choice prayers of the demigods.

Siddhas, Vidyadharas, Gandharvas, and Kimpurusas sang Krsna’s glories and showered flowers that appeared sometimes as an exhilarating fine rain and sometimes as a refreshing gentle breeze.

The demigods prayed to Krsna, “All glories, all glories unto the son of Nanda Maharaja, unto His holy abode, Vrndavana, unto the pure taste of service to His lotus feet, and unto His eternal companions.

“O Lord, You completely deliver one who even once sincerely says, ‘From this day on, I surrender unto You.’ Who can imagine, then, the reward awaiting those who constantly serve You with love?

“Just see the example of the Vraja-vasis! You protected them from Indra’s wrath by lifting Govardhana Hill, declaring boldly to all that Your devotees are never without Your personal care.

“As the condensed form of pure love, You taste the bliss of loving exchange with Your intimate devotees, constantly churning the moods of spontaneous devotion through Your pastimes in Vraja.

“Ever fresh and always youthful, Your transcendental form is the ultimate expression of beauty and sweetness, and because it resembles the hue of a new rain cloud, You are known as Syamasundara.

“Although we demigods try to glorify You, we cannot do so adequately, for You are the exclusive property of Your pure devotees, who want nothing but service to You, their only asset in life.

“And yet we cannot help but be enchanted by the allure of Your bodily features: Your eyes that capture the splendour of the lotus, Your lips that deride the redness of ripe bimba fruit —

“Your glittering teeth, more attractive than a string of brilliant pearls, Your cheerful smile outshining the twinkling of the stars, and Your flowing hair tied in a topknot, more artistic than a camara.

“We are conditioned souls, yet simply seeing You saturates our minds and hearts with ecstasy, and hearing Your singing and flute-playing arouses within us a desire to serve You as the gopis do.

“And thus we have become envious of the bees that hum loudly around the garland decorating Your broad chest, the sight of which induces lusty desires within the hearts of Vraja’s chaste housewives.

“O Lord! Please make us qualified to serve You. Remove our intoxicating pride, free us from the cycle of birth and death, purify us from all-conquering lust, and give us attachment to You.

“Who can fail to be excited in mind and exhilarated at heart as You stroll through the groves of Vrndavana, shining brilliantly like an autumn moon, Your mysterious smile alive with untold pastimes?

“Who can fail to be pleased by seeing Your eyes, restless from transcendental ecstasies, reddish from the intoxication of divine lust, blossoming like lotuses from their search after a certain gopi?

“Who can fail to make You the object of their continuous praise, with Your shimmering golden garments, Your unfathomable self-manifest wisdom, and with that white lotus tucked over Your ear?

“O Lord! You are the source of the Vedic edicts that create good fortune for the world, yet You remain impartial, a witness to the choices people make to either follow or reject Your good counsel.

“But when insubordinates like Indra choose to persecute Your faultless devotees, You cast all neutrality far away and display Your renowned partiality for Your servants by personally sheltering them.

“We will always remember how You effortlessly lifted Govardhana Hill to crush the prowess of King Indra and how You soothed the troubled Vraja-vasis with a colourful bouquet of loving moods.

“Just as You destroy demons like Baka, Vatsa, and Putana, You humble upstarts like Brahma, Kaliya, and Indra. And when the arrogant surrender to You, You forgive them without delay.

“But Your greatest glory is in how You respond to the love of Your devotees, especially the gopis, with whom You enjoy amorous pastimes— as You did with the kumaris, when You stole their clothes.

“In that humorous pastime You captivated the minds of those innocent girls with pleasant words and Your promise to fulfil their desire that You become their husband. When will that day be ours?

“When will You shower us with Your compassion, protect us from the illusion that reigns over us, free us from wicked thoughts, and empower us to purely chant Your holy names? ? Hari!

“You correct the bad behaviour of Your servants, instantly favouring the repentant souls who beg for re-engagement in Your loving service by giving them the inner strength to realise their divine ambition.

“And all this You can achieve by a mere glance. ? Lord! We pray to become the object of that glance, for amongst the most wise and venerable, only You have the power to bless us with ecstatic love.

“When will we join the ranks of the gopis, whose ankle bells create a jingling sound so enchanting that they move Your heart to enjoy intimate pleasure pastimes in the groves along Yamuna’s shore?

“Such pastimes cannot be understood by Mahesa or Kuvera, by jnanis or yogis, by even the Vedas personified. What, then, can the impious make of them with the blunt instrument of warped logic?

“Although we are unqualified, we pray that You make us the objects of Your mercy and give us access to Your wonderful pastimes of love, pastimes we adore, even as we adore You.

“O Krsna! You dismantle the pride of Cupid by the love-sports You relish in the company of Sri Radha. She has perfectly mastered the sixty-four arts with which She singularly pleases You.

“You are witty, clever, attractive, and enchanting as You share Your ever-expanding love with thousands of beautiful gopis, taking the aggressive role in love like a maddened lion of the kunjas.

“O transcendental cowherd boy, whose handsome body resembles a puppet of the kajjala which anoints the gopis' eyes, we pray to You and to Your charming pastimes, which surround You like a dazzling aura.”

The demigods’ devotional prayers resounded through the skies, echoed in the valleys, and swept across the waters of Vrndavana. After hearing them, Krsna mildly smiled at His worshippers.

May the sincere devotees who share these devotional sentiments of the demigods — or who at least aspire to share them — one day attain the fulfilment of their desires within the groves of Vrndavana.

**\* \* \* \***

The worship of Govardhana Hill was performed on the Pratipat day. From the Trtiya until the Navami, Indra tried to pierce the shelter of Govardhana that Krsna held up as an umbrella for His kin.

Then, on the beautiful Dasami, the people of Vraja, rich with the jewels of scriptural truths, invited their king and his entourage to a sumptuous feast prepared by the people themselves.

After Nandaraja had finished eating, the cowherd men talked casually with their king for some time. But their hearts still burned with curiosity, and so they walked Nanda back to his palace.

The Vraja-vasis were intrigued — who was Krsna, really? They did not think much about it during the seven days they spent beneath Govardhana Hill. They simply enjoyed the bliss of Krsna’s pastimes.

But after Nanda Maharaja’s subjects had returned to their homes, perplexity arose within their hearts. They thought, “Now we have seen first-hand how child Krsna lifted Govardhana Hill.

“This feat reminds us of other extraordinary incidents, such as His killing Putana when He was just a baby. We thought such events were due to the brahmanas’ benedictions or Lord Narayana’s mercy.

“But our assumptions must have been false. No ordinary seven-year- old, however much blessed or empowered, could effortlessly hold up the king of mountains for seven days. This is impossible.

“We have no choice but to conclude that Krsna is not a human being. He must be the Supreme Personality of Godhead. Still, in everyday affairs we see that He behaves just like one of our own boys.

“He loves to be coddled. And He becomes morose or restless when His uncles, aunts, and well-wishers — simple cowherd folk, every one of them — fail to give Him the attention He demands.

“He appears to become hungry and thirsty. He steals yogurt and milk. He sometimes plays tricks, tells lies, chatters childishly, and tends the calves with the zest of an ordinary cowherd boy.

“If He is actually the Supreme Lord, why does He do things that are typical of an ordinary human child? This seems inconsistent. And this apparent inconsistency is confusing to us.

They concluded, “We are totally unable to understand the truth of Krsna’s identity. Let us go to the highly intelligent king of Vraja and enquire from him. He shall free us from our doubts.”

An alternate version of the following pastime, in which the subjects of Nandaraja express their doubts to their king, can be found in the appendix entitled “The Vraja-vasis Question Sri Krsna’s Divinity.”

The cowherd men arrived with Nanda Maharaja at his great assembly hall, where they took their seats just in front of their wives and humbly requested their king to resolve the issue.

They said, “Your son’s superhuman activities lead us to believe that He must be God, but since even minor avataras are born in high class families, how could He have taken birth amongst us lowly cowherds?

“Then again, if He is not the Supreme Lord, how could He have held up a mountain like a ball, smashed a cart with His feet as a small baby, or killed the demon Trnavarta at the tender age of one?

“How could He have knocked down a pair of trees while tied up for stealing butter, or seized the crane demon and bifurcated His lightning-bolt-hard beak while tending the cows?

“O King! Your son has killed so many demons that it would tire us to list them all: Vatsa, Dhenuka, Pralamba, Kesi, and Arista are but a tiny sample. But more evidence than this suggests His divinity.

“We residents of Vraja have a spontaneous and natural affection for your son that eclipses all our other attachments. And amazingly enough, He is also attached to us in the same spontaneous way.

“Although Krsna is not our son, we love Him more than our own children, more than our own bodies — more even than our own souls. Unless He is the Supersoul in everyone’s heart, how is this possible?

“And not only are we adults attached to Him, our boys have brotherly love for Him. The cows and calves, forest birds and animals, even the trees and creepers have an ever-increasing, undeniable love for Him.”

Nandaraja smiled as he countered the arguments of his subjects, “If my son were the self-satisfied Paramatma, how could He feel constant love for us cowherds who are always immersed in material life?”

The cowherd men revealed their anxiety. “Yes! It is this obvious contradiction that vexes us: on the one hand Krsna is a seven-year- old boy, but on the other hand He lifted a mountain.”

Nanda Maharaja joined his palms and prepared to answer the Vraja-vasis’ question. A hush descended over the hall as the king leaned against his throne, cleared his voice and said: “My son is not Narayana! He is human. But he is a human in whom Lord Narayana’s potencies have entered in order to mercifully deliver me from danger. Garga Muni told me this personally.”

This enquiry by his subjects filled Nanda Maharaja with bliss. By repeating the words that Garga Muni had spoken about Krsna, Nandaraja benevolently destroyed the Vraja-vasis’ doubts.

Maharaja Nanda began by quoting Garga Muni’s concluding words: “That omniscient sage said to me, ‘O King of Vrndavana! This child of yours is as good as Lord Narayana.

“‘In His transcendental name, fame, qualities, influence, and opulences He is exactly like Narayana. Therefore you should not he astonished by His extraordinary activities.’”

Nanda Maharaja did not want his subjects’ love for Krsna to be constrained by reverence for Krsna’s divinity, and thus he instructed them to treat Him as their son, brother, or friend. He said to them,

“Garga Muni told me not to let my son’s wonderful activities bewilder me into thinking that He is God; rather, he said that I should make Him the object of my compassion and never be indifferent to Him.

“Dear friends! I say the same thing to you. Consider Krsna to be invested with Lord Narayana’s power. He removes everyone’s suffering and is therefore the natural object of your affection.

“Do not put yourselves into needless distress by maintaining this mood of awe and reverence towards Krsna; rather, make this youthful son of mine the object of Your compassion and love.”

Nanda Maharaja’s speech filled the people of Vraja with bliss. And as they glorified Krsna, the king’s joy greatly increased. In that way the entire atmosphere of the great hall became thick with ecstasy.

At that very moment, Krsna and Balarama returned from playing in the forest and entered the palace, accompanied by Their friends. They were surprised to see the divinely intoxicated faces of the gopas.

Krsna appeared in the midst of the Vraja-vasis like the moon rising into the star-filled sky. They at once surrounded Him, appearing like the rings of effulgent light that at times surround the moon.

Krsna at once reciprocated the loving mood of His devotees. His soothing moonlike beauty transformed the Vraja-vasis into nightblooming lotuses unfurling with unlimited splendour.

As the people gazed at Krsna’s lotus face, they prayed for His mercy. And as they held Him tightly to their chests, they felt His touch melt their hearts just as moonlight melts candrakanta jewels.

The entire assembly became like a great ocean tossed by tidal waves at moonrise. By gazing at Krsna, the waves of bliss felt by every person present crashed onto the shore of Krsna’s dusty lotus feet.

After the Vraja-vasis had showered their love on Krsna for some time, they all left for their homes. But they quickly returned, bearing gifts for Krsna, Balarama, Nanda, Rohini, and Yasoda.

They decorated Krsna with beautiful golden cloth, valuable necklaces, armlets, earrings, and crown. Then they proceeded to adorn Balarama and Nanda Maharaja in the same way.

While the men adorned Nanda and his sons, the women dressed and ornamented Yasoda and Rohini in their private quarters. And all the while, the palace resounded with cries of “Jaya Sri Krsna!”

Once they were satisfied with the bounty of gifts they had bestowed upon Krsna, the Vraja-vasis departed, shouting all the way home, “All glories, all glories to the jewel of Vraja-dhama! Long may He live!”

In recounting this pastime — in which the cowherd men put questions to their king about wonderful Krsna — Sukadeva Gosvami invokes the mercy of the Lord with the following prayer:

“Indra became angry when his sacrifice was disrupted, and thus he caused rain and hail to fall on Vraja, accompanied by heavy winds, all of which brought great suffering to the cowherds and animals there.

“When Krsna, who is by nature always compassionate, saw the condition of those who have no other shelter but Him, He lifted Govardhana with one hand, just as a child picks up a mushroom.

“Holding up the hill, He protected the cowherd community and their cows. May He, Govinda, the Lord of the cows and the destroyer of Indra’s false pride, be pleased with us!”

**\* \* \* \***

On the same day that Nanda revealed the truths about his son to the bewildered gopas, an even more bewildered Indra sat in his bedroom, unable to free himself from the burden of his offences to Krsna.

Indra had recognised the error of his ways, but the humility he needed to make amends was covered by false pride, bom from shame over his failures. Still, that shame had also awakened true remorse.

His mind in turmoil, his body devoid of its natural lustre, unwilling to see his wife, and unable to enter the assembly of demigods, Indra thought that his only recourse was to end his own life.

All-knowing Brhaspati came to Indra’s quarters. Because he was the spiritual master of the demigods, after accepting Indra’s worship he rebuked his disciple with the following words: “O Indra! Because you have offended Krsna, naturally you are full of distress. Having lost Krsna’s grace, you are like a tree suffering for want of sunshine — the natural grace of the sun.

“First you were blinded by pride, and so you challenged the Supreme Personality of Godhead. Now, blinded by ignorance, you are planning to end your own life. ? Indra, this plan is most ill-conceived!

“That same Supreme Lord whom you have made an enemy by attacking the Vraja-vasis, you will make even more inimical to you by attacking your own body. How foolish you are!”

Indra deeply regretted his folly. His thousand eyes made a pool of tears on the floor as he stammered, “This past week I have made a string of careless blunders. Please tell me — what should I do now?”

Brhaspati replied, “In such a situation only the creator, Brahma, can help. He is an ocean of wisdom. Take shelter of him. Ask him what steps you can take to rectify this very precarious situation.”

Indra travelled to Brahmapuri full of grief and lamentation. Though at first reluctant, he described to his great-grandfather the details of his offences to Krsna, to Krsna’s associates, and to Krsna’s cows.

After Lord Brahma had heard everything, he shook his heads in disbelief, saying, “Alas! As king of the demigods you have foolishly committed so many grave offences! This will not be easily forgotten.

“The great luminaries who hear of your sins will cover their ears, mock you, and give up your company. Unfortunately, I have no power to free you from the poison of your intolerable arrogance.”

Indra was more dejected than ever to hear such condemning words. His mind at once turned again to thoughts of suicide. Fortunately for him, Brahma thought to give him hope with the following advice: “Great sages like Gautama have declared that one can be freed from offences to Lord Krsna by feeding, circumambulating, and thereby pleasing the cows, who are exceedingly dear to Him.

“Go to Surabhi, the primal mother of the cows. Worship her and solicit her help to invoke Lord Krsna’s grace. If you fail, the demigods will certainly suffer defeat at the hands of the demons.”

Remorse for his offences had already made Indra receptive to good advice, but it was fear of the demons that propelled him to Surabhiloka, where he sadly pleaded his case before the goddess.

King Indra spoke many sweet words to Surabhi, appealing to their familial ties, and convinced her to intercede on his behalf. Together they returned to Lord Brahma who wisely said:

“O mother! Please approach Lord Krsna and assure Him that Indra has sincerely repented for his sins. Then bathe the Lord with milk in order to convince Him to forgive Indra’s transgressions.”

Surabhi agreed to Lord Brahma’s request. She went along with Indra, and both appeared in the sky above Vrndavana on the Ekadasi day, just as Krsna was inspecting Govardhana Hill.

Krsna had gone herding the cows as usual. But because He was still fatigued from His recent pastimes, He sought relief in the tranquil groves and gardens surrounding Giri Govardhana.

Since Balarama had stayed home, Krsna deputed His friends to look after the herd while He strolled alone along Govardhana’s ridge, curious to see if Indra’s lightning bolts had inflicted any damage.

Krsna was aware that Indra was hovering overhead, come to beg pardon for his grave offences, and so He sat down on an effulgent jewelled throne in a secluded garden beside the hill.

Krsna rested His left foot on His thigh, and began to casually play a soothing melody upon His flute, showering untold mercy upon the king of mountains and inviting Indra to come forward.

Unable to discern exactly where Krsna was, at first Indra could not approach the Lord. Then the determination to right his wrongs began to recede. These obstacles were the results of his offences.

Surabhi noted Indra’s confusion and guided his descent, saying, “That mass of darkness beneath us is Govardhana Hill, and what appears to be a newly formed cloud over there is your master, Krsna.

“O deva! That flash of lightning and gently rolling thunder you hear are also not what they appear to be. Actually, they are the splendour of Krsna’s yellow garments and the resonance of His flute-song.”

By the purifying effect of Surabhi’s company, Indra could now see Krsna distinctly. With renewed courage he directed his elephant, Airavata, towards the earth, but Surabhi stopped him.

“O King of demigods! Do not take your elephant with you. Go alone without your elephant carrier and in a humble mood fall flat like a stick before the Lord to offer obeisances at His lotus feet.

“With a pure heart and sweet words convince Him to forgive you. Genuine remorse invokes the mercy of even an ordinary person, not to mention a truly illustrious person like Him.”

But Indra’s determination wavered yet again due to fear that the Lord would reject him. Compassionate Surabhi thus went along with the timid demigod to meet the Lord, leaving Airavata behind.

Together, the two alighted. Once they had landed they offered their respects to Hari, and in her humble way, Surabhi stepped forward and spoke with the eloquence of the goddess of learning: “O Lord!

“You are the tilaka mark of good fortune on the head of King Nanda’s capital. Even if hundreds of Indras were to try to kill the cows and gopas, You would protect Gokula and fill its citizens with bliss.

“The person standing behind me is overwhelmed with grief for his offences to You. Ashamed of his arrogance and thoroughly repentant, he comes to beg Your pardon. Please allow him to submit his petition.”

Krsna’s kindly glance upon the king of heaven inspired him to climb down from his mountain of false pride and fall at Krsna’s lotus feet, the toenails of which shone brighter than the jewels in Indra’s crown.

For a long time Indra lay at Krsna’s feet and wept, equally moved by fear and repentance. Finally, the Lord touched Indra’s crown and brought the humbled demigod to his feet with beguiling words.Krsna said, “? Lord Indra. You are the shelter upon which rest the instructions of great sages. Please do not bow to Me. I am the one who misbehaved by stopping the sacrifice meant to please you.”

Embarrassed by Krsna’s inconceivable humility, Indra placed his joined palms to his head and remained silent, as if he had been struck by his own thunderbolt. What could he say?

Gazing at Krsna’s transcendental beauty and remembering Krsna’s words, Indra felt various spiritual emotions. As the king of heaven stood before his Lord, he resembled a firefly before the sun.

Indra was decorated with a garland and sandalwood paste. He wore a crown and held his thunderbolt, and yet he felt foolish before indestructible Krsna, who was dressed as a simple cowherd boy.

The beauty of Krsna’s limbs, blue-lotus complexion, restless eyes, and mild smile were themselves the ultimate ornaments, proclaiming Krsna’s self-satisfied independence from the creation.

Indeed, to Indra’s eyes it appeared that Krsna’s decorated turban, shining cloth, flower garland, and shark-shaped earrings were made beautiful and effulgent by contact with the Lord’s spiritual body.

For as long as Indra stood mute and motionless, Krsna was happy to remain the silent object of his meditation. Finally, the flapping of Garuda’s wings reminded Indra of his mission.

For the entire time Krsna had been sitting upon the jewelled throne, Garuda had been hovering overhead, using his wings to shelter his Lord from the searing rays of the late morning sun.

Indra was accustomed to issuing orders to the demigods with a voice as grave as thunder rumbling in the sky, but now that he had been humbled he spoke in a sweet and submissive voice, his palms joined.

**\* \* \* \***

Indra said, “Dear Lord! You are the Supreme Personality of Godhead. I know all too well the strength of Your illusory potency, maya. Please do not bewilder me further by posing as a conditioned soul.

“You are eternally situated in pure goodness. Therefore, because You are above the modes of material nature, You can never be at fault. Rather, I have exposed my countless failings, much to my misfortune.

“You appear in this world to kill demons like Putana, protect Your devotees like the Vraja-vasis, curb the pride of fools such as I, and re-establish the principles of religion by instructing the faithful.

“You do all this not out of greed, anger, lust, or envy, but solely out of kindness to the entire creation. Thus You are the well-wisher of everyone — demon, demigod, or devotee — albeit in different ways.”

Indra was still much too concerned about his own fate, and so he continued in a calculating way: “As for this wretched soul, I have been justly punished for succumbing to pride and false ego.”

Indra did not wish to speak in such a way that Krsna might reply, “I lifted Govardhana Hill only to protect the Vraja-vasis, not to punish you. I will leave your punishment to Yamaraja.”

Thus Indra hastily continued, making a show of humility in order to save himself from further chastisement and humiliation: “I am such a fool that I cannot be purified by punishment.

“I therefore beg You to purify me by Your mercy, so that my pride may not rear its ugly head again, and that I may one day be counted amongst the pure souls who faithfully serve Your lotus feet.

“Obeisances unto You, the original Personality of Godhead, unto Your plenary portion, Narayana, and Your further expansions, like Maha-Visnu, Garbhodakasayi-Visnu and, Ksirodakasayi-Visnu.”

“From your prayers,” Krsna interrupted, “I can see that you are a devotee and that the Samvartaka clouds must have acted independently. You should punish them accordingly.”

Krsna wanted the demigod to do more than tender a general apology. He wanted Indra to describe his offences in detail so that he could fully understand the seriousness of what he had done.

Indra then understood that he was speaking with the all-knowing Supersoul, from whom he could not hide anything. Thus he considered it wise to take full responsibility for his colossal blunder.

Nevertheless, Indra still weighed the consequences of his confession. He said, “The Samvartaka clouds are neither to be blamed nor punished, for it was I who ordered them to destroy Vrndavana.”

Krsna raised His eyebrows in astonishment and replied, “But Indra! How is it possible for My devotee to attack Me and My associates?” Indra muttered, “I became angry when my worship was stopped.”

Still not satisfied, Krsna continued, “But I am your master and have every right to stop a sacrifice of which I am the beneficiary and you are merely the medium. I don’t understand how this happened.”

Indra tried to explain, “A person blinded by false pride is capable of doing anything.” Again Krsna persisted, “Although pride may sometimes arise in My devotees, they curb it with intelligence.”

Indra lowered his head in shame and again replied, “I should have used good intelligence to free myself from the influence of pride, but hysterical anger covered my intelligence. Thus I succumbed.”

His many eyes pleading for clemency, Indra prayed, “I have greatly disturbed Your Vraja, and now You have diagnosed my disease like an expert physician. And You have prescribed the suitable medicine.

“O master of illusion! Please be kind to me. With a sidelong glance you create fortune or misfortune. Please free me from the humiliating impurities that force me to degrade myself.”

In the course of this confession, Indra came to humbly accept his faults and to reveal why he had acted so unbefittingly. In response, the Lord now revealed what had been His purpose. Krsna began:

“If the demigods are to be worshipped for proudly enjoying celestial pleasures, then why not worship drunkards and flesh-eaters, who also proudly enjoy the so-called pleasures they covet?”

Then, with the resonant voice of absolute authority, Krsna added, “A man intoxicated by his power and opulence is blind, for he cannot see Me standing nearby with the rod of punishment in My hand.

“Desiring his actual welfare, I drag him down from his materially fortunate position. Indra, you may now go. Execute my order and remain in your appointed position. But be sober, without false pride.”

This grave lesson was meant not only for the king of heaven, but also for all the demigods. And although Krsna told Indra to refrain from arrogance, the Lord did not free him from that ignoble quality.

Now that Indra’s misconduct was rectified, and now that he was free from fear, Surabhi affectionately called the spiritual cows of Vraja to her side. Then, all together they approached Sri Krsna.

Gentle Surabhi attracted Krsna’s attention by respectfully offering Him obeisances in the way that cows do. Krsna smiled and put His palms together in favour, asking, “Mother! Why have you come?”

Surabhi repeated Krsna’s names over and over, then replied, “O soul of the universe! I have come to thank You for protecting my offspring from the wrath of he who was supposed to be our protector and king.

“Since Indra tried instead to destroy the cows of Vraja, he is no longer fit to be our king. You protected us from his madness by lifting Govardhana Hill; therefore, You are our real king — our real Indra.

“O Supreme Lord — now sitting before me in Your form as a cowherd boy — please hear the following request, which I submit on behalf of Brahma and all the cows that wander about in the heavenly realm.

“They humbly propose, ’We are very pleased by Your transcendental pastime of protecting the cows and rescuing them from the wrath of Indra. Your compassion is undoubtedly praiseworthy.

“‘Mother cow, and her husband the bull, selflessly fulfil the needs of humankind; the bulls serve farmers by pulling carts and tilling the land, and the cows please everyone by providing milk in abundance.

‘“The demigods, for example, are especially satisfied by the pure ghee offered to them through the sacrificial fire, and Laksmi-devi is gratified by a profusion of dung from the cows, calves, and bulls.

“‘O immensely powerful Lord! You are our guru because you have delivered us from great danger. Therefore, from this day onwards may You be known as Govinda — one who gives pleasure to the cows.

“‘This is our humble request: Please permit both the cows and the demigods to give You a coronation bath. Let us crown You as our Indra. We wish to call You Govinda from now on.’”

Internally, Krsna was pleased by this request, but externally He feigned reluctance. He enquired, “On whose authority do you propose to conduct this coronation? Or is this your independent desire?”

Surabhi answered, “The go-sukta prayers declare the cow to be an abode of the demigods, of whom You are the protector. It is only fitting, therefore, that You accept the title ‘Monarch of the Cows.’

“And I am not alone in recognising this truth,” Surabhi continued. “Brahma is of the same conviction. And even the dull-witted fool Indra has been purified by perceiving this truth.”

Motioning towards Indra, she continued, “When this miserable soul and I went to Brahma in order to report the injustices Indra had committed, the four-headed creator gave us an order.

“Lord Brahma said, ‘You must immediately go to Krsna, the ocean of mercy, and beg forgiveness for Indra’s offences. Then perform an abhiseka of the Lord and instal Him as the true master of the cows.’”

Krsna nodded His head slightly and asked, “How would such an abhiseka benefit you?” Surabhi replied, “Dear Sir! We desire to perform this rite in order to purify ourselves.”

Surabhi turned to Indra, “Look! As he gazes at your lotus feet, Indra is already performing an abhiseka with the tears flooding from his many eyes, thus disclosing the way in which he wants to worship You.”

Krsna looked at Surabhi and the cows surrounding her, then at Indra and the many demigods assembling behind him. With a gentle smile He consented with the words “So be it” amidst joyous cries of “Jaya!”

**\* \* \* \***

Word of Krsna’s coronation spread quickly. Almost at once the forest around and the sky above Krsna became thick with great demigods, exalted sages, celestial damsels, and supernatural beings.

In their hands they held gifts for their Lord and ingredients for the abhiseka. From their lips came the sounds of auspicious mantras and devotional prayers, which mingled with the celestial music in the air.

A short distance from where Krsna was sitting, Giriraja manifested an elegant throne made of smooth stones, inset with jewels, just suitable for a bathing platform, which Krsna graciously accepted.

Accompanied by Brahma, Narada, Aditi, and Indra, Surabhi stood before the Lord and said, “O Krsna! Places of pilgrimage become holy by the touch of Your feet and the feet of Your servants.

“We beg permission to bring the waters of the celestial Ganga and — so that it may become even more auspicious — to mix it with the milk from my udders. Or is there anything else You desire from us?”

Krsna — a small cowherd boy amidst the controllers of universal affairs — humbly smiled at the august assembly surrounding Him. Then, He simply shrugged His shoulders and replied, “As you wish!”

The demigods joyfully took Krsna’s cue, undertaking various services that had been previously agreed upon. Meanwhile, the wild creatures of the forest, as if invited guests, assembled to witness the festivities.

Varuna held a white umbrella over Krsna. Vayu, trembling in ecstasy, fanned the Lord with a camara. The moon assumed the form of a bejewelled mirror, and the Pancajanya filled the atmosphere with joy.

Lord Visnu’s cakra expanded into many lamps, filling the directions with light. His lotus expanded into many umbrellas to offer shade, and His club into a jewelled pedestal for holding the bathing articles.

One by one, divine beings placed ingredients for bathing upon that pedestal, beginning with the personified forms of the sacred oceans, lakes, and rivers, who brought their waters in jewelled pots.

Next came mother earth, who offered the finest quality soil. The best herbs and medicinal plants offered their extracts, great trees like the banyan, their leaves, and the gods of the forest offered coconuts.

As the nine jewels and eight perfections looked on, the lords of the mountains presented exotic gems, and their wives gave gold cloth, glittering necklaces, golden lotuses, and the finest sandalwood.

The personified prayers and mantras chanted on alternating high and low notes, the god of fire offered aguru incense, and the kings of the snakes became bejewelled flags surrounding the bathing platform.

From the sky, demigods vibrated instrumental music and sang in chorus. Surabhi contributed the panca-gavya, Lord Brahma the pancamrta, and Indra’s elephant, water from the celestial Ganges.

Celestial ladies showered flowers while Gandharvas danced in the sky as part of a special drama performed by the Apsaras, in which they conveyed meaning with gestures and facial expressions.

When all the items for bathing were ready, Lord Brahma ordered the abhiseka to begin. At that point, out of spontaneous affection for Krsna, Mother Surabhi created a lake with her milk.

Brahma inaugurated the worship by washing Krsna’s feet with water from sacred rivers mixed with his own tears, and then offering madhu- parka and tambula, all the time reciting Krsna’s names.

Still embarrassed by his offenses, Indra hesitated to begin the abhiseka. But encouraged by Aditi and other ladies, he ordered the bathing to start by a movement of his eyebrows.

The ladies of heaven were the first to bathe Krsna. Surabhi began, followed by Arundhati, Parvati, Gayatri, Aditi, Sarasvati, Svaha, and others. The five substances they used included Surabhi’s milk.

Then, while all these mothers smeared Krsna’s body with fragrant powders and oils, Brahma, Indra, the Kumaras, the Sapta-rsis, Narada, and many, many others bathed Krsna to their heart’s content.

By the powerful presence of the fulfillers of wishes — kama-dhenu, kalpa-taru, and cintamani — whatever was required for the abhiseka, but was not already present, magically appeared.

Krsna reciprocated with the enthusiastic demigods, acknowledging their offerings by smiling and glancing at them in pleasing ways, but in His heart He was anxious to rejoin the cowherd boys.

The abhiseka was performed strictly according to scriptural injunctions, but all the formality and reverence of the demigods did not please Krsna as much as the Vraja-vasis’ spontaneous love.

Still, Krsna did not want to disturb the demigods’ happiness by interfering with their worship. After all, they were also devotees trying to serve Him, though with regulated devotion.

When the bathing was complete, a few young damsels dried Krsna’s limbs and hair. Then, directed by Parvati, they neatly dressed Him with the best of garments, ornaments, and unguents.

Krsna was used to dressing simply — as a cowherd boy, with dhoti, turban, ornaments, and a garland — but the demigods worshipped Him as the Supreme Lord, dressing Him with the opulence of a king.

They attired Krsna in a bejewelled jacket, with many strands of pearls, thick armlets, sparkling rings, a royal belt, cintamani ankle bells, and shoes set with gems from every corner of the universe.

After offering Krsna an opulent seat beneath a tree, Brahma proceeded to chant elaborate mantras as he worshipped Krsna with sixteen items. Lord Siva then singled out a special small boy nearby.

Lord Brahma recognised the boy as the personified form of the Gopala mantra, who had come to empower the creator, as eminent devotees like Dhruva, Prahlada, and the Vasus looked on.

The worship complete, Brahma sat in a lotus posture and fixed his eight eyes in meditation on the Lord. The other demigods then stepped forward, each in turn, to offer Krsna gifts.

The personified form of the Milk Ocean brought white conch shells, the goddess of Kailasa presented a crystal lota, and the goddess of the Himalayas offered a tray of aromatic wild flowers.

The forest fairies brought a mouth freshener of nutmeg, cloves, and allspice. The earth goddess brought an assortment of natural scents. Some demigods brought an ornament of flowers from Indra’s garden.

Indra’s wife offered tambula made with golden pan leaves, Svaha a brilliant ghee lamp. Other gods and goddesses presented endless offerings of love, Lord Siva a flute he had carved with his own hands.

This handy gift particularly intrigued Krsna. He raised His eyebrows and playfully put it to His lips. With a sweet melody, He delighted the demigods and showed His spontaneous gratitude to Lord Siva.

Finally, Indra and Brahma together painted artistic tilaka on Krsna’s forehead. Then, overcome by bliss, the king of heaven placed a lavish crown on Krsna’s head and declared, “You are the king of all kings!”

An expectant silence fell upon the assembled celestials. Indeed, even the many creatures of Vraja who had been observing the event from the nearby trees and bushes hushed to hear what would happen next.

Indra said, “The most glorious realm in the spiritual sky is Goloka, the eternal home of innumerable gopis, gopas, and cows. And because You rule over and protect them all, You bear the name Govinda!

“O Lord! You are also the protector of the demigods, humans, and cows of this world. Now let us coronate You with that same name — Govinda. Allow us to address You as do Your eternal associates.”

As soon as King Indra had made this proclamation, flowers fell gently from the heavens, gradually filling the sky and the space below, until it felt as though there was hardly room left for air.

At the same time, celestial voices joined the chorus of attending demigods, all softly repeating “Govinda” until the sublime resonance of that name echoed in the valleys and throughout the universe.

Indra handed Krsna the ensign of royalty, a white parasol. The sound “Govinda” filled everyone’s ears, Krsna’s incandescent beauty lit up the directions, and His sweet fragrance permeated the earth.

A towering monsoon cloud of bliss seemed to gather around all living entities, and rapture rained down upon them. For a short time this auspicious downpour transformed the entire earth into Vaikuntha.

The water of the oceans, lakes, and rivers became transparently clear. The sun, moon, and stars glowed very pleasingly. And the many varieties of air moved about gently, giving relief to all.

All natural disturbances stopped. Kings gave up their mutual enmity. Trees sprouted fresh flowers, twigs, and leaves. And the mountains, along with their animals and trees, were all delighted.

The whole world appeared as pleasant and joyful as if it were awash in a tidal wave of nectar. And the hearts of demigods, humans, and animals alike were all immersed in that euphoria.

**\* \* \* \***

The overwhelming joy of the world was felt most keenly by Govardhana Hill. In fact he began to melt in ecstasy, thus threatening to provide an unexpected finale to the coronation.

The demigods began to sink steadily into Giriraja’s now soft stones. Unable to secure a steady footing, they were perplexed about what to do, and they looked helplessly towards Krsna to rescue them.

Krsna first paused to smile at the amusing predicament in which His devotees now found themselves, then He lovingly placed His hand upon the ground and calmed Giriraja as He would a frisky calf.

Simply by Krsna’s reassuring touch, Giri Govardhana could under' stand his master’s desire. He suppressed his ecstasies so that his surface would regain its normal stony firmness.

The crisis averted, Siva set aside his snake ornaments and skull garland, washed off the ashes smeared all over his body, applied fresh tilaka, put on new tiger skins, and happily offered the final arati.

He worshipped Krsna with pure fragrant clay, incense, paddy, an elegant ghee lamp, sweet smelling scents, and various mantras, fob lowing which he sounded a conch shell triumphantly.

In accordance with Vedic injunctions, the wives of the demigods took turns offering arati with ghee lamps, while Narada Muni and Tumburu sang fervent songs about Krsna’s lifting Govardhana Hill.

Indra presented the Lord with a sizeable tray weighed down with sweets prepared in the kitchens of the demigods from the milk of heaven’s cows. And Govinda heartily savoured them all.

Krsna was polite enough to not reveal His thoughts to the demigods, but He did not find even the best treats of heaven a match for the least of Vraja’s candies, especially those made by Sri Radha.

The coronation ceremony now concluded, Brahma distributed maha-prasada to all present. He then requested the wish-fulfilling trees and cintamani gems to award priceless gifts to His guests.

At last the demigods circumambulated Govinda, chanting His names, clapping their hands, and dancing as they went. After offering Him their respectful obeisances, they ascended into the clouds above.

Only Surabhi and Indra remained to await Krsna’s final order. The king of heaven, now purified at heart, glowed with natural splendour, and his eyes glistened with tears of love for the Lord.

In a happy mood, Krsna asked, “O Indra! You are My confidential servant and therefore you should not conceal the contents of your heart. Tell Me truthfully: Is your anger now pacified?”

Indra meekly bowed his head to show that he had learned his lesson. Krsna continued, “I cannot bear to see My devotees polluted by false pride. In fact, it is well known that I punish such persons.

“Indra! I stopped the sacrifice to favour you. Therefore, do not remain envious of Me upon your return to Svarga, but enjoy your position carefully, without again becoming bewildered by wealth.”

King Indra listened patiently and accepted Krsna’s gentle censure. Still, he made no move to leave. Krsna understood that Indra had something more to say and so the Lord invited him to speak.

Indra began, “My dear Lord, I admit that I have committed great offences unto You and to those whom You love, offences for which I will never be able to fully and properly express my feelings of regret.

“But at the same time my heart is engladdened by Your extraordinary chivalrous spirit, Your endeavour to protect Your devotees, Your firm determination, and Your steadiness in lifting Govardhana Hill.

“I am further awed by and I praise Your beautiful bodily features and Your astonishing quality of being pleased simply by accepting Your devotees’ prayers, even after they have offended You.

“O my Lord!” said Indra. “By the grace of Surabhi, Lord Brahma, and the other demigods, my first purpose for coming here has been fulfilled: I have rid myself of sins by overseeing Your coronation.

“Now kindly hear the second reason for my coming. I request that You kill the invincible demons Kamsa, Kesi, and Aristasura, and thereafter bring all the kings of the earth under Your control.

“Regarding this last request I have one special plea: My plenary portion has recently been born from the womb of your paternal aunt, Queen Kunti. He now bears the name Arjuna.

“He will be renowned as the greatest archer of the Bharata dynasty. But more importantly, he will be famous as Your very intimate friend — one who is never happy anywhere without You.

“O Krsna! You know everything that is to happen in the future! After Kamsa’s demise, all the kings of the earth will come together in a great war that will be chronicled in the Mahabharata.

“In that battle, Arjuna, seated on a chariot driven by Your very self, will soundly defeat all the powerful kings and warriors, enjoy the fruits of victory, and gain unending fame.

“I request You to protect my son, Your eternal friend, and personally enlighten him in the science of spiritual knowledge, even as You have enlightened me in humility and pridelessness.

“O Krsna — You who are fixed always in truth — I am Your surrendered servant and Arjuna is my second self. If, therefore, I am at all dear to you, please carry out my request. This is my appeal!”

Krsna paused for a moment to think of His dear friend Arjuna, who, along with his brothers and mother, were sheltered at that time in the hermitages of Himalayan sages. The Lord then replied:

“O Sakra! Whatever you have requested will be done. Of this there is no doubt. Arjuna is My eternal friend. Therefore, for as long as I am in this world, no one will be able to harm him. Rest assured.

“I am also inclined to Yudhisthira and his other brothers. Because of My affection for Arjuna, I will return all of the Pandavas to Kunti unharmed after the completion of the great battle.

“Whatever your son Arjuna may ask of Me, I will always do because of My strong love for him.” And with that, Krsna finished speaking to the lord of the demigods and raised His right hand in blessing.

Indra gratefully bowed before his master. Along with Surabhi, he circumambulated Krsna and then joined the other demigods on the heavenly planets, leaving Krsna alone on His coronation throne.

**\* \* \* \***

While the demigods were concluding their worship of Govinda, the cowherd boys, who had been tending the cows without Krsna, happened to be approaching the site of the coronation.

From a distance the cowherd boys could see some very unusual yet dignified beings having a festival, and their friend Krsna was the centre of attention. This piqued their boyish curiosity.

Before the demigods departed, they had also seen the rambunctious gopas, who called out to Krsna from within a cloud of dust raised by the cows’ hooves and mocked the demigods’ appearance.

The celestials and their wives had departed hastily, leaving many of their personal belongings strewn throughout the forest, for they had not wanted to delay Krsna’s pastimes any further.

When the cowherd boys reached their friend, they embraced Him and smelled His head. But when they noticed the royal parasol, other gifts, and the demigods’ belongings, the boys were perplexed.

In a spate of boyish exuberance they interrogated their friend, “Who were those beings?” “What were they doing?” “How did You come by these gifts?” “Who is the owner of all these strange things?”

Krsna did not want to divulge the truth of the demigods’ visit. He feared that if His friends discovered His true identity, their profound respect might hamper the natural love they felt for Him.

He therefore answered their questions with a string of inscrutable stories. The boys could not understand a word Krsna said, let alone believe His far-fetched tales, and yet they had no better answers.

But then again, consistent, cogent explanations for why and how extraordinary events transpired around Krsna were of no interest to the gopas, whose only quest in life was to love their miraculous friend.

Krsna playfully distracted His friends by decorating them with the ornaments He had received from the demigods. Luckily, they were happy simply to talk, joke, and laugh amongst themselves.

The boys decided to take advantage of the many articles of worship scattered here and there. They had Krsna sit on His throne again so that they could serve Him with ghee lamps and camaras.

Some boys blew conch shells, some bathed Krsna’s lotus feet, others offered flower petals, and still others danced about singing extemporaneous songs glorifying their friend’s heroic deeds.

Meanwhile, the demigods, from far above, gazed in wonder at how Krsna’s childhood friends — friends similar to Him in qualities, form, and nature — spontaneously worshipped Him.

Mouths agape, they stared at the joyful, informal, and impromptu way in which the cowherd boys served their friend, using the priceless articles that the demigods had just offered to Krsna as gifts.

The demigods had thought that to coronate Krsna was the fulfilment of their spiritual aspirations, but now they realised that the worship they were witnessing was in a class of its own.

The cowherd boys, absorbed in blissful reveries, were oblivious to time, but Krsna noticed the sun leaning on the western horizon, and so He said, “We have delayed too long. Let’s go back home.”

As Krsna led His friends and cows back to Nandagrama, the demigods hovered over them, continually showering flowers upon the Lord and His entourage and chanting, “Govinda jaya jaya!”

Until they entered the sanctuary of Nanda Maharaja’s palace, the cowherd boys held the white parasol over Krsna’s head, fanned Him with gem-studded camaras, and blew divine conch shells.

Except for the unrestrained displays of joyful childish love unknown to the residents of Vaikuntha, the boys resembled Narayana’s entourage accompanying the Lord of Vaikuntha to His palace.

As the boys entered Nanda’s courtyard, the king at once noticed the regal parasol and other celestial paraphernalia the boys were wearing and waving about, so he called the village elders to witness.

Reasonably concerned about how his son’s friends had acquired such treasures, Nanda Maharaja deputed some cowherds to do the evening milking. He and his kinsmen then sat down with the boys.

The boys first offered respectful obeisances to the king and his ministers. Upon Krsna’s order, Sridama collected all the gifts given by the demigods and presented them to Nanda Maharaja.

Krsna’s father overflowed with love. He asked Sridama, “Tell me truthfully, from where did you boys get these treasures?” Sridama turned his palms upwards in a gesture of uncertainty and answered:

“We were far away taking care of the cows, and Krsna was alone by Govardhana Hill. When we finally found Him, this parasol and these other things were scattered throughout the forest.

“I do not know what happened, nor would Krsna tell us. However, O wise king, the mere sight of these wonderful things surely declares their origin and their owners. Does it not?”

The elder cowherd men looked at Krsna, who humbly bowed His head as Nandaraja announced, “Well spoken Sridama!” Gopas young and old stood motionless, gawking at Krsna like figures in a painting.

The cowherd men could not help but remember the previous day’s meeting with Nanda Maharaja, who had then said of his extraordinary son, “This boy is just like Lord Narayana.”

They judiciously concluded that the demigods had likely worshipped Krsna with the items the boys now held in their hands. “They must have known the truth of Krsna’s empowerment,” the men concluded.

Madhumangala, in his usual comical role, exclaimed, “O king of Vraja! Please hear me. This afternoon Krsna had us tend the cows while He went wandering to Govardhana Hill alone.

“Some hours later we saw Him from a distance, seated on a large rock at the foot of Giriraja. After He listened to a talking cow, a person with eyes all over his body offered obeisances to Krsna.

“Meanwhile, a white elephant with five heads again and again brought water from somewhere, and then two people, one with four heads and one with five, offered Krsna prayers.

“O King!” said Madhumangala excitedly. “I cannot recall all the details, but at some point many other peculiar beings bathed your son and then observed His coronation with a great festival.”

Just as one of Nanda Maharaja’s ministers was about to suggest that they also perform a sacred ritual for their beloved Krsna, from the sky a thunderous celestial voice proclaimed:

“O King of Vraja! As we demigods performed a coronation abhiseka and accepted your son as ‘Govinda,’ so you should worship Him and likewise crown Him as the prince of Vrndavana.”

Nanda and the elders assembled at the palace were not the only ones to hear those celestial words. The entire cowherd community flocked there, feeling that they had been caught in a downpour of nectar.

Filled with inexplicable bliss, the Vraja-vasis decided that they would celebrate a grand festival of singing, dancing, and music. The acaryas would give blessings, and the elders would give charity to them.

And so it was that the people of Vraja worshipped and crowned Krsna as the prince of their land, dedicating their homes, cows, possessions, and, most importantly, their very selves to His service.

At the climax of the bathing and coronation, hundreds of the most exalted ladies of Vraja, with a great tumult of ululation and other auspicious sounds, offered arati to Krsna with jewelled ghee lamps.

They gave Him gifts of jewels and sweets. Then, accepting Him as the object of filial love personified, they carefully and respectfully brought Him to His home, into the arms of His adoring mother.

Many young gopis, their yearning eyes covered by veils, also came to that festival. And as the opportunity arose for each of them to come close to Krsna, in their hearts arose the desire to marry Him.

Thus, as they looked at Krsna, and as He returned their furtive glances, each cowherd girl thought, “What could be a more fitting addition to this festival than my marriage to Krsna?”

But a lavish wedding was not to be part of the evening’s festivities. So the young unmarried beauties of Vraja lamented their fate and found some solace in criticising the audacity of Krsna’s flute.

They thought, “Alas! Misguided Providence has led Krsna away from us once again! Krsna is like the radiant and glorious sun already set over the western horizon and no longer in view.“To add to the injustice of it all, that dull and stunted bamboo flute — devoid of the faintest trace of virtue — stays in constant touch with Krsna, while we remain at an agonising distance.

“This insignificant instrument happily sips the nectar of Krsna’s lips, embraces His neck, rests on His chest, and again and again finds itself at home tucked in the cloth belt around His waist.

“And all this takes place in plain sight! The people of Vraja don’t even think to object.” At this the gopis began to cry softly, hiding their tears in the corners of their veils, before going on:

“If we mount our rooftops to catch even a fleeting glimpse of Krsna, we become the object of our parents’ or our in-laws’ severe scorn, proving once again that Providence has gone insane.”

That night, after the festivities had ended, Yasoda put Krsna to rest in the rooftop suite meant for gazing at the moon. But He could not sleep, His heart having been broken by the gopis’ sorrowful glances.

With a heart trembling with agitation, Krsna rose in the middle of the night and restlessly paced the roof. An occasional tear-filled glance at the moon gave Him precious little comfort.

The cowherd girls were all beautiful, virtuous, born in saintly families, and full of love for Krsna. Indeed, their yearning to be with Him had brought them almost to the verge of death.

To openly express His “lover’s melancholy” — the pain He felt in separation from them, a pain felt no less intensely by them for Him — Krsna played the flute Siva had given Him earlier that day.

Krsna touched the gopis, who were suffering through their own sleepless night, with a melody played in kedara-raga, a melody that wound its way through the ether of Vraja like a sorrowful breeze.

That melody entered into the gopis’ hearts to reassure them that soon their as yet unattained desire would indeed be fulfilled. That was, after all, Krsna’s eternal promise — na me bhaktah pranasyati.

Standing upon the roofs of their homes, their eyes fixed upon the observatory of Nanda Maharaja’s palace, their ears filled with the sound of Krsna’s flute, the young gopis felt consoled.

Each moon-faced, golden-complexioned beauty thought that Krsna was repeatedly calling her, speaking to her. Overcome with emotion, they all fell to the ground unconscious.

Amongst those beautiful girls laying in a swoon beneath Vrndavana’s starlit night, only Srimati Radharani had thought, “They are all so worthy of His love. And I am so unqualified.”

## Chapter Eleven - Naradavana to Harideva

For devotees who do parikrama solely of Govardhana Hill, sastra advises to begin and end their trek at Manasa-ganga, first offering prayers to Harideva, guardian of Bhauma Vrndavana’s eastern petal.

An alternate place to start and end parikrama is at Radha-kunda and Syama-kunda, where devotees first offer obeisances and prayers to the two liquid forms of the divine couple.

Devotees who follow the full parikrama of New Vraja-dhama, however, will as a matter of course join the path around Giriraja at Rahela, leaving it at Ganthuli to continue around Vrndavana Village.

I also began to circumambulate Govardhana at the forest of Rahela, yet I still tried to remember all the holy places situated around the Hill I would miss by taking that route.

Actually it is impossible to describe or even to remember all the sacred sites on and around Giriraja because they are innumerable; even Brahma cannot count them, let alone name them all.

Because Govardhana Hill is a form of Lord Krsna — an extraordinary form at that — its unlimited glories, qualities, and form are inconceivable to conditioned and liberated souls alike.

But by seeing, touching, and hearing about the wonders of Govardhana, sincere devotees are blessed with the fulfilment of their spiritual aspirations and gain residence at the foot of this sacred hill.

Like Krsna, Govardhana Hill reciprocates the mood and surrender of his devotees. Great devotees and sages, therefore, have described his form variously, according to their realisations.

Some devotees envision him in the shape of a resting peacock with his head tucked into one side. These sages see Kusuma-sarovara as his face, Manasa-ganga as his neck, and Mukharavinda as his mouth.

With this vision, the two tear-filled eyes of this divine peacockshaped hill are Radha-kunda and Syama-kunda, and his tail begins at Balarama-sthali and ends at Puncari-kunda.

Other sages present a different and more detailed description of the peacock, arguing that the one just mentioned is general and only accounts for the most famous of Govardhana Hill’s limbs.

They say that the area below Srngara-mandala, south of Aniyora, is Govardhana Hill’s mouth, Manasa-ganga his eyes, Candra-sarovara his nose, and beautiful Govinda-kunda his lips.

Syama-kunda is his chin, Radha-kunda his tongue, Lalita-kunda his cheeks, Gopala-kunda his ears, Kusuma-sarovara his inner ear, and Mauli-sila, the stone marked with Krsna’s crown, his forehead.

Citra-sila, the stone decorated with pictures, is his head, Vadani-sila, the musical stone, his throat, Kanduka-tirtha his two sides, Ausnisa- tirtha his waist, Drona-tirtha his back, and Laukika-tirtha his belly.

Kadamba-khanda is his chest, Srngara-mandala his life air, Krsna’s footprints his heart, Krsna’s handprint his intelligence, Airavata’s footprints his feet, and Surabhi’s hoofprints his wings.

Puncari-kunda is his tail, Vatsa-kunda his strength, Rudra-kunda his anger, Sakra-sarovara his desire, Kuvera-tirtha his determination, Brahma-tirtha his cheerfulness, and Yama-tirtha his ego.

Faithful hearing of either of these descriptions of Govardhana Hill’s inconceivable form removes all sins and makes the hearer a qualified Vaisnava, eligible to return to Goloka Vrndavana.

Readers may question how self-realised souls can see Govardhana’s form in such disparate ways. For example, how can one say his eyes are Radha-kunda and Syama-kunda and another Manasa-ganga?

As explained by Krsna Himself in Srimad-Bhagavatam, Govardhana Hill is not different from Krsna, who appears to His devotees in various forms according to their desires to serve and worship Him.

And if a person still has doubts about how or why Krsna would act as a servant of Himself, he need only look as far as Lord Caitanya, who is Lord Krsna acting as a perfect servant of Himself.

As my parikrama of Govardhana Hill continues, I will describe the holy places just mentioned. But before I do, let me elaborate on but the tiniest portion of Giriraja’s glories.

From the beautiful lotus of Lord Krsna’s abode flow bountiful streams of nectar. The mountains to the north and east, like bumblebees, drink that nectar and thus grow robust and ecstatic.

The king of these mountains is Govardhana. From a jewelled summit he rules over his lofty subjects — Varsana, Nandagrama, Trikuta, and others — ensuring that they relish the nectar of Krsna’s pastimes.

Govardhana Hill is aptly named, for he nourishes (vardhana) Krsna’s cows (go) with his grasses. He is for this reason, amongst many others, renowned as the best servant of Sri Hari (hari-dasa-varya).

The wise also tell that because he nourishes (vardhana) the gopas, gopis, and cows (go) with the sweetness of Krsna’s youthful pastimes, this great devotee-expansion of Krsna is called Govardhana.

One who doubts how a mountain can serve Krsna may consider Jiva Gosvami's elaborate answer. The acarya reveals, in detail, the rites Govardhana performs for his worshipable Deity, Krsna.

Govardhana offers Krsna splendid jewelled boulders as suitable sitting places. And the cooing of his innumerable resident birds offer a fitting welcome for a worshipable person.

Like a first class pujari, Govardhana offers the padyam of his swiftly flowing streams filled with lotus flowers, durva grass, and syamaka seeds, and the arghya of deer hoofprint puddles full of darbha grass.

With his many ponds, upon whose shores grow jati, lavanga, and kakkola creepers, Govardhana Hill offers Krsna acamana. And with fresh yogurt, ghee, and honey he offers madhu-parka.

With the waters of a clear spring at his summit, Govardhana offers clear bathing water; with golden tree bark, fresh garments; and with flower pollen, sandal paste, and coloured minerals, fragrant ointment.

From vines like malati, Govardhana offers pleasing flowers; with the dust raised by the cows’ hooves, incense; and with the glistening of his many jewels, a glittering lamp even in daytime.

Govardhana decorates Krsna with gunja berries, peacock feathers, and clusters of flowers, offers Krsna delicious meals of fruits and roots and a mouth-rinse of flower-scented water and tulasi leaves.

Govardhana’s blooming flowers, like the campaka, move in a gentle breeze to offer Krsna arati. His trees, like the nakula, serve as graceful parasols, whose branches move in that fragrant breeze to fan Krsna.

Govardhana’s cavorting peacocks offer a festival of dancing, the cooing of his cuckoos offers sweet singing, and the abundant beds of colourful flowers offer Krsna soft couches for reclining.

As Govardhana Hill faithfully performs all these services to please Krsna, honest observers can hardly doubt his well-deserved reputation as the best of the Lord’s servants (hari-dasa-varya).

And if someone says that Govardhana Hill is a mere mountain and that descriptions of his personified form and services are simply fanciful or imaginary, Vaisnavas object with an emphatic no.

Govardhana Hill is a fully conscious flood of love for Krsna. He may appear inanimate to conditioned souls, but to those whose eyes are anointed with love (prema), he reveals his true nature.

Although Govardhana is made of rocks and has taken birth as a hill, Sri Radha — the greatest spiritual authority — confirms that his multifarious opulences signify the greatest ecstatic love for Krsna.

Radha says, “Glorious Govardhana Hill is the best amongst My Lord’s servants, for he offers his entire body for the pleasure of Krsna and Balarama, Their playful friends and joyful cows.

“When these two brothers climb Govardhana Hill, the touch of Their lotus feet causes many symptoms of ecstasy to appear in Giriraja, beginning with his bodily hairs — the blades of grass — standing erect.’’

And for those who still do not believe that the grass on the slopes of Giriraja are his bodily hairs standing in ecstasy, let them see for themselves how the sound of Krsna’s flute makes the grass stand up.

The rest of the gopis confirm that the goal of the Vedas, as taught by great souls — to serve Krsna with love — is made tangible by the example of Govardhana Hill’s ecstatic love.

The gopis say, “If one boldly yearns to follow the path of the great souls, the mahajanas, one can look and clearly see that path splendidly manifest on the slopes of Govardhana Hill.”

Vaisnavas envision Govardhana Hill to be like a flag, a standard bearing the insignia of intense sweetness, firmly placed in the land of Vrndavana to proclaim by his glories the wonders of vraja-bhakti.

**\* \* \* \***

The boulders on Govardhana Hill are splendid jewels, soft to the touch, which take the form of thrones, couches, and other furnishings in response to Krsna’s desire for such comforts.

Krsna walks barefoot on the stony slopes of Giriraja, a practice that causes the cowherd girls such distress that they often faint at the thought of stones pricking Krsna’s tender soles.

The stones of Govardhana Hill, however, are not ordinary. Wherever Krsna, Balarama, the gopas, or even the cows step, the stones turn soft, giving them the sense of walking on feather pillows.

Not only are the stones on Govardhana Hill soft — proving the gopis’ concerns unfounded — but the cushioned surface of the mountain makes it the favourite playground of Krsna and His friends.

The beauty of the forests on and around Govardhana Hill cannot be fully described because they cannot be likened to anything but themselves. Even the forests in Vaikuntha pale by comparison.

Some forests contain only a few trees, and others have more trees than one could count. Some forests have a single type of tree, such as the kadamba, others have unlimited varieties of plant life.

Some forests are orderly and easy to navigate, with trees in straight lines on well-kept grass. Others are jungles overrun with bushes, creepers, and undergrowth, making them nearly impenetrable.

Some forests are known for being the residence for one type of bird, like the cuckoo, others for a specific flower, like the golden campaka, and still others for their medicinal herbs or leaves, like nima.

All these forests serve various purposes in Krsna’s pastimes. Some are places for grazing the cows, others playgrounds for the gopas, and still others settings for trysts with Radharani and the gopis.

Each forest is decorated with aromatic flowers, scented breezes, secret groves, jewelled seats, flower sofas, singing birds, clear-water lakes and streams, and its own special mellow of love for Krsna.

Each forest has a presiding deity who serves Krsna in a unique way, and in many forests there are sylvan goddesses who make additional arrangements for the comfort of Krsna and His friends.

Fruits, flowers, and berries, along with honey, nectar, and pollen, are but a few of the gifts offered to Krsna, His friends, and His lovers by the trees, vines, and bushes of Govardhana’s forests.

A small sandalwood forest grows on Govardhana Hill. Gentle breezes passing through its trees carry a refreshing aroma to Krsna, defeating even the scented breezes from the far-off Malaya Hills.

Snakes generally flock to the refreshing coolness of sandalwood trees, but few ever come to this forest. And a wonder is that even if a poisonous snake happens to enter, its poison is at once neutralised.

Moreover, the snakes that enter this forest are conscripted into service by Govardhana Hill, who makes them into valuable players in Sri Krsna’s pastimes, as described briefly here:

One day, just near this forest, a contrary, fawn-eyed gopi left Krsna in the lurch and set out on the road back to Her husband’s home. On the way She encountered a large black snake obstructing Her path.

She shrieked with fright, ran back to Krsna, threw Herself into His arms, and begged Him to protect Her. Such are the arrangements made by Giriraja’s forests for Krsna’s pastimes.

Of course the most dangerous snake on Govardhana Hill has two legs and is best controlled by mystical mantras known only to the milkmaids of Vrndavana, as shown by the following incident:

Once a group of gopis walking near Govardhana Hill spotted Krsna coming their way. One of the girls said to her friend, “Here comes the serpent-king, Krsna, slithering down Govardhana Hill.

“My dear beautiful friend, since you do not know the snake-charming mantra needed to tame poison-bearing Krsna, you and your timid companions should at once flee from this place, and do so quickly.

“However, since I am an expert snake-charmer who regularly travels this forest of Vrndavana, I will now weave a magical spell and bring this Krsna-snake under my control.”

Vrnda-devi supervises every forest, with its opulences, animals, and deities. Taken together they all comprise a part of the forest of Vrndavana, the glories of which are daily sung to Lord Narayana.

Also amongst the distinctive features of Govardhana Hill are his beautiful caves. Unlike the ordinary caves one would expect to find on an ordinary mountain, these caves are special in many ways.

Of course, like ordinary caves, these are ideal playgrounds, secluded hideaways aloof from the summer heat. But unlike ordinary caves, these are decorated with marble, mosaics, paintings, and jewels.

Jewel lamps illuminate these caves, made comfortable with couches and stocks of delicious snacks and drinks. All these things are invisible to those unqualified to participate in Krsna’s pastimes.

When it rains, these caves keep Krsna and His friends dry, and in stronger downpours they can even expand into subterranean palaces big enough to accommodate Nanda Maharaja’s entire herd of cows.

Sometimes when it rains Krsna enters alone into a cave and eats some roots and fruits, and at other times He rests in the cool comfort of a cave during the midday heat while His friends massage His feet.

It was in one of these caves of Govardhana that Sridama mourned Krsna’s departure from this world. Eventually Sridama cried himself to sleep, not to awaken for many thousands of years.

In the meantime Krsna appeared again, in Kali-yuga, as Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu. He missed Sridama terribly and feared that His mission would not succeed in the absence of His dear friend.

Later, Lord Nityananda visited Govardhana Hill and was able to waken Sridama by shouting his name. Nitai then convinced him to return to Navadvipa and join the Lord’s sankirtana movement.

But Krsna’s most treasured pastimes in the caves of Govardhana Hill are with the milkmaids of Vrndavana, especially those pastimes He enjoys with Sri Radha. They are the essence of His amorous sports.

While walking with the gopis, Krsna sometimes takes Radharani into a cave where They enjoy confidential pastimes. Pastimes such as these constitute the inner meditation of Giriraja’s heart.

One cave favoured by Radha and Krsna, known as Manikandali, is located near the southern tip of Govardhana Hill. It later became the bhajana-kutira of the great devotee Raghava Pandita.

In another cave, the divine couple were once resting on a bed of flowers when Radha suddenly awoke and tried to make off with Krsna’s flute. But in the process She woke up the flute’s owner.

Krsna restrained Radharani. She tried to escape from His grasp — suppressing Her laughter, Her eyes tearing up — but Krsna wrapped Her in His arms and made Her lie down next to Him.

On another occasion, in an expansive cave on Govardhana Hill where the ground is made of jewels, Krsna brought His girlfriends and requested that they perform a rasa dance there.

At the conclusion of the dance, Krsna made Radha sit on a flower throne as He crowned Her queen of the rasa dance. The gopis and demigoddesses brought gifts in recognition of Radha’s sovereignty.

Srimati Radharani — whom Lalita-devi fans as Sri Radha rests with Krsna in a cave of Govardhana — is so much identified with these caves that She is given names in connection with them.

One such name is Govardhana-guha-laksmi (“the hidden treasure of Govardhana’s caves”), another is Govardhana-guha-geha-grhini (“the housewife whose home is a cave in Govardhana Hill”).

And when Krsna moved to His capital in Dvaraka and married, He often remembered His pastimes with Radha in the caves of Govardhana, as recorded by Uddhava in a letter to Lalita-devi:

“Dvaraka’s king does not at all desire the bevy of princesses who wait for Him in jewelled palaces, on beds softer than sea foam and decorated with camaras and jewelled peacocks.

“Instead, continually remembering Radha’s artistic expertise at amorous pastimes on a stone bed in a cave at the base of Govardhana Hill, He repeatedly faints, again and again.” Devotees pray thus:

“O Govardhana! When, while wandering at your feet amongst the forests there, will I enter one of your glorious caves to find kunkuma strewn on the ground during Radha’s and Krsna’s loving pastimes?”

“O Govardhana Hill! Please grant to me the residence near you that will guarantee the sight of the youthful divine couple as They enjoy amorous pastimes in your caves.”

“O gracious Govardhana Hill! ? king of mountains! Will you one day fulfil my desire for Sri Radha to take me into a cave on your sacred form and there tutor me in the art of melodious singing?”

**\* \* \* \***

The beautiful lakes and ponds surrounding Govardhana Hill resemble a necklace of turquoise beads placed by heavenly King Indra on his former adversary’s chest as an offering

Some lakes, like Govinda-kunda, are filled with Krsna’s bath water; others, like Syama-kunda, with water from holy places; still others, like Navala-kunda, with the liquid form of the divine couple’s love.

Some Vaisnavas say that there are fifty-four lakes full of nectar surrounding Govardhana Hill, yet others say that the actual number of lakes cannot be counted because they are unlimited.

Scriptures state that during the month of Karttika all the holy lakes of the universe take shelter of Vrndavana. Acaryas have singled out fifty-six of the lakes in Vraja as most prominent.

Radha-kunda, Syama-kunda, Kusuma-sarovara, Brahma-kunda, Dana- nivartarna-kunda, Sandarsana-kunda, Gauri-kunda, and Govinda-kunda are some of these lakes, here mentioned for my purification.

Before I narrate the pastimes linked with the most prominent of these lakes, first let me describe some of their unlimited glories, of which the waves of milk lapping the shores of Svetadvipa sing.

The Sanskrit word for lake is saras or sara, and when used to refer to the lakes around Govardhana, a secret meaning is revealed: when the syllables are reversed it becomes rasa, meaning “nectar” or “mellow.”

Devotees thus know that to bathe in any of these lakes is to bathe in the nectar of devotional mellows, a bath that purifies the heart and bestows the qualifications necessary to acquire one’s coveted rasa.

Such transcendental benefit is achieved, however, only if one’s devotional practices are without offence. If they are not, the effect of bathing in Giriraja’s lakes will be to slowly remove one’s bad habits.

The crystal clear waters of Govardhana’s lakes should be honoured as one would honour the water that has washed the Lord’s lotus feet (caranamrta): they should be sipped with reverence.

Within these waters live untold kinds of fish and aquatic creatures like turtles, none of which are ordinary living entities. Indeed, some are even fallen devotees or great souls secretly engaged in bhajana.

In some lakes, jewelfish feed on the diamonds and rubies lying on the lake bottoms, and in others the fish recite beautiful poetry describing Krsna’s eternal love for Srimati Radharani.

The surfaces of these lakes abound with regal swans and playful ducks, and their shores swarm with birds that bathe in and drink from their waters, knowing well the salutary effects of such practices.

Yogamaya displayed her highest artistic skill when she arranged the bouquets of blue, gold, and red lotuses that constantly dust the lakes with their pollen and sweeten the water with their honey.

To further beautify Govinda’s creation, Yogamaya added abundant varieties of aromatic flowers, blossoming vines, and flowering trees, the sight and smell of which makes one’s head swim with prema.

The floral decor of Govardhana’s lakes is appreciated by swarms of intoxicated bees who hover throughout the area. These tiny humming devotees are connoisseurs of both floral and divine rasas.

No ordinary insects, these bees resemble Krsna in every way: their bodies are blackish, they wear a yellow covering, they always seek nectar, and they are never faithful to any one flower.

The shores of Govardhana’s lakes are decorated with beautiful staircases that sometimes surround an entire lake, sometimes stretch along one shore, or are sometimes placed here and there.

These staircases are made of gold or marble inlaid with valuable gems, and they extend deep into the water so that a bather may either stand at the waterline or submerge in water up to the neck.

In between and beyond these staircases stand many beautiful trees and creepers, which blossom and bear fruit year-round because of always drinking through their roots the sacred waters of these lakes.

Some trees stand stately and tall, others bend over to touch the water. Some stand in groves, others alone. Some have platforms around their bases, and yet others have hollows wherein Krsna sometimes hides.

Maharaja Yudhisthira brought his brothers to live as trees by the banks of Syama-kunda. Who, then, are the great souls absorbed in Krsna consciousness on the bank of Apsara-kunda or Surabhi-kunda?
