As the rays of morning sunlight grace the lakes on Govardhana Hill, these pools of luminescence dot its valleys like glistening ornaments along the splendid length of Radharani’s braid.

The rustling leaves, buzzing bees, chirping birds, and lapping waves resounding from Govardhana’s lakes produce a grand sankirtana sweetened by airborne pollen and cooled by spray-bearing breezes.

This is the sankirtana of Radha’s and Krsna’s glorious names, qualities, and pastimes. Its vibration enchants the hearts of those who sing it, those who hear it, and even those who hear about it.

The king and queen of Govardhana’s lakes are Syama-kunda and Radha-kunda. Devotees describe these lakes as the liquid forms of the divine couple, and alternately as the liquid form of Their mutual love.

Everyone agrees that Krsna’s qualities are fully present in Syama- kunda and Radha’s qualities in Radha-kunda. Therefore, they are like oceans of sweetness in which the jewels of divine love abound.

The lakes of Govardhana Hill — not unlike their mountain master— serve Krsna unreservedly with body and soul in ways that I can only begin to list, and even then, insufficiently.

In the summer these waters are sources of refreshment and rowdy sport for Krsna and His friends. In the more secluded forests, they are places for splashing games and confidential pastimes with the gopis.

During the day, the lakes provide drinking water for the cowherd boys and their cows and irrigation water for the gardens, pastures, and agricultural fields of Vrndavana.

At night these are places where Krsna enjoys lakeside strolls, enacts the rasa dance, and bathes with the gopis — places where saintly Vaisnavas go to weep, having had divine visions of these pastimes.

What has been written about Govardhana’s lakes applies equally to his rivers, creeks, and waterfalls — Giriraja’s flowing ecstasies, liquid transformations, and unrelenting tears of bliss respectively.

Eager to be with Krsna wherever He roams and curious to witness first-hand Giriraja’s peerless service, a tributary of Yamuna-devi meanders past the foot of Govardhana Hill.

On the banks of this blackish sister of Ganga-devi is a blue-canopied dock known as Nllamandapika, where Krsna demands a toll from Radha and the gopis before He will allow them to cross the river.

And at another landing place, called Paranga, on the banks of Manasa-ganga, Krsna tethers His boat, Suvilasatara, in which He takes Radha and the gopis on nautical adventures.

The waves of Manasa-ganga gently rock the boulders on its shores. It was brought to Vraja by Krsna Himself and is therefore a thousand times more glorious than the Ganga brought to earth by Bhagiratha.

Govardhana Hill and its waterways — Yamuna and Manasa-ganga — embrace one another in the ecstasy of love for Krsna. As a result, the essential qualities of one are sometimes transferred to the other.

And in response to Krsna’s flute-song, Govardhana’s stones sometimes flow like the water of a river, and the Yamuna’s or Manasa-ganga’s waters sometimes become solid like the stones of a mountain.

All glories to the beautiful lakes and rivers of Govardhana Hill whose banks are home to purified sages trembling in ecstasy at the mere thought of the pastimes Radha and Krsna enact there.

May the waves of Radha’s and Madhava’s pastimes — quarrelling over a toll by Govardhana Hill, boating in Manasa-gariga, collecting tax to cross the Yamuna — flood my parched, desert-like heart.

**\* \* \* \***

The secluded valleys of Govardhana Hill reverberate with the sounds of cuckoos singing on the fifth note, and his proud peaks are platforms for dancing peacocks and peahens.

The branches of Giriraja’s trees are residence to many types of colourful singing birds and his slopes serve as lookouts for curious deer to climb and, from a distance, watch Krsna tending His cows.

Sometimes the peacocks mistake Krsna’s peacock-feathered turban for a blue rain cloud and begin to dance with such jubilant abandon that any observer would be agitated with divine love for Krsna.

O friend! If you see stunned docs on Govardhana Hill, the grass falling half-eaten from their mouths, know for certain that Krsna has stolen their hearts with His flute-song or sidelong glance.

And if from a distance you see assorted animals — boar, deer, bears, rabbits — gathering on a peak of Govardhana Hill, you can be sure that they are watching Krsna gracefully dance nearby.

I cannot adequately glorify the beautiful insects, birds, and animals that live on Giriraja. They sustain themselves not by eating, but by drinking the blissful nectar exuded by their transcendental home.

And the groves of Govardhana Hill! Only Anantadeva with His thousand mouths could attempt to do justice to these kunjas, which draw all manner of living creatures into Krsna’s confidential service.

These groves resound with the cheerful chatter of parrots and the perpetual chirping of birds, both of which mix with the taste and aroma of honey oozing from colourfully blooming flowers.

When that mixture ferments by the touch of the sunbeams streaming through the forest canopy, a special kind of nectar is created, a stimulant for Radha’s and Krsna’s amorous pastimes.

“May the cowherd boy, who is a festival of ecstatic bliss for the hearts of the fortunate creatures residing in the groves of Govardhana Hill, eternally enjoy transcendental pastimes in our hearts!”

Govardhana is more than a playground for the gopas’ prince. Giriraja is Radha’s informant on Krsna’s whereabouts and Her impetus for remembering Her beloved, as shown by the following two pastimes.

Once, while suffering from Krsna’s absence, Srimati Radharani appealed to Govardhana Hill: “O Giriraja! ? king of mountains! You shine very splendidly in this holy land of Gokula!

“With your many lofty peaks thrusting into the sky, please look around in all directions and tell Me where Krsna, the jewel of the cowherd boys, is now enjoying His pastimes.”

On another occasion Radha mistook Govardhana Hill — decorated as he was with blue clouds and a flash of lightning—for Krsna with His peacock feather and yellow garments. Lalita then said to Radha:

“Where are the yellow garments You mentioned? Where is the peacock feather? Where is Krsna, the enemy of Kamsa? ? desperate friend, blinded by passion, please do not fall into illusion like this.

“Look closer! Wipe away the tears of love that make You see Syama in every nook and cranny of Vrndavana. What You see is actually a lightning flash, clouds, and a rainbow above Govardhana Hill.”

Gopas and gopis alike frequent the hill, but unless Krsna brings the two groups together — as in the dana-keli pastimes — they are careful not to cross paths. In this endeavour, Govardhana assists them.

If, while walking on Govardhana Hill, Krsna’s elder brother Balarama sees a trail of golden stones, He thinks, “The daughter of King Vrsabhanu must have just walked this way.”

Balarama then changes course to avoid an awkward situation. And Srimati Radharani does the same when She sees a trail of crystal stones and thinks, “Dauji must have just come this way.”

But if Sri Krsna sees a single golden stone while walking on Govardhana Hill, He embraces it as if it were His beloved. And if He sees a trail of golden stones He follows it, hoping to meet Sri Radha.

And whenever Sri Radha sees a sapphire-coloured stone, She thinks She has found Krsna. She embraces it in the same mood with which Lord Caitnnya once embraced a stone of Govardhana Hill.

O devotees! Let your minds be enchanted by the charm of Govardhana Hill, whose groves and caves enchant even the necklaces, ornaments, and red foot lac of the gopis.

Just worship this ornamental playground decorated by a sapphire gem, Krsna, and many golden necklaces, the gopis. Their pastimes of love are the adornments and bangles that embellish Govardhana Hill.

Remember always Govardhana Hill, where peacocks dance jubilantly, bees and cuckoos sing melodiously together with other birds’ harmony and rhythm, to honour that best servant of Sri Hari.

Govardhana Hill — and the land around in all directions — is filled with many lakes, groves, and other charming places of Lord Krsna’s pastimes. To hear of them is sublime refreshment for our ears.

The preceding portrait of Govardhana Hill is meant to inspire us to better appreciate that great servant of the Lord as he appears in Bhauma Vrndavana and is represented in New Vraja-dhama.

Before turning my attention to the sacred places around Govardhana, I prostrated myself at Giriraja’s feet, and recited a prayer composed by Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami, a resident of Radha-kunda.

That prayer ends with a most appropriate verse: “Although I am a cheater and a criminal, unlimitedly merciful Lord Sacinandana, who is very dear to you, has given me to you.

“O Govardhana! Please do not consider whether I am acceptable or not, but simply grant me residence near you.” I would repeatedly sing this verse throughout my tour of gracious Govardhana Hill.

Again I prayed to Hanuman to protect me from committing offences while circumambulating the best of mountains, and then I set out for my first destination, chanting the following mantra:

“Obeisances to you, who are the crown of Goloka and who sit on Vrndavana’s lap! Obeisances to Govardhana, the parasol of the Supreme Personality of Godhead!”

**\* \* \* \***

Outside the parikrama path is Naradavana, a tranquil forest that surrounds beautiful Narada-kunda. Here Narada Muni composed his treatise on bhakti, and also fulfilled his spiritual aspiration.

Narada resides here eternally. Devotees who desire his blessings for pure devotional service sit here, offer prayers, chant Krsna’s names, and recite the sutras written by this great sage.

Once, Narada was speaking with his brother, Lord Siva. Narada expressed an interest to do something neither of them had done: see Radha’s and Krsna’s sublime amorous pastimes.

Narada said, “I have seen all of the Lord’s incarnations throughout countless millennia. I have even seen Krsna playing as a child in the dust of Gokula and herding the Surabhi cows with His friends.

“But,” continued the sage, “I have not been privileged to see Krsna’s pastimes with the gopis, much less His loving exchanges with Sri Radha. Dear lord! Please arrange that I may behold this vision.”

Mahadeva knowingly smiled and informed Narada Muni that he could not award the sage entrance into Krsna’s intimate pastimes. How could he? He did not enjoy such a privilege himself.

Nonetheless, Lord Siva gave Narada a mantra and sound advice. He told him to enter Vrndavana forest and pray to Vrnda-devi for a vision of Radha’s and Krsna’s eightfold reoccurring daily pastimes.

Narada followed the instructions of Lord Siva and came to Vraja, where he first performed a full parikrama and then wandered about Vrndavana forest chanting his mantra and calling out to Vrnda-devi.

After a long search Narada finally came to Brahma-kunda. There he found Vrnda-devi and her assistants discussing the arrangements for Radha’s and Krsna’s upcoming nocturnal pastimes by the Yamuna.

Vrnda-devi at once rose to greet her guest and, after offering him a seat, enquired how she could serve him. But when Narada disclosed his intentions, she humbly declined. Vrnda-devi explained:

“To enter or to even witness the pastimes of the divine couple one must first develop spontaneous devotion to Them. And then, by intense longing, he should seek out the patronage of a gopi of Vraja.

“Without following in the wake of the gopis,” continued Vrnda, “not even the foremost of the gods — Brahma or Siva, perfect in regulated devotion — can enter any one of the countless groves of Vraja.”

When Vrnda saw Narada’s despondency, she became inclined to support his cause and advised him to take shelter of Vrndavana by cultivating the rarely-attained mood of the gopis.

Narada Muni followed Vrnda-devi’s advice and came here. He gave up all comforts, living beneath a tree and performing austerities with the same single-mindedness that he had done as a boy.

As a result of his practices, Narada Muni acquired a sense of spontaneous attraction as well as the good fortune to meet Vrnda-devi again, this time as she was walking by his place of bhajana.

Narada fell at the goddess’s feet and implored her to introduce him to Radha and Krsna. Vrnda praised his newfound disposition as an asset, but nonetheless found his male body a crippling liability.

Finally, Vrnda-devi took pity on Narada. She led him to the northwestern corner of Kusuma-sarovara and told him to dip into the water, which transformed his body into that of a graceful gopi.

Narada, now renamed Naradl-gopi, followed Vrnda-devi to Radha- kunda, where the divine couple and Their friends were enjoying midday pastimes: swinging, bathing, and playing dice.

Thus, by the grace of Vrnda-devi, Narada Muni was able to see the astakaliya-lila of Radha and Krsna. But Krsna recognised the sage and asked him to remain at Narada-kunda and write the bhakti-sutras.

Narada explained that Daksa had cursed him never to be able to stay in one place, but Krsna replied that Vraja was not part of the creation, and therefore Daksa’s curse held no sway there.

Once again Vrnda-devi guided Naradi-gopi to Kusuma-sarovara, where the transformed sage bathed in the southeastern corner of the lake and regained his original masculine form.

After this episode he penned the Narada-bhakti-sutra, which hints at the topmost attainment of vraja-bhakti in the verse: “Now, therefore, I will try to explain the process of devotional service.”

An area surrounding Naradavana in New Vraja-dhama — bound by Yugala-kunda, Khelanavana, and Deravali — marks the place the Vraja-vasis encamped in their exodus from Chatikara.

Within the waters of Kilala-kunda in Khelanavana, Krsna sometimes enjoys splashing and swimming with the gopis, and at other times He and the gopas forget themselves while playing ball on its banks.

From Naradavana I proceeded north towards Kusuma-sarovara. There I turned left and walked until I met the rim of the whorl of Krsna Valley, which leads back to the parikrama path near Yugala-kunda.

By taking this route devotees visit many holy sites in an area rich with memories of Krsna’s pastimes. Fittingly, this area is the flower and vegetable garden of Radha-Syamasundara.

The first stop on the parikrama path was the famous Panca-tirtha-kunda, said to contain the purifying waters of five sacred rivers: Gomati, Narmada, Sarayu, Vetra, and Kanci.

It is also said that five other holy places reside on the bathing ghatas of that lake: Ganga, Puskara, Prayaga, Kuruksetra, and Gaya. Bathing or taking the dust from here is equal to visiting all ten places.

I frequently visit this place in Bhauma Vrndavana because its expansive kunda and two surrounding palatial temples most resemble the temple complex I would one day like to build for Radha-Syama.

There is a three-storey house just south of Panca-tirtha-kunda. While sitting on the rooftop there that overlooks the complex, I meditate on how wonderful such edifices would look in New Vraja-dhama.

Not far distant from Panca-tirtha-kunda is the beautiful forest of ever-blooming flowers, aptly named Puspavana, “the forest of flowers.” At its heart lies Kusuma-sarovara, “the lake of flowers.”

Amongst the many pastimes connected with Kusuma-sarovara, the perfection achieved by the great sage Astavakra is retold at length in the appendix entitled “The Liberation of Astavakra Muni.”

The path that separates the two sides of Krsna Valley’s garden atop Govardhana Hill corresponds to the road in Bhauma Vrndavana that connects Govinda-kunda, Govardhana town, and Surya-kunda.

In Krsna’s time people used that road, but rarely did anyone enter into the very confidential pastime places — Puspavana and Radha- kunda— which lie to the left and right sides of the road respectively.

By inspiring a reluctance within their minds, Yogamaya prevents people who would disturb — or who are otherwise unqualified to see — the divine couple’s pastimes from entering these forests.

For this reason, only the gopis and Krsna go to Puspavana. And if accidentally someone does go, they do so when Krsna and His beloveds are enjoying themselves in some other part of Vraja.

Beautiful Puspavana sits beside Giriraja and is made up of many adjoining sub-forests of specific kinds of trees. For example, very famous Asokavana is comprised of ever-blooming asoka trees.

Kadamba, bakula, and campaka trees grow in some of Puspavana’s other sub-forests. Creepers that tend to favour specific trees — as the malati creeper favours the punnaga tree — also find shelter there.

Comfortable sitting places and secluded groves complement by their colour the flowers unique to each sub-forest. Gold couches, for example, match the honeysuckle blossoms in Kadambavana.

One thing is common, however, to all the forests of Puspavana: diamond-dust paths wind their way throughout the natural wonders of its groves, illuminating them both day and night.

Vrnda-devi always keeps her sylvan goddesses busy in caring for these forests, which blossom all year round, thus making Puspavana superior even to the gardens of the demigods.

At the centre of Puspavana — like a vaidurya gem set in a brilliantly shimmering circle of pearls, rubies, emeralds, diamonds, and coral — is beautiful Kusuma-sarovara, also known as Sumanah-sarovara.

Sounds of the happy birds and intoxicated bees living near its banks echo across the deep and tranquil waters of this lake, complementing the gentle, rhythmic lapping of its waves.

After rediscovering Radha-kunda and Syama-kunda, Caitanya Mahaprabhu came here and saw Govardhana Hill tor the first time. He then paid obeisances to Giriraja and bathed in the lake.

The origin of Kusuma-sarovara is revealed during a pastime in Asokavana in which Krsna combed, braided, and decorated Radha’s hair. Local residents narrate the story in a charming way.

**\* \* \* \***

One day in the month of Bhadra, when the trees are espedally rich with blossoms, Radha and Her friends came to Puspavana to collect flowers for the festival of Sanjhi — and with the hope to see Krsna.

As it happened, Krsna was wandering alone in the forest enjoying the natural beauty of Vrndavana, as He often does, when He came upon the cowherd girls busily collecting flowers.

With the desire to tease Radha, as He also often does, Krsna pretended to be the gardener of a fictitious king named Kamadeva who was greatly offended that the gopis were plundering his gardens.

Krsna confronted them: “Who is picking the king’s flowers?” But the haughty gopis feigned complete indifference to His question, pretending not even to notice their challenger.

Receiving no reply, Krsna rolled His eyes in mock anger and exclaimed, “Arrogant thieves! Immediately identify yourselves or face My wrath, which even death personified fears.”

Sri Radha raised Her eyebrows and replied matter-of-factly, “We are no one!” Hearing this, Krsna’s mind reeled, having understood the inner meaning of Her words: “We belong to no one but You.”

Krsna suppressed the ecstatic shivers in His body and replied, “You are indeed someone! You are the ones responsible for defoliating this forest and the ones who have offended the king who rules over it.”

“Defoliate the forest!” Radha snapped. “You ruin all of Vraja herding Your thousands of cows through its forests, running amok with Your destructive friends, and then You dare accuse us?”

Krsna spoke up in His own defence, “Because we graze our cows under the king’s sanction, I am faultless. You, however, take flowers without permission, therefore Your thievery should be punished.”

Radha replied to this accusation, amidst the gopis peals of amused laughter, “How could You — O faultless, pious soul — judge who is with or without fault, and therefore who should be punished?”

Krsna was about to answer, but Radha cut Him off, “While still an infant You killed a woman, throughout childhood you stole anything You could get Your hands on, and now in youth You killed a bull.

“Add to that Your uncontrollable penchant for seducing the wives of other men, and still You stand there, a cowherd-cum-sheriff, and propose to punish us for plucking flowers in observance of a festival.”

Caught off guard by Radha’s retort, Krsna looked around uneasily for relief from His embarrassment. Instead He found the gopis adding fuel to the fire of His humiliation by crowding around Him to poke fun.

The cowherd girls taunted Krsna: “O faultless protector of bulls! ? incorruptible guardian of chaste women! When did picking flowers become a greater sin than killing a calf?”

Before Krsna could melt into a bottomless pool of His own chagrin, Sri Radha calmed Her friends and, as the real sovereign of Puspavana and every other forest in Vraja, made the following decree:

“For falsely stating that this forest belongs to some imaginary king, for impersonating a constable of that king, and for the attempted imprisonment of innocent gopis, this boy must be punished.”

The cowherd girls all applauded and called out, “Tie Him up! Throw Him in jail!” But Srimati Radharani — kindly queen that She was — sentenced Him to the labour of dressing Her tousled hair.

This punishment was greatly satisfying to the gopis and Krsna alike, and so the entire entourage entered a pavilion in Asokavana nearby, featuring flower archways, swaying vines, and jewel lamps.

Radhika sat before a golden table equipped with combs, brushes, and a bejewelled mirror, while Krsna scurried about the sylvan garden collecting the best flowers He could find to decorate Radha’s hair.

Soon, Krsna returned to the kunja carrying a basket that overflowed with flowers. Curious spectators — parrots, cuckoos, and peacocks — followed in His wake and filled the air with their voices.

Radhika patiently waited beneath an awning of newly blossomed flowers. Refreshments and snacks were there for Her enjoyment as She watched Krsna finish preparing for His appointed task.

Eagerly Krsna asked, “Lalita! Is there water I can use to purify these flowers?” While there were liquids like nectar, grape juice, and sherbet in abundance, her answer was, “No. There is no water.”

Of course the Yamuna and many holy lakes were close by, but Krsna did not want to risk losing this opportunity. “What if She changes Her mind, or becomes impatient and returns home?” He reasoned.

Clever Krsna quickly came up with a solution. As His dazzling smile flooded the garden, He pulled His flute from His belt and declared, “Don’t worry! I will summon water from every holy place.”

A few paces away from the grove, Hari struck the ground with His flute. A sacred lake at once appeared, filled with glistening clear water shimmering in ecstasy at the prospect of serving Sri Radha.

On each of its four well-shaded shores, attractive jewel-studded staircases greeted Radha and Krsna, and from the surface of its deep waters, multi-coloured lotuses spilled their pollen onto the lake.

That pollen, mixed with the already aromatic water, perfumed the air with a mind-enchanting fragrance, while trickles of honey oozed from the trees around the kunda and sweetened its water beyond limit.

The lake was rich with giddy jewelfish flitting about just below its surface, and it was surrounded by opulent gardens blessed by kadamba trees which were home to singing birds and hanging vines.

Even if one were able to search Vaikuntha, the heavenly planets, and the nether regions from end to end, he would never find a lake that could match the beauty, opulence, and sweetness of that kunda.

Because the centre of Puspavana was called Kusumavana, this lake is also known as Kusuma-sarovara, and because it was the site of Krsna’s intimate service to Radha, it is the heartbeat of Giri Govardhana.

Without wasting time, Hari descended one of the lakeside ghatas, chanted appropriate mantras, and collected a palmful of water that He then lightly sprinkled over His basket of flowers to purify it.

In the meantime, Rati-manjari removed Radha’s veil, gently unwound Her braids, unravelled Her hair, brushed it slightly, and bound it with a silk ribbon before turning over her service to Hari.

Krsna stood beside Radha’s chair like a beggar anxiously expecting alms. Noting His eagerness, Rati said, “O lotus faced Radha! Someone is waiting impatiently at Your door. Should I let Him in?”

From the reddish corners of Her lotus eyes, Radha glanced casually at Krsna as if to say yes. Without hesitating He stepped behind Radha and gazed at Her hair like a brahmama contemplating his deity.

It was as if the buds of Radha’s thoughts, through constant absorption in Krsna, had turned black like Him and, watered by the nectar of love for Him, had sprouted from Her head in the form of hair.

Krsna placed His left hand on Radha’s head and brushed Her hair with His right, expertly opening and closing His left hand while dragging the brush through the streams of Her Yamuna-like tresses.

With an intricately carved ivory comb, Krsna parted Srimati Radharani’s soft, satiny hair in the centre, forming a perfectly straight path all the way from Her forehead to the top of Her head.

Rupa-manjari stood beside Govinda with a golden tray that held a tiara, a jewelled lotus, and jasmine garlands. When their eyes met, her thoughts were clear: “Hari! Your expertise knows no limit.”

Krsna then stood in front of Radhika and placed the tiara upon Her head. From the tiara hung strands of pearls that Krsna plaited into Radha’s hair along with garlands of tiny fragrant flowers.

When Krsna hung the diamond-studded lotus from the end of Radha’s braid, drops of perspiration gathered on His forehead, and when He wove flowers into the braid, His hands shook in ecstasy.

Hari stepped back to examine the braid hanging down Radhika’s back, and then He silently took the basket of flowers from Rupa, kneeling before Radha to decorate Her face and the rest of Her hair.

Great devotees treasure this vision: the personification of transcendence kneeling before the personification of love to decorate Her hair with flowers — flowers symbolic of His love for His devotees.

Scriptures say that there is no perfection greater than service to Hari, but those who are truly wise know that service to Radha is an even higher perfection because it is the highest service to Hari.

His work complete, Krsna stood in front of Radha and proudly declared, “Now it is finished.” Radhika cast a curious glance at Her friends and said, “All right, let us see the fruit of Your labour.”

As Radha gazed into the mirror, Krsna walked around behind Her to peer over Her shoulder, the gopis gathering around as well. Radha’s first impulse, however, was to look at Krsna’s reflection, not Her own.

Like a thirsty cakora bird, She drank the rays from His moonlike face, now even more beautiful due to signs of His labour. Finally, Their eyes met and He beseeched Her, “Dearest! What do You think?”

Only for Krsna’s pleasure did Radha examine Her own reflection. It was incomparably beautiful — the eternal residence of loving sacrifice in which Her forehead was a golden sacrificial altar, the yajna-sala.

Her curly locks were like the magic syllables of that sacrifice, Her multi-coloured tilaka a smara-yantra, Her black eyeliner the sacred ash, and the decorative flowers uncontaminated offerings.

The movements of Her eyelashes were like Vedic mantras, Krsna was the head priest, Her rosy lips a camphor-scented fire, and the tears brought to Krsna’s eyes by that fire’s smoke, love’s final offering.

Radha spoke to Krsna in the mirror: “Syama! No one has ever decorated Me as nicely as You have. You are truly the presiding deity of adornment. Hari! You have outdone even My dearest friends.”

Radha’s words made Krsna beam with happiness. This had been the consummation of a long-cherished desire, the attainment of which prepared fertile ground for future desires only Radha could fulfil.

At one corner of Kusuma-sarovara is a temple dedicated to the Deities of Radha-Vanabihari, who were unfortunately stolen. Their pose depicted Krsna carefully combing Radharani’s hair.

**\* \* \* \***

Another pastime related to this area took place on a Sunday. Radha and Her friends were on their way to Surya-kunda, stopping first at Puspavana to collect flowers to use in their worship of the sun god.

The gopis were in a hurry and so they fanned out, each one hastily picking flowers and collecting them in her own basket, anxious to get to Surya-kunda where — rumour had it — Krsna was waiting for them.

But rumours can’t always be trusted. Sometimes rumours are actually misinformation, intended to distract naive girls from Krsna’s actual plan, which, on this occasion, was to surprise the gopis at Puspavana.

Krsna hid and watched as the cowherd girls arrived at Puspavana. Then He moved to a branch of a green campaka tree dense with leaves, just above the path upon which Radha was picking flowers.

Krsna’s weight on the branch made it bend earthward so that when Radha came there She could easily stand on Her toes and pull down the branch with Her left hand to find the fresh blossoms above.

While Radha was still holding on to the branch, Krsna moved swiftly onto the trunk of the tree, freeing the branch of His weight, and easily lifting Radharani off Her feet and into the air.

Radha reacted instinctively, dropping Her basket and grabbing the rising branch with Her right hand, which left Her dangling in the air. Desperate, She called for help, “Lalita! Visakha! Help Me! Help Me!”

As Radhika continued to call for help, Krsna, unseen by Her, climbed down from the tree and, long before any gopi could come to Her rescue, innocently walked beneath Radha and asked:

“Dear lady! Are You engaged in some strange austerity by which You hope to make Me a prisoner in Your home, or is there another explanation for these impressive acrobatics?”

Radha was incensed by Krsna’s nonchalance, but Her shawl was stuck on a twig and, unable to free Herself, She continued to dangle in mid-air, Her feet conveniently hanging at the height of Krsna’s chest.

Krsna eagerly took the dust from Radha s lotus feet and asked, “May I help You down?” To which Radha replied, “Don’t You touch Me! I’m a chaste new housewife, and My friends will be here at any moment.”

But by some arrangement — no doubt the covering influence of a certain someone’s internal potency — Radha’s friends could not hear Her urgent cries for help, even though they were within earshot.

Krsna looked with feigned innocence into Radha’s fiery eyes as He again passed His right palm across the bottoms of Her lotus feet and sighed, “It seems that Your friends are too far away to hear You.

“Dear Princess Radha! In such an emergency, surely there could be no harm in taking help from a well-wisher such as I, who enjoy a reputation for being as honourable as You are chaste.

“However,” Krsna said with a hint of resignation, “if you are determined to hang around waiting for Your friends, then I will attend to My cows nearby who obviously need Me more than You.”

Radha saw Krsna turn and begin to walk away, and so She called after Him, “Wait! Take Me down.” Krsna stopped and turned around but stayed where He was. He replied, “I am not used to lifting weights.“To lift You off that branch will be a great effort for Me. Shouldn’t You speak to Me in a more humble and polite way if You think that I should strain Myself to help You down?”

Radha looked up again at Her shawl. Should She drop to the ground and let it tear? But that would leave Her partially uncovered in front of Krsna. Her friends would surely make much of that!

Looking down at Krsna, tears welling up in Her eyes, Radha begged, “Dear Syama! You have literally made Me a dancing puppet. Please! Save Me from this predicament before I am humiliated.”

His eyes locked on Hers, a sly smile on His reddish lips, Krsna took a few steps towards Radha and said in a silky voice, “I feel Myself blessed to serve the royalty of Varsana by some menial task.”

Unknown to beautiful Radha, Her friends witnessed this entire episode from their own hiding place. They saw how carefully Krsna unhooked Radha’s cloth from that branch. Will She ever know?

At the far corner of Kusuma-sarovara I offered my respectful obeisances near the grass and creepers, recalling that the great soul Uddhava had once prayed to assume such a form.

After witnessing the depth of the gopis’ love for Krsna, and especially after hearing Radha’s soliloquy to a bee, Uddhava had concluded that the cowherd damsels were paragons of transcendental love.

Uddhava was, therefore, reluctant to return to Mathura with its formalised modes of worship. He decided that he would rather reside in Vraja and learn the gopis’ path to spontaneous paramour love.

He thought, “Sri Radha is Krsna’s dearest beloved, and the other milkmaids daily enjoy the privilege of speaking with Her and seeing Her lotus feet. Thus they also win the favour of my Lord.

“I will not go back to Mathura. Instead I will reside here in Vraja and incessantly chant the names of Krsna while serving the gopis in order to attain their uniquely infallible devotion to the Lord.”

These thoughts moved Uddhava to depart from Nandagrama. But on his way back to Radha-kunda — his place of residence while living in Vrndavana — he began to reconsider his plans.

As he passed by Puspavana, he realised that he was duty-bound to resume his service to Krsna in Mathura, and that he should qualify himself before trying to directly acquire the gopis’ level of devotion.

And even if he did beg the gopis for the dust of their feet, Uddhava was of royal lineage as well as Krsna’s cousin. The gopis respected him as superior and would never make him the object of their mercy.

Sagacious Uddhava thought, “Even if I pitifully beg the gopis to place their blessed feet upon my head, they will not do so. I must devise a way to get their blessings without their knowledge.”

Uddhava looked towards Govardhana Hill and at once recognised Puspavana, recalling that the gopis would regularly come here to collect flowers and meditate on Krsna’s pastimes.

Uddhava whispered, “If I were born as the grass in this forest, the gopis would surely step on me. Unknowingly, they would shower me with the dust of their feet and stain me with the lac painted there.”

It was then that he vowed, “Until I become a creeper by the shores of this lake, I shall perpetually — not just three or five times a day — offer obeisances to the dust of the feet of the cowherd women of Vraja.”

Thus Uddhava dedicated the remainder of his life — right up to the end in Badarikasrama — to glorifying the gopis’ love for Krsna, and by that sankirtana he became qualified to take birth as grass in Vraja.

By growing near Kusuma-sarovara, Uddhava was not only powdered by the dust of the gopis’ feet, he was bathed in their tears of separation, thus by Krsna’s grace his desire was doubly fulfilled.

At Uddhava-baithaka devotees glorify the gopis with the enthusiasm of Uddhava’s prayer,

*vande nanda-vraja-strinam pada-renum abhiksnasah*  
*yasam hari-kathodgitam punati bhuvana-trayam*

**“I repeatedly offer my respects to the dust from the feet of the women of Nanda Maharaja’s cowherd village. When these gopis loudly chant the glories of Sri Krsna, the vibration purifies the three worlds.”**

According to scripture, at nearby Uddhava-kunda Uddhava narrated Srimad-Bhagavatam to the queens of Dvaraka to relieve them from the suffering they felt after Krsna’s departure from this world.

How did Lord Krsna’s queens have the opportunity to hear the Bhdgavatam in Vrndavana if they were kidnapped by cowherd men as Arjuna was escorting the residents of Dvaraka to Indraprastha?

Taking help from Srila Prabhupada and the commentaries of acaryas, this dilemma is addressed in the appendix entitled “Which Queens of Dvaraka Heard Srimad-Bhagavatam at Kusuma-sarovara?”

When Maharaja Yudhisthira retired, he not only made Pariksit Maharaja the new emperor, but he also made Vajranabha responsible for the kingdom of Mathura and the protection of Krsna’s wives.

Since the Lord’s disappearance His queens were understandably morose. To endure life without their transcendental husband was for them a great affliction; indeed, they would have welcomed death.

As they commiserated amongst themselves, the queens — made even more beautiful by their sorrow — noticed that one of their peers, Kalindi, was always inexplicably smiling, even cheerful.

When they asked her how she could be so happy in the absence of her Lord, Kalindi laughed softly to herself and replied, “O Mothers! You think that Sri Krsna is absent, but I say that He is with us now.”

One queen enquired, “How can you say that our husband is with us when the entire universe has testified to His disappearance and, in fact, continues to mourn His absence even today?”

Joyful as always, Kalindi answered, “I will tell you the secret of how I always see Krsna by my side.” The queens gathered around Kalindi as she spoke, “You all know me to be the river Yamuna personified.

“But I have another identity. I am also Visakha, a maidservant of Lord Krsna’s most beloved Srimati Radharani. Visakha, having been born at the same time as my mistress, shares all of Her qualities.

“When Akrura took Krsna to Mathura, we gopis were so heartbroken that we gave up eating, sleeping, and even moving about. The news around Vrndavana was that we would soon die without our Lord.

“Krsna heard about this and sent His intimate friend and devotee, Uddhava, to rescue us. He delivered beautiful messages that gave us the strength to continue living until Krsna returned in person.”

Another queen interjected, “Uddhava is well known to all of us. But what did he say that gave you life?” Kalindi answered, “He taught us how to see Krsna by always hearing about His glories.”

Yet another queen spoke up, asking Kalindi to say more, “How will we receive those same lessons so that we too can smile once again?” Kalindi said, “Ask Uddhava to teach you the transcendental science.”

The queens looked at each other, perplexed. They were in Dvaraka when Krsna had sent Uddhava to Badarikasrama to instruct the sages residing there in the science of pure devotional service.

How could the queens ask Uddhava, who was so far away, to free them from despondency? “We are unable to go to the Himalayas to see him. It appears to us that we are cursed to suffer forever.”

Kalindi felt their sorrow and said, “Uddhava may be teaching spiritual wisdom in Badari, but he was also granted a residence in nearby Vraja, where he is present in the form of grass and creepers.

“If you want to hear from Uddhava then simply call for him on the banks of Kusuma-sarovara in the forest of Puspavana where he resides as creepers, eager to obtain the dust once graced by the gopis’ feet.”

Accompanied by Maharaja Pariksit and Vajranabha, the queens came to Kusuma-sarovara, where playing various instruments and singing devotional songs they began a great festival of sankirtana.

At the peak of their sankirtana, Uddhava suddenly appeared from a nearby grove, wearing yellow garments and a gunja necklace and glowing like a sapphire set in gold, sparkling in the rays of the moon.

Uddhava offered his respects to the queens and embraced Maharaja Pariksit, saying, “I know that the influence of Yogamaya has caused these queens to suffer anxiety due to their separation from Krsna.

“I also know that you have brought them here hoping to make Krsna appear and thus help these ladies regain their proper consciousness. In this age, that is only possible by chanting and hearing about Krsna.

“The best way to hear about Krsna is to hear from Srimad-Bhagavatam, the literary incarnation of the Lord, which I shall now recite continuously for one month and fulfil these ladies’ desire.”

King Pariksit was overjoyed at this wonderful turn of events, but his joy quickly turned to sorrow when Uddhava ordered him to depart and conquer all directions in order to subdue the influence of Kali.

Nevertheless, Uddhava reassured the young emperor that soon he too would be promoted to Krsna’s eternal abode by hearing Srimad- Bhagavatam from the great sage Sukadeva Gosvami.

Pariksit Maharaja, encouraged by these tidings, set off at once to conquer the globe. With Vajranabha’s help, Uddhava at once began a one-month recitation of Srimad-Bhagavatam.

So powerful was the recitation that Krsna’s transcendental pastimes manifested themselves before the queens. With devotional vision, they could see Krsna’s form of eternity, knowledge, and bliss.

Astonished, everyone present could understand that they were eternally situated within Krsna — always connected to Him, just as the rays of the moon are always connected to the moon.

Freed from their affliction, no longer separated from their beloved, the queens who belonged to Krsna’s eternal pastimes returned to His abode, suddenly disappearing from the sight of ordinary people.

But in the groves around Govardhana, and in the other forests that grace Krsna’s home on this planet, they enjoy eternally with Krsna, visible only to those devotees fully dedicated to pure devotion.

We should always remember the essential role that hearing and reciting Srimad-Bhagavatam plays in our becoming Krsna conscious. In this connection, we can meditate on the following beautiful verse:

“This Bhagavata Purdna is as brilliant as the sun, and it has arisen just after the departure of Lord Krsna to His own abode... Persons... in the Age of Kali shall get light from this Purdna.”

Thus even when He is said to have left this world, because His body is Srimad-Bhagavatam, because He and this greatest of Puranas are identical, Krsna can still be seen by hearing Srimad-Bhagavatam.

We will hear the pastime of Uddhava’s narrating Srimad-Bhdgavatam at Govardhana in greater detail when, on the far side of the hill, our parikrama reaches Uddhava-kunda.

**\* \* \* \***

The forest adjoining Puspavana is named Syamavana because its closely-knit trees form a thick canopy that shelters the ???? from even the strongest sunlight, making it dark (syama) and cool.

It is also called Syamavana because here Radha once disguised Herself as Krsna. When Her bewildered girlfriends saw two Krsna’s sitting side by side they could only say, “Who is Syama, and who is Syama?”

This was Krsna’s way of tricking each of the gopis into being alone with Him in a kunja. Thus He was able to have loving exchanges with all the gopis that otherwise would have been difficult to realise.

One morning, after leaving the gopas at nearby Gvala-kunda, Krsna met with Radha and Her friends at Syamavana. While the other gopis were talking, He convinced Radharani to join in His deception.

He smeared Her limbs with black aguru and musk, dressed Her in His clothes and ornaments, gave Her His flute, wrapped a turban around Her head and placed a peacock feather on it, and then sat beside Her.

Krsna gave Radha rudraksa beads and told Her, “Close Your eyes and pretend that You are Me, chanting a mantra that by its power has transformed Me, Radha, into a second Krsna. And don’t speak!”

When the gopis entered the grove they froze in their tracks and rubbed their eyes. “What land have we stumbled upon that has two Krsnas?” they exclaimed to one another in wonder.

But the maidservants nearby, who earlier had been sworn to secrecy, weren’t much help in providing an answer. Instead they replied, “We don’t know. We found Them like this when we arrived.”

Vrnda explained, “Paurnamasi gave Krsna a siddha-mantra, and it appears that He — now chanting silently — has transformed Radha — who is sitting beside Him — into a perfect likeness of Himself.”

One gopi asked, “How could He possibly benefit from creating a double of Himself.?” And another answered, “This way He can enjoy Radha without having to be on the lookout for Jatila.”

The gopis giggled and made wry faces until Citra incited panic amongst the girls by saying, “O friends! What will we say to Jatila when we come home and she asks us, ’Where is my daughter-in-law?’”

Nandimukhi pacified them. “Don’t worry!” she said. “Krsna will protect Jatila’s faith in Radha and return Radha to Her female form. But let’s separate Them before this mantra causes more trouble.”

The gopis crowded around, casting suspicious glances at Radha disguised as Krsna who appeared to be deeply absorbed in meditation. Lalita urged Krsna, “Quick! Put on Your own clothes and let’s go.”

Perfectly imitating Radha’s voice and feigning complete bashfulness, Krsna said, “Lalita! Before going anywhere I have to reveal to you, in confidence, how I came to be embarrassed in this way.”

When the gopis heard Krsna speaking in Radha’s voice they were convinced that He was She, but still they wondered why Radha resembled Krsna in every way, and yet Her voice remained unaltered.

“O friends!” He said in Radha’s voice. “Krsna hypnotised Me. I can’t say exactly what He did, but when I came back to consciousness He was pouring some mystically empowered water all over Me.

“As He did so, My body became like His. Only because the water did not enter My mouth did My voice remain unchanged. But... ” Krsna stopped as the gopis leaned forward to hear more clearly.

“Whatever else happened cannot be freely said, but I also cannot keep it to Myself. I will tell each of you one by one in another solitary kunja, but I’m afraid I cannot do so in front of all of you together.”

The gopis curiosity was piqued, and thus Krsna was able to lead Lalita into a secluded grove nearby, where He sat down on a flower-couch and patted the seat beside Him so that Lalita would sit there.

When Lalita sat down, Krsna put His arm around her and placed a kiss on her lips. Lalita, startled, pulled away saying, “Radha! What are you doing?” Krsna replied, “This is the secret that I had to tell you.”

To Lalita’s further dismay, Krsna then began to speak in His own voice and behave as freely with Lalita as He wished, enriching their loving exchanges with the mellows of laughter and astonishment.

Thereafter, sworn to confidence, Lalita left the kunja. Feigning innocent rapture, she told Visakha, “O friend! You won’t believe it. Go in right away and ask Radharani what happened to Her.”

Like Lalita-devi before her and all the other cowherd girls after her, Visakha was at first shocked by Krsna’s advances but finally surrendered to the occasion in the spirit of being a good sport.

But when the gopis all met together again, they found similar marks on each other’s bodies. Unable to restrain their laughter, they embraced one another and then, holding hands, returned to Radha.

Still dressed in Krsna’s clothes and evidently still chanting Her mantra, Radha neither looked up at Her friends nor said a word to them. She simply sat with the corner of Her eyes turned up playfully.

“? crown jewel of mantric yogis!” said Lalita-devi, an amused smile sprouting on her lips. “Now that Your desire has been fulfilled, please tell us: Why are You still observing a vow of silence?”

Radha pretended to be just awakening from a dream. She opened Her eyes, looked at the gopis and said, “Friends! When did you come?” She glanced around and then added, “Where is that impudent boy?”

As Radha reached up and grabbed the peacock feather from Her turban, throwing it to the ground, Lalita pretended that the cowherd girls knew nothing of Krsna’s schemes or whereabouts.

She began, as convincingly as possible, “You are here disguised as Krsna, but so is another gopi not far away. She tried to trick us into embracing her, but she was clearly an impostor, so we left.”

Just at that moment, Krsna suddenly entered. Having heard what Lalita had said, He spoke to the gopis in Radha’s voice, “Friends, beware! That shameless boy is a bag of crafty tricks.

“How can you crowd around Him after He has altered My beautiful golden body? Quick, let's all run away at once and hide from Him together in some isolated cave of Govardhana Hill.”

At that cunning suggestion, all of them, Radha, Krsna, and all the gopis began to laugh uncontrollably. And their laughter spread to the trees, birds, animals, and lastly to good-natured Giriraja.

Finally, the gopis removed the black from Radha’s limbs and dressed and ornamented Her like a gopi. Joined by Their girlfriends, Radha and Krsna then enjoyed swing pastimes in a kadamba forest nearby.

**\* \* \* \***

At the south end of Syamavana, near the famous throne Ratna-simhasana, is a beautiful clearing where Radha and Krsna enjoyed the rasa dance. Balarama even enjoyed here once with His own gopis.

I stopped walking and recalled that special event on the full moon night of Phalguna, known as Holika-purnima or Gaura-purnima, when Krsna and Balarama played Holi here with the girls of Vraja.

The moon was special that night, surrounded by glittering stars, caressed by a gentle breeze bearing the aroma of mallika flowers, serenaded by bumblebees driven mad by that delightful aroma.

The two brothers wore flower garlands and spotless garments. Their limbs were beautifully decorated and anointed. The gopis, bound to Them by ropes of affection, endearingly sang Their glories.

The two Lords then praised the nightfall with Their own incomparable voices. They sung the glories of the rising moon and stars, of the jasmine-scented night air and intoxicated bees.

That singing showcased the full range of musical sounds all at once. It was a truly divine performance that brought indescribable happiness to the ears, minds, and hearts of all living beings.

Upon hearing that song of unprecedented beauty, the gopis were stunned. Forgetting themselves, they did not notice their fine garments becoming loose or their hair and garlands dishevelled.

While Krsna and Balarama thus played according to Their own sweet will and sang to the point of apparent intoxication, a servant of Kuvera named SarikhacCida came upon the scene, lurking in the dark.

The demon had been sent by Kamsa to kidnap Srimati Radharani and take Her to Mathura to be that evil king’s wife, but when Sankhacuda saw the other beautiful gopis, he decided to capture them for himself.

As Radha and Krsna sat conversing on the diamond-studded ratna- simhasana, Mukhara suddenly came by in search of her granddaughter, forcing Krsna to slip into the darkness of the forest.

With Krsna momentarily out of sight, Sankhacuda saw his chance and seized Radha by Her soft, long braid. Brandishing a large stick, he herded the other gopis together, driving them off towards the north.

The cowherd damsels had no other shelter than Krsna and Balarama, and so they cried out to them, “Krsna! Rama!” The brothers urgently replied, “Do not fear!” and ran after the demon.

Krsna and Balarama saw the gopis to be just like helpless cows being stolen by a rustler. The brothers then tore mighty sala trees out of the ground and ran after the demon brandishing Their weapons.

Krsna called out, “Wicked demon, offensive to Radha, I feel great pain in My heart because if I kill you now — which I certainly will — I will not he able to spend the rest of eternity torturing you.”

When Sankhacuda saw Krsna and Balarama coming towards him like the personified forces of time and death, he was filled with fear. Confused, he abandoned the gopis and fled for his life.

The gopis were fatigued from their ordeal, and thus Balarama protected and consoled them as they rested. Meanwhile, Krsna continued the chase and followed the demon wherever he went.

Krsna was eager to fulfil His vow to kill the wicked Sankhacuda. He also wanted to reclaim the famous conch-shaped Syamantaka jewel that shone like a second sun atop the demon’s turban.

Finally Krsna overtook the demon and with the chivalrous strength of His arms attacked the hideous Sankhacuda, who had fearsome curling fangs in his horrible mouth.

From a distance the gopis watched the fight and cried, “Krsna is as delicate as a newly sprouted tamala tree compared to this giant whose arms are like palm trees and whose chest is like a mountainside.

“Alas, in this terrible battle Krsna does not even have an expert friend to help Him. If Queen Yasoda were to see this sight she would certainly give up her life on the spot.”

The peacock feather in Krsna’s hair danced as He threw the sala tree at Sankhacuda. The demon fell but at once rose again and began to punch Krsna with his fists while making a terrible roaring sound.

Krsna grabbed Sankhacuda with His powerful arms, then playfully whirled the demon around and around before hurling him to the ground, just as easily as a hurricane uproots a lotus flower.

Again Sankhacuda rose to his feet. He grabbed Krsna with his fearsome hands and threw Him to the trembling earth. But Krsna stood up, undisturbed, and playfully punched the Yaksa with His fist.

Struck by the Lord, Sankhacuda’s body shuddered and fell to the ground like an uprooted banyan tree. Krsna then jumped on the demon’s chest and punched him again and again until he died.

The gopis could see that the demon was dead and that they were no longer in danger. They watched as Krsna took the jewel from the demon’s head, just as a pious man accepts a well-earned reward.

Krsna rose to His feet, straightened His clothes, polished the Syamantaka jewel by rubbing it on His wrapper, and humbly offered the jewel to Balarama, just as a vassal would offer tax to a king.

Balarama understood that Krsna had wanted to give the Syamantaka jewel to Radharani, but had He done so the other gopis would have been jealous. Instead Krsna had given it to His brother.

Not one to incur the wrath of Vraja’s damsels, Balarama handed the jewel to Madhumangala. And Madhu, understanding the mind of both brothers, presented it to Srimati Radharani.

Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami has described the Syamantaka jewel’s circuitous route,

*dattah pralamba-ripunodbhata-sankhacuda-nasat pratosi-hrdayam madhumangalasya —*  
*Hastena yah sumukhi kaustubha-mitram etam kim te syamantaka-manim taralam karisye*

**“O beautiful-faced one! Will I make the Syamantaka jewel the central jewel in Your necklace?**

**“After the death of proud Sankhacuda, it was given by Balarama to cheerful-hearted Madhumangala, and Madhu gave this jewel to You. And since then it has been the friend of the Kaustubha jewel.”**

Of the obstacles to pure devotion, Sankhacuda represents the unwanted tendency to seek sense gratification and fame through the practice of sadhana, a tendency that naturally leads to selfish pride.

A visit to the Ratna-simhasana, Radha’s and Krsna’s throne, in Syamavana should remind us to carefully root out such tendencies from our own hearts and to tactfully avoid those persons who do not.

Just behind the throne of the divine couple is beautiful Ratna-kunda, and beside the kunda is a rasa-vedi, where Krsna would dance with the gopis, whose mere touch would make the trees burst into bloom.

From Ratna-simhasana I walked past Krsna’s footprints — melted into a stone due to the sound of His flute. A cast of these footprints is displayed in front of Radha-Syama’s altar in New Vraja-dhama.

Visitors are shown the footprints of the Lord and taught how to touch them and respectfully place the dust of the Lord’s feet to their heads. In this way they enjoy a new and transcendental delight.

Continuing on, I wandered into Gvalavana, one of the forests that adjoins Syamavana, and from there I walked to Gvala-kunda, which in Vraja is rarely visited and is not properly maintained.

Gvala means go-wala, or cowherd boy, a fitting name for this area, as it is a favourite resting place for Krsna’s cowherd boyfriends. They often take their lunch here, or pause for a late morning snack.

The boys let the calves graze on the soft grasses of Govardhana Hill while they sit on the ground surrounding Krsna, who sits in the centre, and open the lunch packs that they bring from home.

Like the whorl of a lotus flower surrounded by its many petals and leaves, Krsna is the cynosure encircled by rings of gopas, each of whom looks at Krsna hoping that He might look back at him.

The hungry cowherd boys eat their lunches on all manner of makeshift plates. Some boys use rocks, flowers, or the bark of trees. Some use their baskets, and some use fruits or bunches of leaves.

But all the cowherd boys enjoy their lunch with their hero, Krsna, freely sharing with one another the variety of tastes found in the various preparations they bring from home.

While the boys taste one another's lunches, they joke and laugh amongst themselves, and Krsna, looking around at all of His friends, also jokes with them, inspiring jubilant laughter as He eats.

Krsna sits with His flute tucked into the right side of His sash and His horn and cow-driving stick in the left. In His hands He holds a mixture of yogurt, rice, and pieces of fruit between His fingers.

The demigods who behold this sight are struck with wonder at how the Personality of Godhead, who eats only sacrificial offerings (yajna-bhuk), is now casually eating with His friends in the forest.

Srila Prabhupada was once meditating on this pastime while having his evening massage. He described it like this:

**"Every day Krsna, Balarama, and the gopas would go into the forest.**  
**"Before they would go, their mothers would make lunches and place them in tiffins. Krsna's mother, Yasoda, would prepare a very nice lunch of puri, halava, kachori, and laddu.**  
**"And the other cowherd boys... their mothers were not so opulent, so their tiffins would have capati, rice, sabji, and other things like that. Then they would go and play in the forests all day.**  
**"Because they were little boys they cared for the calves, not the cows, and when at midday they would stop for lunch, sometimes one of the cowherd boys would steal Krsna's lunch pack.**  
**"The thief would begin throwing Krsna's tiffin around and the boys would play 'keep-away' with Krsna until finally the thief would throw his lunch-box to Krsna, and they would sit down to eat.**  
**"The cowherd boys would then crowd around and eat Krsna's puri, halava, kachori, and laddu, and Krsna would sit with them, and He would eat their capati, rice, and sabji."**

Srila Prabhupada concluded his narration by saying,

**"I just want to go back to the spiritual world and eat kachori and laddu with Krsna. That's all."**

And closing his eyes, His Divine Grace said nothing more.

**\* \* \* \***

Srila Prabhupada's description of the cowherd boys stealing Krsna's lunch pack is reminiscent of another prasada theft that took place in Gvalavana. But this time Madhumangala was the victim.

One afternoon, on their way back from conducting the Surya-puja at Surya-kunda, Madhu and Krsna met up with the other cowherd boys, who had been waiting for them at Gvalavana since late morning.

The gopas embraced Krsna, chided Him for leaving them for so long, and enquired about what He had been doing. Krsna responded by hinting that something valuable was hidden in Madhu's scarf.

Madhumahgala had received some very special sweets from Jatila in gratitude for his brahminical services. Unwilling to share them with anyone, he concealed his reward by tying it in a knot in his scarf.

But after Krsna tipped the boys off to Madhu's edible treasure, Balarama approached the comical king of the brahmanas and probed, "Madhu, tell us. What do you have concealed in that cloth?"

Madhumangala curtly replied, "Prasada of the sun god." Suspicious of Baladeva's intentions, Madhu tucked his treasure under his arm as Balarama continued, "From where did you get such treats?"

Madhu tucked the sweets tighter under his armpit and replied, "I satisfied the good people of Vraja by performing sacrifices. Out of gratitude they have justly rewarded me for my brahminical labour."

"Let me see what it is!" persisted Balarama, but Madhumangala could sense his own impending doom. Determined to conceal his treasure he replied, "No! You and Your friends are voracious and greedy."

Madhu's voice shook, "Just by seeing my prasada you will lust after it." But Rama was not deterred, "Bato! Why are you so greedy? Keep a portion for yourself and share the rest with your friends."

Raising his head and sticking out his chest in pride, Madhumangala declared, "Friends! At this time, I am not inclined to partake of this heavenly preparation myself, nor will I share any of it with you."

Balarama stepped closer to Madhu and whispered to him in mock confidence, "These boys are agitated by hunger. They will take your sweets by force. Better offer them some to pacify their appetites."

Madhumangala's feelings were hurt. He replied disdainfully, "I consider Your friends as less significant than blades of grass. Neither do I think very highly of You, Rama — a prince without a throne.

"I am a brahmana, a god on earth. And despite the many warnings of the Vedas, you ignorant cowherd boys dare to threaten my property. Do you not know the power of a celibate brahmana like me?"

As Madhumangala rambled on, Balarama signalled to the cowherd boys, who quietly surrounded the brahmana boy and then requested that he share some of his prasada with them.

Still, Madhumangala adamantly refused. To more securely protect his wealth, he rolled the scarf containing the prasada into the creases at the waist of his dhoti and crossed his arms defiantly.

Suddenly one boy came up from behind and covered Madhumangala's eyes with his hands. Another boy deftly removed from around Madhu's waist the scarf that held the prasada.

While Madhumangala ranted in protest, decrying the gopas and their unrefined qualities, the thieves ran hither and thither each snatching their share of the loot and making a great show of their pleasure.

Meanwhile, one boy came from behind and untucked the back-fold of Madhu's dhoti. As Madhu reached around to fix it, another boy came and opened the front-fold, exposing Madhu's underwear.

In retaliation for this insult, Madhumangala aimed a volley of abusive words at the conspirators, further inciting them to knock off his turban, untie his sikha, and steal his flute and stick.

Madhu laughed, cried, growled, scolded, and cursed, and while attempting to hold up his dhoti with one hand — his face glowing red — he grabbed Krsna's stick and clumsily chased the boys.

Some gopas stood their ground, parrying and fencing with Madhu, awkwardly waving their sticks in the air. Finally, Krsna diplomatically restrained His friends and embraced the angry brahmana boy.

Krsna gave back Madhu's stick and flute, replaced his turban, retied the dhoti around his chubby waist, and returned the plundered scarf with a few crumbs of the sacred prasada still left in its folds.

His afternoon snack entirely consumed, Madhumangala cursed the boys: "You have stolen, by force, the property of a pure-hearted brahmana and eaten the prasada given to him as daksina.

"You are all impure, whimsical, and arrogant. I shall now return to Vraja and tell everyone about your impudence!" Balarama affectionately wrapped His arms around Madhu, but it was of no use.

"You!" yelled Madhu. "You are responsible for these boys' sins. You instigated their rebellion against the established sastric order." Pursing his lips and tucking his thumbs into his belt he concluded:

"I will not speak to any of you until you have begged for my pardon, atoned for your sins, changed your ways, and," Madhu added, "compensated for your offences with one thousand bundi-laddus."

In ways like this, Krsna played with His friends in Gvalavana. And in the afternoon, when the calves were tired and the boys hungry, they all gradually made their way back home to their parents.

Walking along the whorl of the lotus of New Vraja-dhama, I followed the direction the Manasa-ganga would flow and widen into a lake, stopping at that place where great souls reside, Cakra-tirtha.

The great souls to be remembered here are the Sudarsana cakra, Lord Krsna's ultimate weapon; Lord Siva, as Cakresvara Mahadeva; and Sanatana Gosvami, the eldest of the six Gosvamis of Vrndavana.

As Krsna held Govardhana Hill aloft, the Sudarsana cakra flew around the base of the mountain and with its intense heat evaporated the streams of water flowing down Giriraja' slopes.

After Krsna had put the hill back in place, the cakra, who is generally associated with Lord Visnu and His pastimes, requested Krsna that he may continue to reside somewhere in the Govardhana area.

Krsna felt greatly obliged to His eternal weapon. He also wanted Sudarsana to witness the spontaneous loving devotion of the Vraja- vasis, so the Lord gave him Cakra-tirtha as a place of bhajana.

Lord Siva, Parvati, Ganesa, and Nandi also reside here. Therefore, by chanting Hare Krsna to Cakresvara Mahadeva, a pilgrim can easily acquire the benefits gained by trekking all the way to Mount Kailasa.

Mahadeva is known by the name Cakresvara because he resides at Cakra-tirtha. Still, some devotees say that Siva is so known because it was he who ordered Sudarsana to dry up Indra's torrential rains.

Sanatana Gosvaml is intimately linked with Cakra-tirtha because he lived here — worshipping Radha and Krsna in a cottage facing Lord Siva — and sadly, because he also left his body at this place.

There is no distress more painful than separation from Krsna's devotee. As Sri Sanatana himself said, "When we are separated from a devotee even for a moment, we cannot enjoy happiness."

Devotees, therefore, should learn to appreciate their juniors, peers, and seniors while they are all still physically present and not wait for them to leave this world before valuing or glorifying them.

On the order of Caitanya Mahaprabhu, Sanatana Gosvami came to Vraja in 1515 at the age of twenty-seven, and until his expiry at the age of seventy he lived at different places throughout the dhama.

Wherever Sanatana Gosvami took up residence, either in Vrndavana, Govardhana, Kamyavana, or Nandagrama, he would never be far from a deity of Lord Siva. In fact, he would worship Mahadeva daily.

The reason that Sanatana always lived near Lord Siva is, in the Gosvami's own words, because "Lord Siva increases one's love for Lord Krsna," and because the two great souls are closely related.

Sanatana Gosvami is said to be the combined form of Lavariga-manjari and Sanatana Kumara. The Kumara, born from Brahma's mind, is thus the brother of Siva, who was also born of Brahma.

Sometimes, when absorbed in his internal identity as Lavariga- manjari, Sanatana Gosvami would meditate on how the gopis used to come to Cakra-tirtha, then known as the boat landing, Paranga.

From this place he would daily circumambulate Govardhana Hill along the much longer outer parikrama path, offering one thousand one hundred and eight obeisances to Giriraja as well.

Once, at the advanced age of sixty-five, as Sanatana was performing Govardhana parikrama in the heat of the summer, he was passing by Kusuma-sarovara when he sat down briefly to rest.

At that time, Krsna appeared in the guise of a local cowherd boy and fanned the Gosvami with His upper cloth. The boy said to Sanatana, "Baba! You are too old to be doing parikrama every day."

The cowherd boy's fanning had an indescribable effect on the Gosvami. "This boys' fanning has the same soothing effect on my body that his voice has on my heart," Sanatana thought.

And although Sanatana was almost crying from exhaustion, he tried to conceal his condition from the boy, patiently explaining that he had taken a vow to perform Govardhana parikrama every day.

The cowherd boy laughed and then, embracing Sanatana, He picked up the startled Gosvami, put him on His shoulder, and started to walk, saying, "Don't worry! Then I will carry you!"

In an attempt to stop the boy, Sanatana said, "Please put me down! My vow is to walk around Govardhana Hill, not to be carried around it! Besides, how is it that You are so strong? Who are You?"

Krsna was very pleased with Sanatana's determination, but He was also very concerned for the sadhu's failing health. With these two factors in mind, the Lord provided a wonderful solution.

Krsna took Sanatana — who was by now doubly suspicious of the boy's identity — and led him closer to Govardhana Hill. There Krsna placed His foot on a large stone, leaving an impression on it.

Finally Sanatana understood who this cowherd boy was. Falling on the ground, he bathed Krsna's feet with his tears and — devotional scholar that he was — offered the Lord very beautiful prayers.

Once Sanatana's composure returned, Krsna said, "Take this stone and worship it daily with tulasi leaves and water, then walk around it seven times. That will be equivalent to circling the entire hill."

And with that Krsna disappeared. Sanatana Gosvami then took the large stone back to Cakra-tirtha, and for the rest of his life he served the sila exactly as the Lord had personally instructed.

At another time, Sanatana became so disturbed by the trouble the mosquitoes at Cakra-tirtha were causing him that he began to make arrangements to relocate to Dvadasaditya-tila in Vrndavana.

Cakresvara Mahadeva, concerned that he would lose Sanatana Gosvami's company, assumed the form of an elderly brahmana and, approaching Sanatana's kutira, asked Sanatana why he was leaving.

Sanatana Gosvami replied, "The mosquitoes here are such a nuisance that I cannot do bhajana properly. Without proper bhajana, what is the purpose of continuing to live at Cakra-tirtha?"

Siva was relieved. He knew that he could easily resolve Sanatana's difficulty. The Lord said, "Prabhu! Please remain here for one more day and see if Krsna does not make some arrangement for you."

Lord Siva then left to summon the demigod in charge of mosquitoes. Mahesvara ordered that all mosquitoes quit the area of Cakra-tirtha at once, and no longer disturb that great devotee.

Immediately Sanatana Gosvami noticed that there were no more mosquitoes either in his cottage or anywhere else in the area, and so he continued to live and worship at Cakra-tirtha in great happiness.

Guru-purnima, the full moon day of Asadha (June-July), is the appearance day of Srila Vyasadeva. On this day, all the devotees of Vraja offer respect to Sanatana Gosvami as Vyasa's representative.

In the year 1558 thousands of Vraja-vasis, along with the leading Vaisnavas like Rupa, Jiva, Raghunatha Dasa, and Gopala Bhatta Gosvamis, came to Cakra-tirtha to observe this festival.

When the devotees arrived, Sanatana appeared to be absorbed in deep meditation. Nonetheless, they lovingly requested that he take them on parikrama of Manasa-ganga as he usually did.

However, the devotees found that Sri Sanatana was completely unresponsive. They let out a great cry of lamentation, realising that he had left them forever and entered into Krsna's eternal pastimes.

The Vaisnavas bathed and dressed the body of Sanatana then mournfully carried him around Manasa-ganga and on to Vrndavana, where they placed him in samadhi near Madana-mohana's temple.

Sanatana Gosvami was so dear to the Vraja-vasis that, from the day of his disappearance, more people circumambulate Govardhana Hill on Guru-purnima than any other day, even on Govardhana-puja.

At Cakra-tirtha we offer our respects to Sanatana Gosvami at his bhajana-kutira and to Cakresvara Mahadeva at the temple of Lord Siva, which faces the residence of the great Gosvami.

Further along the bank of the lake is Saubhari Muni's palace, where he lived with his fifty wives. His palace is close to the residence of Maharaja Mandhata, the father of Ambarisa Maharaja.

**\* \* \* \***

All holy places in this world are replicas of their original prototypes located in Vraja, therefore the original form of the Ganga, flowing from the Himalayas to the sea, is found in Manasa-ganga.

In order to best please Krsna with her opulences, Ganga-devi in Vraja constantly studies the Lord's mind, and therefore she who flows north to south across Govardhana Hill is known as Manasa-ganga.

The waters of Manasa-ganga reveal the inner truths of Giriraja, purify devotees of all sinful reactions, remove their misunderstandings about pure devotion, and qualify them for service to Radha and Krsna.

In other words, although bathing in the Ganga that flows from the Himalayas to the sea bestows liberation, bathing in the Ganga that flows across Govardhana Hill bestows love for Krsna.

And although Manasa-ganga eternally flows in Vraja, it remained invisible until Krsna invoked its presence and gave it to the Vraja-vasis. For this reason Krsna is called Manasa-gangara-dani.

During the first phase of Krsna's boyhood, Manasa-ganga was despondent because, unlike her younger sister Yamuna, she had not yet played an active role in the Lord's pastimes.

Ganga revealed her sorrow to Yamuna and requested her sister to petition Krsna on her behalf. When the two rivers approached the Lord, He reassured them that Ganga's desire would soon be fulfilled.

It would not be long before Krsna did indeed call upon Manasa-ganga to help resolve a crisis that required her to appear before all His cowherd boyfriends and later to give testimony before the Vraja-vasis.

The crisis arose just after Krsna killed Vatsasura, a demon who had been cursed to take the form of a calf and who had come to Vrndavana at King Kamsa's request in order to kill Nanda's sons.

The demon had planned to catch Krsna and Balarama off guard by mingling with the other calves. He had falsely presumed that he could mingle with Nanda's immense herd, undetected.

But the brothers could easily tell the difference between a demon and a surabhi calf. Sneaking up on Vatsasura from behind, Krsna caught him by the legs, whipped him around, and threw him into a tree.

The demon was thrashed so badly that he gave up his life. And when his lifeless body fell from the tree, the gopas congratulated Krsna, saying, "Well done!" as the demigods showered flowers from above.

But once the boys had celebrated Krsna's victory, they conferred amongst themselves and concluded that Krsna should purify Himself by bathing in the Ganga because He had killed a sacred animal.

Krsna was dismayed at the prospect of leaving Vraja. Not wanting to interrupt the pleasure of playing with His friends for even a moment, He implored Ganga to reveal her transcendental form.

Thus invoked by Krsna's mind (manah), Manasa-ganga became visible to the Vraja-vasis. Where it bisected Govardhana Hill it formed a beautiful lake and then flowed off in the direction of Govinda-kunda.

From that time on, Manasa-ganga and the forests on her banks became yet more jewels in the gopas' treasure house of playgrounds where they could deeply submerge with Krsna in pastimes of love.

Krsna and Balarama like to stroll the banks of Manasa-ganga along with cowherd boys such as Dama, Bala, and Mahabala, who sing Krsna's glories as the cows and calves follow behind.

When the boys sit down in a solitary forest and play games, tell jokes, or chat amongst themselves, the calves sometimes wander off to distant forests, and the gopas become anxious for them.

At these times Krsna plays a haunting melody on His flute and the frisky calves at once come running to Him. Then His friends — freed from all anxiety — again resume their games.

Sometime after the appearance of Manasa-ganga, the Vraja-vasis excitedly discussed making a pilgrimage to bathe in the waters of Ganga-devi, perhaps at Haridvara, or maybe even at Gangotri.

One evening the brahmanas told the cowherd men about a pastime that illustrated the extraordinary benefits derived by just touching— what to speak of bathing in — the waters of the Ganga.

The sages recounted how Garuda had once caught a snake. As he was carrying the poor creature off somewhere to eat it, they passed over the Ganga and the snake's tail just barely skimmed the water below.

By simply touching the pure water of the Ganga, the soul of the snake instantly left that body and manifested a divine, four-armed form that resembled, in every detail, a resident of Vaikuntha.

Garuda could understand that by the grace of the river Ganga the transformed snake had attained liberation, and so Garuda personally carried that fortunate soul back to Vaikuntha.

The Vraja-vasis' desire for a pilgrimage to Mother Ganga grew day by day until it matured into the determination to go, at which point they made preparations to depart at an auspicious time.

When Krsna saw His household servants loading clothing and provisions into Nanda Maharaja's best bullock cart, He enquired from His mother and father where they were planning to go.

Nandaraja lovingly smiled at his son and replied, "My dear boy! Our entire village will make a pilgrimage to the sacred Ganga for our purification, and You and Your friends will all come along."

But Krsna had no interest in leaving His Vrndavana playground, nor did He want the Vraja-vasis to think that any place of pilgrimage, anywhere in the world, was equal to or superior to Vraja.

Krsna made a wry face and replied, "But father, there can be nothing as purifying as living in Vrndavana. Why then should we put ourselves through so much inconvenience just to visit a lesser place?"

Mother Yasoda scolded Krsna, pulling His ear, "O Visnu! Do not offend Mother Ganga, who constantly washes Lord Visnu's lotus feet. There is nothing as purifying as the embrace of her waves."

But Krsna would not be intimidated. He grabbed His mother's hand as she tugged on His ear and replied, "Mother! If you are determined to bathe in the Ganga, why not do so here in Vrndavana?"

Nanda and Yasoda exchanged patronising smiles as Krsna continued, "Manasa-ganga, flowing over Govardhana Hill, is no different from the Mother Ganga flowing at Haridvara. Bathe here!"

Krsna's parents tried to explain to their son that Manasa-ganga was just the nickname for a local river of Vraja, but as usual it was no use. Their headstrong son insisted that Ganga herself flowed by Giriraja.

In desperation, Nandaraja challenged Krsna, "We will cancel our trip to Haridvara if Ganga-devi, riding on a white crocodile, personally appears in the waters of the river you call Manasa-ganga."

Krsna clapped His hands and said, "All right, let's go right now and pray to Ganga-devi. Surely she will appear to us!" With that they at once set off for Govardhana Hill and Manasa-ganga.

Krsna's playmates, then their parents, and finally everyone else followed Krsna to the shores of Manasa-ganga, where they watched Him bow down, touch the water, and offer prayers with joined palms.

The Vraja-vasis were positive that Manasa-ganga was not the Ganges and that Krsna's claim was surely make-believe, yet they loved Him so much that they could not but indulge His stubborn ways and whims.

But then, suddenly, a soft glow appeared at the centre of Manasa- ganga lake and from it a divine woman emerged riding upon a great white crocodile. It was Mother Ganga herself!

Dressed in a white sari, decorated with many strands of pearls, bedecked with costly ornaments, her complexion moon-white and her composure peaceful, Ganga-devi was stunningly beautiful.

In her two graceful, creeper-like arms she carried a pot and a white lotus, and upon her forehead she displayed a benevolent third eye through which she saw all phases of time simultaneously.

At first the Vraja-vasis could not trust their vision, but then they heard Krsna's sweet voice, "It is Mother Ganga. She has come to prove that Manasa-ganga is indeed her very own form, here in Vraja."
