"O Prabhu! Why are you sitting here hungry and thirsty? Why don't you beg some food to eat? What kind of meditation are you undergoing for which you must neglect the needs of your body?"

Madhavendra Puri's heart became deeply pacified by seeing the boy's extraordinary beauty. Any thoughts of hunger or thirst were driven to a distant place simply by hearing the boy's sweet words.

By way of reply the saint enquired, "Who are You? Where do You live? And how did You know I was fasting?" The boy said, "Sir! I am a cowherd boy, and I reside in this village, where no one needs to fast.

"Anyone can come to our village and beg for food — a little milk at the very least — but to those souls like your good self who have vowed never to beg for food, I supply whatever they need."

The boy pointed to Govinda-kunda and continued, "Some women came here for water. When they saw you they sent Me with this milk. Please take it. When I finish milking My cows, I'll return for the pot."

Saying this, the extraordinary cowherd boy left. After drinking the milk, Madhavendra Puri, wonderstruck, kept looking down the path from where the boy had first appeared, but He did not return.

The vision of that cowherd boy so perplexed the equipoised sage that he was unable to sleep that night. Chanting the Hare Krsna maha- mantra instead, he finally dozed off just before dawn.

The same cowherd boy then appeared to Madhavendra Puri in a dream. Taking the saint by the had, the boy led him to a bush in the heart of the jungle and suddenly exclaimed, "I live in this bush!

"For many days I have been observing you, and because of your ecstatic love for Me I have been wondering, 'When will My dear devotee Madhavendra Puri come here to serve Me?'

"My name is Gopala, the lifter of Govardhana Hill! I was installed by Vajra as the authority here, but when the Muslims attacked this area, My priest hid Me in this bush and ran away, never to return.

"While concealed here I have suffered from cold, rain, wind, and heat. But now that you have come I am happy. Please call the people of the village and carefully remove Me from this bush." Cleanse My body with large quantities of cold water, construct a temple on top of Govardhana Hill, and instal Me there with all due pomp. By gaining My audience all fallen souls will be delivered."

The boy then disappeared, and Madhavendra Puri woke up. Once he had considered his dream, the following realisation arose in his mind, "I saw Lord Krsna directly, but I could not recognise Him!"

Struck by ecstatic love, Madhavendra Puri lamented and fell to the ground. He cried for a long time, but eventually with great effort he became calm by fixing his mind on Gopala's order.

Madhavendra Puri bathed and then went into the village, where he assembled the people to tell them of his dream and Gopala's order. The villagers all happily agreed to help him.

With machetes the men exuberantly cleared a path to where Gopala had long been concealed. When they finally reached the debris- covered Deity, they were astonished by His beauty.

In accordance with the Lord's order, the strongest villagers carried Gopala to the hilltop, placed Him on a makeshift throne of stones, and watched as brahmanas bathed Him with water from Govinda-kunda.

Meanwhile, the village elders sent milk, yogurt, and ghee, the women provided various foods, sweetmeats, and garments, and the children brought a huge quantity of tulasi leaves and flowers.

When all these items had been gathered, Madhavendra Puri began the ceremonial bathing of Gopala to the accompaniment of the cowherds' devotional singing, dancing, and instrumental music.

After Gopala was bathed, massaged with oil, and bathed again, He was dressed in new garments, anointed with sandalwood pulp, and decorated with necklaces and with garlands of flowers and tulasi buds.

From the moment Gopala was taken from His hiding place, word of His appearance spread throughout Vrndavana, and all the Vraja- vasis spontaneously made arrangements to greet their beloved Lord.

An open kitchen was set up at the foot of Govardhana Hill where ten brahmanas cooked grains, seven made capatis, and five cooked wet vegetable preparations with spinach, roots, and fruits.

By the time Madhavendra Puri had completed the worship of Gopala, the brahmanas had spread cloth upon the ground and were arranging all the bhoga on palasa leaves for offering to the Deity.

The countless stacks of capatis, hills of rice, mounds of vegetables, and pots of yogurt, milk, and buttermilk reminded everyone of the Annakuta ceremony performed for Giriraja long ago.

Madhavendra Puri personally offered everything to Gopala who, being very hungry, ate everything, although by His transcendental potencies the preparations appeared untouched.

Once Gopala had been satisfied with nice food, Madhavendra Puri arranged for a cot, covered it with a makeshift canopy, spread a new sheet over it, and then asked Gopala to lie down and rest.

Thereafter, Madhavendra Puri requested all the villagers to honour Gopala's maha-prasada. They did so happily and were thrilled with bliss, marvelling at the spiritual potency of the great Vaisnava saint.

Day after day the elders of Vraja's villages approached Madhavendra Puri to sponsor Annakuta ceremonies for the Lord, and thus, day after day, Gopala relished great feasts in His honour.

In the meantime, Madhavendra Puri initiated some pure-hearted brahmanas and allotted to them various services to Gopala, thereby inaugurating and standardising the Lord's worship.

The news that Krsna was being worshipped on top of Govardhana Hill attracted throngs of people. Thus having seen Gopala's beauty and felt His kindness, their hankering and lamentation vanished.

In addition to the villagers who brought food for Gopala, merchants made presentations of gold, silver, and rich cloth for His treasury, and a wealthy landowner offered to construct a temple for the Lord.

And since Gopala could not be a cowherd without cows, every family of Vrndavana felt it their duty to contribute one cow each. Thus Gopala had soon acquired a herd that numbered in the thousands.

The residents of New Vraja-dhama should emulate the devotion the Vraja-vasis of Bhauma Vrndavana showed for Gopala. Such devotion is the essence of living in the dhama.

If all the devotees were to make Radha-Syamasundara the cynosure and the top priority in their lives, then the spiritual atmosphere would draw everyone, even guests, spontaneously to Their Lordships.

Krsnadasa Kaviraja writes, "The ideal place to execute Krsna consciousness is Vrndavana, where the people are naturally inclined to love Krsna, and Krsna is naturally inclined to love them.

Madhavendra Puri entrusted the worship of Gopala to two sannyasi disciples and then considered the Lord's desires fulfilled. And he lived happily by Giriraja's side in what is now Yatipura.

After two years had passed, Madhavendra Puri again had a dream in which Gopala appeared to him, this time with a new request: "Even after two years My body has not yet cooled down.

"Please bring sandalwood from Jagannatha Puri, grind it into paste, and smear that over My body to cool Me. Since no one else can do this service, you must do it yourself. Therefore, go quickly."

Madhavendra Puri was joyful that Krsna had again requested some service from him. After ensuring that the regular schedule of Gopala's worship would continue, he started out for Jagannatha Puri.

While passing through Santipura, Madhavendra Puri visited the home of Advaita Acarya. Upon observing Madhavendra Puri's ecstatic love, the acarya requested initiation from him.

After initiating Advaita Acarya, Madhavendra Puri continued on his journey, stopping briefly in Remuna, where Gopinatha increased the saint's devotional fame by stealing a pot of condensed milk for him.

From Remuna, Madhavendra Puri continued to Jagannatha Puri where he was given sandalwood and camphor, as well as an assistant to carry the goods and the travel documents for the toll collectors.

On his way back to Vraja, in Remuna, Madhavendra Puri had a third dream, in which Gopala gave him another instruction. His lotus face beaming with affection for His devotee, Gopala said:

"There is no need for you to leave Remuna. Grind the sandalwood together with the camphor and then daily smear that paste on the body of Gopinatha until it is finished.

"Since there is no difference between My body and the body of Gopinatha, when you smear the sandalwood pulp on the body of Gopinatha you will naturally be smearing it on My body as well.

"My bodily temperature will come down, and your service to Me will have succeeded. With strong faith in Me, do not think twice but simply carry out this order. Do the needful."

Madhavendra Puri carried out the order of the Lord. Every day all summer long Gopinatha's body was anointed with sandalwood paste, and the bodies of both Deities became pleasantly cool.

The stock of sandalwood was used up by the end of summer. Madhavendra Puri then returned to Jagannatha Puri and stayed there with great pleasure during the four months of the rainy season.

The loving relationship between the Deity of Krsna and His devotee is very sweet and intimate. Gopala understood that the Muslim toll- keepers would have greatly harassed Madhavendra Puri.

Considerate of His devotee's welfare and wishing to ensure the success of Madhavendra Purl's labour, Gopala accepted the sandalwood pulp through the body of Gopinatha.

To examine Madhavendra Purl's intense love for Him, Gopala ordered the saint to bring sandalwood from Nilacala. And when Madhavendra passed this test, the Lord became very merciful to him.

Dear devotees! Remember Madhavendra Puri and recite the verse he composed, ayi dina-dayardra natha he mathura-natha kadavalokyase / hrdayam tvad-aloka-kataram dayita bhramyati kim karomy aham:

"O My Lord! O merciful master! O master of Mathura! When shall I see You again? Because of My not seeing You, My agitated heart has become unsteady. O most beloved one, what shall I do now?"

This verse is like the moon because it spreads illumination all over the world. It is like a Kaustubha gem amongst the numberless invaluable poetic jewels that deal with the mellow of pure devotion.

Just as continuous rubbing increases the aroma of sandalwood, continuous recitation and deliberation upon this verse increases the depth of one's understanding of its importance.

It is said that this verse was spoken by Srimati Radharani Herself and that by Her mercy only did it appear in the heart of Madhavendra Puri, who recited it over and over again towards the end of his life.

We should follow in the footsteps of Madhavendra Puri, who attained the ultimate goal of life by constantly reciting this verse. We should always chant it as well, hoping to become recipients of divine grace.

"O My Lord! O merciful master! O master of Mathura! When shall I see You again? Because of My not seeing You, My agitated heart has become unsteady. O most beloved one, what shall I do now?"

**\* \* \* \***

Above Srngara-sthali, atop Govardhana Hill, is the place where Madhavendra Puri performed Gopala's installation ceremony and where the Deity's first temple was constructed.

Gopala's worship continued in this temple for almost two hundred years until Aurangzeb's soldiers threatened to raze the temple and destroy the Deity, at which time Gopala was moved to Nathadvara.

Another site associated with Gopala is on the eastern side of Govinda- kunda where Madhavendra Puri rested, where he received milk from a mysterious gopa, and where he had his wonderful dream.

Before leaving Srngara-sthali, I turned towards Gauri-tirtha and offered my respects, first to Gauri, and then to Candravali, who favours this place in the same way that Radha does Her own lake.

Vaisnavas respect every holy place of Vrndavana, just as they respect every Vraja-vasi. Yet, at times, they show a preference for certain holy places, as they do for certain associates of the Lord.

Therefore, although devotees of Radha and Krsna show great respect for Gauri-tirtha, they do not aspire to reside or serve there, no matter how beautiful and attractive that place may be.

Why are devotees partial to some pastime places and yet avoid others? The appendix entitled "Spiritual Affinity and How It Applies To the Places of Krsna's Pastimes" answers this question.

Followers of Sri Rupa — whatever their eternal forms — are followers of Sri Radha. Rupanugas, therefore, exhibit a transcendental partiality to Radha's personality, service, and pastime places.

And since Candravali is Radha's competitor, rupanugas maintain their distance from all things associated with Candravali, lest their proximity to her be interpreted as a sign of their spiritual allegiance.

Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami showed the extreme limit of aloofness from Candravali by refusing buttermilk because it had been brought in a leaf cup from Sakhi-sthali, Candravali's residence.

Devotees who have not reached the perfectional stage of pure devotional service (prema-bhakti) should not imitate the kind of transcendental aversion shown by Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami.

Nevertheless, devotees at every stage of Krsna consciousness should know that devotional service is meant to have direction. The more advanced the devotee, the more specific that direction becomes.

With these thoughts fresh in my mind I sat down and, while faithfully chanting the names of Radha and Krsna, recalled one of Their humorous pastimes at Gauri-tirtha.

The full moon day during the month of Sravana is called Sravana-purnima, and because it is popularly considered an auspicious day for lovers, it is also known as Saubhagya-purnima.

Early on that day, Radha's husband Abhimanyu appeared at Paurnamasi's cottage in a typically angry mood. His usual anger was, however, mixed with an undercurrent of newfound determination.

Paurnamasi greeted Jatila's unfortunate son by enquiring, "My dear boy! Why have you come here?" To which Abhimanyu replied, "I'm here to ask your permission to take my wife Radha to Mathura."

Paurnamasi was shocked. She thought, "If Radha had not descended along with Vraja, this entire creation — devoid of divine love — would have been useless. Indeed, there can be no Vraja without Her."

Paurnamasi asked, "Why would you want to take Radha away from Vraja?" Abhimanyu answered, "Govardhana Malla informed me that my wife has been carrying on with Krsna in an intimate way.

"Therefore I've been advised to leave Vrndavana and take my impressionable wife beyond the grasp of that libertine prince whom no one in this kingdom seems willing or able to control."

Paurnamasi placed her hand tenderly on Abhimanyu's arm and said, "Son! This is a trick. Kamsa— king of Mathura and Govardhana Malla's good friend — has hatched this plan to steal away your wife."

But Abhimanyu's mother and sister had also poisoned his mind against Krsna. Despite Paurnamasi's attempt, Abhimanyu was adamant— he would take Radha to the supposed shelter of Mathura.

Still hoping to reverse this unfortunate turn of events, Paurnamasi hit on an idea. "I shall give you my blessings to move to Mathura if you can catch Radha and Krsna red-handed," she said.

"If they are lovers — which I don't believe — then They will certainly meet today, on Saubhagya-purnima." She had captured Abhimanyu's imagination. Now all she had to do was quickly come up with a plan.

"I know that you have long wanted Radha to worship goddess Gauri with the same dedication that Candravali does, hoping that you might attain a wealth of cows comparable to Govardhana Malla's herd.

"Today I shall initiate your wife into the worship of Gauri-devi. I shall instruct Radha to worship the goddess by her lake, Gauri-kunda, so that you may also become the master of millions of cows.

"If what you say is true, Krsna will certainly come there to meet your wife. If you can personally catch Them together, then I will not only bless your journey to Mathura, but I will help pack your belongings."

Abhimanyu felt very encouraged by Paurnamasi's support. He immediately solicited Candravali's friend Padma to spy on Gauri-tirtha and inform him if she spotted Krsna in the area.

Ever eager to eliminate Radha — Candravali's rival in the contest for Krsna's affections — Padma thought that Abhimanyu's plans to move house to Mathura sounded like a perfect coup in her mistress's favour.

As promised, Paurnamasi taught Radha the mantras for worshipping Gauri. But she also made a prediction that would soon displease Abhimanyu: Radha would attain Her heart's desire at Gauri-kunda.

Before long, parrot spies informed Krsna that Radha would go to Gauri-tirtha, and so He made fitting arrangements for the gopas to herd their cows along the eastern side of Govardhana Hill.

When Krsna, Balarama, and the gopas arrived near Aniyora, on the plea of wanting to see the beauty of the forest around Sankarsana- kunda, Krsna left them, His heart set on romantic adventure.

Since the summer rains had receded, the forest surrounding Sankarsana-kunda was opulent with blossoming flowers, aromatic herbs, and ripe fruit, its leaves a thousand shades of green.

Alone, Krsna strolled along the banks of the lake, wherein playful fish leaped and waterfowl swam. Its calm waters reflected the rain-spent clouds overhead, their colour once likened unto His own.

Krsna watched a gentle breeze upon the water blow a blue lotus into a clump of golden lotuses. He thought, "This favourable omen must surely mean that today I will meet a certain golden-hued gopi."

While walking through the forest of asoka trees between Sankarsana-kunda and Gauri-kunda, Krsna acknowledged the greetings of its many birds and returned those greetings with a song from His flute.

Meanwhile, Padma had cleverly deduced that if Paumamasi had sent Radha to Gauri-tirtha, Krsna would come there as well, so she schemed to put Candravali on the path between Krsna and Radha.

Krsna was walking along the easterly path when He heard voices behind Him. He turned around hoping to see a certain group of gopis, but was crestfallen to find instead another group, headed by Padma.

**\* \* \* \***

Disappointed, Krsna leaned on His cow-herding stick and muttered, "Perhaps I misread the omen of My friend the lotus. I yearned to meet Radha, but Candravali has come to Me instead."

Candravali's face blushed with shyness as her friend Padma held her hand and spoke to Krsna, "I just overheard You say something about the friend of the lotus, and here is my friend Candravali."

Krsna looked at beautiful Candravali and thought, "She is surely no substitute for Radharani, but because Candravali's love for Me is pure and strong, how can I help but make her happy?"

Krsna's mind was filled with thoughts of Sri Radha, but He openly glorified Candravali's beauty and her fidelity to Him. Then He proceeded to guide her through the solitary forest.

Afraid that His beloved Radha may have already reached Gauri-tirtha by another path, Krsna directed Candravali and her friends towards Sankarsana-kunda, whose splashing waves wash away all sins.

At the base of a huge kadamba tree Krsna said, "Look! Glorified by the songs of bumblebee poets and fanned by the camara whisks of surabhi cows' tails, this regal kadamba stands with crown held high."

Just as Krsna was saying this, He caught sight of Lalita and Vrnda. They were walking towards Him from the direction of Gauri-tirtha and were furtively whispering to each other.

Candravali and her friends noticed that Krsna's eyes had suddenly become restless and His face flushed. They turned and saw the pair of gopis approaching, eliciting caustic remarks from Padma.

As Lalita and Vrnda approached Candravali's entourage, the rival gopi-camps exchanged icy glances and fiery words that resulted in a virtual tug-of-war, with Krsna at its centre.

The thinly-veiled hostility between Candravali's and Radha's friends quickly escalated into a full blown verbal war, which Krsna tried to mediate with equivocal language meant to pacify both sides.

For example, when Padma sarcastically volunteered to let Krsna go to wherever Radha was waiting and abandon Candravali, who may not be as qualified as Radha to associate with Him, Krsna cleverly replied:

"O splendidly beautiful friend! I vow that I have no love for any proud or crooked gopi who even bears the fragrance of Srimati Radharani, what then to speak of Radha Herself."

But to Lalita and Vrnda, Krsna's words meant, "O splendidly beautiful friend! I vow that I love the jubilant gopis who bear the fragrance of Radha, but I have no love for Radha's enemies."

By appearing to take both sides, however, Krsna hardly reconciled the tensions caused by their natural differences — Candra's conservative and Lalita's liberal dispositions — but fuelled them instead.

The feud between the gopis could have continued indefinitely had not Candravali's grandmother Karala inexplicably appeared, shaking her cane, hobbling, and shrieking, "Stop, scoundrel! Stop!"

Krsna was completely caught off guard. And with Giriraja to the left, Govardhana Malla herding cows to the right, an exposed pasture behind Him, and cruel old Karala in front, He had nowhere to hide.

Taking full advantage of the element of surprise, Karala condemned Krsna for being a black snake swallowing the chastity of pious girls and Candravali for being a hussy thirsting after Krsna's bimba lips.

Lalita shrewdly diverted Karala's attention from Krsna by blaming Padma, saying, "O noble lady! This meeting should not be blamed on Krsna or Candra but rather on the matchmaker, Padma."

Padma cast a fierce glance Lalita's way but was forced to retreat when Karala threatened the young gopi with a walking stick and accosted her with harsh words, "O maidservant! O unchaste woman!

"In your eagerness to assume a new occupation as matchmaker you have become expert at breaking up the homes of pious people, but how will you escape from my stick, you home wrecker?"

Not easily daunted, Padma replied, "O lady! I do not understand why, when I have simply followed your orders to take this girl to Govardhana, you are now so displeased with me."

Padma continued as if struck by a sudden realisation, "Oh! Now I understand! You wanted me to take Candravali to her husband, Govardhana Malla, not to Govardhana Hill. Please forgive me!"

Now it was Karala who was taken aback. Temporarily pacified by Padma's false explanation, she took Candravali's hand and grunted, "O housewife who goes wandering in the forest! Come with me!"

As Candra unwillingly left, stealing wishful glances at Krsna, Padma threatened Lalita, "You just wait. Just wait. I will pay you back! I will go to Jatila and tell her a thing or two about you, you matchmaker!"

With that, Padma marched off in a huff, as Krsna smiled at Lalita. Vrnda-devi handed Him two campaka flowers and said, "Radha sends You these two blossoming flowers from Gauri-tirtha."

Krsna accepted the gifts, but He had begun to worry that His friends might suddenly appear in search of Him. "Let Me briefly console My friends and cows," He said, "and I will meet you two by Gauri's lake."

The two girls made their way back to Gauri-tirtha, but instead of going directly to the lake, they hid in a nearby grove hoping to secretly observe the unchaperoned meeting of the divine couple.

They could see Radha sitting upon a platform beneath a kadamba tree. Lotus flowers decorated Her delicate ears, swinging braid, and right hand. Her beauty put the goddess of fortune to shame.

A hedonist would think Srimati Radharani to be beauty personified, a moralist would think Her to be the glory of transcendental virtue personified, but what would a Vaisnava think?

A pure-hearted Vaisnava, situated safely beyond the modes of material nature and truly attached to Krsna's service, would recognise Her for what She is: the pinnacle of transcendental love personified.

Radha could hear Krsna's honey-sweet flute-song, so charming to the surabhi cows, and She could see Him leaning against His stick, which resembled a sceptre that confirmed His position as the king of gopas.

As wind-blown branches obstructed Her view of Krsna, Radha's head moved this way and that. She drank with Her eyes the intoxicating liqueur of Krsna's beauty, like an insatiable drunkard drinks his wine.

From Hari's neck hung a knee-length garland of kadamba flowers. The flute in His hands shimmered in the afternoon sun. Two flower bud earrings dangled from His earlobes.

Freshly drawn red tilaka graced His forehead. His black hair glowed with a bluish sheen, igniting Radharani's heart with the fire of passionate love, spreading its florid hue to all Her limbs.

Radha watched as the peacock named Tandavika spread his tail feathers and danced for the pleasure of Syamasundara, a monsoon cloud of nectar and the cynosure of Yasoda's motherly love.

Reassured that all was well with His friends and cows, Krsna headed towards Gauri-tirtha and His beloved. As He approached, Srimati Radharani hid Herself in a grove of madhavi vines.

Krsna arrived at the kunda, but Radha was nowhere to be seen. Telltale footprints, however, led Him to Her hiding place. Stealthily, He approached from behind and covered Her eyes with His hands.

Although at first hesitant, Radha, by the influence of Krsna's touch, was carried away on waves of bliss and shared amorous pastimes with Her beloved. This sight inspired Vrnda to say to Lalita:

"As a little child She played in the dust of the courtyard, Her ears newly pierced and Her hair, no longer than a cow's ear, tied in a bunch upon her Head with a coloured ribbon.

"Where did that Radharani so expertly learn the amorous arts that just now empowered Her to defeat the unconquerable transcendental Cupid of Vrndavana, Sri Krsna?"

**\* \* \* \***

At the completion of Their pastimes, Radha and Krsna timidly left the madhavi grove, took Their places in a bench beneath a desire tree, and acted as if They had been sitting there all the while.

Seeing that their time had come, Lalita and Vrnda left their hiding place. After briefly exchanging pleasantries, Lalita went to organise Radha's evening floral arrangements, and Vrnda stayed behind.

Radha accompanied Lalita a short distance down the path, giving Krsna a chance to solicit Vrnda's help in teasing His beloved. He wanted to increase the intensity of Their mutual affection.

Krsna planned to make Radharani angry with Him and thus enjoy Her sulky beauty. Then, after winning Her over once again, He could savour the fresh taste of Their reunion.

When Radha returned, Krsna intentionally provoked Her. He said, "My dear Candra" and then feigned embarrassment for having accidentally addressed Her by the name of Her rival.

Unsuspecting, Radha fell for the bait. With the forest as Her audience, She called out in defiance, "Alas! How is it possible that after hearing this name My two ears do not explode?"

Krsna simulated an attempt to soothe His beloved, but when He intentionally called Her "moon-faced," He successfully stoked the embers of Her disappointment into flames of angry indignation.

Eyebrows arched like Cupid's bow in battle, lotus eyes agitated like those of a captured animal's, and Her general countenance contorted by outrage, Radha's face delighted Krsna to no end.

Krsna lifted the edge of the veil that now concealed Radha's face and said with mock-innocence, "O radiant one! Don't misinterpret My affectionate words and sour the sweetness of Our pastimes."

Incensed, Radha slapped Krsna's hand and recoiled from Her malefactor. "Vrnda!" She fumed. "How can I tolerate being ridiculed by Candravali's pet deer, who sees His mistress even in Me!"

Vrnda knew that trying to appease Radha now would only inflame Her mistress's anger and serve Krsna's intended purpose, so she advised, "O friend, Radha! You are the most intelligent of the gopis.

"Please don't suddenly, and without reason, chase away with the fearsome staff of jealous anger Your own black pet deer, Krsna. He is, really and truly, Your faithful lover."

Now even angrier than before She had heard Vrnda's counsel, Radha turned on Her heels and left both Vrnda and Krsna behind, saying as She went, "I will not remain here to be the puppet of a debauchee."

Krsna's smile was wide with mischief, and His eyes danced with glee. He approached Vrnda in private and requested her to provide clothes and cosmetics by which He could masquerade as a young girl.

In confidence, He disclosed His plan to enter the temple of Gauri as Vrnda's little sister then ran off to don His disguise while Vrnda went in search of Radha, whom she soon found complaining to Her friends.

Lalita and Visakha, however, refused to commiserate with their friend; instead, they scolded Her for indulging in a sense of false pride that had cheated Her out of Krsna's company.

Lalita said, "Everyone knows that Krsna cannot think of another woman in Your presence." To which Vrnda added, "And now that You have berated Him, He will be reluctant to meet with You again."

Krsna's absence and Her friends' criticism made Radha remorseful. She repeatedly sighed as Lalita asked, "Where is Krsna now?" And Vrnda answered, "Disclosing His sorrow to Nikunjavidya."

The other girls looked at each other and then asked in unison, "Who is Nikunjavidya?" Laughing aloud, Vrnda replied, "Just see your ignorance! Who is Nikunjavidya, you ask."

As the three withered from embarrassment, Vrnda continued, "Nikunjavidya is the goddess of Bhandiravana, my younger sister, and the guardian of the treasure chest of Krsna's jewel-like secrets."

Hearing of Nikunjavidya's confidential relationship with Krsna, Visakha ordered Radha, "Go to Nikunjavidya at once. And in front of Krsna beg Her to help repair the rift between You and Him."

Taking heart, Radha hurried with Her friends to Gauri's temple. But when She looked in, She did not see Krsna, only the deity and a girl making earrings from peacock feathers in an otherwise empty temple.

After bowing to Gauri from the doorway, Radha turned to Vrnda and said, "Your sister is extraordinarily beautiful!" The other gopis agreed, "The source of her beauty must be Krsna's great trust in her."

Radhika hesitated in shyness and whispered to them, "Even though we have never met, I still feel very eager to speak with her." Just then Nikunjavidya's delicate voice drifted from the temple.

"Sister Vrnda! It seems that Radha does not recognise Me even though I have seen Her a thousand times." Spontaneously drawn to the young goddess, Radha approached the temple door and asked:

"O knower of the groves (Nikunjavidya)! Where is He who lives in the groves (Nikurijanagara)?" The goddess did not reply, and then Vrnda gave Radha a hint, "Show my sister more affection."

Radha entered the temple and sat beside Nikunjavidya. The two girls smiled affectionately at each other, but the goddess was mysteriously silent. Noting this, Vrnda again prompted Radha.

With some encouragement, finally Radha was able to fully confide in Nikunjavidya. She begged, "O goddess of Bhandiravana! Bestow your mercy upon Me so that I may again meet and serve Krsna."

Still Nikunjavidya failed to respond, until Radha extended the soft creepers of Her arms and embraced the silent goddess. With that touch, the goddess suddenly became extremely responsive.

Lalita and Visakha were happy to see Vrnda's sister return Radha's embrace. But when Nikunjavidya began to kiss and caress Radha in a way that seemed manly, they became slightly alarmed.

Still helping to maintain Krsna's ruse, Vrnda reassured the gopis that such expressions of affection were not uncommon for goddesses, but Radha had another idea. "Deception!" She cried.

Radha disentangled Herself from Her new friend's embrace. With a furrowed brow and trembling voice She scoffed, "A fine trickster You are!" Vrnda could barely suppress her laughter.

Just as Lalita and Visakha also came to realise that Nikunjavidya must be someone other than the goddess of Bhandiravana, a terrible sound echoed in the courtyard of the temple.

**\* \* \* \***

Look my son!" howled a jackal-like voice. "Perched atop that stone lion is Krsna's peacock, Tandavika. And wafting in the air is the smell of musk and aguru, the unmistakable scent of that rogue Krsna."

Jatila hobbled out of the forest with the help of her son, Abhimanyu. Their unexpected arrival shocked everyone. But much more than shocked, Radha and Krsna were literally frozen in fear.

"Mother!" they could hear Abhimanyu reply. "You are right. Krsna must be here. A short while ago I saw Balarama enter Nandagrama with His cows and friends but without Krsna. He must be here."

Jatila sniffed the air like a dog and added, "It's just as Padma warned me. Do you smell that sweet fragrance? There is someone with that reckless libertine — no doubt my wanton, unchaste daughter-in-law."

"Mother!" snapped Abhimanyu. "To hell with Paurnamasi's advice! First I will tear Maharaja Nanda's son limb from limb, and then at once I will take Radharani away to Mathura."

The gopis frantically signalled for Krsna to escape, but there was only one entrance to the small temple. If Krsna tried to leave, He would rush straight into Abhimanyu's stout arms.

"Outlaw! Scoundrel!" roared Abhimanyu at the top of his lungs. "I have caught You red-handed." He burst into the temple in a rage, pushing the gopis out of his way and causing Radha to faint in fear.

When Abhimanyu found his wife lying lifeless before an effulgent young girl, with Krsna nowhere to be seen, he blurted out, "Who is that girl?" Thinking quickly, Lalita replied, "It is Gauri-devi herself."

Jatila had tottered in behind her son. Now also struck with wonder, she pointed a bony finger at Krsna and demanded, "Who is that fair-complexioned girl who illuminates this temple with unusual beauty?"

Parroting Lalita, Abhimanyu answered, "She is clearly the beautiful queen of Lord Siva, goddess Gauri, emerged from the deity form. And now Radha lies before the living deity, prostrate in prayer."

Mother and son offered respects to the goddess as Visakha explained, "At Paurnamasi's request, and for the sake of Abhimanyu — Her dear, dear husband — Radha worshipped goddess Gauri.

"Devi soon appeared, but when She told Radharani why She could not bless Abhimanyu with a million cows, your chaste and loving wife fell to the ground shouting, 'Mercy! Mercy!'"

Frightened, Abhimanyu asked to know the cause of his wife's distress, to which Visakha solemnly replied, "My mouth will not repeat what my ears have heard." Pointing at Krsna she added, "Ask the devi"

Krsna knew He would have to keep up His ruse if He wanted to save Himself from certain bodily harm and His beloved from public insult, and so in the voice of a goddess He said to Abhimanyu:

"O proud Abhimanyu! Much as I would like to fulfil your chaste wife's request, I cannot transgress the service of Lord Siva, to whom Kamsa is destined to offer your head in sacrifice two evenings hence."

Jatila shrieked and wailed, "O goddess, have mercy! Let my son live!" The horrible sound roused Radharani, who quickly assessed the scene and also called out, "Have mercy! Let him live!"

With the gravity of a goddess, Krsna replied, "Radha! I have already told You. It will be very difficult to avert this fate." Radha rose to Her knees, bowed repeatedly, and made a show of plaintive prayer.

"O maintainer of the gopis!" said Radha. "Since nothing is impossible for You, please be merciful to this person. As You are never separated from Your beloved, please let Me never be separated from Mine."

As Abhimanyu and Jatila went on prattling for pity, Krsna continued to speak in the voice of a female celestial, "O Radha! Blessed milkmaid! You have conquered Me. You have won My heart.

"With the ropes of pure devotion You have bound Me, whom great sages and ascetics cannot attain. Thus You will receive the fulfilment of Your desire and Your dearly beloved shall be spared.

"However," concluded Krsna, unable to suppress His smile, "if for even one day You fail to worship Me here at Gauri-kunda, that day shall be the last for Your foolish husband."

"O kind goddess," interjected Abhimanyu, "I shall never take Radha to Mathura, instead She shall stay always in Vrndavana and thus worship You here daily, without fail. This is my solemn vow."

Jatila was elated by this turn of events. Clumsily embracing Radha, She blessed her daughter-in-law by saying, "O virtuous girl who brings happiness to Vraja. I am saved! I am saved!"

To make the most of this golden opportunity, Krsna also addressed Abhimanyu sternly, "O gopa! Do not forget that Radha is the source of your good fortune, and never lose faith in Her."

To propitiate the goddess, Abhimanyu tried to divest his family of the burden they bore from persecuting Radha. "O devi!" he stammered. "Sometimes, just to tease my mother, Subala dresses up like Radha.

"When envious and ignorant people see the gopa Subala in disguise, cavorting with Krsna, they gossip about some preposterous scenario involving my wife and thus spoil the name of our entire family."

"Fortunately," Lalita interrupted, "your faith in Radha never wavers." Blushing, Abhimanyu whispered, "Mother! Let us return home and stop the servants before they cart our household goods to Mathura."

Abhimanyu and Jatila made a hasty exit. Once they were out of earshot, Krsna and the girls burst out in relieved laughter, mimicking the ridiculous exchange between Jatila, Abhimanyu, and Gauri-devi.

Just then Paurnamasi arrived. As the architect of the entire pastime, she contributed to the drama by humorously reiterating her earlier instruction that Radha worship Gauri with great devotion.

Radha and Krsna left the temple as Their girlfriends praised Krsna for having turned the tables on Abhimanyu, thus averting the great calamity of having to witness Radha suffer in separation from Krsna.

Still dressed as Nikunjavidya, but speaking in His own voice, Krsna said, "The sharp edge of Radha's anxiety has been removed, and Her friends and maidservants are similarly relieved and delighted."

Krsna turned to Paurnamasi, eager to please the guru of the Vraja- vasis. He touched her feet and said, "O lady possessed of all transcendental opulences! May I do something to please you?"

Crying tears of joy, Paurnamasi answered, "O friend of Vraja! From You who are imbued with every divine quality, I beg that You and Radha eternally enjoy amorous pastimes in the groves of this forest."

Krsna and His associates never leave Vrndavana, so Paurnamasi's plea was much like Narada's request that Krsna's devotees' taste for serving Him never be satiated; it already was, and will be, ever thus.

Silent, Krsna looked at the white-haired sorceress of Vraja-mandala as if to say, "Goddess! It is already so. Don't you have any desires that are as yet unfulfilled?" Paurnamasi continued:

"O Krsna! You are the springtime that makes the madhvika vine of Radha blossom with happiness. May persons in whose hearts devotion for You has already sprouted and who open their ears to and —

"Worship drops of the pure and splendid nectar ocean of Your amorous pastimes in Vrndavana, attain that kingdom of transcendental sweetness and swim in its waves of pure love."

Krsna at once proclaimed, "So be it!" He removed His disguise, donned His gopa dress, and hurried off to Nandagrama, where His parents were anxiously wondering about His whereabouts.

Such are the pastimes at Gauri-kunda. Before moving on, I prayed to the Gauri-devi who allegedly appeared from "her" deity that I may reap the rewards of the blessing for which Paumamasi appealed.

O gentle Vaisnavas! When you come to Gauri-tirtha, do not let pride or neglect rob you of the chance to beg for that which we all desire, a place in the loving pastimes of Radha and Syamasundara.

## Chapter 13 - Surabhivana to Airavata-kunda

I turned away from Gauri-tirtha and continued along the *parikrama* path, keeping Surabhivana and its welcoming ambience to my right. This expansive forest stretches all the way to Govinda-kunda.

Surabhi and Indra bathed Krsna in this forest. The Lord’s bath water formed a pool now called Govinda-kunda, where Krsna was crowned the indisputable king of the cows — Govinda.

To pay tribute to this momentous pastime and to invoke a vision of Govinda-kunda, Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami prays,

*nicaih praudha-bhayat svayam sura-patih padau vidhtyeha yaih —*

*Svar-ganga-salilais chakara surabhi-dvarabhisekotsavam*  
*govindasya navam gavdm adhipata rajye sphutam kautukat tair yat pradurabhut sada sphuratu tad govinda-kundam drsoh*

**“Humbled by great fear, Indra grasped Lord Krsna’s lotus feet and, accompanied by a** ***surabhi*** **cow, performed the coronation festival of bathing them with the water of the celestial Ganges.**

**“In this way Lord Krsna’s kingship over the cows became splendidly manifest. I pray that Govinda-kunda, the lake created by that ceremonial bath, may eternally appear before my eyes.”**

This pastime has already been described in great detail in a previous chapter. Devotees who come to this sacred place would do well to read it and immerse themselves in the nectar *of govinda-lila.*

Like Radha-kunda, Kusuma-sarovara, and other holy *tirthas,* Govinda- kunda is eternally related to Govardhana Hill, with spiritual forms in both the manifest and unmanifest realms.

Just as the *gopis* apparently created Radha-kunda with their bangles, although the *kunda* exists eternally, Govinda-kunda appeared from the milk that bathed Krsna, although that *kunda* is also eternal.

In the years immediately following Krsna’s coronation *abhiseka,* up until the disappearance of the Lord, Govinda-kunda continued to display the full sweetness and beauty of its eternal form.

Govinda-kunda’s eternal form has beaches decorated with *cintamani* pebbles and made soft by diamond dust. Ornate golden bathing *ghatas* grace its four sides, and marble pavilions its four corners.

Surrounded by the dense forest of Surabhivana, groves of desire trees filled with buzzing bees, and gardens laden with fruits and flowers, Govinda-kunda is well concealed from the eyes of materialists.

Because it is tucked away behind the tail feathers of Giriraja’s peacock form, Vasudeva engaged Bhaguri Muni to conduct a sacrifice there to protect Krsna and Balarama from Kamsa’s atrocities.

And because Govinda-kunda is so secluded, Radha and Krsna, while boating along the Manasa-ganga, would dock at the lake to enjoy its waters and surrounding gardens without concern for intruders.

Although the waters of Govinda-kunda are the essence of pure devotional service in the form of Krsna’s *caranamrta,* when Radha and Krsna sport there, that essence is refined to quintessence.

Just as cream, the essence of milk, when churned becomes butter, the water of sublime Govinda-kunda, when churned by the pastimes of Radha and Krsna, becomes liquid *prema.*

A materialist who even casually touches its waters is relieved of sin, hut a devotee who bathes there carefully, meditating on Krsna, gains a spiritual form qualified to serve the divine couple.

In truth, the only difference between the water of the river Yamuna and that of Govinda-kunda is that the former appears blackish and the latter whitish, at times even tasting like milk.

Because the liquid touch of Govinda-kunda gives the benefit of one hundred fire sacrifices, Vasudeva selected this spot thinking that the potency of Bhaguri’s mantras would be amplified a hundredfold.

Even today, pure and attentive devotees can hear Indra’s prayers to Krsna echoing there, what to speak of the anthem sung by the birds at sunset: “O Krsna! You are the king of the cows, Govinda!”

After circumambulating Govardhana, Madhavendra Puri bathed at the southern *ghata* of Govinda-kunda and then sat beneath a nearby tree to rest for the night, a spot now known as his *bhajana-kutira.*

While Madhavendra Puri chanted Hare Krsna in ecstatic absorption, an unknown cowherd boy came with a pot of milk, placed it before the saint, and with a smile spoke to him in a very sweet way.

The identity of that mysterious boy and how He led Madhavendra Puri to the Deity of Gopala in the bushes of Srnigara-sthali has already been described in the previous chapter.

Devotees who visit Madhavendra Puri’s *bhajana-kutira* pray to him — the seed of the desire tree of pure devotional service — for the blessing of loving devotion to Radha and Govinda.

Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu personally brought the desire tree of pure devotional service to earth. As its gardener He also sowed the seed of that tree and sprinkled upon it the water of His will.

It is said that Madhavendra Puri, who eternally resides in Goloka as a desire tree, is the storehouse of all devotional service to Krsna. It was in him that the seed of devotional service first fructified.

Before Madhavendra Puri appeared, the prescribed methods of won ship in his disciplic succession, the Madhava *sampradaya,* were marked by ritualistic ceremonies with hardly a tinge of love for God.

Madhavendra Puri was the first person in that spiritual lineage to exhibit the symptoms of love of God, and his foremost disciple was Isvara Puri, the spiritual master of Caitanya Mahaprabhu.

At the last stage of his life, infirm Madhavendra Puri was unable to tend to his own bodily needs. Out of affection, Isvara Puri cleaned his guru’s body without ever thinking that it was material.

Always chanting the Hare Krsna *maha-mantra* and reminding Sri Madhavendra Puri during the last stage of his life about the pastimes of Krsna, Isvara Puri, of all his god brothers, gave the best service.

Extremely pleased with the kindness of his disciple, Madhavendra Puri thus blessed Isvara Puri with the words, “My dear boy! I can only pray to Krsna that He will be pleased with you.”

By the grace of his spiritual master, Isvara Puri consequently became a great devotee in the ocean of love of Godhead, and Caitanya Mahaprabhu later accepted Isvara Puri as His own spiritual master.

To teach others how to remain qualified to receive the mercy of the spiritual master, Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu visited the birthplace of Isvara Puri and collected some earth from the site.

The Lord treated this earth with great care and reverence. In fact, He would eat a small portion of it daily, thus inaugurating a tradition followed even today by devotees who visit that place.

Let us also emulate this attitude of submission to our gurus and *acaryas,* which Srila Prabhupada explains in the following way, “It is by the mercy of the spiritual master that one becomes perfect.”

**\* \* \* \***

After offering prayers to Madhavendra Puri, Isvara Puri, and Srila Prabhupada, I strolled through the area around Govinda-kunda in order to view some of the many sacred sites found there.

Behind Madhavendra Puri’s *bhajana-kutira* resides a self-manifest replica of the Gopala Deity discovered by Madhavendra Puri. The original Deity is now worshipped in far away Nathadvara.

A little to the south is a small hill called Indra-tila. There, as Krsna sat on a stone throne, Indra, Surabhi, and other demigods bathed Him with ingredients they had brought from heaven.

Milk from the *surabhi* cows and water from the celestial Ganges were both offered during Krsna’s *abhiseka.* The milk flowed west to form Govinda-kunda and the water flowed north into Manasa-ganga.

A devotee who faithfully follows the process of pure devotion to Krsna and who gazes upon this hill is sure to attain freedom from past sinful reaction, success in *sadhana-bhakti,* and elevation to Goloka.

Between the western bank of Govinda-kunda and Govardhana Hill is a large Govardhana-sila known as the Jhulana-sila because a picture of Radha and Krsna on a swing is visible on the stone.

Govardhana Hill is a silent witness to the pastimes of Radha and Krsna, and Krsna and Balarama. For the benefit of faithful devotees, Giriraja manifests images of Their pastimes on his stones.

Near the *bhajana-kutira* are the footprints and handprints of Krsna, as well as the hoofprint of Surabhi, all left on Govardhana’s body when his stones turned soft due to his feelings of ecstatic bliss.

After witnessing the *abhiseka* of Gopala, Govardhana Hill became so overwhelmed with emotion that he began to melt in ecstasy, as he often does when he hears the sound of Krsna’s flute.

Krsna noticed that His feet were sinking into soft stone and comforted Giriraja with the sound of His sweet voice and the touch of His right hand. Those marks remain as evidence of these pastimes.

Ordinary places like Kurujangala, Pancala, and Surasena became holy places by the mere touch of Krsna’s lotus feet. Who can imagine the sanctity of Giriraja, who bears full imprints of the Lord’s soles?

In Surabhivana on the northern bank of Govinda-kunda Madana-mohana enjoys pastimes with His eight principal *gopis,* pastimes that are well known to the parrots, peacocks, and cuckoos of the area.

On Govinda-kunda’s eastern bank stand Radha and Govinda, whom Vajra originally installed as the presiding Deities of this area. Lord Brahma also worships these Deities for creative inspiration.

"I worship Govinda, the primeval Lord and first progenitor. He tends cows that fulfil all desires, and resides in an abode surrounded by millions of purpose trees and built with spiritual gems.

“He is always served with great affection by Radha, the embodiment of His ecstatic potency, and by hundreds of thousands of *gopis,* embodiments of the extensions of Radha’s bodily form.”

Between Govinda-kunda and the *parikrama-marga* is Nipa-kunda. The area that surrounds it is called Drona-ksetra because Krsna once served yogurt to the cowherd boys in cup-shaped leaves from its trees.

While their cows grazed peacefully in the shelter of Surabhivana, the *gopas* lay on the banks of Nipa-kunda, repeatedly wishing aloud for yogurt to help combat the effects of the stifling midday heat.

Krsna was reclining in the shade listening to the rueful complaints of His friends when the sound of girlish voices and tinkling anklets from the nearby forest entered His ears, bringing Him to His feet.

Peering over the top of a magnolia bush, Krsna saw Radharani with Her coterie of *gopis* coming down the road towards Him carrying what surely must have been be pots of yogurt on their heads.

Krsna didn’t waste a moment. Calling out the names of His friends, like Subala and Sridama, Krsna jumped out from behind the bush and into the cowherd girls’ path, daringly brandishing His stick.

The cowherd boys quickly crowded around behind Krsna, halting the girls in their tracks and greedily eyeing their pots of yogurt with the same keen interest that Krsna was eyeing their youthful beauty.

Indeed, Krsna was so mesmerised by the treasures just barely obscured by Radha’s veil that He forgot the plan of action until He heard Subala call out, “Hey, girls! What have you got in those pots?”

When the *gopis* simply turned their backs in response, Krsna came to His senses. He struck the ground with His stick and announced, “Just a minute! You can neither pass nor turn away without paying a toll!”

To which a chorus of *gopis* replied, “Your greedy mind is obsessed with taxing those over whom You have no jurisdiction. Let’s see how much tax You collect after You are locked up in Karhsa’s prison.”

Flexing His muscles, Krsna challenged, “Who cares for Kamsa, or his prison! You go ahead and complain to him. That would only give Me an excuse to go to Mathura and kill him sooner than I’d planned.”

Roused by Krsna’s bravado, the cowherd boys blew their horns, shook their sticks, and filled the valleys of Govardhana Hill with cheers and shouts of “Down with the king!” and “Kill Kamsa!”

In the midst of this commotion Sridama tactfully informed Krsna that the pots contained yogurt his sister had prepared the night before, yogurt too good to be sold to strangers at the market.

Krsna stepped forward and deftly lifted a pot of yogurt off the nearest *gopis* head. The cowherd boys followed suit, each claiming their prize with surprisingly little resistance from the girls.

Radha had made the yogurt especially for Krsna and His friends, and She had enlisted the help of Her girlfriends to carry it to the market knowing well that by walking past Giriraja they would be ambushed.

As the boys triumphantly placed the pots on the ground and prepared to devour their contents in full view of their victims, Lalita-devi said, “Krsna may be fearless amongst His friends in this secluded forest.

“But after we report this theft to the king, we will see how bold He is surrounded by powerful soldiers in the city of Mathura.” With Radharani in the lead, the *gopis* then left for their homes.

While Krsna’s and His friends’ mouths watered over their ill-gotten gains, servants like Raktaka and Patraka collected large cup-shaped leaves from the nearby *kadamba* trees.

They sat in the cooling shade of a giant *kadamba* tree, very pleased that the leaves of the area could serve as cups *(dona)* from which to eat yogurt; thus, the boys named that sacred place Drona-tirtha.

From that time on the cowherd boys would come to the banks of Nipa-kunda and eat the food they brought from home out of the leaf-cups provided by the saintly trees of Surabhivana.

Devotees who come to this place, give yogurt in charity, or drink yogurt from a leaf-cup and then bow down to offer respects to Giriraja, will soon return to Goloka from where they will never fall.

**\* \* \* \***

A little further along the *parikrama* path I offered my respects to two places closely connected to each other: Dhuka Dauji on the ridge of Govardhana Hill and the Mukuta- sila just below at the hill’s base.

The relationship between these two places is described in the following pastime: Lord Balarama was once tending cows on the top of Govardhana Hill. Feeling tired, He sat upon a large stone to rest.

As He scanned the horizon, Balarama noticed movement in the distance near Candra-sarovara. In order to get a better view He crouched down to peer beneath the branches of the trees.

The movement He saw turned out to be His younger brother, Krsna, dancing with the *gopis.* Balarama rarely witnessed His brother’s *rasa* dances, so He was not about to pass up a chance to see this one.

Still resting on his haunches, Balarama followed His brother’s movements with fascination, being careful not to let Krsna or the *gopis* catch Him peeping through the spaces in the trees.

Even goddess Sarasvati would be unable to describe the transcendental happiness created by the combined sound of lutes, drums, flutes, ankle bells, and many tinkling ornaments.

How then could anyone adequately describe the wonderful singing, graceful hand gestures, sparkling jewels, and beautiful, slender *gopis,* who were being embraced by the dark monsoon cloud, Krsna?

Krsna’s dance steps and graceful movements so absorbed His brother’s consciousness that Lord Balarama’s complexion gradually turned from autumn cloud white to rain cloud black.

And upon closer inspection, Lord Balarama noticed something quite unusual: each and every *gopi* looked strikingly similar in appearance and dress, almost as if they were all the same girl.

Balarama blinked His eyes and looked again; Krsna was not expanding Himself to dance with many *gopis* simultaneously, but Radha and Krsna were both expanding Themselves to dance alone.

This *rasa* dance, which is rarely heard of and even more rarely seen, happens when Radha and Krsna, dissatisfied with being a single couple, expand Themselves to become many dancers at once.

Astonished by what He saw, Balarama leaned forward to get a better look. As He did, His crown toppled from His head and rolled forcefully down the hill, gaining speed until it hit a large stone.

Balarama’s ecstasy had been transmitted to His crown, which upon striking the stone, caused it to melt, leaving behind the impression of a crown, a memorial to Dauji’s spying ways.

The privilege of seeing this unique dance made this mountain lookout a favourite place of Lord Balarama, one He would often revisit, sometimes just to remember this pastime.

As Krsna’s elder brother, Balarama has a quality of love for Krsna mostly fraternal, with hints of servitude and parental affection, but without a trace of amorous love — the *gopis’* singular privilege.

Balarama’s male body facilitates all three of those loving moods with Krsna. In Krsna’s amorous pastimes, however, Krsna is the only male, so His brother has no part to play in that *rasa.*

And because Balarama is Krsna’s elder brother, Radha always treats Balarama with the respect born of a formal relationship, so Balarama remains distant from Krsna’s *madhurya-rasa* and its presiding goddess.

Nevertheless, Balarama, eager to enter into Krsna’s intimate loving pastimes and Radha’s circle of gopi assistants, expands Himself as Ananga-manjari, Radharani’s younger sister.

Taking birth in Varsana, Ananga-manjari shadows Srimati Radharani as little girls often do their elder sisters. As a *manjari,* she serves both Radha and Krsna in the mellow of amorous love.

Anariga-manjari’s complexion is golden and Balarama’s white. She is thirteen years old and He is sixteen, but both dress in clothes the colour of blue lotuses, and both are exceptionally beautiful.

At the centre of Radha-kunda is Anariga-manjari’s lotus-shaped island grove called Svananda-sukhada-kunja. From there she makes arrangements for the divine couple’s midday pastimes.

Thus, by the grace of Radha (Krsna’s pleasure potency), Balarama (Krsna’s potency of existence) enters into the pastimes of amorous love as Ananga-manjari, the essence of transcendental cupidity.

Previous a*caryas* have revealed to us Anariga-manjari’s service to Radha and Krsna and her willingness to appear in the lives of devotees who place themselves under her direct guidance.

Let us offer our obeisances to Radha’s younger sister, Anariga-manjari, whose body overflows with the bliss of the divine couple’s pastimes. Visakha taught Ananga the art of confidential service.

Thakura Bhaktivinoda, who revelled in the waves of ecstasy flooding out from Navadvipa-dhama, has described how his own spiritual perfection blossomed by the grace of Ananga-manjarif:

“While I am in *samadhi,* two wondrous figures will appear before me; I will recognise one to be myself, Kamala-manjari, the eternal assistant of the other, Ananga-manjari, the goddess of my heart.

“Anariga-manjari will introduce me to her companions, assign me to the service of preparing camphor for the divine couple, and reveal to me Their many pastimes within the *dhama,* saying:

‘“Near Sri Pulina is the *rasa-mandala,* where, surrounded by a billion *gopis*, Gopendra-nandana steals the hearts of all by His dancing with Radha, the presiding goddess of the *rasa* dance.

“‘If by great fortune someone sees this dancing, which has no earthly counterpart, they will at once drown in that nectar and will be unable to give up the happiness of that astounding sight!’

“Unable to describe what I have seen, I will lock it in my heart, gaze upon it constantly, and enter into its wonders while humbly serving in my own grove under the direction of the *sakhis.*

“Ananga-manjari will continue to kindly escort me around the *dhama,* showing me Dhira Samira, Vamsivata, and the wide bank of the Yamuna River where we will meet Rupa-manjari.

“When Rupa-manjari asks who I am, my mistress will reply, ‘She is Kamala-manjari, a maidservant of Lalita-sakhi and devoted follower of Sri Gaurariga. Please be merciful to her.’

“Hearing this, Rupa-manjari will lightly touch me with her merciful hand, thereby infusing my being with spontaneous loving devotion and the intense desire to follow in her footsteps.

“Then I, Kamala-manjari— with complexion like lightning, dress sparkling with a star-like pattern, and ornaments decorating every limb — shall fall at Rupa-manjari’s lotus feet, crying.

“After pitifully begging Rupa-manjari for exclusive shelter at Radha’s lotus feet, she and Anariga-manjari will take me to the grove of Lalita-sakhi, who is deeply absorbed in thoughts of service to Radha.

“Again I shall fall at the lotus feet of beautiful Lalita-sakhi, the life and soul of the divine couple, and when she asks Ananga-mahjari about my identity, my mistress will answer, saying:

“‘O friend! This person is a resident of Navadvipa! She begs to become your maidservant and to serve Radha and Krsna by following your instructions. Please look kindly upon her.’

“Pleased, Lalita will tell Ananga-mahjari, ‘Dear friend! Give this girl a grove beside your own. And when you go out to perform service to Radha, take her with you. In time Radha will bless her.’

“Following this order, Ananga-manjari will make me her own maidservant, and when she goes to serve the divine couple she will affectionately and graciously allow me to accompany her.

“While serving the divine couple from a distance, I will occasionally catch a glimpse of Them, and when Radha is finally pleased with me, She will personally order me, saying, ‘Do this!’

“By constant practice I will perfect the service given to me by Sri Radha, and by expertly carrying it out I will please Her and Krsna; thus, hopefully I shall receive some of Their ornaments as a reward.”

Thakura Bhaktivinoda concludes his prayer for a spiritual body and eternal service by remembering Ananga-manjari, by whose grace he is drowning in ecstatic love for Radha and Krsna.

**\* \* \* \***

The beauty of Apsaravana extends from Govinda-kunda all the way to Puncari. This idyllic forest teems with chirping birds and prancing animals. Colourful flowers and fruit hang from its trees.

Each and every tree and creeper in this forest has the power to fulfil all desires, its dust can cleanse misunderstandings from the heart, and the woodland creatures teach the highest truths.

As I walked down the p*arikrama* path towards Puncari, the tail end of Govardhana Hill, I looked to my left at Paitha and again recalled the wonderful pastimes that Krsna had performed there.

When the Samvartaka clouds first began to inundate Vrndavana and its citizens in driving rain, Krsna met His friends at Paitha and revealed His plan to lift Giri Govardhana like a giant umbrella.

Krsna knew that if He lifted Giriraja without giving the *gopas* fair warning, unaware of His infinite strength, they might die from shock at seeing their darling Gopala in such a dangerous predicament.

To that end, Krsna gathered His friends beneath a giant *kadamba* tree and submitted His proposal, which they unanimously rejected as beyond the scope of even Krsna’s supernatural abilities.

At the climax of their debate in the pouring rain, the *gopas* gave Krsna an ultimatum, “If You can bend the great branches of this tree into a circle, then we might believe You can lift Govardhana Hill.”

Without wasting a moment, Kṛṣṇa tied the branches of the tree into a circle (paīṭhā). Then, with the blessings of His friends, He prepared to lift Govardhana Hill by dressing up like a wrestler about to fight.

Amidst a chorus of cheering gopas, Kṛṣṇa entered a nearby cave that extended far under Govardhana. Several moments later, at the very centre of Girirāja’s underbelly, He lifted the hill above His head.

On the bank of Nārāyaṇa-sarovara stands the temple of a four-armed Deity of Kṛṣṇa, Caturbhuja. This temple is constructed on the ruins of the original temple that was demolished by Aurangzeb.

In the back wall of the temple is the entrance to the cave that leads underneath Govardhana Hill. The pūjārīs will not give access to the cave as they say a large snake guards the entranceway.

The lifting of Govardhana took place in the fall of Kṛṣṇa’s seventh year. In the spring of His ninth year Kṛṣṇa made Paīṭhā the site for another sublime pastime — this time with the gopīs and Śrī Rādhā.

Prior to the rāsa dance at Candra-sarovara, Kṛṣṇa left His girlfriends and came to Paīṭhā with a special purpose in mind: to confirm Rādhā’s pre-eminence amongst all other gopīs.

The gopīs followed Kṛṣṇa’s footprints and came to a grove in Paīṭhā where they thought they had found Kṛṣṇa. On closer inspection, however, they realised that they had found Lord Nārāyaṇa instead.

Actually, it was Kṛṣṇa disguised in a four-armed form, but the gopīs could only see Him as Lord Nārāyaṇa, so they simply offered the Lord their respects and asked if He could help them to find Nanda’s son.

The gopīs’ ecstasy increased from feelings of separation as they searched for Kṛṣṇa, but upon finding the four-armed Lord their ecstasy disappeared because they had no attraction to Nārāyaṇa.

This shows that Kṛṣṇa is more attractive than Nārāyaṇa. Kṛṣṇa attracts the mind of the goddess of fortune, but Nārāyaṇa fails to attract the minds of the gopīs; hence, Kṛṣṇa is the original Lord.

This pastime also confirms the Vedic statement, narākṛti paraṁ brahma, “The original feature of the Supreme Absolute Truth is His human-like form” — further evidence of Kṛṣṇa’s greater glory.

Moments after the other gopīs had left, Rādhā arrived on the spot. Impelled by His love for Her, Kṛṣṇa resumed His two-armed form (paīṭhā-gale), firmly establishing the pre-eminence of Śrī Rādhā.

Śrīmatī Rādhārāṇī’s ecstatic feelings of separation from Kṛṣṇa were so strong and Her desire to see Him so intense that Kṛṣṇa was unable to maintain His four-armed form in Her presence.

Kṛṣṇa was able to remain disguised as Lord Nārāyaṇa in the presence of the gopīs, but not in the presence of Rādhārāṇī, consequently Her love for Him is superior to the love of all others.

Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇavas accordingly praise Śrīmatī Rādhārāṇī as the foremost gopī of Vṛndāvana because She surpasses all in beauty, qualities, fortune, and above all else, in love for Kṛṣṇa.

In the mood of Śrī Rādhā, Caitanya Mahāprabhu would enact a similar pastime with the Deity of Ālālanātha in Brahmagiri, which is on the seashore, sixteen miles south of Jagannātha Purī.

The history of Lord Ālālanātha and His connection with Kṛṣṇa’s four-armed form at Paīṭhā is described in the appendix entitled “The History of the Deity of Lord Ālālanātha at Brahmagiri.”

Lord Jagannātha is absent from His temple for two weeks each year. During those times, Caitanya Mahāprabhu would feel the same feelings of separation as Rādhā felt at Candra-sarovara.

Just as Rādhā and the gopīs searched after Kṛṣṇa in the forests of Govardhana, Caitanya Mahāprabhu went in search of Lord Jagannātha in the forests lining the seashore near Purī.

And just as the cowherd girls found Lord Nārāyaṇa hiding in a grove at Paīṭhā, Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu found four-armed Lord Ālālanātha hiding in the historic temple at Brahmagiri.

Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura says that Lord Ālālanātha was forced to reveal His two-handed Śyāmasundara form to Śrī Caitanya, just as Kṛṣṇa was forced to reveal His to Śrīmatī Rādhārāṇī.

Upon seeing the Lord of His life once again, Lord Caitanya fainted in ecstasy and fell unconscious onto what was left of the original Brahmagiri, melting the rock and leaving an impression of His body.

Thus, in His form as Lord Caitanya, Kṛṣṇa was able to taste the bliss Rādhārāṇī felt when He transformed from Lord Nārāyaṇa back to Śyāmasundara and removed Her suffering with a smile.

There is a lake in Paīṭhā called Nārāyaṇa-sarovara that marks the place where Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa finally met, and Aiṁṭhā-kadamba is the nearby tree Kṛṣṇa tied into a circle to reassure His friends.

Śrī Rādhā found the Lord of Her life again at Paīṭhā, but because Her intense feelings of separation from Kṛṣṇa were responsible for Her success, the predominant mood of this place is separation from Kṛṣṇa.

For that reason, when thoughtful devotees come to Paīṭhā for bhajana, they do so with a mind to console Śrīmatī Rādhārāṇī’s sorrows as She desperately searches for Kṛṣṇa in these forest groves.

Generally, each pastime place has its own predominant mood, and devotees who visit places of pilgrimage adjust their own moods so that they are in harmony with each place. This is a principle of parikrama.

Lord Caitanya would not tolerate even a hint of rasābhāsa amongst His followers. Pilgrims in New Vraja-dhāma should follow the Lord’s instructions and likewise avoid the incompatible mixing of mellows.

The mellows of vraja-bhakti are difficult to understand and even more difficult to perfect, but by the mercy of Caitanya Mahāprabhu such things can be accomplished with ease.

Therefore in introducing the pastimes of the Lord, Kṛṣṇadāsa Kavirāja Gosvāmī writes, *kathañcana smṛte yasmin duṣkaraṁ sukaraṁ bhavet / vismṛte viparītaṁ syāt śrī-caitanyaṁ namāmi tam*:

**“Things that are very difficult to do become easy to execute if one somehow or other simply remembers Lord Caitanya. But if one does not remember Him, even easy things become very difficult.”**

**\* \* \* \***

I walked through Apsarāvana, my mind filled with the pastimes at Paīṭhā. Before I knew it, I had arrived at a hill called Nṛsiṁha-ṭīlā, from which the protector of devotees surveys this area.

Nṛsiṁhadeva is generally known to safeguard the society of devotees from hostile elements and to protect one’s devotional creeper from the choking effect of unwanted non-devotional habits.

Here in Apsarāvana, the Lord of Prahlāda also protects advanced devotees from the distress of being separated from Kṛṣṇa. Feelings of separation such as these make their lives nearly unbearable.

In such a state, devotees feel that even a moment drags on like a millennium, tears fall from their eyes like torrents of rain, and they perceive everything in the world as vacant without Kṛṣṇa.

By affectionately stroking the young Prahlāda Mahārāja’s head, Nṛsiṁhadeva reassured him that he was now free from any further torment from his demonic father, Hiraṇyakaśipu.

In a similar way, Lord Nṛsiṁha — by His merciful glance, the touch of His lotus feet, and the sweet sound of His voice — puts devotees’ fears to rest and reassures them that they will soon meet Kṛṣṇa.

The Lord says to His devotee, “Dear child! Stay in this forest, and with a purified heart, worship the pastimes of Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa by chanting Their holy names. Soon you will see Them.”

Lord Nṛsiṁhadeva will place His lotus feet upon the heads of the most fortunate devotees, and in their hearts He will inspire the sort of love that allows one to behold Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa with purified vision.

It is said that this black stone Deity of Nṛsiṁha is over one thousand years old. The Lord holds Hiraṇyakaśipu on His lap, bifurcating the demon with His nails. All glories to Lord Nṛsiṁha!

Dear devotees! Just worship Lord Nṛsiṁhadeva, for He is very kind to His servants. He came to New Vraja-dhāma in order to remove the obstacles that prevent us from attaining pure loving devotion.

In the forest at the foot of Nṛsiṁha-ṭīlā lie two lakes: Pūñcāri-kuṇḍa, which is also known as Navāla-kuṇḍa, and glorious Apsarā-kuṇḍa. Both lakes are very beautiful, and very secluded.

Because this area is so far removed from the thoroughfares upon which people regularly travel and transport their goods, Kṛṣṇa likes to bring His girlfriends here to stage the rāsa dance and other pastimes.

When, at Paīṭhā, Rādhā revealed that Nārāyaṇa was Kṛṣṇa in disguise, the gopīs praised Her cleverness. Then they all returned to Candra-sarovara to amaze the moon god with their dancing.

Kṛṣṇa then led the gopīs through the private gardens and groves of Govardhana Hill, proudly sharing with them His servant’s many opulences — blossoming trees, crystalline lakes, and singing birds.

In Pūñcāri, the amorous procession broke out into a rāsa dance. That passionate circle whirled like a churning rod and the rope, Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa, pulled that rod of gopī dancers.

The milk being churned was prema, which quickly separated out into two: buttermilk and soft butter. The buttermilk flowed into Apsarā-kuṇḍa, while the soft butter collected in Pūñcāri-kuṇḍa.

Absorbed in relishing the tastes of love, Kṛṣṇa and the gopīs continued churning, unmindful that in their intense endeavour to churn the milk of love they had also churned themselves.

As cream liquefies when over-churned, Kṛṣṇa, Rādhā, and the cowherd girls turned to liquid. Liquid Kṛṣṇa entered the water of Pūñcāri-kuṇḍa, and the liquid gopīs, Apsarā-kuṇḍa.

The creatures of the forest surrounded the two lakes in wonder. Had Kṛṣṇa and His consorts, lost to prema, melted into pools of their own mutual love and put an end to their pastimes as individuals?

Of course not! Wonderful pastimes like this are transcendental manifestations of the Lord’s internal pleasure-giving potency, enacted for the delight of Kṛṣṇa, His companions, and His devotees.

Kṛṣṇa and the gopīs may have melted and mixed together, but they did not lose their individuality — just as Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa unite in the body of Śrī Caitanya, yet eternally exist in two separate forms.

Indeed, it is the inconceivable nature of the Absolute Truth that He is simultaneously one with, yet different from, His many personal, energetic, and marginal expansions.

The same pastime potency that allowed Kṛṣṇa and His associates to merge with the waters of these two lakes again returned them to their full forms of splendour, much to the relief of Vraja’s forest creatures.

Because Rādhā and the gopīs are the origin of the Apsarās — whom the cowherd girls far excel in beauty, skill, and learning — the lake from which they re- emerged has always been known as Apsarā-kuṇḍa.

And because Śrī Kṛṣṇa, the eternal youth (nava), again came forth from the sublime waters of Pūñcāri-kuṇḍa, the wise worship this lake as a fountain of youth they call Navāla-kuṇḍa.

How divine love can cause Kṛṣṇa’s mind and body to melt and then again assume their original natures is another feature of transcendence inconceivable to conditioned souls.

Nevertheless, great devotees describe such things in relation to Śrī Kṛṣṇa, the supreme beloved, and Śrī Rādhā, the supreme lover, who together constitute the two integral aspects of divine love.

For example, while meeting, Rādhā’s and Kṛṣṇa’s attraction for each other can be so intense that it causes Their minds to merge together in a condition known as manobhava, or transcendental Cupid.

This manobhava can also melt the bodies of the divine couple. Vṛndā explains: “O Lord! You live in the forest of Govardhana Hill, and like the king of elephants, You are expert in the art of amorous love.

“Your heart and Śrīmatī Rādhārāṇī’s heart are just like shellac, which have now melted in Your spiritual perspiration; now, who can distinguish between You and Her?

“Your newly invoked affection, which is like vermilion, having mixed with Your melted hearts for the benefit of the whole world, has painted both Your hearts red within this palace of the universe.”

Any water mixed with water from the river Ganges becomes gaṅgā-jala. In a similar way, the waters of these two lakes, mixed with Rādhā’s and Kṛṣṇa’s ecstatic love, transformed into liquid prema.

Moreover, in much the same way that anything touched by the ocean of nectar becomes nectarean, whoever bathes without offence in Navāla-kuṇḍa and Apsarā-kuṇḍa becomes filled with ecstatic love.

The trees, creepers, animals, and birds of Apsarāvana have also become surcharged with prema by drinking the water of these lakes. Thus the potency of this forest resembles the potency of Premavana.

On the bank of Apsarā-kuṇḍa is the temple of Apsarā-bihārī. There is also a temple commemorating Daujī nearby, and Lord Śiva is present as Apsareśvara to protect the lake and relish its divine ambience.

**\* \* \* \***

Tradition tells that the original Apsarā-kuṇḍa was created from the water used by the Apsarās to bathe Kṛṣṇa. Twenty-four incarnations of the Lord are said to reside eternally on its banks.

These incarnations include Matsya, Kūrma, Varāha, Vāmana, Hari, Buddha, Pṛthu, Prahlāda, Vyāsa, Bhṛgu, Dhanvantari, Paraśurāma, Rāmacandra, Kapila, Dhruva, Nārada, Haṁsa, and Hayagrīva.

These transcendental persons are invisibly present, either directly in Apsarā- kuṇḍa’s manifest state, indirectly in the unmanifest abode, or mentally through Their meditations on Vṛndāvana.

Kṛṣṇa’s incarnations all have Their own abodes where They enjoy pastimes with Their companions. But, like everyone else, They also long to taste the sweetness of Kṛṣṇa and His vraja-līlā.

To satisfy His expansions, whether direct or empowered, Kṛṣṇa allows Them to reside both visibly, as Deities in the temples of Vraja, and invisibly, at different pastime places in Their original forms.

For the pleasure and delight of His associates, Kṛṣṇa also allows His incarnations to become visible through Him, albeit with forms and qualities enriched by His own unique attributes.

In other words, Kṛṣṇa’s incarnations usually appear possessed with only a degree of Kṛṣṇa’s opulences (like wealth, fame, and so on) and a unique form (such as a boar in the case of Lord Varāha).

But when Kṛṣṇa personally manifests the form of Varāha in Vṛndāvana, He does so with all of His own opulences and special qualities intact, but outwardly He appears as a boar.

When Kṛṣṇa displays the form of an incarnation, the Vraja-vāsīs may also display their own expansions as associates of that particular incarnation in order to complement the Lord’s pastimes.

The wonderful thing about these special pastimes is that their awe and reverence never eclipse the sweetness, humour, and simplicity of vraja-bhāva, whichever forms Kṛṣṇa and the Vraja-vāsīs display.

One day the gopīs accused Kṛṣṇa of improper conduct. In His own defence, Kṛṣṇa not only insisted that He respects religious principles, but also insisted that He is religion personified, Lord Viṣṇu.

But the gopīs were unmoved. They chided their boastful friend by pointing out the only two things they could see He had in common with Lord Viṣṇu: His dark complexion and His affection for Kamalā.

Miffed but undaunted, Kṛṣṇa set out to confound the gopīs’ uncharitable opinion; He effortlessly sprouted ten extra arms and the ornaments of Viṣṇu, calling upon Sudāmā to take the form of Garuḍa.

Then twelve-armed Kṛṣṇa climbed on Garuḍa’s shoulders and the two repeatedly flew in circles above the dumbfounded gopīs’ heads, while the demigods showered flowers from heaven, chanting, “Jaya!”

On another occasion, the cowherd boys were preparing to wrestle in the soft sands of Apsarāvana, just between the tail end of Govardhana Hill and the waters of Navāla-kuṇḍa.

The boys faced off in two groups, the younger gopas headed by Kṛṣṇa, the older ones headed by Balarāma. Before beginning, each team made much of its own prowess and belittled that of their opponents.

Kṛṣṇa, however, would not tolerate being ridiculed for His weakness. In a shrill, pipsqueak voice He decried His accusers, insisting that He was stronger than anyone in the universe, what to speak of any gopa.
