The older boys slapped their sides, doubled over in exaggerated laugher, amused by Kṛṣṇa’s bravado. The younger boys were embarrassed that their Kṛṣṇa had yet again put His foot in His mouth.

Kṛṣṇa stood in the clearing between the two groups, red-faced with anger. He kicked the ground, shook His fist, and called out, “All right My friends! All right! You’ll see what I can do.”

Without further warning, Kṛṣṇa transformed from a petulant cowherd boy into a gigantic angry boar that stood up on His rear legs, flashing fearful tusks and thrashing about His menacing hooves.

Black as a tamāla tree with eyes red like rubies, the boar grew larger and larger, soon towering above the treetops, shaking His snout. Birds flew from their roosts. Animals retreated to the thickets for shelter.

Terrified, the boys glanced here and there hoping to find Kṛṣṇa. Just as they were about to call out to Him, the great beast began to speak in a voice both deeply resonant and eerily reminiscent of Gopāla.

“You think that I am so weak!” thundered the boar. “Then watch Me lift this entire planet!” Falling on all fours, He stabbed the ground with both tusks, making the earth shudder in pain.

The loud bellowing of Kṛṣṇa as Varāha made the boys cover their ears in fear. As He dug at the earth with His tusks, Varāha threw soil, stones, and trees into the air like the vortex of a powerful tornado.

Not knowing where else to turn, the boys called out to their only shelter, “Kṛṣṇa! Save us! Forgive us!” At Balarāma’s request, Madhumaṅgala stammered the Puruṣa-sūkta, *sahasra-śīrṣā puruṣa* —

Full of fear and unable to comprehend where Kṛṣṇa had gone, or from where this great boar had come, the boys followed Balabhadra’s example by falling to the ground and offering obeisances to the Lord.

Meanwhile, Kṛṣṇa realised that His unconventional attempt at persuasion had filled His friends with fear and Apsarāvana with chaos. “Besides,” He thought, “I have made My point!”

Kṛṣṇa withdrew His varāha-rūpa and once again stood amidst the gopas, who were still hugging the ground and praying for dear life. But mayhem turned to serenity, and soon everything was as before.

For a moment there was absolute silence. While the gopas rose to their feet, Kṛṣṇa straightened His belt, rubbed the soil from His hands, and smiled casually at His shell-shocked friends.

Their fear of the great boar was quickly dispelled, and in its place the cowherd boys were filled with wonder at what Kṛṣṇa had just done. That wonder greatly increased their untainted love for Him.

Nevertheless, they gave no thought to Kṛṣṇa’s divinity. The boys simply clapped and cheered as they crowded around Kṛṣṇa, saying, “Well done, my friend!” and “How do you perform such magic?”

One by one, each boy embraced Kṛṣṇa tightly. The wrestling tournament was abandoned in favour of swimming in Surabhi-kuṇḍa with everyone playfully imitating Varāha’s roar on the way there.

And although the boys had been impressed by Lord Varāha’s display of strength, most of them were still convinced that — magic and mysticism aside — they could beat Kṛṣṇa in a proper wrestling match.

On yet another occasion, the gopas left their cows grazing nearby and came to Apsarāvana to re-enact Lord Rāma’s battle with Rāvaṇa, determined to teach that demon a lesson he would never forget.

Kṛṣṇa would be Rāma, Balarāma would be Lakṣmaṇa, and because no one wanted to side with Rāvaṇa, the cowherd boys would be the monkeys and bears that saved Sītā and brought victory to Rāma.

Nonetheless, some boys complained that without real-life enemies their play would be dull, so it was agreed that the dark trees on the far side of Apsarā- kuṇḍa could stand in for Rāvaṇa and his Rākṣasas.

The boys dressed as vānaras and Kṛṣṇa dressed as Rāma. When they were ready, Lakṣmaṇa handed a bow to Rāma and said, “O brother! Rāvaṇa has rejected our peace offering! You must kill him!”

The monkeys and bears whooped and howled and jostled each other as animals do. Then, running along the banks of the lake, they pretended to cross the stone bridge to Laṅkā.

In the mood of Rāma, Kṛṣṇa shouted commands to His army, while Balarāma, posing as Lakṣmaṇa and intent on doing battle with Indrajit, released arrow after arrow into the forest beyond.

All at once the soldiers in Rāma’s army noticed that the enemy trees seemed to look very much like Rākṣasas, and a certain expansive, top-heavy kadamba tree looked very much like ten-headed Rāvaṇa.

When a variety of celestial projectiles began to land all around the attacking vānaras, their charge came to a halt as they considered, with no small amount of trepidation, the wild cannibals ahead.

Seeing His army’s fear, Rāma raised His bow and yelled over the violent tumult the enemy forces were creating, “Great monkeys of Kiṣkindha! Why do you fear these insignificant Rākṣasas?”

Tossing dirt and rocks in all directions, a missile exploded only feet away from Rāma as He said these words, but the son of Daśaratha was unmoved. He continued to rally His reluctant soldiers.

“Hanumān! Sugrīva! Aṅgada!” shouted Rāma. “Just look at your weapons! Consider your powerful arms! You know you have nothing to fear. Nīla! Jāmbavān! Panasa! Who is your match in this world?”

As Lord Rāmacandra called out the names of His warriors, the costumed cowherd boys actually transformed into brawny monkeys, fully endowed with armour, weapons, helmets, and tails.

For a moment, the monkeys didn’t know what to make of their newfound identities or that of their friends’, but with only the slightest push from Yogamāyā, all indecision vanished.

Seeing Kṛṣṇa as Rāma, with His unmistakable green complexion, Balarāma as His younger brother, Apsarā-kuṇḍa as the ocean, and the Rākṣasas ready to fight to the bitter end, the monkeys charged ahead.

By the Lord’s pastime potency, the innocent make-believe of the gopas transformed into the real war between Rāma and Rāvaṇa, the gopas assumed their forms in rāma-līlā, and Vraja became Laṅkā.

Indeed, the war waged on and on, as if the armies on both sides would prefer death to surrender, until a peculiar sound drifted onto the battlefield and brought the battle to an abrupt end.

“Rāma! Kṛṣṇa! Where are You? The time for supper is long past!” It was the voice of Mother Yaśodā. She had come out in search of her sons who were late for Their evening meal. “Kṛṣṇa? Where are You?”

All of a sudden Rāvaṇa and the Rākṣasas disappeared. The monkeys were gopas once again. Just moments before, Lakṣmaṇa was duelling with Indrajit, but now Balarāma found Himself fencing with a tree.

The strength of Mother Yaśodā’s love had subdued the power of Yogamāyā; her desire that Kṛṣṇa should eat was greater than Yogamāyā’s desire to facilitate Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes.

And since even Kṛṣṇa could not resist the strength of His mother’s love, when Yaśodā appeared by the banks of Apsarā-kuṇḍa, He was no longer Rāma, but a little boy who had become too absorbed in play.

With Kṛṣṇa on her right and Balarāma on her left, Yaśodā took her sons’ hands and led Them away from the battlefield, as They animatedly recounted the fierce offensive They had waged that day.

“And what was the result of Your fight with Indrajit?” she asked Balarāma. Kṛṣṇa excitedly replied, “Because you interrupted their duel at a critical moment, My brother never got to kill the demon.”

Yaśodā nodded and replied, “That’s all right. Tomorrow You can come back and finish the battle with Indrajit.” Hearing this the cowherd boys cheered and Kṛṣṇa yelled, “Monkey soldiers! It’s time to eat.”

**\* \* \* \***

Standing in Apsarāvana, vibrant with Kṛṣṇa’s diverse pastimes and rich with the natural beauty particular to Vraja’s forests, I considered leaving the parikrama-mārga to visit places on Govardhana Hill.

In Bhauma Vṛndāvana devotees will not set foot on Girirāja, but in New Vraja- dhāma they must, as service necessitates that they tend flower gardens, vegetable patches, and fruit trees there.

This is one of the many tangible differences between New Vraja-dhāma and Bhauma Vṛndāvana — two distinct, and distinctly merciful manifestations of the one transcendental reality.

Whether or not it is appropriate to walk on Govardhana Hill must be determined according to the truths (tattva) and moods (bhāva) that a specific manifestation of the dhāma itself reveals.

For instance, in Goloka Vṛndāvana the Vraja-vāsīs graze their cows on the slopes of Girirāja, a practice that was likewise followed in Bhauma Vṛndāvana while Kṛṣṇa was physically present there.

Even today some of the local Vraja-vāsīs in Bhauma Vṛndāvana walk freely on Govardhana Hill. However, practising devotees, whatever their level of Kṛṣṇa consciousness, will not.

But in Śrīdhāma Māyāpur, without incurring any offence, both locals and sādhakas live, work, and worship on Koladvīpa, which is known to be a manifestation of Girirāja in Lord Gaurāṅga’s dhāma.

Therefore, while within their hearts devotees respect Girirāja in New Vraja- dhāma the same way they would in Bhauma Vṛndāvana, they nonetheless walk and serve on it like the devotees in Koladvīpa.

I walked the short distance along Govardhana Hill to the place where Indra first worshipped Kṛṣṇa. It is easy to find because it is located beside a certain stone that represents Vādanī-śilā, the musical stone.

Once the cowherd boys entertained themselves by dancing and playing music on their flutes and horns, but they were disappointed because they had no drum to keep the beat.

Kṛṣṇa set His flute on the ground and reached for a large rectangular rock nearby. On the surfaces of that stone drum He played intricate rhythms that won Him the loving approval of His friends.

That stone still sounds like a drum when struck, and so it is also known as the Bhājana-śilā. Just seeing it, touching it, or hearing the sound it emits is said to destroy the results of the greatest sins.

Indra-vedī is one of the many places associated with King Indra’s attempts to nullify the offences he committed against Kṛṣṇa. All of these places are situated on or around Govardhana Hill.

At Reheja, Indra fell to the ground at Kṛṣṇa’s feet, at Indra-vedī he offered the Lord prayers, at Indra-kuṇḍa his repentant tears collected, and at Indra-ṭīlā he bathed Kṛṣṇa and gave Him the name “Govinda.”

Devotees who have committed offences to Lord Kṛṣṇa in the course of their devotional service come to Indra-vedī in New Vraja-dhāma and fall flat upon the ground to pray for His forgiveness.

Bowing down at Indra-vedī, I prayed, “O my dear Lord! I have committed so many offences against You with my body, words, and mind that it is not possible for me to count them all.

“Whenever I contemplate whether I will ever be freed from the burden of my misdeeds, I become disheartened. Nonetheless, I have heard that You are very kind-hearted and forgiving.

“If You pardon my transgressions, as You pardoned those of Indra, then I can be hopeful that my worship will eventually bear fruit in the form of attachment to You. Please, therefore, forgive me!”

O devotees! In the matter of freeing yourselves from the network of illusion, remember that the only way to accomplish this task with ease is by receiving the grace of Lord Caitanya. There is no other way.

Although Kṛṣṇa pardoned Indra for his offences, the king of heaven once again fell under the sway of intoxicating pride when Kṛṣṇa went to Amarāvatī to acquire a pārijāta tree for Queen Satyabhāmā.

Instead of happily granting Kṛṣṇa’s request, Indra fruitlessly did battle with the Lord. And once again he had to be humbled before the demigods just as he was after his attacks on Govardhana and Vraja.

Indra no longer trusted his own mind. With nowhere else to turn he again approached Surabhi for shelter. He said, “Kṛṣṇa is beyond my comprehension; therefore, I keep committing offences against Him.

“I have heard that in Kali-yuga the Lord will perform His pastimes in Nadīyā, but I fear that I will once again fall into illusion and insult Him as I have done before. Please save me from my own wickedness.”

Mother Surabhi did her best to reassure Indra. She persuaded him to accompany her to Navadvīpa where, with fixed attention, they together worshipped Lord Gaurāṅga beneath a large banyan tree.

By chanting Lord Caitanya’s name they quickly attained a vision of His charming form and smiling face, and they listened happily to His honey-sweet voice as He pacified Indra. “I know your wish,” He said.

“I will soon appear in the town of Nadīyā. At that time You will no longer be ensnared by the net of My illusory potency. Indeed, you shall serve Me free from false ego. Do not worry!”

Kṛṣṇa has warned that overcoming His divine material energy is very difficult, but those who surrender to Him, as Indra surrendered to Gaurāṅga, are personally protected from illusion by the Lord.

Just downhill from Indra-vedī, upon one of Govardhana Hill’s larger stones, is the impression of Airāvata’s foot, left when Indra’s gigantic elephant approached Kṛṣṇa to offer his respects.

At that time the stones on the hill were already semi-soft. Girirāja was in ecstasy, both at the touch of Kṛṣṇa’s lotus feet, and in anticipation of Kṛṣṇa being worshipped and bathed by the demigods.

Therefore, when Airāvata stepped on this stone he left the imprint of his foot which devotees revere, not only as evidence of this pastime but also as evidence of the universality of devotional service.

Devotional service to Kṛṣṇa is not reserved for humans. It is available to souls in any form of life — be they plant, animal, demon, or demigod — whoever expresses the desire to surrender to the Lord.

Rāmacandra had already enlisted into His army forest creatures of all manners and descriptions when Vibhīṣaṇa, Rākṣasa by birth, came to serve the Lord. Rāma accepted the demon without hesitation.

Speaking aloud so that those monkeys who were suspicious of Vibhīṣaṇa could hear, He said, *sakṛd eva prapanno yas tavāsmīti ca yācate / abhayaṁ sarvadā tasmai dadāmy etad vrataṁ mama*:

“If one surrenders unto Me sincerely, saying, ‘My Lord, from this day I am fully surrendered unto You,’ I always give him protection and make him fearless. That is My vow.”

Mindful of the universality of Kṛṣṇa’s mercy, I prayed in silence broken only by the occasional birdsong and rustling leaf. Within the temple of my heart reverberated a prayer that I have enshrined there.

“I am very weak and lazy to do even the smallest devotional service, and so when I hear of the peerless and difficult services perfected by great devotees of ancient times, my heart burns with despair.

“But when I remember that everyone is splashed by the waves of Your mercy, not only the demigod Brahmā but even the most insignificant creature, my heart is sprinkled with cooling drops of hope.”

From Airāvata’s footprint I returned to Pūñcāri and then continued south to visit Lauṭhā, the cowherd friend of Kṛṣṇa, also known as Pūñcāri ka Lauṭhā because of his connection to this area.

Lauṭhā is very stout. Like the other cowherd boys, he used to share with Kṛṣṇa the lunch his mother had packed and in turn taste the lunches Mother Yaśodā had sent with Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma.

Two very similar stories explain why Lauṭhā continues to sit beneath a tree by Govardhana Hill, and why, after such a very long time, he remains as stout and strong as ever.

The first story tells how, on the day Akrūra was taking Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma to Mathurā, Lauṭhā came here to meet his friend Kṛṣṇa as usual, somehow unaware of the two brothers’ imminent departure.

When Kṛṣṇa and Balarāma finally appeared, They were not with Their friends or the cows, but riding on a chariot with Akrūra. Confused, Lauṭhā asked Kṛṣṇa where He and His brother were going.

Kṛṣṇa answered, “King Kaṁsa has asked Us to visit Mathurā for a wrestling match.” Lauṭhā asked, “But when will You be back?” Kṛṣṇa replied, “Some time soon. Probably the day after tomorrow.”

Lauṭhā was perplexed. Kṛṣṇa had never left Vṛndāvana before. And He had never wrestled with anyone other than the cowherd boys, and even that was just for fun. How would He fare against real wrestlers?

Thinking he would tempt Kṛṣṇa to stay in Vṛndāvana, Lauṭhā offered his lunch to his friends, “My mother cooked some delicious kachorīs for us. Come on, let’s share them before You go.”

Kṛṣṇa’s eyes welled up with tears of love. He wanted to stay, but He simply had to go. There was no choice. Meanwhile, Akrūra repeatedly glanced over his shoulder to remind Kṛṣṇa of Their mission.

“Give Us your lunch,” suggested Kṛṣṇa, “and We will eat it on the way.” Tears fell from His eyes. Lauṭhā immediately swung his lunch pack over the side of the chariot and into Kṛṣṇa’s trembling hands.

“If You’re determined to go,” said Lauṭhā, “then I’ll wait here until You come back. And if You don’t come back the day after tomorrow, I’ll stay here until You do.” By this time Lauṭhā was crying too.

Kṛṣṇa could only nod His head sadly in response. As Akrūra started the chariot, Kṛṣṇa placed His hand on His uncle’s shoulder to stop him. “What will you eat?” Kṛṣṇa asked Lauṭhā.

His friend replied, “I won’t have an appetite for anything until You return!” Kṛṣṇa smiled, “Then I will pray to Lord Nārāyaṇa that even if you don’t eat, you will neither lose weight, nor get hungry.”

The two boys laughed as Akrūra again started the horses. Lauṭhā and Kṛṣṇa continued to wave to each other until the chariot turned the bend around Sutalavana, near Samāi, and went out of sight.

The second version of this pastime takes place much later. After the Vraja-vāsīs had returned from Kurukṣetra, Kṛṣṇa came to Vṛndāvana in order to take the Vraja-vāsīs back to Goloka.

The cowherd men and women mounted their chariots one by one, along with their animals. When it was Lauṭhā’s turn to go, he approached the vehicle but would not get aboard.

Taking Kṛṣṇa’s hand in his, Lauṭhā said, “Vṛndāvana is my home and You are my friend. I can leave neither. But I think that even if You go now, You will return, so I will just wait for You here.”

As Lauṭhā said this, he stepped back, and the chariots left for Goloka without him. Kṛṣṇa knew His friend would never eat without Him, and so He blessed Lauṭhā to remain fit even without food.

Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes are invisible to material eyes, but they always go on in Vraja. As Caitanya Mahāprabhu, Kṛṣṇa was again visible in Vraja, and while circling Girirāja, He was no doubt reunited with His friend.

It is also commonly accepted that Lauṭhā, also known as Lauṭhā Bābā, is the gopa who expands himself to become the invincible warrior and eternally devoted servant of Lord Rāmacandra, Hanumān.

**\* \* \* \***

Enclosed by the concave arc of the parikrama path where it bends around Girirāja’s southern side is a small forest called Sutalavana. On the opposite side of the path is another forest called Śyāma-ḍhāk.

In the dense groves of Sutalavana is Sutala-kuṇḍa, a small lake where Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa would rest after enjoying a rāsa dance. Only a little distance away is the famous cave, Maṇikandalī.

In Jagannātha Purī, Śrī Caitanya Mahāprabhu, while absorbed in ecstatic devotional trance, once saw Himself as a gopī who had come to Govardhana Hill in search of Kṛṣṇa and Kṛṣṇa’s cows.

In His trance Caitanya Mahāprabhu saw Kṛṣṇa surrounded on all sides by grazing cows, climbing Govardhana Hill and playing His flute to attract the incomparably beautiful cowherd girls of Vṛndāvana.

Sure enough, Śrī Rādhā and Her gopī friends did not fail to heed the call of the flute. They rushed there to meet Kṛṣṇa, dressed in their best clothes and decorated with their most costly ornaments.

When Kṛṣṇa saw that Śrī Rādhā had arrived, He enticed Her to enter one of Govardhana’s caves with Him. Meanwhile, the other gopīs sent Lord Caitanya to pick flowers from the gardens nearby.

Kṛṣṇa enacted similar pastimes at Maṇikandalī, which is like the bejewelled earring in the treasure chest of Vraja’s pastime places. This cave’s secrets are known only to itself and to the divine couple.

Perhaps Rāghava Gosvāmī, also known as Rāghava Ācārya, knows these secrets as well. After all, he lived and worshipped in Maṇikandalī cave during the entire time he spent in Vraja.

Moreover, Rāghava Gosvāmī was not only a great scholar and follower of Caitanya Mahāprabhu, but was also, in his perfected form, one of Rādhā’s eight most confidential girlfriends, Campakalatā.

At Maṇikandalī, the guides of New Vraja-dhāma and devotees in general should contemplate the character of Rāghava Ācārya, the first pāṇḍā to escort devotees around the many holy places of Vraja.

The word paṇḍā means “knowledge,” and a brāhmaṇa who, by his devotional knowledge, has subdued all material conceptions of life and therefore conquered Kṛṣṇa, earns the name “pāṇḍā.”

This name is conferred upon Vaiṣṇavas who are very learned in the moods, narrative details, and specific locations of the pastimes that Kṛṣṇa enacted throughout the many, many places in Vraja.

Not only are pāṇḍās well versed in the secrets of the dhāma, but because they also know the purpose of doing parikrama there, pāṇḍās can assist visitors in reaping the full benefit of their pilgrimage.

Because pāṇḍās once revealed the secrets of the holy places, they were respected as tīrtha-gurus, and it was customary for thankful pilgrims to look after the maintenance of the pāṇḍās and their families.

Unfortunately, in this Age of Kali both pāṇḍās and pilgrims have neglected their duties. Visitors fail to maintain both pastime places and pāṇḍās. At the same time, pāṇḍās often exploit innocent pilgrims.

To re-establish the pāṇḍā tradition, guides in New Vraja-dhāma should not be maintained directly by pilgrims, but through the management. This will help preserve the guides’ purity.

Moreover, in reciprocation for receiving expert guidance in the dhāma, visitors should make arrangements, through the management, to maintain the pāṇḍās and pastime places of New Vraja-dhāma.

Rāghava Ācārya was an example of the perfect pāṇḍā, Śrīnivāsa Ācārya and Narottama Dāsa Ṭhākura were exemplars of the perfect pilgrims, and Jīva Gosvāmī was an example of the perfect sponsor.

Bhakti-ratnākara describes how Jīva Gosvāmī decided one day to send Śrīnivāsa and Narottama on a tour of the holy places of Vraja, but he did not know who should guide them on their tour.

Just at that time, Rāghava Ācārya arrived from Govardhana. After being well received he told Śrī Jīva, “I have decided to tour all of Vraja. For that reason I have come here for your blessings.”

Śrī Jīva was very happy to hear this news, and he asked Rāghava Ācārya whether he would be willing to take his two disciples along. The ācārya at once replied with an emphatic yes.

Śrīnivāsa and Narottama bowed at the feet of their guide and were in turn affectionately embraced by Rāghava Ācārya. After introductions were complete, the three Vaiṣṇavas set out on parikrama.

Only devotees who have been led on pilgrimage by the followers of Rāghava Ācārya can understand the true benefit of seeing the places of Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes through the words and moods of a great soul.

Rāghava Ācārya was from a Kulīna brāhmaṇa family in South India. A great scholar, he had written a book called Bhakti-ratna-prakāśa. Even though so qualified, he was always kind to the poor and fallen.

He lived mostly in the cave Maṇikandalī, but he would sometimes visit Raghunātha Dāsa Gosvāmī at Rādhā-kuṇḍa, and at other times he would visit the other devotees in Vṛndāvana.

Always glorifying the pastimes and attributes of Rādhā, Kṛṣṇa, and Caitanya Mahāprabhu, Rāghava Ācārya was absorbed in spiritual bliss. Although he rarely ate, he never suffered the pangs of hunger.

Rāghava Ācārya was situated on the transcendental platform, free from all material attachment. When his ecstasies became too much to control, he would shed copious tears or roll in the dust of the dhāma.

Rāghava Ācārya’s body was always decorated with that transcendental dust because he knew it to be the best dress, ornament, and means of purification for residents of Vṛndāvana.

This conduct instils faith in taking full shelter of the dust of the dhāma. That inestimably powerful dust has been blessed by the touch of Kṛṣṇa’s lotus feet and the feet of His eternal companions.

By dint of his spiritual acumen, Rāghava Ācārya understood that Śrīnivāsa and Narottama were of the same spiritual disposition as he, so he was pleased to reveal to them all that he knew.

Pilgrims who have a devotee like Rāghava as pāṇḍā, and a pāṇḍā who has devotees like the two students of Śrī Jīva as pilgrims, are perfectly matched to churn the ocean of the Lord’s Vṛndāvana pastimes.

While residing at Maṇikandalī and chanting the mahā-mantra, Rāghava Ācārya would often be absorbed in his eternal spiritual identity as the young gopī friend of Rādhārāṇī, Campakalatā.

A beautiful girl of fourteen years, Campakalatā goes to a grove of campaka trees in Khadiravana and collects flowers for the special garlands she strings for Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa.

She also tends her own yellow flower-filled grove, called Kāmalatā, on the northern bank of Rādhā-kuṇḍa. The famous kitchen where she prepares gourmet delicacies for Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa is also there.

In that kitchen, Campakalatā teaches Rādhā’s maidservants the culinary arts, and when her students become accomplished, they host Kṛṣṇa and the gopīs with midday feasts that rival the taste of nectar.

Once, while such feasting was in full swing, Kṛṣṇa humorously asked, “Tell Me: Who walks around, although she is a motionless vine, and leaves her husband far behind to embrace the black tamāla tree?”

Rādhā met Kṛṣṇa’s challenge with Her good-natured reply, “It is Campakalatā.” Another gopī took the opportunity to affectionately tease Campakalatā, who was cooking nearby with her assistants.

The gopī said, “O Mukunda, fond of surabhi cows and decorated with the Kaustubha jewel! Campakalatā is glorified by all the beautiful gopīs of Vraja as being most fragrant and most charming.

“Since You appear to be like a splendid dark rain cloud, don’t You think You would be even more attractive if You were decorated with a fresh lightning- flash, like Campakalatā?”

Campakalatā stepped out of the kitchen to respond, “O Rādhā! So many gopīs in Vraja are gifted at amorous sports. What did You do to make Kṛṣṇa neglect them all in favour of You?”

These riddles and jokes not only brought pleasure to the hearts of all present but also enhanced the flavour of the tasty foods they ate, thereby making the banquet even more relishable.

All glories to Campakalatā-devī, who as the great saint Rāghava Ācārya took Narottama and Śrīnivāsa on a tour of Vraja and revealed to them and the world the treasures of Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes places.

**\* \* \* \***

On the edge of Śyāma-ḍhāk is a garden known as Samāi. Its name is derived from the word Śyāmā because Kṛṣṇa came to Śrīmatī Rādhārāṇī at this place dressed as a gopī by that name.

At a rāsa dance festival at Apsarāvana, for reasons known only to Śrī Rādhā, She became displeased with the arrangements Kṛṣṇa had made and withdrew to pout at secluded Śyāma-ḍhāk.

Rādhārāṇī coolly rejected Kṛṣṇa’s efforts to pacify Her, and just when Kṛṣṇa was on the edge of despair, Viśākhā suggested that He approach Rādhā in disguise to appease Her more tactfully.

Kṛṣṇa followed this advice and dressed as Śyāmā-sakhī. Camouflaged as a gopī He was able to capture Rādhā’s attention long enough to transform Her marked indifference into dire longing.

This exchange at Samāi was similar to a later pastime that happened further along the parikrama path, when Kṛṣṇa appeared before Rādhā in the guise of an elderly brāhmaṇī.

Rādhā naturally offered all respect to the scholarly woman, who introduced Herself as a master of astrology, yoga, rituals, and mantra, as well as a patroness of the pious young girls of Vraja.

Eager to know whether the stars foresaw an impending meeting with Kṛṣṇa, Rādhā asked the brāhmaṇī, “O knower of all things! What is the topmost abode of happiness in all the three worlds?”

As the woman casually swept Her hand across the horizon, She said, “The cowherd pastureland of Śrī Kṛṣṇa.” Surprised at such an agreeable answer, Rādhā asked deeper philosophical questions:

“What is the foundation of happy life for all living entities within the universe?” Without hesitation, the brāhmaṇī replied, “Singing songs about Śrī Kṛṣṇa, the prince of Vṛndāvana.”

“How is it that this old lady is so Kṛṣṇa conscious?” thought Rādhā. With increasing interest and curiosity She then asked aloud, “What eagerness bestows incomparable auspiciousness?”

Again, accompanied by a meaningful smile, came the instant reply, “Dear girl! You should know that nothing ends in greater auspiciousness than the desire to obtain the company of Kṛṣṇa.”

Rādhā raised Her gaze from the brāhmaṇī’s feet to Her face. She saw an unusual twinkle in the old lady’s eyes. That, combined with such spontaneous absorption in Kṛṣṇa, made Rādhā wonder, “Who is She?”

Thinking that no one else was around to hear, Rādhā softly asked, “What is most enjoyable?” The woman sighed with longing and said, “The beauty and sweetness of the transcendental Cupid, Śrī Kṛṣṇa.”

Rādhā doubted that a complete stranger could disclose the confidential secrets of Her heart. “Either this brāhmaṇī is truly all-knowing or a friend in disguise,” She thought.

Unable to restrain Her curiosity, Śrīmatī Rādhārāṇī revealed Her mind, “O noble lady. You truly know everything. What is the goal of all activities, for all people, in all places?”

The old woman took a step towards Rādhārāṇī and looked Her straight in the eyes as She spoke, this time in Kṛṣṇa’s voice, “The unrestricted attainment of Śrī Kṛṣṇa’s company.”

For a moment, neither of Them spoke, but both looked to see if there was anyone nearby. When They saw no one, They began to laugh so uncontrollably that only Their embrace could steady Them.

May Kṛṣṇa’s fraudulent mischief — His prankish answers to Rādhā’s cunning questions — protect us all from the dangers of frivolous talks and the poison of unnecessary learning.

At the centre of the forest at Śyāma-ḍhāk is a clearing called Reheja, which is where, accompanied by Mother Surabhi, Indra made his first awkward overture to Kṛṣṇa in an attempt to propitiate the Lord.

Kṛṣṇa was unimpressed by Indra’s apology because as the Supersoul within He knew that the underlying sentiment was nothing more than fear — Indra’s fear of losing his position as king of heaven.

Walking north along Govardhana Hill, Kṛṣṇa ignored Indra just as a bull would ignore an egret tagging along. Only when Surabhi interceded did Kṛṣṇa acknowledge the demigod’s presence.

Also in Śyāma-ḍhāk, at the western edge of the forest, the trees provide another favourite picnic spot for the cowherd boys because the leaves are shaped like cups, as they are in Droṇa-tīrtha.

While the cows grazed in the pastures on the forest’s edge, the boys would rest in the shade of these trees, and when they became hungry they would eat their lunches from those leaf-cups.

The trees in Vraja are not ordinary trees. Some are great souls in the mellow of neutrality, who by the arrangement of Yogamāyā have appeared here to participate in Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes.

Others, like the Bhāṇḍīra-vaṭa tree, are eternal associates from Goloka Vṛndāvana who have appeared on earth as part of the dhāma to serve Kṛṣṇa’s transcendental pastimes in intimate ways.

Both these categories of trees taste the ecstasies of prema by growing in Vṛndāvana, by seeing Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes, and by rendering service to Him according to their qualification.

When Kṛṣṇa sees the stately trees of Vṛndāvana, with their beautiful reddish buds and their heavy crop of fruits and flowers, He smiles gently and speaks kind words to them.

Kṛṣṇa asks them, “O My friends the trees. O wise ones. Is everything all right with you today? Did last night’s gentle rains quench your thirst and nourish your flowers and fruits?”

In the dark of night the trees often faint, unable to see Kṛṣṇa anywhere. But by hearing the soothing sound of Kṛṣṇa’s voice or the celestial music that flows from His flute, the trees come back to life.

Those trees rejoice by instantly blooming with flowers, shedding honey tears, and shaking their leaves in ecstasy. To honour Kṛṣṇa they smile with their blossoms and sway happily in the breeze.

As Kṛṣṇa passes by, the trees bend down in adoration and touch His feet with the tips of their branches. In this heightened state of ecstasy, sap drips from their pores and muddies the ground.

After Kṛṣṇa has passed and left them behind, the trees lament their immobility, for unlike other more fortunate creatures, they are unable to follow Kṛṣṇa in His wanderings throughout Vraja.

The trees decry their lamentable state with inspired sentiments born of separation. They characterise themselves as “rooted to the mode of ignorance” and point to their previously committed offences.

Their friend the wind helps them, however, to bow down again and to touch Kṛṣṇa’s footprints with the tips of their branches. And they weep, hoping to be freed of the bad habits that caused their suffering.

Of course, for the trees — or any other creature for that matter — to take birth in Vraja means they have accumulated great piety. Otherwise, great souls like Uddhava would not pray to be born there.

And yet, just as love makes the gopīs condemn their human births and desire births in the family of Kṛṣṇa’s bamboo flute, ecstatic love inspires the trees to condemn their “lowly” births.

Wherever trees may happen to grow in Vraja, they should be counted as great souls. Nevertheless, all trees are not equally great nor do they all share the same mood in their loving devotion to Kṛṣṇa.

Pastime places display their own hierarchy of lesser to greater spiritual intimacy — from Vṛndāvana to Govardhana to Rādhā-kuṇḍa — and so the trees in those places reflect that same spectrum.

Moreover, each pastime place has a dominant devotional mellow — fraternal, parental, or amorous. The trees in those places mix the dominant mellow with their basic mood of neutrality.

Spiritual hierarchy gives rise to transcendental variety in Kṛṣṇa’s activities and abode, yet these comparisons should never lead one to think that in Vraja one living being is higher than another.

The proof of this eternal truth lies in the prayers of the gopīs, the most intimate devotees, who decry their own births and declare every living thing in Vraja to be more fortunate than they are.

**\* \* \* \***

Go-kuṇḍa is a small lake that lies on the edge of Śyāma-ḍhāk forest. Whenever Kṛṣṇa and the cowherd boys come here with their cows, they all drink deeply from its clear, sweet waters.

A person who lives in Krsna consciousness lives purely, without contaminating the environment Krsna has designed to provide the bodily necessities of man and beast alike.

From the edge of Sutalavana all the way to Uddhava-kunda is a beautiful forest *of kadamba* trees called Kadambavana, which appears in New Vraja-dhama as an enchanting fruit orchard.

In Kadambavana, between Manikandali and Yatipura, there are four important lakes marking the most noteworthy pastime places: Indra-kunda, Airavata-kunda, Surabhi-kunda, and Rudra-kunda.

First I came to Indra-kunda, where Indra’s tears of remorse for having offended Krsna collected in a depression in the earth, after flowing down from Indra-vedi and along the base of Govardhana Hill.

By contact with this water, real contrition for one’s offences will arise within the heart, and thus the doors of self-rectification and complete forgiveness will open, one after the other.

This lake is also called Carana-kunda, because Indra’s tears washed Krsna’s lotus feet (*carana*). The transcendental touch of those lotus feet conferred Indra’s tears with divine grace.

When Caitanya Mahaprabhu did *parikrama* of Govardhana Hill He rested here — as He did in many other places around Giriraja — and enjoyed the beauty and seclusion of this lake.

Lord Caitanya’s circumambulation of Govardhana Hill, like the rest of His visit to Vraja, was in no way ordinary; it was not the sort of *parikrama* that is commonly seen, heard of, or performed.

In circumambulating Govardhana Hill, Caitanya Mahaprabhu followed the path of intense ecstatic love, which sometimes coincided with the established *parikrama* path, and at other times diverged.

His excursion took many days, and also included the forests and pastime places that surround Govardhana Hill. The details of the Lord’s route are given by those fortunate souls who saw or met Him.

From the moment Caitanya Mahaprabhu appeared on earth, He overflowed with transcendental bliss, but when He entered Vrndavana the intensity of that bliss increased a millionfold.

The bliss of Caitanya Mahaprabhu is inexplicable and inconceivable. Our only hope to understand a drop of it is to examine the extraordinary behaviour the Lord displayed under its influence.

In every forest the Lord visited, He would eagerly embrace each tree and vine. At the sight of cows in Vraja’s pastures, the Lord would run to them, wild with happiness, and then fall to the ground in tears.

The sight of a peacock feather would cause Caitanya Mahaprabhu to tremble, fall to the ground, roll about, and cry out madly. He would then rise up and run to and fro, only to collapse again, unconscious.

Once, at the sight of a herd of calves running through the forest with tails raised, the Lord fainted in ecstasy and fell into bramble hushes on the roadside. The local cowherds had to carefully untangle Him.

Upon seeing the forest creatures in Kadambavana, the Lord again fainted in ecstasy. The deer came forward to lick the foam from His mouth, and the birds drank the tears as they fell from His eyes.

A cowherd reported that Caitanya Mahaprabhu had forgotten His *sadhaka’s* vow and, in the ecstasy of a *gopi* in search of Krsna, had climbed to the top of Govardhana Hill.

Whether He found Krsna there or not, Lord Caitanya’s ecstasy caused Him to faint and fall from a considerably high cliff to the ground, where He lay unconscious, oblivious to His condition.

The Lord soon regained consciousness and continued to wander through Kadambavana until He saw a flowering grove and cried with such abandon that the trees also shed tears and the deer fainted.

If He could not find Krsna — either in a cave on Govardhana Hill or in a nearby grove — Lord Caitanya would roar with grief, a roar that stopped peacocks from dancing and caused them to cry in fear.

Because of the extreme ecstasies of separation felt by Lord Caitanya, in which His heart repeatedly broke and His body appeared to sustain injuries, His companions became fearful for His welfare.

Therefore, after Lord Caitanya had completed His Govardhana *parikrama,* Balabhadra Bhattacarya and the other devotees out of love forced the Lord to leave Vraja and return to Jagannatha Puri.

**\* \* \* \***

From Indra-kunda one can see the original temple Madhavendra Puri inspired a wealthy landowner to build for Gopala on top of Govardhana Hill, the place where Gopala was first worshipped.

I walked further along the *parikrama* path and arrived at Surabhi- kunda, which marks the place where Mother Surabhi interceded on King Indra’s behalf as he tried to pacify Krsna.

On Ekadasi, during the bright half of Karttika, two days after He put Govardhana Hill back in its place, Krsna left Nandagrama with His friends and cows for the luscious pastures of Giriraja.

Towards midday Krsna left the company of the cows and *gopas* to circle the perimeter of Giri Govardhana, curious if Indra’s thunderbolts and pelting rains had damaged the hill or its forests.

The power of Giriraja far transcends the power of Indra, but just as Garuda offered a wing to the *vajra* weapon, Giriraja, after seven days of onslaught, showed signs of damage out of deference to Indra.

In the places where thunderbolts had struck, shattering rocks and felling trees, and where the rainwater had flowed leaving deep gullies and other signs of erosion, Giriraja revealed his modesty.

Krsna appreciated Govardhana’s respect for Indra’s authority and the invincibility of Indra’s thunderbolt. That humility underscored the difference between the demigods and Sri Krsna’s devotees.

As Krsna continued to inspect Govardhana Hill, a train of forest animals followed behind Him and a convoy of birds hovered above Him; all expressed their affection with characteristic sounds.

As Krsna passed by, wherever there was some damage to Govardhana Hill, the rocks, trees, or turf resumed the original states they had been in prior to King Indra’s malicious assault.

The nests that had fallen again appeared in the trees, the lairs that had been flooded again filled with dry grasses, and the beehives that had been crushed appeared whole again on their branches.

Forest gardens flowered as before. No longer overrun by landslides, paths were dry and secure. Flooded lakes receded, their waters clear. In short, Govardhana Hill shone in its original eternal splendour.

From afar Indra observed the beauty of Krsna and the marvels of His restoration. After offering obeisances from a distance he approached the Lord with folded hands, but received no recognition.

Krsna walked along, smiling at the animals, occasionally petting a deer or chattering with a peacock, and whenever Indra tried to get Krsna’s attention the Lord acted as if the demigod were not there.

Finally Krsna stopped, looked up at Indra, momentarily met his glance, and then shook His head disdainfully as He walked away with His animal retinue, leaving Indra to burn in the slow fire of neglect.

The king of heaven watched as Krsna strolled away, a cowherd boy surrounded by dancing deer, rabbits, and bears. The chastened *deva's* eyes flooded with tears, knowing full well he was spiritually doomed.

Indra was absorbed in materialism but not ignorant of devotional science. Sobered by defeat, he knew that his offences to Krsna’s devotees were more serious than his offence to Krsna Himself.

Indra recalled how Krsna had once expressed His inability to forgive Durvasa Muni for his offences to Maharaja Ambarisa. The Lord had advised the Muni to immediately beg forgiveness from the king.

“But I have offended so many Vaisnavas,” thought Indra in hopeless lamentation. “How can I beg forgiveness from every cow and Vrajavasi in this land of numberless cows and devotees?”

Indra was bewildered. The thought that his nearly infinite *vaisnava- aparadhas* might very well permanently uproot his accumulated devotional assets made him again consider suicide.

As the sight of Krsna walking off into the distance dissolved into his tears, Indra turned to Mother Surabhi as the only island of hope in an otherwise vast and lonesome universal ocean of hopelessness.

His voice choked, unable to speak, Indra wordlessly communicated to Surabhi, requesting that she fulfil the purpose for which she had accompanied him from heaven: to intercede on his behalf.

Surabhi was wise in the ways of devotion. True to her word, she had Indra follow her as she set out after Krsna, finally coming abreast of the Lord in the garden now home to her lake, Surabhi-kunda.

The Lord was just as heedful of her presence as He had been indifferent to Indra’s. As Surabhi respectfully addressed Krsna, He turned to face His devotee, tilted His turbaned head, and smiled.

Gentle Surabhi felt great ecstasy in the presence of the master of the *surabhi* cows. She repeatedly chanted His names, “Krsna! Krsna!” Finally, she had to compose herself before beginning her prayer.

Surabhi said, “O Lord of the universe! You are the greatest mystic. By Your inconceivable might, You protected my descendants and overpowered foolish Indra, who tried to kill us all.

“Therefore it is You, not he, who is our worshipable king, our Indra. Thus, by the order of Lord Brahma, and on behalf of all the demigods, we wish to offer You an *abhiseka* and install You as our king.”

As they spoke, Krsna and Mother Surabhi walked along the *parikrama* path, climbed up the slope of Govardhana Hill, and came to the place now known as Indra-vedi.

Actually, Surabhi never directly asked Krsna to forgive Indra because she felt herself unqualified to do such a thing; rather, she asked the Lord to forgive Indra at the request of the universal father, Brahma.

Krsna played the part of a cowherd boy just as subordinate to the demands of the creator as was Surabhi. Thus He yielded to her request and agreed to be bathed, and to forgive Indra’s offences.

Surabhi’s merciful patronage represented the forgiveness of the cows, but foolish King Indra, deluded by false pride, made no effort to approach the Vrajavasis directly in order to beg their forgiveness.

The closest he came to achieving the Vrajavasis forgiveness was this: One of the fruits of performing an *abhiseka* is that all living entities give up their animosity towards one another and become friends.

Thus, even though the intention of the *abhiseka* was to inspire the Vrajavasis to forgive Indra, because he did not personally apologise to them, he enjoyed only partial relief from those offences.

The remnants of Indra’s offences to the Vrajavasis became the seeds for further offences to Krsna, of which I have already spoken, and the likes of which all Vaisnavas should very attentively avoid.

As I sat at Surabhi-kunda, I contemplated this very serious aspect of pure devotional service, an aspect I always emphasise whenever I described this pastime to devotees at this sacred place.

Lord Caitanya warns us that committing an offence at the feet of a Vaisnava, while simultaneously cultivating the creeper of devotional service, will cause the leaves of the devotional creeper to dry up.

Offences of this type are compared to mad elephants that uproot the creeper and break it. Devotees must protect their creepers by fencing them in so that the mad elephant of *vaisnava-aparddha* cannot enter.

To construct this fence around our devotional creepers, we should surround ourselves with the company of pure devotees and abandon the company of non-devotees.

In such exalted company, devotees should think themselves lower than the straw in the street. They should be more tolerant than a tree, devoid of false prestige, and ready to offer all respect to others.

Because Vaisnavas are compassionate towards the fallen, and because they fulfil all desires, their company — specifically their transcendental teachings — can bestow the topmost perfection.

**\* \* \* \***

By his divine riches, the forest surrounding Surabhi-kunda is like a full moon; it causes the tides in the shoreless ocean of Radha’s and Krsna’s desires to enjoy pastimes by Govardhana to rise unlimitedly.

A single drop of the ecstasy this transcendental forest inspires within the divine couple far exceeds the vast nectar ocean formed when all other spiritual ecstasies are pooled together.

Filled with wonders that both delight and baffle the mind, blessed by every wonderful fragrance, sweetness, and charm, the beauty of this forest attracts the heart of even the goddess of fortune.

The monarchs of all flowers blossom here year round, and therefore it overflows with *mallika*, *lavanga*, *jdti, yuthika, kadamba, campaka, sthalaravinda, sirisa*, *kunda*, *ketaki, kusumbha,* and *kimsuka* blossoms.

Kadambavana echoes with the voices of birds, like the male and female parrots who recount the confidential pastimes of Radha and Krsna, or the cuckoos and their soft, enchanting *“Kuhu,* kuhu.”

Around the lake are secluded groves ornamented with blossoming flowers and jewel-laden vines, furnished with flower-cots, decorative lamps, and every other comfort Radha and Krsna could wish for.

Glistening, clear nectarean water fills the lake upon which ducks and swans ride gently rolling waves. Jewel-studded staircases line its four shores, and the most splendid *kadamba* trees shade its banks.

Abounding with multi-coloured clusters of *saroja* and *kumuda* lotuses, the deep waters of this lake are refreshingly cool, and reflections of the encircling groves shimmer and dance on its surface.

One fragrant glade is home to a beautiful gemstone cottage draped in flowering vines, whose jewelled floors, crystal pillars, and lapis lazuli dome unfailingly inspire wonder in the minds of the *gopis.*

It was in this cottage that Radha and Krsna were scheduled to meet one night and where, Their plans not quite having come together, a quarrel broke out, kindling the fire of Their loving exchanges.

Krsna was on time for Their rendezvous, but Radha and Her *gopi* friends were late. Amongst other reasons, Radharani had difficulty in escaping unnoticed from Her husband’s home.

Krsna waited and waited, but when Srimati Radharani — the sustainer of Krsna’s life — still had not arrived, He became restless by the influence of love and decided to venture out in search of Her.

Wandering and wandering, Krsna became confused in the moonless night and somehow ended up near a place He did not want to be — a grove of *kadamba* trees not far from Nandagrama.

Meanwhile, Radha had finally arrived at the designated grove by Surabhi-kunda. She was jubilant until She saw that Krsna was nowhere to be found. Then She became despondent.

Now it was Srimati Radharani’s turn to wait and wait. In Her distress, She became so emaciated that Her gold and coral bracelets slid off Her wrists and fell to the ground one by one.

Her friends tried to comfort Her, but only the sight of Krsna could truly console Sri Radha. Therefore they sent out one intelligent *gopi* to find Krsna and bring Him back without delay.

Unable to find footprints in the dark, that gopi followed the compass of her heart. The needle of that compass was her love for Krsna, and its true north always pointed in whichever direction He was.

In due time the *gopi* arrived at a distant grove of *kadamba* trees where she saw many peacocks dancing and found Krsna all alone, thoughtfully gazing at the stars in the moonless sky.

The *gopis* desire to reunite Krsna with Radha defeated the anger she felt towards Him and she said sweetly, “O Madhava! My dear but unhappy friend Radharani eagerly waits for You at Surabhi-kunda.

“She risked everything to escape from Her in-laws, and braved the dangers of a dark, snake-infested forest just to be with You. How could You insult Her by loitering here in Your father’s kingdom?”

Krsna wanted to explain but couldn’t, as the *gopis* anger got the better of her: “Radha looks everywhere for the handsomely smiling flute player who is always decorated with a peacock feather.

“Unable to find Him, She constantly embraces Him within Her heart. Still, She remains unsatisfied and thinks, ‘I’m no yogi. I don’t want to just meditate.’ Thus She wanders about in agitation.”

Already heartbroken, Krsna felt even more devastated to hear this report. Yet He was unable to interrupt the *gopis* monologue: “One moment She gets up to leave, the next moment She sits back down.

“She is so distracted that She does not care to rearrange Her dishevelled hair and clothes. Instead, She calls out in a piteous voice and showers the undergrowth with tears as She rolls on the ground.

“Sometimes Radha runs back and forth. Sometimes She falls down unconscious. At other times She rips Her sari in frustration, breaks Her flower bracelets, or scatters the pearls of Her necklace.”

Krsna held His face in His hands. But the *gopi,* thinking she was successfully shaming Hari, just went on, “One moment She babbles at a tree, the next moment She stumbles towards the Yamuna.

“Her eyes filled with tears, She requested me, ‘O friend! Please find My Krsna, and when you do, with words filled with affection, tell Him that I place all My love at His lotus feet.’”

The *gopi* paused and then appealed to Krsna in a more subdued voice, “Now I think I understand the meandering ways of Your love for Radha. I’m confident that You will follow me and meet Her.

“How could You possibly neglect someone who considers Her own life insignificant when compared to fulfilling Your every desire and reciprocating Your passionate youthful love?”

It may have appeared that Radha’s *gopi* friend did not take into account Krsna’s intense and factual desire to meet Radha, but according to time and circumstance her speech was perfect.

The rules of love dictate that to further soften the heart of an already eager lover and make him more submissive, a messenger may accuse the lover of being callous to the anguish suffered by his beloved.

And as that *gopi* messenger carried on reprimanding Krsna, the creatures of Kadambavana were filled as well with the pain of separation in empathy for Radharani's suffering.

In that condition the deer and birds wailed, the trees and creepers shrivelled up, the bees stopped humming, the fish stopped swimming, the flowers wilted, the stones softened, and water solidified.

Krsna’s anxiety came to a fever pitch. Concerned for the situation at Surabhi-kunda, He went to Radha at once and submitted Himself before Her, much to the delight of Varsana’s princess.

But in Vraja the ways of love are crooked; thus when Krsna arrived, even after She had almost perished in His absence and despite Her genuine relief at His presence, Radha neglected Him.

Krsna tried to embrace Her, but Radha backed away. When He tried to talk to Her, She stubbornly maintained Her silence. And when Krsna pleaded for Her affection, Radha denied Him.

Such are the ever fresh pastimes of Radha and Krsna within the groves of Kadambavana. Remembering them frees devotees from passion’s poison. May these nectarean pastimes protect us all!

**\* \* \* \***

Before leaving Kadambavana, I recalled Sukadeva Gosvamis praise of the powerful effect that hearing about Krsna’s playful affairs with the *gopis* has on those with pure and strong faith.

I prayed, “O forest of Vraja! May that faith with which great souls hear of Krsna’s loving pastimes with the *gopis* — thereby attaining devotional service, freedom from lust, and sober minds — he mine.”

Repeating my prayer over and over while walking alone, I came to Airavata-kunda, where Indra’s elephant deposited the waters of the celestial Ganges he had brought for Krsna’s coronation *abhiseka.*

Originally the purifying effect of this lake equalled that of the Ganges River where it enters the saltwater ocean, but by contact with the soil of Vraja it now rivals the potency of Govinda-kunda.

The river Ganges’ water once washed Lord Vamanadeva’s lotus feet, which are covered by a pinkish powder. Hence, the river also acquired a pinkish colour and the name Visnupadi.

But in the same way that the name “Krsna” is a thousand times more potent than the name “Vamana,” the water that has washed the lotus feet of Krsna is one thousand times more purifying than the Ganges.

Because the dust from Krsna’s feet is scattered throughout Kadambavana — as it is throughout all of Vraja — that dust naturally mixes with the water of Airavata-kunda, making it more potent still.

There is, however, another kind of exceptional dust that increases the sanctity of Airavata-kunda, of any water, indeed of any thing, even more so than the dust of Krsna’s lotus feet.

That dust is the dust from the feet of Krsna’s pure devotees, who are free from personal desire, rapt in meditation on the Lord’s pastimes, without enmity, of equal temperament, and peacefully disposed.

And amongst devotees there is a transcendental hierarchy from Sri Prahlada, to the Pandavas, to the Yadavas, to Uddhava, to the Vraja-vasis, and finally to the *gopis}* the best of whom is Sri Radha.

Therefore, when the dust of the *gopis’* lotus feet mixes with the waters of any lake or river of Vraja, those waters achieve a superlative purity, beyond which only one mystical and ineffable liquid remains.

That liquid can be found in parts of rivers like the Yamuna or in lakes like Radha-kunda in which the *gopis* have bathed, and into which their *kunkuma,* foot lac, eyeliner, and other cosmetics have dissolved.

How something already pure becomes even more pure, especially by contact with a devotee, is described in the appendix entitled “The Purifying Effect of the Dust of Devotees’ Feet on Sacred Waters.”

With the following prayer Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami praises the many lakes garlanding beautiful and glorious Govardhana Hill,

*Sri-brahma-kadamba-khanda-sumano-rudrapsaro-gaurika —*

*Jyotsna-moksana-malyahara-vibudhdrindra-dhvajady-dkhyay-*  
*yani srestha-saramsi bhanti parito govardhanadrer amu nide chakraka-tirtha-daivata-giri sri-ratna-pithany api*

**“Sri-sarovara, Brahma-sarovara, Kadamba-khanda-sarovara, Sumanah-sarovara, Rudra-sarovara, Apsarah-sarovara, Gaurika-sarovara, Jyotsna-moksana-sarovara, Malyahara-sarovara, Vibudhari-sarovara—**

**“Indra-dhvaja-Sarovara, and many other beautiful lakes are splendidly manifested as so many beautiful jewels in a circle around divine Govardhana Hill. Let me glorify these transcendental lakes.”**

## Chapter 14 - Rudra-unda to Nima-grama

I offered my obeisances to Airavata-kunda before continuing my *parikrama* through the orchards of Giriraja, walking in the direction of Rudra-kunda, nestled amongst the trees of Rudravana.

In Bhauma Vrndavana, Rudra-kunda is found in the centre of a larger lake, Haraji-kunda, named after the *gopa* Hara, who played ball with Krsna in a nearby clearing called Gendoka-ksetra.

Vaisnavas come to secluded Rudra-kunda to pay their respects to Lord Siva in his original and most confidential form as the best of all devotees of Sri Krsna *(vaisnavdnam yatha sambhuh*).

Pure devotees never pray to demigods for material benefits, but if a demigod is a pure servant of Krsna — and Lord Siva is such a pure servant — devotees do pray for loving devotional service.

Lord Siva is well known for living in five prominent places in Vraja, but these are not his only residences. He also lives in every forest, beside every lake, and near every grove, hill, and place of pastime.

He does so because he is eager to see Krsna’s pastimes throughout all eight parts of the day. The wise know this secret and thus visit Krsna’s *lila-sthanas* only after seeking Lord Siva’s blessings.

If the deity of Lord Siva is present at a holy *tirtha,* devotees bow before that form and offer him water from a nearby *kunda,* or in the absence of a *kunda*, at least some dust from that holy place.

Lord Siva is especially pleased to receive the remnants of food offered to Radha and Krsna, and devotees should accept Lord Siva’s remnants when given to them, as did Caitanya Mahaprabhu.

Bhrgu Muni once cursed Siva’s followers. Since that incident, pious souls have avoided eating Siva’s remnants. But this curse applies to those who make a distinction between Siva and Krsna, not to others.

As the spiritual master is to be worshipped on the same level as Krsna *(saksad-dharitvena*), Lord Siva is to be seen as no different from Krsna because both are very dear to Krsna *(priya eva tasya).*

Thinking in this way, I bowed to Rudra-kunda and prayed, “O king of devotees! Intoxicated by *prema,* you incessantly dance by the shores of this lake and loudly sing Krsna’s names and pastimes.

“Because you fulfil the desires of your devotees, I request you to bless me with unflinching faith in my beloved Radha-Syamasundara, in Sri Guru, and in you, who are completely surrendered to Krsna.”

Once while deep in meditation here, Siva suddenly called out to the divine couple, “O abodes of mercy! Please be kind to wayward souls and drive away their fears with the gift of pure devotion to You.”

Tears of compassion cascaded from Lord Siva’s eyes and onto the ground, just as the river Ganga falls in powerful streams from his matted locks onto the peaks of the Himalayas.

Those tears formed a lake, later called Rudan-kunda, or Rudra- kunda, because the crying (*rudan*) of Rudra was its genesis. One can easily attain the mercy of the universal father by bathing there.

Devotees will know when they have attained Siva’s mercy. *Karma, jnana, yoga,* and *muka* will no longer attract them; the influence of *maya* gone, they will feel secure in the shade of Krsna’s lotus feet.

Dear devotees, take heed! The Vraja-vasis’ tears are also mixed with the tears shed by Lord Siva, making Rudra-kunda doubly effective in bestowing loving devotion to Radha and Krsna.

The *gopis* once came to this part of Govardhana Hill expecting to see Krsna herding His cows but were heartbroken to learn that He was far away on the banks of the river Yamuna.

Unable to suppress their sorrow, the *gopis* ran to the banks of Rudra- kunda in this secluded forest, where they cried so profusely that their tears caused the *kunda* to overflow its banks and wash their lotus feet.

Sometime later Krsna and Balarama passed this lake on Their way to Mathura. The local Vraja-vasis followed the brothers until they could walk no further, then they too sat by Rudra-kunda and wept.

But not all the pastimes associated with this lake are sad. Rudra- kunda’s deep and clear water, ideal for bathing, made the spot a favourite of Krsna, His cowherd boyfriends, and the *gopis.*

Sometimes, while the cows and cowherd boys rested by the banks of Go-kunda in Syama-dhak, Krsna would sneak away and meet Radha and a few of Her intimate companions at Rudravana.

For relief from the effects of the midday heat, Radha and Krsna would change into bathing clothes and enjoy the refreshing water of Rudra-kunda, which is always full of blossoming, aromatic lotuses.

They would swim together into the middle of the lake where Krsna would abandon Radha — who was not such an expert swimmer — as She desperately treaded water to keep from going under.

Radha would panic and call out to Krsna, “Help Me! I’m going to drown!” Then valiant Krsna would come to the rescue and enjoy Her helpless state as She clung tightly to His neck for safety.

Scripture also says that Rudra-kunda is the embodiment of Giriraja’s anger. This anger is directed at those who fail to observe the Annakuta ceremony, or who argue that Krsna’s form is an illusion.

The supernatural snakes who ornament Lord Siva’s body become furious with such egoistic fools whom they search out, bite, and poison with the venom of misfortune and false doctrines.

Dear Vaisnavas! Do not hesitate to offer prayers to Lord Siva, thinking him to be a mere demigod. Lord Caitanya offered prayers to Siva when the two met. Are we not, then, on safe ground to do so?

Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvat! Thakura once arrived at the train station in Bhuvanesvara and at once offered his obeisances to Lingaraja Siva, reciting this well-known prayer:

**“O Siva! O gatekeeper of Vrndavana. O you who are accompanied by goddess Uma! O you who carry the moon in your hair. O Lord worshipped by Sanandana-kumara, Sanat-kumara, and Narada Muni!**

**“O Gopisvara! O one worshipped by the** ***gopis*****! I beg you to bestow upon me love for the lotus feet of the divine couple, who perform joyous pastimes in Vraja. I repeatedly offer my obeisances to you.”**

The area of Kadambavana that ends at a level sandy plain leading to Yatipura is also known as Kadamba-khandi, another place where Krsna enjoys *rasa* dances with the *gopis.*

No other activity enjoyed by the Lord blends together so many varieties of loving exchanges and moods with as much ecstasy as does the *rasa* dance. Hence it is a source of unrivalled pleasure for Krsna.

Like every one of the Lord’s transcendental pastimes, however, each *rasa* dance must come to an end, an end that draws the curtain on Krsna’s upward spiralling ecstasy, leaving Him hungry for more.

On one such occasion, Krsna and the *gopis* had lost themselves to each other in a love-filled festival of dance, song, and music, until reluctant Yogamaya had to tactfully bring the pastime to an end.

With great difficulty Radha’s friends coaxed Her away from Krsna and put Her on the path leading back to Yavata, the path that gradually led Her out of Krsna’s sight.

Let us meditate upon Krsna, who in Kadamba-khandi, at the first light of dawn, stood listless, flute in hand, tearfully looking down the path upon which Radha had just disappeared from view.

Meditate upon a lonely, dark-complexioned cowherd hoy, who would trade all the material and spiritual universes in His dominion for just one more honey-soaked smile from a fair-skinned milkmaid.

**\* \* \* \***

Yatipura, or Gopalapura, became famous — after Madhavendra Puri had installed Gopala on top of Govardhana Hill — as the place where the *sannyasi (yati)* stored Gopala’s grains and kept His cows.

Madhavendra Puri also lived at this place, along with some of the servants of Gopala. From here they would all climb the hill, as per the Deity’s order, to render Him all kinds of services.

As long as Madhavendra Puri remained in Vrndavana, he would daily arrange a full *annakuta* offering to Gopala, equal to the offering the Deity had received on the day He was installed.

This part of Govardhana Hill is also known as Srngara-sthali, and it is related to the place of the same name on the opposite side of the hill where Madhavendra Puri discovered Gopala Raya.

Gopala was found on the eastern side of Govardhana. He was then installed on top of the hill, and His granary and servants were housed on the western side. All these places are in Srngara-sthali.

It is also said that every evening at sunset Gopala returns to Srngara- sthali in Yatipura to enjoy pastimes with His associates, and at dawn He returns to Nathadvara where He spends His days.

Beneath a large tree at the entrance to Yatipura is one of Lord Caitanya’s resting places and a small lake known as Surya-kunda, where the Vraja-vasis once worshipped the sun god.

Caitanya Mahaprabhu rested at three places during His *parikrama* of Govardhana Hill: Indra-kunda near Manasi-ganga, Surya-kunda at Yatipura, and Moksa-kunda just outside Govardhana town.

Scripture offers guidelines to devotees performing *parikrama* of the holy places: one should avoid practices or austerities that obstruct one’s absorption in Krsna’s pastimes and thoughts of the *dhama.*

Thus Caitanya Mahaprabhu taught by example that one should rest upon becoming weary or while in pain on *parikrama.* Then, once one is refreshed, *parikrama* can be resumed in the proper consciousness.

The tomb of Viththalesvara — to whom Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami transferred custodianship of the Deity Gopala — and a stone of Govardhana called the Dandavat-sila are found in Yatipura.

One who offers prostrated obeisances *(dandavat)* and then circles this stone seven times is freed from offences committed during *parikrama* or any other offences made inadvertently against Giriraja.

On the outskirts of Yatipura is Mara-kunda where Madhavendra Puri kept Gopala’s granary. There the Deity’s grains were husked, cleansed, and sacked before being sent to the Lord’s kitchen.

The husking of wheat and thrashing of rice made a depression in the ground, a basin the rains soon filled to form a lake. The locals called it Mara-kunda — the lake formed by beating *(mdra)* grains.

On the opposite side of the *parikrama* path is a small forest known as Vilasavana. The secluded nature of this forest makes it a special favourite of Radha and Krsna for Their charming pastimes *(vilasa).*

Sometimes Radha and Krsna sit in the shade of Vilasavana’s trees and marvel at the beauty of Govardhana Hill, and sometimes They cool off in the transparent waters of a lake called Vilasa-kunda.

One spring day Radha and Krsna made a plan to meet at Surabhi-kunda. To avoid attracting attention, Krsna was to come, alone, from the south, and Radha, with only one maidservant, from the north.

Krsna arrived at the appointed place at the appointed time, but Radhika was not there. “Never mind,” He thought. “It’s a woman’s prerogative to be late for a meeting with Her beloved!”

But Radha was very late. At first Krsna merely struggled with impatience, but as time wore on He became increasingly concerned for Radha’s safety, knowing well that She and Her friend were alone.

And there was good reason for concern. Rumour had it that Karhsa was distracted to no end by reports of Radha’s breathtaking beauty, and that he had been making plans to steal Her away to Mathura.

The very thought of Kamsa’s impudence made Krsna clench His fists and grit His teeth. Unable to wait any longer, He set out in the direction from which Radharani should have come.

Radha was not late because of any grave mishap, but unforeseen circumstances had nonetheless delayed Her arrival. On a detour through Vilasavana to collect flowers for Krsna She lost an ankle bell.

She had retraced Her steps to the *parikrama* path and then come back to Vilasavana. “I am sure I lost it somewhere amongst these trees,” She told Her young companion, “but where, I don’t know.”

As a princess, Radha had many ankle bells at home. She would hardly feel the loss of one. But She was particularly fond of this one because Krsna had given it to Her at a very special moment.

Shining jewels studded this delicate golden chain, from which hung tiny golden bells. The *cataka-*like sound of this anklet could immediately chase away the swan of Krsna’s patience.

Determined to find the anklet, Radha called it by name, “Ratna- gopura! Where are you?” But receiving no reply simply compounded the anxiety She felt for being late for the rendezvous.

Just then Krsna happened to be walking along the main road. As He passed the turnoff to Vilasavana, He heard a woman’s voice coming from the forest. “Ratna-gopura! Where are you?”

“That’s Radha’s voice!” He thought, taking the turnoff to Vilasavana. Krsna hurried into the forest to find Radha and Her girlfriend intently searching the woods, their eyes fixed on the ground.

Radha was so absorbed in Her search that She did not notice Krsna until She came upon two blackish feet just in front of Her. Looking up, She happily exclaimed, “O Syama! I’m so happy You came.”

“What are You searching for?” Krsna asked. Srimati Radharani answered, Her every word resounding with fixed resolve, “I’ve lost My favourite ankle bell and am determined to find it!”

Looking left and right and then back at Radha, Krsna said, “It doesn’t seem to be here. Let’s go to Surabhi-kunda as We planned. Later I’ll have another anklet made to replace the lost one.”

“No!” replied Radha. “I will not leave My dear anklet alone in these woods.” Krsna winced. He was eager to spend the precious little time He had sporting with Radha, not looking for lost jewellery.

“Can’t we look for it another time?” He suggested. But Radha’s dismissive glance was a sure sign that if He continued to challenge Her, His time would be completely lost in a heated argument.

Krsna acquiesced, “Here, I’ll help You find it.” He held Radha’s right hand in His left, and side by side They searched the forest floor, look' ing under bushes and the roots of trees, without success.

Radha let go of Krsna’s hand and pointed to the far end of the forest garden. “You look over by the lake,” She said. “I’ll look here, and My maidservant can search the path one more time.”

The idea of leaving Radha did not appeal to Krsna in the least. He appeared to be submissively following Her order, but within His mind He resolved to bring this lost-ankle-bell saga to a speedy end.

While searching the flower garden by Vilasa-kunda, Krsna came upon a beautiful desire-vine blossoming with different coloured flowers so aromatic that they would have made Cupid’s head spin.

Krsna cut off a length of vine, expertly braided it into a flower anklet, and called out, “Look at this!” He quickly ran over to Radha and, without showing Her His artwork, tied it on Her bare ankle.

Radha looked down expecting to see Her favourite anklet, only to find a floral substitute. Unimpressed, She chided, “Why don’t You look for My jewellery instead of playing with flowers.”

Once again Krsna returned to the *kunda.* Radha had become so stubbornly fixed on Her evasive anklet that the entire day could be lost, along with Krsna’s chances for loving exchange.

He needed a better plan, and He needed it quickly. Krsna took the flute from His belt and dug at the ground as He prayed, “Dear mother earth! Please return My beloved’s lost anklet at once.”

No sooner had Krsna pronounced these words than the hole He had dug and the area surrounding it was filled with anklets, some identical to the lost one, others more beautiful and more opulent.

“Look! Look! I found it,” Krsna called out. “It's here.” Radha and Her friend rushed over to where Krsna was kneeling. As their expectant eyes met the ground, their mouths dropped open.

As a greedy king would revel in his trove of royal jewels, Krsna was running His fingers through heaps of ankle bells in the ground, each one more dazzling and ornate than the one before.

Radha was speechless. Not willing to take any risks, however, Krsna dropped the anklets in His hands and retrieved one that perfectly matched the missing jewellery. “Here it is!” He declared.

She took the ankle hell from Krsna’s hand. Still incredulous, but Her voice filled with both gratitude and curiosity, Srimati Radharani asked, “Where did all the other ones come from?”

Krsna replied with a knowing smile, “By remaining in constant touch with Your lotus foot, the lost anklet became a *cintamani* gem that has now transformed the earth it touched into anklets.

“Dearest! If You don’t put it on at once,” Krsna joked, “then the whole of Vraja will be paved with ankle bells.” At that, Radha broke out in the first broad, beauteous smile of the day.

As a reward for Krsna’s resourcefulness, Srimati Radharani gracefully put Her lotus foot forward and said, “Then You had better secure it on My foot Yourself, so it doesn’t fall off again.”

Not one to pass up a gift of Providence, Krsna thankfully took the anklet from Radharani’s hand and attentively fastened it around Her ankle, taking as much time as He possibly could.

That morning Radha and Krsna remained in Vilasavana and enjoyed intimate pastimes, attended upon by one young girl, and when midday arrived, each left on the same path by which They had come.

Vaisnavas say that many anklets with tiny golden bells are still to be found on one bank of Vilasa-kunda, but they also say that only those who have unflinching faith in the divine couple can find them.

During Aurangzeb’s reign, soldiers were dispatched to destroy the deities of Vrndavana, and Harideva was immersed here in Vilasa- kunda because the lake’s depth discouraged anyone from searching it.

All glories to Vilasavana! There, all things lost are easily found. There, even souls who have lost their eternal identities as servants of Krsna can find them, by the grace of this forest and the holy name.

**\* \* \* \***

Continuing my *parikrama* towards Govardhana town, I came upon a small garden that represents another garden in Vraja. In that garden Krsna likes to play hide-and-seek with His friends.

In fact, Krsna likes that garden so much that when Gopala’s temple was being built on the hilltop, the Deity instructed His *pujari* to include a window in His room so that He could see the garden.

Two prominent features of this garden, in addition to its beauty and tranquility, are the two ancient trees Jnana and Ajnana, in connection to which two different transcendental pastimes are told.

In the first, Srimati Radharani, accompanied by two maidservants, Jnana and Ajnana, met Krsna in this garden. Because Krsna did not know these girls, He felt shy in their presence.

Krsna’s sheepishness annoyed Radha and embarrassed the two girls, who then transformed themselves into trees to facilitate the spontaneity of the divine couple’s rendezvous.

In the second version of this pastime, the damsels of Vraja were once wandering around Govardhana Hill asking the trees, birds, and animals if they knew where Krsna was herding His cows that day.

When the girls asked these two trees, one tree responded, “I know” *(jnana),* and the other said, “I don’t know” *(ajnana).* From that day on the trees have been called Jnana and Ajnana.

To the left, at a distance from the *parikrama* path, is the village of Ganthuli and the lake called Gulala-kunda, both of which are connected to Radha’s and Krsna’s pastimes of playing Holi.

The Vraja-vasis celebrate the Holi festival from Vasanta-pancami until a few days after Phalguna-purnima. During that period, Krsna and Balarama are given leave from cow herding.

Holi is played on various days in various towns. For example, in Varsana Holi is on the ninth day from the full moon, Nandagrama on the tenth, and Vrndavana the eleventh to the fifteenth.

During the festival, the children of Vraja are allowed to play Holi in the forest every day, but they can only play in the towns and villages on scheduled days, when the adults also join in the fun.

Once, on the first day of Holi, the *gopas* conspired to attack the *gopis.* Early in the morning they lay in wait in the forest just outside Nandagrama as Radha and Her friends came to cook for Krsna.

As the girls passed through a small grove of *kadamba* trees, the boys leaped out from behind the trees and attacked, throwing coloured powder and spraying different liquid dyes on the unsuspecting *gopis.*

In no time the air became a rainbow-coloured haze, thick with cries of “Victory! Victory!” When the air cleared, the girls were covered head to toe in hues of bright red, blue, green, and orange.

In the confusion of flying colours, cheering boys, and shrieking girls, Krsna dove into the vibrant melee and quickly embraced Srimati Radharani, repeatedly kissing Her on the cheeks.

But Lalita-devi quickly came to Radha’s rescue. After pulling Her away from Krsna, Lalita gathered the gopis together and herded them to Nandagrama, where after bathing and dressing, they began to cook.

Ganthuli was the ideal place for playing Holi. It was remote, yet conveniently situated near the road towards Dig. And it had a lake in which the *gopas* and *gopis* could bathe after playing.

Srila Prabhupada writes that according to hearsay, Radha and Krsna first met in Ganthuli, and that the name comes from Their undoing the knot *(gantha)* that had tied Their cloth together.

One spring day a great Holi battle raged in Ganthuli. The *gopis* on one side were evenly matched by the *gopas* on the other. Indeed, neither side could gain a decisive victory over their opponents.
