Eventually, Radha became fatigued from over-exertion and sat down upon a golden throne. A breathless Krsna soon joined Her, and together They cheered on Their respective followers.

The boys and girls threw balls of crimson powder that either exploded on target or burst in the air, settling upon the trees, vines, and grass, as well as on the faces, limbs, and clothes of the combatants.

Madhumangala would run to the front lines and incite the girls by dancing around. When the *gopis* charged, throwing balls of powder or squirting liquid colour, he would retreat to hide behind the boys.

Most of the children threw colours, but some played instruments and others sang. Excited birds like the peacock and cuckoo would then add their calls to the music while circling above the battlefield.

Intoxicated by the fun, some *gopis* decided to play a trick on Radha and Krsna. Approaching Them from behind, the girls tied the divine couple’s outer garments together with a knot *(gantha*).

Then, from the front, both the boys and girls suddenly attacked their peacefully seated monarchs, dousing Them so completely with colour that They looked like two vermilion Hanuman deities.

Naturally, Radha and Krsna tried to escape the surprise attack, but when They rose to flee, they were restrained by Their tied cloth. Thus They could not avoid being pelted by Their rowdy followers.

Humiliated, Radha and Krsna ran for shelter in a *kunja.* There Krsna was able to use the pointed end of His syringe *(phaguya)* to untie the knot. Both leaders then rejoined Their warring armies.

An alternate narration tells that when Radha and Krsna retreated to Their throne the Holi battle subsided. Then, as the divine couple’s clothes were knotted, the boys and girls sang wedding songs to Them.

Radha and Krsna, flustered by these new developments, rose from Their places but were unable to part ways. Further humiliated, They vented Their anger at Their mischievous friends.

Once the merriment had subsided, Krsna sent the boys home. He and the *gopis* then bathed in the nearby lake, washed the *gulala* from their bodies, and again dressed in fresh clothes and ornaments.

It is said that at times during the spring season coloured dyes become visible in the waters of Gulala-kunda, the sounds of unseen children playing Holi permeate the air, and the trees are dusted with *gulala.*

By the time Caitanya Mahaprabhu came to Vrndavana, Ganthuli had already become a bustling village. Mahaprabhu interrupted His *parikrama* of Govardhana Hill to stay in Ganthuli for three days.

As previously mentioned, Caitanya Mahaprabhu had wanted to see beautiful Gopala, but the Deity was worshipped on top of the hill and the Lord would not set foot upon the holy mount.

While bathing and resting at Govinda-kunda, however, Lord Caitanya heard that, out of fear of the Muslim king’s soldiers, the priests of Gopala had taken the Deity into hiding at Ganthuli.

Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu continued circumambulating Govardhana Hill, and when He reached Yatipura He detoured to Ganthuli. Upon seeing Gopala, Mahaprabhu was overwhelmed with ecstatic emotion.

The Lord then recited this famous verse: “May Sri Krsna, who raised Govardhana Hill with His left arm as if it were a toy and whose eyes are like the petals of a lotus flower, protect you.”

For the rest of that day and the days that Gopala remained in Ganthuli, Caitanya Mahaprabhu chanted and danced before the Deity, continuing His unparalleled pr*ema-sankirtana.*

On the fourth day, news arrived that the king’s soldiers had returned to Mathura and that Gopala could return to His temple. Lord Caitanya went with the Lord, along with a large crowd of people.

Caitanya Mahaprabhu chanted and danced all the way to Govardhana Hill, amazing everyone with His display of divine love and inspiring them to chant the name of Krsna, “Hari! Hari!”

When Gopala arrived at the foot of Govardhana Hill, however, Caitanya Mahaprabhu remained there, offering His obeisances to the Deity and feeling blissful that Gopala had kindly satisfied His desire.

The pastime of Gopala leaving His temple to please Caitanya Mahaprabhu shows how kind the Lord is to His devotees, who are overwhelmed by love when they hear such sweet dealings of the Lord.

To fulfil Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s eager desire to see Him, the Deity of Gopala orchestrated a drama in which He left His grand temple on the hilltop to hide in a simple village cottage.

Gopala lived in a bush so that Madhavendra Puri could find and serve Him, and in the same way, the Deity lived in a cottage so that Caitanya Mahaprabhu could see and glorify Him.

Devotees who recognise that Krsna is not merely the supreme enjoyer (*rasika-sekhara*) but that He is also very kind to His devotees (*bhakta- vatsala),* regularly go to see the Lord in His Deity form.

**\* \* \* \***

I walked along the foot of Giriraja, as did Lord Caitanya in Bhauma Vrndavana long ago. The Lord marvelled at the serenity of Krsna’s abode, which has been home to great sages since time immemorial.

Govardhana town is not a mere urban centre or marketplace, but is, like Mathura, home to many places made holy by the pastimes of Krsna or by pastimes of the Lord’s transcendental devotees.

Govardhana is as ancient as Mathura, and it was the capital of Vraja for many millennia after the reign of Lord Ramacandra. What follows is the history of how the famous town of Govardhana was founded.

In ancient times, Haryasva, a descendant of Maharaja Iksvaku, ruled the kingdom of Ayodhya. Haryasva’s wife was Madhumati, the beautiful daughter of the demon Madhu.

Haryasva’s brother drove him out of his kingdom, but the unfortunate king took shelter of his father-in-law, who granted him all the land in Vrndavana, except for the city of Madhuvana.

The demon Lavana already ruled Madhuvana, so Haryasva looked for a suitable place to erect his own capital, finally selecting a scenic valley that spanned the middle of Govardhana Hill.

It was in this valley, between two peaks of Govardhana Hill, that Haryasva built his palace, and around it, a city named Govardhana, which became the capital of his kingdom.

Third in descent from Yadu was the son of Haryasva, Bhima, under whose reign Rama commissioned Satrughna to kill Lavanasura and destroy Madhuvana. In its stead, Satrughna erected Mathura.

After Satrughna returned to Ayodhya, Mathura remained in the possession of his descendants until the time of Kamsa, who made Mathura the capital of his kingdom, which included Vraja.

During the time of Lord Rama, Maharaja Mandhata, the son of Yuvanasva, lived in Mathura. Mandhata was so powerful that thieves and rogues, including even the great Ravana, feared him.

By the mercy of Lord Ramacandra, Maharaja Mandhata later became emperor of Bharatavarsa. Indeed, from Mathura, he ruled the entire world, consisting of seven islands, without anyone to contest him.

Mandhata knew the principles of self-realisation; he therefore worshipped Lord Visnu, who comprises all the demigods, by performing sacrifices, and gave immense charity to the *brahmanas.*

Maharaja Mandhata had three sons: Purukutsa, Ambarisa, who was a great devotee, and Mucukunda, a potent mystic yogi. He also had fifty daughters who all accepted the great sage Saubhari as their husband.

Later I will describe the place in the Yamuna where Saubhari Muni performed austerities and where he offended Garuda, as a result of which he fell down from the life of celibacy.

But now I will describe how, on the northern bank of Manasa-ganga, Saubhari Muni lived in an opulent palace with fifty beautiful wives until the time he became detached from them.

Saubhari Muni was engaged in austerity in the waters of the Yamuna. Upon seeing a pair of fish mating, he became sexually inclined and approached Mandhata for one of the king’s daughters.

The King was amicable to the yogi’s proposal, but before Saubhari was to choose a bride, the Muni decided to use his mystic power to transform his old, infirm body into a young, attractive one.

Saubhari Muni returned to the king’s palace as a handsome youth who could attract even the celestials, and all fifty princesses were so infatuated that they willingly accepted him as their husband.

On the strength of his austerities and the perfection of his mantras, Saubhari Muni manifested an opulent home in Govardhana town, replete with parks, gardens, and clear water lakes.

The gardens were fragrant with varieties of flowers, opulent with singing birds and humming bees, and always resounding with the expert performances of professional musicians.

The great sage’s residence was staffed with well-dressed maidservants and valets and furnished with beds, seats, ornaments, bathing facilities, and all the accoutrements of a royal household.

Surrounded by such opulence, the Muni quickly forgot the goal of life and lost himself to the temporary pleasures that come from absorption in family affairs with so many beautiful wives.

Mandhata Maharaja once came to visit his daughters and was so struck with wonder at the household opulence of Saubhari Muni that he decided to abandon his post as emperor of the world.

Like Visvamitra, who became astounded by the superior yogic power of Vasistha Muni, Mandhata understood that austerity and penance offered greater rewards than those won by royal influence.

Saubhari Muni continued to enjoy heavenly pleasures, but he was not satisfied, for sense gratification merely inflames one’s material desires. It may offer momentary relief, but it does nothing to satisfy the soul.

The influence of Govardhana, Krsna’s eternal abode, gradually purified the heart of Saubhari Muni. One day he found himself sitting alone, contemplating the cause and the results of his downfall.

Finally the spirit of devotional detachment again arose within the sage’s heart. He then left his opulent household and, along with his wives, retired into the forest of Vrndavana.

Again Saubhari Muni performed severe penances and turned his mind to pure devotional service, by which he and his wives attained perfection and returned to the spiritual world.

Another resident of Govardhana town was a rich *brahmana* devotee of Lord Balarama named Artha Vanta, who lived during the time of Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s advent in Navadvipa.

As a pure devotee, this *brahmana* was always absorbed in thoughts of Lord Balarama’s character and pastimes, and he was firmly convinced that one day he would see his worshipable Deity.

Knowing that Krsna and the cowherd boys eternally tend their cows on Govardhana Hill, the *brahmana* would always wander around the area, calling out the names of Balarama in a faltering voice.

Meanwhile, Lord Nityananda had come to Vrndavana and was staying at Srngara-vata. As the Supersoul, He decided to fulfil the *brahmanas* desire; thus He went to Govardhana on pilgrimage.

Lord Nityananda stayed in a secluded place, out of the public eye, but the incomparable beauty of His spiritual body bewildered the few saints who were fortunate enough to see Him.

One day, as the *brahmana* was wandering around Govardhana, he saw Nityananda Prabhu. By Nityananda’s beauty and effulgence the *brahmana* understood that He must not be an ordinary person.

Carrying offerings of yogurt, milk, cheese, and butter, the *brahmana* came to Lord Nityananda, offered his obeisances and prayed, “Please bless me with *darsana* of my beloved Lord, Rohini-nandana.”

Lord Nityananda withheld His identity but accepted the offering. He then gave His remnants to the *brahmana,* who returned to his home, ate the *maha-prasada,* and became agitated with love.

That night the *brahmana* saw Lord Nityananda in a dream, first as an *avadhuta* and then as Lord Balarama, dressed in silk finery and ornamented with precious jewels set in gold.

The next morning the *brahmana* hurried to where he had first met Nityananda Prabhu. After falling at the Lord’s feet and offering Him prayers, the *brahmana* narrated what he had seen in his dream.

Lord Nityananda held the *brahmanas* hand, and mildly smiling, spoke the essence of spiritual truths. He then held up a small stone and said, “Cover this *sila* in gold so that I can wear it on My neck.”

The *brahmana* devotee of Lord Balarama bowed and said, “I will do as You order. But in my dream I saw You decorated with golden ornaments. I would also like to have those fashioned for You.”

Lord Nityananda shook His head and replied, “One day I will be decorated as You have seen, but for now your service is to set this Govardhana-sila in gold and tell no one of your dream.”

The *brahmana* followed the Lord’s orders, and Lord Nityananda wore the Govardhana-sila around His neck, staying on the outskirts of Govardhana town for a few days more before leaving Vrndavana.

Many years later, at the house of Raghava Pandita in Panihati, Lord Nityananda expressed a desire to wear ornaments as envisioned by the Govardhana *brahmana,* and His followers happily obliged Him.

**\* \* \* \***

Past Govardhana town, beside the *parikrama* path, are the woods called Sakhivana. The village named Sakhi- sthali and the lake known by the names Sakhi-kunda and Lilavati-kunda are also found here.

Govardhana Malla a revenue collector for Karnsa and close friend of Radha’s husband, Abhimanyu, lives there with his wife Candravali, whom scriptures declare to be Krsna’s second-favourite *gopi.*

To say that Candravali is Radha’s competitor is to speak the truth, but what kind of competitor is she? She is a competitor whose sole purpose is to enhance the divine couple’s loving pastimes.

Radha, and Radha alone, is the foremost abode of ecstatic love for Krsna. For the purpose of nourishing that love, Candravall expands from a tiny particle of Radha’s transcendental form.

Candravall appears as Radha’s competitor *(vipaksa)* in order to nourish the divine couple’s love for each other. But her role as Radha’s rival is only a facade, one of the many wonders of divine love.

The competition between Radha’s *gopis* and Candravalis *gopis* makes spiritual mellows glorious. But because Radha alone controls Krsna, She consistently defeats Her rivals, who therefore always sulk.

Amongst the many differences in the love these two *gopis* have for Krsna, one stands out above all others: Candravall thinks herself to be Krsna’s property, while Radha thinks that Krsna is Her property.

Radha’s love *(madhu-sneha)* is more attractive to Krsna because, like honey (*madhu*), it is sweet and familiar, whereas Candravalis love *(ghrta-sneha*), like ghee (ghrta), is cool and formal.

Candravali and Radha are closely related as cousins because their fathers, Candrabhanu and Vrsabhanu, are brothers. This blood tie further intensifies their rivalry for Krsna’s love.

Srimati Radharani’s friends marry into families of Yavata in order to serve Radharani; likewise, Candravali’s companions marry into the families of Sakhi-sthali to serve Candravali.

It is said that the sixty-four main girlfriends of Candravali also live in Sakhi-sthali and that they excavated Lilavati-kunda in much the same way that Radha’s many companions excavated Radha-kunda.

During Uddhava’s visit to Vrndavana, he would sometimes sit beneath a large tree by the banks of Lilavati-kunda, and *gopis* from every group would come to hear him talk of Krsna’s pastimes.

Uddhava would describe the opulence of Krsna’s city, palaces, and queens, and also of Krsna’s constant longing for Vraja and the *gopis.* When the girls heard of Krsna’s sorrow they all cried together.

The various groups of cowherd girls competed for Krsna’s love in various ways and with various moods, but in their desire for Krsna’s happiness, they were all of one heart.

The milkmaids of Vraja knew that neither the opulences of Dvaraka nor the love of His queens could make Krsna as happy as could the simplicity of Vraja and the *gopis’* paramour love, and so they cried.

At first Radharani was reluctant to hear anything from Uddhava. In fact, She openly revealed Her doubts, “O friend! I do not know what good will come from the mouth of this youth.

“Once, he approached My rivals, spoke My name, and confirmed to them that I was the queen of Krsna’s life. Now I fear this messenger will tell us that Krsna’s love for Me is no more.

“O friend! I will not hear anything he has to say, nor will I ask him any questions about Krsna. Indeed, I will not stay at this place a moment longer. Let us return to our homes at once.”

To reassure the milkmaids that Krsna had not forgotten them — but quite the opposite, that He was thinking of them at every moment — Uddhava raised both his hands for emphasis.

Having caught their attention, Uddhava spoke these amazing words,

*viyogininam api paddhatim vo na yogino gantuim api ksamante*  
*yad dhyeya-rupasya parasya pumso yuyam gata dhyeya-padam durapam*

**“O** ***gopis,*** **separated from Krsna! Your good fortune is most rare. While perfected yogis meditate on the transcendental form of the Supreme Lord, that same Supreme Person constantly meditates on you.”**

A wave of satisfaction swept through the *gopis*’ hearts as they heard Uddhava’s declaration, and they exchanged cheerful smiles. But Radha’s friend remained troubled, and so she addressed him:

“Since the day of Krsna’s departure Radha continually cries, and the tears that wet Her face will not allow Her to rest. O wise one! Can you tell us what Radha can do to get even a moment’s sleep?”

Uddhava knew that Radha could only find solace in the messages he had brought from Krsna if there was some hope that Krsna would return to Vrndavana, and so he addressed Her directly.

“O Radha with beautiful thighs!” said Uddhava. “Don’t stain Your moonlike face with tears. Lord Hari, who is an ocean of mercy, will be merciful to You again. He will return to You. Do not fear!”

These words comforted Srimati Radharani, and She settled down amongst the cowherd girls of Her own group. Then Uddhava, as requested by Krsna, read a message to Her.

Taking up a palm leaf letter penned by Krsna, Uddhava looked straight at Radha to tell Her this message was specifically for Her. Then, his hands and voice trembling slightly, he read the contents.

“O My beloved! On the day We first met, Your love appeared suddenly and without cause. I now live in the hope that Your love has neither diminished by My offences, nor increased by My prayers.

“I live by the memory of Your love, which is always full of nectar and which destroys the miseries of the three worlds, but after such a long time I cannot quite describe Your intense feelings for Me.

“Separated by a great distance, the possibility of our conversing directly is distant. So be it! The possibility of My embracing Your transcendental body is also distant. So be it!

“However, again and again I bow down before Your lotus feet and beg: O girl with beautiful eyebrows! Please always count Me amongst Your followers and please send Me a letter written by Your own hand.”

Day after day the *gopis* spent their time in this way — hearing from Uddhava the messages Krsna had sent to them, and hearing of their Lord’s many pastimes in the far-off metropolis of Dvaraka.

And although loving ecstasy awoke many contrary emotions within the gopis— sometimes critical of Krsna, sometimes sympathetic to Him — they could not stop hearing about their beloved.

Even as the *gopis* denounced Krsna for the cowardice of killing Vali and the cruelty of disfiguring Surpanakha, though allegedly willing to give up His friendship, they couldn’t give up talking about Him.

Many devotees who, having heard Krsna’s pastimes just once, give up their comfortable homes and affectionate families to live in Vrndavana and beg for their living, just like birds.

Likewise, the *gopis* could not give up hearing about Krsna’s qualities and pastimes, even if that hearing constantly reminded them of their separation from Him and the misery they felt as a result.

Thus, in Sakhivana, reputed for its large-leafed *paldsa* trees, Uddhava spoke of Krsna to the *gopis* and rode the waves of their ecstatic bliss as pieces of driftwood are tossed about by the sea.

Later, while Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami was residing at Radha-kunda and living on as much buttermilk daily as one *paldsa* leaf-cup could hold, his friend Dasa came to Sakhi-sthali for its large leaves.

The Vraja-vasi, Dasa, thought to ease the pain of Raghunatha’s severe austerity by giving him buttermilk in a larger leaf-cup, but when the Gosvami came to know from where the leaf had come he was upset.

It displeased Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami to receive service from anyone not favourable to his worshipable Deity, Srimati Radharani, not to mention from Radha’s chief rival, Candravali.

By rejecting the larger leaf-cup of buttermilk, Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami instructed the followers of Lord Caitanya in the process and the mood of single-pointed devotion *(ekanta-bhakti).*

Narottama Dasa Thakura has requested us to adopt this form of devotion, in which one is exclusively attached to his or her worshipable Deity, serving that Deity without deviation.

One example of *ekanta-bhakti* is Hanumanji, who spoke the following famous words, “Although He is not different from Lord Narayana, lotus-eyed Lord Rama alone is the be-all and end-all of my life.”

**\* \* \* \***

Just past Sakhi-sthali is one of my favourite places of pilgrimage Uddhava-kunda. Whether in Bhauma Vrndavana or in New Vraja-dhama, I always stop here for a long rest and deep contemplation.

Vajranabha established this place (complete with a *kunda,* temple, and Deities) as a memorial to Uddhava, who lives in this area as grass, and who appeared here in person to recite *Srimad-Bhagavatam.*

The entire area from Uddhava-kunda across Govardhana Hill to Kusuma-sarovara is intricately linked not only to Uddhavaji but also to Vajra, Maharaja Pariksit, and the original renovation of Vraja.

Sitting against an apple tree, I leaned back to remember the pastimes that precipitated Uddhava’s narrating *Srimad-Bhagavatam* by Kusuma-sarovara and leading a *kirtana* throughout this entire area.

Dear Vaisnavas, please hear this narration with care, for doing so will strengthen your devotion to Krsna, mature your faith in the *dhama,* and inspire you to pursue *prema* as your only goal in life.

Maharaja Yudhisthira passed on the aegis of the world empire to his grandson Pariksit, posted Vajra, the great-grandson of Lord Krsna, as the king of Mathura, and then completely renounced this world.

Not long after the Pandavas had rejoined Krsna's pastimes in the spiritual sky, Pariksit Maharaja decided to visit Mathura, where the queens of Dvaraka had been placed under King Vajra’s care.

The king of Mathura, being exclusively devoted to the Supreme Lord Sri Krsna, received his great devotee-uncle not only as a servant receives his master but also as one pure devotee welcomes another.

Pariksit Maharaja was very affectionate to Vajra and assured him that he need not concern himself with finance or defence. Vajra needed only to care for Krsna’s wives and to enjoy the prosperous kingdom.

“Avoid all anxieties,” said Maharaja Pariksit, “and leave them to me.” Vajra was humbled by the emperor’s kindness, and so he removed his crown, joined his palms at his chest, and replied: “Since your father, the great Abhimanyu, personally taught me how to use a bow, I have no fear of enemies. However, I have been troubled by the desolation of the forests surrounding Mathura.

“My capital city is thriving, its residents prosperous and happy, but the area that surrounds Mathura is virtually uninhabited, its villages deserted. To where have all of Vraja’s people gone?”

Pariksit Maharaja could at once understand from Vajra’s statement that, at the time of Krsna’s leaving the world from Prabhasa, the Vraja-vasis had also left their residences in Vrndavana.

In response to what Pariksit could perceive as a new pastime beginning to unfold, he invited Sandilya Rsi, the spiritual master of Nanda Maharaja, to advise Vajra under the given circumstances.

When Sandilya received Pariksit Maharaja’s invitation, he at once left his cottage and proceeded to Mathura. There he was greeted respectfully and honoured with the proper pomp and ceremony.

Quickly grasping the situation, Sandilya Muni consoled the two devotees by unravelling the mysteries of Krsna’s disappearance, and by explaining their duties as guardians of the Lord’s spiritual legacy.

The sage said, “Please listen attentively! The root "vraj" means "to spread", and because the transcendental, blissful land of Sri Krsna is spread everywhere, it is called Vraja.

“In the eternal land of Vraja, Krsna’s very self *(atma)* is Sri Radha, and because He eternally takes pleasure (*rama)* in Her, scriptures and sages call Him *atmarama*, He who enjoys within Himself.

“In addition to Radharani, a multitude ofgopis, *gopas,* and cows fulfil *(apta*) Krsna’s every desire (*kama*), and therefore He is also known as *apta-kama,* one whose desires are all fulfilled.

“Now, when Krsna appeared on earth in the region of Mathura, He manifested before everyone’s eyes His eternal abode and His eternal companions like Radha, Balarama, and Yasoda.

“At that time some fortunate souls — demigods, devotees, and others— entered Krsna’s abode and transcendental pastimes, and by contact with the Lord and His associates, they attained perfection.

“After Krsna’s *mausala-lila,* the demigods — whom Krsna had taken to Dvaraka—returned to heaven, the devotees were elevated to the status of eternal associates, and others, like Kamsa, were liberated.

“Thereafter, the land of Vraja, and Krsna’s eternal companions there, again became imperceptible to material vision, so when ordinary people came to visit they saw only abandoned land and went away.

“O Vajranabha, son of Aniruddha!” said the sage emphatically. “Ordinary people see your kingdom as the twelve desolate forests of Vrndavana, but realised souls see the eternal abode of God.

“For the welfare of the common man and transcendentalist alike, colonise the original villages of Vraja and name them according to the corresponding pastimes Krsna enacted there.

“Rebuild Govardhana, Dirghapura, Mahavana, Nandagrama, and Brhatsanu. Restore the river banks, lakes, groves, and mountains to their original glory. In this way you will serve the earth.

“O pious king! Do not doubt this! By my blessings and by the strength of your own devotion, you will succeed. Your kingdom will flourish, your subjects will prosper, and the Lord will be pleased.

“For future generations, restore and preserve the places of Krsna’s pastimes, and you will gain not only unending fame but also the mercy of this land of divine existence, knowledge, and bliss.”

Sandilya Muni paused for a moment to catch and hold Vajra’s gaze, “Listen my son! By the blessings of Vrndavana, you and Krsna’s queens will meet Uddhava and receive from him perfect knowledge.

“Hearing the truths of Krsna from Uddhava will qualify you to hear Krsna’s pastimes. And when you hear Krsna’s pastimes with love, you will be able to enter into them and meet the Lord.

“Heard from Uddhava, the transcendental message of the Lord, *Srimad-Bhagavatam,* will dry up the ocean of misery that has engulfed Krsna’s wives and will reward you for the service of unveiling Vraja.”

At that, the great sage Sandilya stopped speaking. Absorbed in thoughts of Krsna, he returned to his *asrama,* leaving Maharaja Pariksit and King Vajra embracing each other in great joy.

**\* \* \* \***

Having received Krsna’s order through Sandilya Muni, Maharaja Pariksit sent architects, engineers, craftsmen, artists, and masons to Mathura from his own capital city, Indraprastha.

Under the guidance of Sandilya Muni, Vajra identified, named, and re-established the many places of Vraja-mandala in which Krsna had enacted pastimes with the *gopis* and *gopas.*

The king founded villages, towns, and settlements. He dug ponds, wells, and lakes, constructed temples, shrines, and bathing *ghatas,* and installed many Deities to promote exclusive devotion to Krsna.

Vajra invited good people from surrounding lands to live in Vraja. To the *brahmanas* of Mathura, who had previously been the monkeys of Lord Rama, he gave the duty to care for the temples and deities.

Not since Krsna and the Vraja-vasis had physically lived there had Vrndavana *dhama* known the happiness it now enjoyed, as Vajra breathed new life into the precincts of Vrndavana.

Constantly engrossed in glorifying Krsna, the new residents of Vrndavana tasted the highest bliss. Elated over their idyllic life, they spontaneously eulogised their kingdom and its ruler.

Everyone in Mathura-mandala was happy except for the queens of Dvaraka, who in stark contrast were constantly morose due to intense feelings of pain in separation from their beloved husband.

In a previous chapter we heard how the wives of Krsna and their guardian, Vajra, were able to enter into the bliss of *vraja-lila* by hearing *Srimad-Bhagavatam* from Uddhava.

That pastime took place just next to Kusuma-sarovara, where Uddhava appeared in human form from the creepers of Puspavana in response to the *kirtana* of Pariksit Maharaja and Krsna’s wives.

The *sahkirtana* of Paroksit made Uddhava appear in person, and that *kirtana* ended in a conversation that revealed Uddhava’s glories; therefore, Uddhava-kunda is the ideal place to hear of these things.

Dear readers! Uddhava Mahasaya, in his own way, is as unique a companion of Lord Krsna as Narada Muni. Thus I now feel compelled to glorify Sri Uddhava at length.

Please note one aspect of Uddhava’s spiritual journey: although Uddhava was an advanced devotee, Krsna wanted him to assimilate *vraja-bhakti,* just as Lord Caitanya wants all of His followers to do.

After Pariksit, Vajra, and Krsna’s queens had come to the bank of Kusuma-sarovara and bowed to honour the ground cover there, they asked Kalindi how they could make Uddhava appear in person.

Beautiful Kalindi-devi replied, “Uddhava is especially fond of *hari- kirtana.* If we loudly chant the holy names of our beloved Krsna, surely he will appear and grant us an audience.”

Pariksit Maharaja eagerly called for *karatalas,* drums, flutes, and *vinas.* Attendants soon delivered them, and the ladies, who were all expert in singing and dancing, chose their favourite instruments.

Before the *kirtana* commenced, the two kings agreed that Vajranabha should protect the sanctity of the festival by personally standing guard in order to avoid any disturbance by Kali or his agents.

Thus, during the *kirtana* and the ensuing conversation with Uddhava, Vajra circled Puspavana and Sakhivana, always alert to prevent the entrance of any non-devotional elements.

From the events leading up to Vajra’s restoration of Vrndavana, Pariksit could see Vaisnava history in the making, and as a transcendentalist he could feel the spiritual energies unfolding.

Thus, when he accompanied Vajra and the queens to Kusuma- sarovara, he knew that he was taking part in a spiritual event that would bring about significant and irreversible changes in his life.

Indeed, as soon as the sound of Pariksit’s *karatalas* rang in the air, he felt the presence of a divine force that raised his voice far above the clouds and submerged his soul deep into heavenly *kirtana-rasa.*

The queens, similarly inspired, responded to Pariksit’s lead. Their voices blended with the vibrations of the music, the tapping of their feet, the swirling of their dresses, and the jingling of their ornaments.

The emperor’s powerful frame moved gracefully and his loosened hair swayed about his shoulders as he raised his long arms to the sky and sang Krsna’s names in a voice that could melt a heart of stone.

The queens moved in concentric circles, some singing, some clapping, some playing musical instruments. The sacred name of Krsna danced amongst them as Krsna had once danced amongst the *gopis.*

As Pariksit Maharaja became more and more absorbed, his voice trembled in ecstasy. The queens responded by opening their hearts and flooding him, the forest, and Kusuma-sarovara with their *prema.*

Drawn by the enchanting sounds, the forest creatures surrounded the *kirtaniyas,* their eyes engrossed in the queens’ movements, their ears absorbed in the king’s singing, their hearts dancing to Krsna’s names.

Pariksit had often sung with Vaisnavas, but he had never experienced a *kirtana* in which devotees were so absorbed in the name, so devoted to the owner of the name, so overwhelmed by the bliss in the name.

But the queens were not ordinary Vaisnavas, nor even great Vaisnavas. They were Krsna’s eternal companions whose love for Him and whose taste for His names was second only to that of the *gopis.*

Like a leaf raised higher and higher by the wind, Pariksit Maharaja’s heart soared through firmaments of bliss to the sounds of singing, music, ankle bells, and the names Hare, Krsna, and Rama.

At first the Emperor and the queens sang by the banks of Kusuma-sarovara, but guided by *prema* they eventually moved throughout Puspavana, across Giriraja, and around Sakhivana.

As he twirled and swayed, intoxicated by the intensity of a *kirtana- rasa* previously unknown to him, Pariksit, the owner of unlimited wealth, thought of his kingdom as insignificant as discarded straw.

In the presence of the queens’ love-saturated *kirtana,* everything but the holy name was forgotten: gone were Pariksit’s horses, armies, family, kingdom, material identity, and sovereignty over the earth.

In the company of the queens, for whom Krsna was everything, there was only the name and nothing more — nothing — until their chanting abruptly stopped and its echoes receded into the distance!

Pariksit opened his eyes to see the queens standing like golden dolls, facing a grove by Kusuma-sarovara, motionless but for their lips, which continued to softly vibrate the holy name, “Krsna, Krsna!”

Looking carefully, the emperor watched as a solitary figure rose up from the creepers, like mist rising from a lake — a man of complexion dark like the Yamuna and limbs cool like her waters.

The man’s arms were strong like lightning bolts, his chest broad as a doorway, his eyes soft like lotus petals. His yellow silk garments, lotus garlands, and restless eyes were reminiscent of Lord Krsna.

Gazing into the man’s beautiful face, the emperor thought, “Having attained *svarupa-mukti,* this great soul has the same bodily features as the Lord and must therefore be that foremost amongst friends.”

Pariksit and the queens slowly approached the man, and with joined palms the emperor respectfully asked, “Dear Sir! Please tell us who you are. Where have you suddenly come from, and why are you here?”

The man smiled and said in a voice like rolling thunder, “O Visnurata! You know who I am. Since you and these distinguished ladies have called me with your *sankirtana*, need you ask?”

Pariksit lowered his face to his joined palms in great respect. Pleased with the emperor’s humility, the man identified himself: “I am Uddhava, the insignificant servant of Hari’s servants.

“I live here in Vrndavana to collect the dust from the lotus feet of the *gopis.* Today I have become indebted to you because you have showered me with the invaluable nectar of *nama-sankirtana ”*

**\* \* \* \***

Uddhava and Pariksit stood face to face like two lotuses growing in a pond of golden lilies — Krsna’s beautiful queens, who were already familiar with Uddhava, their Lord’s beloved cousin and advisor.

Pariksit was overwhelmed by Uddhava’s presence. The emperor smeared the dust of the feet of Krsna’s great devotee on his head, and Uddhava in turn embraced the king and sat him on a nearby bench.

After each of Lord Krsna’s wives greeted Uddhava, and were greeted by him in turn, the queens, who had come to Kusuma-sarovara just to hear from Krsna’s friend, took their seats before the two men.

The women of Dvaraka placed their shawls upon the ground and sat in concentric semi-circles before Uddhava and Pariksit, and to serve the queens, the earth of Vrndavana became cushion-soft.

In the shade of a blooming *bakula* tree, where birds sang of Krsna’s pastimes and bees hummed in ecstasy, the two great devotees sat, eager for each other’s company and for talks of the Supreme.

Uddhava looked at King Pariksit and saw a noble face, a sinless man, a heart full of devotion, a recipient of Krsna’s mercy, and a Vaisnava qualified to hear of Uddhava’s mission and Krsna’s departure.

Uddhava’s relationship with Krsna was unique: after Krsna had left Vrndavana for Mathura, and then Mathura for Dvaraka, Uddhava had become Krsna’s foremost counsellor and most intimate friend.

Who else but Uddhava had delivered letters to Krsna’s parents, friends, and *gopis,* had instructed the Vraja-vasis about Krsna’s divinity, and had become the itinerant professor of *vraja-bhakti?*

Exalted, virtuous, and possessed of a transcendental form like Krsna’s, Uddhava had lived in Vrndavana for eight months, and during that visit he realised the pure love the Vraja-vasis have for Krsna.

From spring to fall, Uddhava had lived in the area near Radha-kunda and pacified the hearts of the Vraja-vasis— Radha, Nanda, the *gopas* and *gopis* — by constantly speaking to them about Krsna with love.

After returning to Mathura, Uddhava had described to Krsna the sorrow of the Vraja-vasis, and by gentle persuasion had convinced Krsna to reciprocate His kinsmen’s love by paying them a visit.

Uddhava visited Vrndavana again and again, each time to deliver letters from Krsna and pacify the Vraja-vasis. And after returning to Dvaraka, each time he would convince Krsna to visit His kin.

And finally, before returning to His eternal abode, Krsna bestowed transcendental potency upon Uddhava and empowered him to teach the residents of Badarikasrama about *vraja-bhakti.*

Thus Uddhava had become Krsna’s special envoy who would make the confidential glories of the Lord — spontaneous loving service to Him — known for all time and in all places throughout the world.

He would become the great teacher of *Srimad-Bhagavatam,* the shelter for the followers of the Vraja-vasis, and the one to remove the sufferings of devotees who saw the world without Krsna as vacant.

Uddhava knew that, as Arjuna’s grandson and Yudhisthira’s successor, Pariksit Maharaja had been empowered with the same potency and charged with a similar mission as Uddhava had been.

Whereas Uddhava would teach the world by speaking to Vidura, the saints of Badarika, and Krsna’s queens, Pariksit would teach the sages of the universe by hearing the same message from Vyasa’s son, Suka.

Uddhava touched the emperor’s arm and said, “My son! Reveal your heart to benefactors, but keep your devotion far from non-believers. This was the practice of our Lord, who always hid behind His maya.”

Then, with a reassuring glance, Uddhava said, “O King! Why did you and these fair ladies — all personally known to me — summon me from my devotional trance? Please let me know.”

Pariksit Maharaja said, “Respected Uddhava! You are Krsna’s counsellor, disciple, servant, and friend. When the Lord enjoyed His meals, you would stay nearby to receive remnants from His plate.

“When you massaged the Lord’s lotus feet you would fall asleep holding them closely to your chest. When the Yadus pined after Krsna, you would cheer them up by repeating His joking words.

“And when the Lord presided over His court in Dvaraka, as His prime minister you would give Krsna matchless counsel, counsel He would accept and follow as if He were an ordinary king.”

Pariksit Maharaja bowed his head slightly as he continued, “Intensely absorbed in Lord Krsna’s lotus feet, you have abandoned the forms of matter and attained a transcendental body resembling the Lord’s.

“O worshipable Uddhava! We have been informed by Sandilya Muni that you possess confidential teachings about the Lord, teachings known by pure devotees as *Srimad-Bhagavatam.*

“That infallible sage has therefore ordered us to hear you sing *Srimad*- *Bhagavatam* and thus attain the perfection of our hearts’ desire, the vision of our dearly beloved, Lord Sri Krsna.

“Kalindi-devi told us that you reside in this area as bushes, shrubs, and other plants. We came here to place our request before you. I also wanted to ask you a specific question that has long puzzled my mind.”

Uddhava raised his eyebrows in a charming way, “I have been instructed to speak the essence of all the Vedas, but because that recitation will take a long time, my son, first ask your question.”

Pariksit Maharaja looked at the queens for their approval. They nodded their heads so he cleared his throat slightly, folded his hands and said, “Revered Sir! Honourable Uddhava!

“You had the good fortune to be always at the Lord’s side, even while He was preparing to depart from this world. If we are worthy, please tell us, whom did He think of last, and what was His last desire?”

Pleased at Pariksit’s enquiry, magnanimous Uddhava smiled broadly and closed his eyes in serenity, only to see his divine, dark-complexioned friend, Krsna, gorgeously dressed, smiling as always.

Uddhava was lost in ecstasy for a moment. Krsna then prompted him to speak: “Dear Visnurata! Make the most of life! Do pious deeds to benefit the world; those who live for others are truly good.

“To the lost souls cursed by the darkness of Kali you will be famous as one who brought them the light of *Srimad-Bhagavatam.* Devotees who chalk out the path to the Lord are second to none.”

Uddhava glanced respectfully at the queens, knowing that what he was about to say would cause them pain. But their eagerness to hear about Krsna forced him to put all reluctance aside.

Again turning to the son of Abhimanyu, Uddhava said, “Now listen as I narrate my last days with Krsna, who, having revealed His eternal pastimes on earth, had decided to return to His own abode.

“After telling you these things, I shall recite *Srimad-Bhagavatam* for one month. Vajra and all these glorious ladies will then enjoy the great festival of bliss that is Krsna’s personal company.”

The forest animals and birds were also eager to hear from Uddhava but thought themselves unqualified to sit with the queens. They silently settled in behind the two Vaisnavas in another semi-circle.

From the vantage point of the demigods two great souls were surrounded by concentric circles of listeners, half of which were the chaste wives of Krsna, and the other half were the animals of Vraja.

**\* \* \* \***

Uddhava said, “Once, desiring to see Krsna’s human-like form, a group of Gandharvas, Apsaras, Nagas, Pitas, and other demigods, headed by Brahma and Siva, arrived in Dvaraka.

“They covered the Lord with divine garlands and praised His transcendental power. He in turn revealed to them the imminent destruction of the Yadus and His departure for Vaikuntha.

“As soon as Krsna spoke these words, fearful omens confirmed that the world would soon be in ruin. O King! Other than by Krsna’s desire, how could inauspicious signs appear in all-auspicious Dvaraka?

“After the demigods had departed, Lord Krsna called together the wise amongst the Yadu dynasty, reminded them of the *brahmanas’* curse, and convinced them to go to Prabhasa-tirtha without delay.

“The Lord said, ‘By acts of charity, bathing at Prabhasa-tirtha, and bestowing opulent gifts upon Vaisnavas, we shall certainly cross these terrible dangers, just as one crosses a great ocean in a suitable boat.’

“By these exchanges I could understand the Lord’s intention to return to Vaikuntha. While the Yadus yoked their horses to their chariots, I approached Hari in a secluded place and offered Him my obeisances.

“With joined palms I said, ‘O Kesava! I cannot tolerate being separated from Your lotus feet for even a fraction of a moment. I beg You, therefore — take me with You to Your eternal abode.”’

Uddhava continued, “Visnurata! My son! What do you think? Could I have continued to live for a moment without He who was more precious to me than my own body, my own life, my very self?

“While eating, sitting, walking, standing, bathing, lying down, and even while enjoying diversions, I had been serving Him constantly. How could I have lived without His physical presence?

Uddhava paused for a moment and then said, “Krsna bestowed upon me a smile more charming than songbirds greeting the morning sun. He would no longer conceal from me His final pastime.

“Leaning peacefully against a cushion as white as the foam of milk, He said, ‘O friend Uddhava! You have understood My plan to withdraw the Yadus from the earth and to return to My own abode.

“‘In answer to the prayer of Brahma, I descended to this world along with Baladeva and other associates from Goloka Vrndavana and performed many activities on behalf of the demigods.

“‘Dear cousin! My mission on earth is now complete. Brahma and Siva are praying for Me to again take up residence in My eternal Vaikuntha abode — and I am also inclined to do so.

“‘Due to the *brdhmanas*’ curse, the entire Yadu dynasty will surely fight amongst themselves and perish. O Uddhava! On the seventh day from today, the ocean will rise up and swallow Dvaraka.

“‘Then Kali will quickly assert his influence. All piety will be purged from the earth. The vast majority of humans will be compelled to commit every type of sin, and the religious will find no shelter.’

“Krsna leaned forward and held my hand. His voice quivered and His eyes were downcast as He went on speaking, ‘Uddhava! When I depart from this world, do not remain in Dvaraka! Do not stay here.’

“Hari then paused for a moment. He did not look at me, but spoke softly, His voice full of emotion, ‘Give up all attachment to your personal friends and relatives and fix your mind on Me.

‘“Observe all things with equal vision. Always fully conscious of Me, wander over the earth giving shelter to others by speaking about My form, qualities, and pastimes, as you have personally witnessed them.’

“Then my beloved friend and well-wisher, the Lord of the three worlds, gave me instructions by which to sever the knots of material attachment. What may have been many hours passed in an instant.

“O King! I drank with my ears the ambrosia of His words and tasted within my heart the elixir of divine wisdom. From Him I heard the science of *sdnkhya,* the process of yoga, and the path of renunciation.

“After explaining the system of *vamasrama-dharma,* my Lord spoke of the influence of the modes of material nature, the principles of creation, and the methods of practising *jnana, yoga,* and *bhakti.*

“As He had done with Arjuna, Krsna concluded His instructions to me with an appeal: ‘Surrender to Me in pure devotion, be confident of My protection, and perfection will he yours, rest assured.’

“Then, looking into my eyes, His voice quivering slightly, He said, ‘Friend! I will go, but you must stay. Go to My *asrama* called Badarika in the Himalayas and purify yourself by bathing in its holy waters.

“‘Within sight of the river Alakananda, rid yourself of sinful reactions, dress in the bark of trees, eat what is available in the forest, and associate with saints who strive to attain Me without motive.

“‘Meditate constantly upon My instructions, assimilate their essence, and fix your thoughts upon Me. In this way you will surely cross beyond the three modes of nature and finally come back to Me.’

Uddhava stopped for a moment and looked around at his audience, who with tearful eyes could barely tolerate to hear his words but at the same time could not deny their eagerness to continue listening.

“As I heard Krsna’s words,’’ continued Uddhava, “my heart sank into an unlimited ocean of despair. Why would He leave me behind? What would I do in the world alone without Him?

“Knowing my mind, Hari reminisced about His many adventures. He said, ‘During My pastimes, I satisfied the devotees who desired to see Me. I also married thousands of queens, headed by Rukmini.

“I killed innumerable demons in various places, by various means. I attended reunions and ceremonies, enjoying with friends and relatives in Vrndavana, Mathura, Dvaraka, and Hastinapura.

‘“To please mother Devaki, I descended to Sutala and blessed Bali Maharaja. To satisfy Sandipani Muni I entered the kingdom of death and retrieved his dead son. Thus Yamaraja was able to see Me.

“‘When Satyabhama longed for *parijata* flowers, I went to heaven to collect them. There I met Aditi and Kasyapa. Then to please Maha-visnu and His associates, I travelled with Arjuna to Vaikuntha.

“‘With the exception of Nara-Narayana Rsi and the *Paramahamsas* of Badarikasrama, who are most eager, I have answered the prayers of all the devotees who keenly desired to see Me.

“‘Listen My friend! For one hundred and twenty-five years I have been busily engaged on earth. Now My scheduled time here is finished, but I have yet to bless these great sages in the far north.’

“Krsna stopped, placed His hand on my forearm, and while smiling, said, ‘Uddhava, you are My confidante, indeed, you are non-different from Me, thus you are the right person to go to Badarikasrama.’

“The moment those words entered my ears, I went deaf, my body burned with fever, and my mind became absorbed in Krsna’s departure. Only after a time could I again pay attention to His words.

“‘I have given you transcendental knowledge by which one becomes detached from matter. I have also arranged for you to witness firsthand and assimilate the pure devotion of the *gopis* of Vrndavana.

“‘And now I have revealed to you the mystery of My departure. Convey these secrets to Nara-Narayana and the sages. Seeing you and hearing from you will completely satisfy them.’

“Krsna squeezed my hand and with a voice full of emotion said, ‘Tell them how the Vraja-vasis love Me, speak to them of Srimati Radharani, of My mother, of Sridama, of My cows.

“The pure loving devotion of the Vraja-vasis is a treasure lying beyond the limits of knowledge. Hearing about it will bestow perfection upon the sages of Badri, and will please Nara-Narayana.’”

Suddenly Uddhava became overpowered by emotion. Tears streamed from his eyes, his voice choked up, and he stopped speaking. Pariksit and the queens struggled to suppress their tears while they waited.

The ecstasies of separation from Krsna and the influence of Krsna’s love for the Vraja-vasis assailed Uddhava’s heart like gale-force winds and mountainous waves toss a boat at sea.

**\* \* \* \***

Pariksit Maharaja softly chanted the names of Krsna. The soothing transcendental sound gradually calmed Uddhava, who wiped away his own tears with his *cadara,* cleared his voice, and said:

“Thus addressed by Lord Krsna, I could say nothing, for my voice was stolen by sorrow. Seeing my unhappiness, the Lord, my dearest friend, was so distraught that He could speak no more.

“Neither of us was able to speak. We sat for a long time simply gazing at each other through our tears. At last, from within my heart, I heard Krsna’s soft, resonant voice utter sadly, ‘Go! My friend, go!’

“I rose to my feet like a haunted person and circumambulated the Lord once, twice, three times. I looked at Him, but He could not bear to look at me. Instead He stared out into the garden as if distracted.

“I abandoned any hope that He would change His mind. My head reeled as I fell to the ground before Him and placed my head upon His soft lotus feet. That was all I could do.”

Uddhava continued with difficulty, “Unable to bear the sorrow and fear produced by imminent separation from my Lord, whose company I could not give up, I drenched His lotus feet with my tears.

“But my Lord and friend still declined to speak. I bowed before Him again and again. I tried to say His name but my voice would not come, and thus I could only sob, as does a child.

“I felt as if a lance had been plunged into my very being. I placed His slippers on my head, held them to my chest, and then departed, blinded by tears, oblivious to where I was going.

“O Visnurata! How could I, His eternal servant, leave Him and then continue to live without Him? At that moment I thought I would lose my mind, but then again I heard His voice within me.

“It was the same instruction He had given to Arjuna, one that Arjuna and I had often discussed while sitting together in a solitary garden of Dvaraka, speaking of our mutual well-wisher.

‘“Always think of Me... thus you will come to Me... I promise.’ Following His order, I placed Lord Krsna deep within my heart, bound Him with the ropes of love, and secured His company forever.

“I left everything else behind except the Lord and His orders, which I carried within my heart. And as other Yadus, perplexed, observed the state I was in, I left Dvaraka and headed north for the Himalayas.”

Ecstasy no longer allowed Uddhava to speak, plunging him into rapt meditation on Krsna instead. Deeply moved by Uddhava, Pariksit was also deeply perplexed because his question remained unanswered.

What were Lord Krsna’s last instructions? So as not to disturb Uddhava’s mood, the emperor spoke gently, like a breeze whispering through a forest: “O beloved of Hari! Most fortunate Uddhava!

“You have kindly agreed to remove my doubts, but according to what you have said, you left Krsna before the battle at Prabhasa. How, then, do you know His last words and His final desire?”

Uddhava opened his eyes and gazed at Pariksit’s humble and gentle face, at the mighty hero who had rejoiced in many battles but who now sat before him like a pauper desperately begging alms.

Pariksit Maharaja’s voice, features, and bearing were so similar to Krsna’s that Uddhava vividly remembered his Lord. Thus fortified, his mind soothed, he answered the emperor:

“On route to Badarikasrama, I received news that the reeds growing by the sea at Prabhasa had turned to iron, and your family members, cursed by *brdhmanas*, had become intoxicated and quarrelled.

“Pulling out the iron reeds, they attacked and struck each other, and in their madness they even set upon Krsna and Balarama. Their fates thus sealed, the Yadus slew one another until none remained alive.

“The Lord, my master, had instructed me to go north, but I had to see Him once more. I retraced my steps because it was impossible for me to bear separation from His lotus feet.

“O Visnurata!” continued Uddhava. “How can I tell you the details of what happened at Prabhasa? Your family members were all glorious, and may your memory of them preserve those glories.

“I found the Lord by the River Sarasvati, alone and deep in thought, leaning against a young banyan tree, His right foot resting upon His left thigh. He was preparing to leave this world, yet He was cheerful.

“Now that the members of His own dynasty were gone, Hari’s mission was complete. The last burden of the earth had been removed, and with Balarama gone, so was Krsna’s last reason to stay.

“O Emperor! How can I properly describe the Lord, whose body was blackish, eternal, full of bliss, and very, very beautiful. His large lotus eyes were peaceful but reddish like the rising morning sun.

“He appeared with four hands, each bearing its own symbol. He was dressed in yellow silk garments, and His VaijayantI garland was surrounded by bees intoxicated by singing His nectarean glories.

“Providence had arranged for the great sage Maitreya, Vyasa’s friend, to arrive there before me. When I arrived, the Lord, the eternal well-wisher of everyone, was speaking kindly to the sage.

“When Krsna saw me, He stopped speaking and greeted me with a smile and a love-laden glance. He allowed me to offer my respects to the elderly Maitreya and then rest a moment.

“Though a learned scholar very much attached to the Lord, Maitreya was not yet an unalloyed devotee, and thus Krsna’s behaviour with the sage displayed a slight ambivalence.

“The Lord turned His attention to me, appearing relieved and happy at my presence. He addressed me with the same intimate affection that had always characterised our friendship.

“Tell me truly, can there be a more kind-hearted master than the son of Yasoda? Hearing His words of solace, my own voice choked with tears and the hairs on my body stood erect like needles.”

**\* \* \* \***

I composed myself and thought how best to use those final moments. I submissively enquired, ‘My Lord! If you think me qualified, kindly explain the transcendental knowledge You delivered to Brahmaji.’

“Casually nodding His head, my lotus-eyed Lord then instructed me about His own transcendental position. He disclosed knowledge that He had concealed even from the creator himself.

“O King! Without the blessings of mature pure devotion one cannot understand the confidential pastimes Krsna enacts with His devotees in Dvaraka, Mathura, and Vrndavana— especially Vrndavana.

“The glorious months I had spent here in Vraja were the best of my life. I was young, proud of my learning, and overconfident in my devotion. I foolishly considered the Lord as one of my relatives.

“Amused by my arrogance, the Lord sent me to Vraja and showed me the love these simple villagers have for Him. He humbled my pride and taught me the topmost truth: only *vraja-bhakti* can control Him.

“From the *gopas* I came to know of pure servitude and intimate friendship, from Yasoda and Nanda I learned of pure parental affection, and from the *gopis,* especially Radha, of pure amorous love.

“O one protected by the Lord! Look around. Here in Vrndavana every grain of sand and every blade of grass is made from the same conscious, eternal bliss that permeates the hearts of the Vraja-vasis.

“Krsna’s pastimes in Vraja resonate with sweetness, but they are secret, unknown to any other place. These pastimes always control Krsna’s mind, even when He is amidst the opulence of Dvaraka.

“To reveal this confidential secret, Krsna appeared in this world. With a desire to help me understand *vraja-bhakti,* He brought me here under the pretext of delivering letters.

“To reawaken this knowledge in me, He spoke it again, just before He left this planet. Then, somehow thinking me qualified, He ordered me to speak on His behalf to the great sages at Badarikasrama.

“Krsna’s desire was that I disclose the confidential nature of His Vraja pastimes to the Deities Nara-Narayana, to the sages who lived in Badarikasrama, and to other qualified devotees.

*“Vraja-bhakti* shines more resplendent in a heart finely polished by feelings of separation from Krsna; therefore, the Lord waited until His last moments on earth to deliver this order to me.

“The Lord knew that I would better remember the mood of the Vraja-vasis after His departure, so just before He left He deputed me with a message, more confidential than any item of Vedic knowledge.

“Concluding His talks, Krsna said, ‘O Vasu! I know that in days of yore you desired to achieve My companionship, a friendship very difficult to obtain. Now I award that friendship unto you.

‘“Your present life is the last and the best, because now you have My ultimate favour. Serve as My agent in this world a little longer and thereafter go to My transcendental abode and he with Me eternally.’

“The Lord smiled sweetly and said: ‘Your devotion is pure and unflinching, and your having visited Me in this lonely place is a great boon for you. Now, as I have already instructed, go to Badarikasrama.’

“Krsna’s voice choked with affection. At that moment Maitreya Muni became free from all impurities and understood the importance of entering into the Lord’s company in the mood of loving devotion.

“I rose to my feet as ordered. How I reached my destination in Badarikasrama I cannot say. Certainly He who is everywhere and who guides the movement of all living entities directed me.

“Long ago in Vrndavana the seed of a desire had been planted in my heart, and over time it grew into a mature vine that fructified as I climbed ever higher and higher, following the course of the Ganges.

“Having heard of Krsna’s pastimes with the *gopis* at Kusuma-sarovara, I desired to be ground cover on the bank of that lake, in a garden where the *gopis* would come to pick flowers.

“How much I had yearned to receive on my head the priceless dust from the feet of the Vraja-vasis as they walked here and there, absorbed in the supreme ecstasy of Krsna *prema!*

“Would Nanda Maharaja come in search of his cows? Would the *gopas,* full of fraternal love, come and play? Would the *gopis,* remembering their pastimes with Krsna, come this way?

“Perhaps Sri Radha, Lalita, or their maidservants would place their lotus feet upon my stem, leaves, or flowers. By the blessings of their touch I would become qualified for their exclusive variety of love.

“With these thoughts in mind, I carried the mission of the Lord in my heart. Remembering His final teachings, in which He had directed me to see Him present everywhere and in everything, I reached Badari.

“? Visnurata! Everything the Lord foretold came to pass. After revealing His teachings to the chosen sages, I engaged in austerities while feeling great separation from Him and from Vrndavana.

“In due time my beloved Lord bestowed His mercy upon me. And thus, leaving my worldly body behind, in one form I entered eternal Dvaraka and in another I appeared as the ground cover in this area.

“And yet in a third form, visible only to purified souls, I continue to reside in Badarikasrama and serve the Lord by preaching the truths of vraja-bhakti in the way He requested me to.”

Uddhava’s eyes filled with tears. He sighed deeply and then continued, “O saintly emperor, favoured by Providence! You were eager to know Lord Krsna’s final desire. Listen as I reveal it to you.

**\* \* \* \***

As I circumambulated Krsna, whose heart was heavy with feelings of separation from His devotees, He said, ‘O friend! On your way to Badarika, you will meet Vidura by the Yamuna’s shore in Vrndavana.

“He alone amongst the Kauravas would not tolerate evil Duryodhana’s conspiracies against the Pandavas and refused to take part in vaisnava- aparadha for the sake of worldly gain.

“He, more than anyone still alive, deserves to hear the science of Bhagavata-Dharma. Teach Vidura the essence of what I taught you and then direct him to Maitreya to learn the details of pure devotion.’

“As the Lord remembered Vidura’s innumerable good qualities, He said, ‘It is My desire that your words liberate Mahatma Vidura from material entanglement and grant him the final destination.’”

Again Uddhava stopped speaking, this time to consider whether or not to describe Krsna’s departure. He decided against it because everyone present, including him, would be overcome by sadness.

Uddhava then brushed aside all thoughts of Krsna’s disappearance and said, “Those were His final words. The Lord wanted Vidura to hear the narration of His wonderful pastimes — Srimad-Bhagavatam.

“As the Lord foretold, one evening I happened to meet Vidura on the banks of the Yamuna. We sat together and talked about Krsna’s pastimes and potencies until the sun appeared over the horizon.

“Vidura then cried piteously, overwhelmed by the ecstasy of love, as he heard that Krsna remembered him while quitting this world. By the Lord’s will, after our talks I sent Vidura to meet Maitreya.”

Parlksit Maharaja breathed a deep sigh of relief. He thought, “So this was Krsna’s last thought and last desire. How wonderful!” The mystery that had long perplexed his mind was solved.

Krsna had thought of Vidura and wanted to liberate His devotee by having him hear Srimad-Bhagavatam directly from those personally instructed by the Lord — Uddhava and Maitreya.

The satisfaction Parlksit Maharaja felt soon gave birth to another question, a question he had seen in the beautiful faces of the queens who were attentively seated before Uddhava Mahasaya.

Unable to restrain himself, Parlksit enquired, “If Krsna thought of the liberation of Vidura, he must have also thought of the welfare of these queens, whose love for Him is second only to that of the gopis.”

Startled, Uddhava sat erect as the intelligent emperor extended his powerful arms as if to embrace the helpless audience. Then Parlksit continued, “These ladies are not ordinary devotees.

“They are repositories of the highest love for Sri Krsna. They now live in Vraja, sustained only by the potency of the dhama and remembrance of their Lord’s transcendental pastimes.

“Will they languish here forever, never to be reunited with their Lord? Is there no way to secure their liberation? ? knower of everything! Please explain this apparent inequity to us all.”

Uddhava marvelled at the emperor’s devotion. Here was a true Vaisnava, a compassionate ruler seeking the ultimate welfare of his subjects. Uddhava took Pariksit’s hand and said, “O Visnurata!

“Because the Lord could never neglect His dearmost consorts, He has inspired you to ask me this question. Not only did He think of them at the time of His departure, but He thinks of them now, and forever.

“Krsna arranged for His queens to be brought here to be blessed by the dust of Vraja. And now, in the same way as Vidura, by hearing Srimad-Bhagavatam from me they will again attain Krsna’s company.

“Concerned as you are for their welfare, make fitting arrangements for this recitation. In that way, you, Vajra, the queens, these animals, indeed the entire world will benefit.”

Enlivened at the prospect of hearing Srimad-Bhagavatam with the queens, Pariksit Maharaja asked, “Where should this recitation take place and for how long? And what process are we to follow?”

Uddhava replied, “I will recite the pastimes of Krsna here at Kusuma- sarovara for the period of one month, but the preparations for that recitation should be made by Vajra who has been standing nearby.

“Since Krsna has left this earth, Kali has been empowered to put obstacles in the path of any good works, such as this recitation. Fully armed, therefore, you should proceed on a campaign of conquest.

“Search all directions. And when you find Kali, subdue and restrain him. Thus assisted by you, I will be able to speak Srimad-Bhagavatam unhindered, and these queens will be able to rejoin their Lord.”

Parlksit Maharaja felt as if the earth had shifted under his feet. Having been brought so close to Krsna that he could almost touch Him, he was now being ordered to go and hunt down the devil.

The emperor fell at Uddhava’s feet. Unable to restrain his tears, he cried, “O dear one! I cannot ignore your order and therefore I shall find Kali wherever he is and restrain him.

“But please tell this servant who has fallen at your feet how he will be able to hear Srimad-Bhagavatam and how he will be able reap the benefit of the most auspicious of all scriptural recitations?”

Uddhava Mahasaya stroked Parlksit Maharaja’s head and at the same time motioned to Vajranabha, who was watching the proceedings from a sentry’s lookout atop a nearby hillock.

As Vajra approached and prostrated himself, Uddhava comforted Parlksit Maharaja with sweet words, “O Emperor! You need not worry for your deliverance, which has already been ordained by the Lord.

“When I speak Srimad-Bhagavatam only a few select devotees will hear it. My recitation here in Kusuma-sarovara will not bring the blessings of this transcendental literature to the ignorant masses.

“But your fate is different! After subduing Kali, you will hear Srimad- Bhagavatam from Sukadeva Gosvami, who will have personally assimilated it from the instructions of his father, Vedavyasa.

“The great sages of the universe will be present when you hear Krsna’s pastimes from Sri Suka. And at the end of that recitation both you and your mother will attain Krsna’s eternal abode.

“From that time on Srimad-?hagavatam’s fame will spread over the earth, and your name will be inextricably linked with the scripture, wherever devotees recite it. Thus your fame will also spread.”

Pariksit Maharaja sat up and wiped the tears from his face. His mind melted into joy at the thought of hearing Srimad-Bhagavatam and being an instrument for others to hear its transcendental vibration.

Now it was Uddhava’s turn to cry. He had become so attached to this young emperor, whose beauty, devotion, heroism, and humility reminded him of Pariksit Maharaja’s grandfathers, the Pandavas.

He also cried because he knew that after subduing Kali, Parlksit would live for only seven more days before dying by the bite of a snake-bird, and the earth would never again know such a king.

Uddhava raised his right hand and said, “O Visnurata. You will enter Goloka Vrndavana, of that there is no doubt. Therefore arm yourself with infallible weapons and give us shelter from Kali.”

Pariksit Maharaja placed that order in his heart, and his head upon Uddhava’s feet. He then bowed before the queens, who blessed him, saying, “May victory attend you!” Lastly he embraced Vajra.

The emperor then circumambulated the area where Uddhava and the queens were seated, mounted his chariot, and headed for Indraprastha to assemble his army and conquer the earth.

**\* \* \* \***

Vajranabha made the arrangements for the recitation of Srimad-Bhagavatam: he placed his only son, Pratibhu, in charge of the kingdom and then sat with Krsna’s queens to hear Uddhava speak.

Because both the speaker and the audience were elevated transcendentalists, that recitation of Srimad-Bhagavatam was full of wonders so numerous I could not possibly describe them all.

A glimpse of the wonders of Uddhava’s narration, presented in the context of his explanation of one verse originally spoken by the gopis to Krsna, can be seen in the appendix entitled “Tava Kathamrtam.”

As Uddhava spoke philosophical truths, his audience fully and perfectly assimilated them, and as he described the process of creation and annihilation, they could visualise those events taking place.

As Uddhava spoke of Krsna’s different incarnations, the pastimes appeared before the entire assembly, and as he spoke of Krsna’s Vrndavana pastimes, everyone appeared to enter into them.

Thus, at the foot of Govardhana Hill for an entire month Uddhava joyfully narrated Srimad-Bhagavatam. And finally the audience attained the fulfilment of their desire — Krsna’s company.

Krsna appeared in person, and those who heard His pastimes saw themselves situated with Krsna: Vajra upon the right foot of the Lord, the queens as His own inseparable potency, the animals in other ways.

Freed of material conceptions and feelings of separation from the Lord, Vajra, the queens, and other observers all gained entrance into Krsna’s eternal pastimes in the unmanifest realm.

The Skanda Purana says that all those who heard Uddhava speak the Bhagavatam disappeared from the view of ordinary men, but can be seen in Krsna’s Vraja pastimes by those with love anointed eyes.

His mission accomplished, Uddhava looked over beautiful Puspavana, calm Kusuma-sarovara, and devoted Govardhana Hill; it was the home he had requested from Krsna, and he loved it.

He had served the Lord's wives by admitting them into Krsna’s pastimes, and he would soon receive the dust from their feet as they walked upon him while collecting flowers at Kusuma-sarovara.

And Vajra, who had dispelled the fear of those who depended upon him, resumed his place as the lightning bolt on Krsna’s right sole. In such a fortunate position, he too would soon tread upon Uddhava.

Sitting alone, Uddhava was overwhelmed with spiritual ecstasy. His eyes shed tears, his voice choked up, the hairs on his body stood erect. Then, as suddenly as he had appeared, he vanished into the grass.

As devotees walk here while serving Radha and Syama, they should always remember Uddhava and the wonderful recitation of Srimad- Bhagavatam he vibrated at Kusuma-sarovara.

Uddhava-kunda is a place to remember Uddhava and the glories of Srimad-Bhagavatam, which should not be equated in any way with any other scripture, because it is Krsna himself.

The Hare Krsna maha-mantra is identical with Krsna and superior to all other Vedic mantras, being the form of Krsna. For the same reason Srimad-Bhagavatam stands above all other Vedic scriptures.

Krsna and the gopis will personally appear wherever a verse, or even half a verse of Srimad-Bhagavatam is spoken; thus, those who neglect to recite this book are killers of their own souls (atma-hanah).

But those who regularly study, hear, and recite Srimad-Bhagavatam with loving devotion elevate not only themselves to the highest perfection, but also their relatives past, present, and future.

Members of the four social orders (varnas) who regularly hear Srimad- Bhagavatam are blessed with primary benefits — enlightenment and devotion — as well as secondary benefits.

For the brahmanas, the secondary benefits are enlightenment in learning; for the ksatriyas, victory over enemies; for the vaisyas, ample wealth; and for the sudras, good health.

Contact with Srimad-Bhagavatam blesses all men and women with the fulfilment of their desires. Who, therefore, will not continually resort to hearing or chanting this transcendental literature?

The book Srimad'Bhagavatam comes into one’s possession only after many lives of pious deeds. What fortunate soul, therefore, in whose home this scripture resides, will fail to study it with care?

Uddhava said that he became a lover of Krsna when he received Srimad-Bhagavatam from his guru, Brhaspati. What disciple, therefore, will not ask his guru to recite this great work?

At the beginning of the universe, the three predominating deities — Siva, Brahma, and Visnu — all requested that Krsna empower them in their respective duties of creation, maintenance, and destruction.

Because Brahma and Siva fulfil their duties through the modes of passion and ignorance respectively, they also requested that Krsna help them to remain unaffected by the influence of those modes.

Krsna gave Srimad-Bhagavatam to each of His guna-avataras and told them to read it in order to accomplish their tasks. Brahma read the Bhagavatam for a week before beginning the process of creation.

He does this at the end of his every night to become free from the seven coverings of material energy — five gross and two subtle. Thus he is able to recreate the universe without hindrance.

Lord Visnu reads Srimad-Bhagavatam for an entire month in order to perfectly perform the duties of protecting the pious, annihilating the demons, and reestablishing religious principles.

Thus empowered hy the divine potency of Srimad-Bhagavatam, Lord Visnu fearlessly appears in incarnations such as the fish, the turtle, and the dwarf to realise His transcendental objectives.

Lord Visnu expounds on Srimad-Bhagavatam for a month with Laksmi-devi as His audience, but when she is the speaker and He the audience, the discourse takes two months and is of superior quality.

Laksmi’s recitation is better because — unlike Visnu who is always distracted by His duties of maintaining the universe — Her service is concentrated only on the Lord, and thus Her mind is carefree.

Lord Siva recites Srimad-Bhagavatam to Parvati and his associates for the period of one year to gain the power of destruction in all its four phases — continuous, occasional, total, and final.

The modes of recitation of these three great personalities are all transcendental (nirguna), but there are three other ways of reciting Srimad-Bhagavatam: in passion, goodness, and ignorance.

Recitation in the mode of passion often lasts for one week and is preceded by various kinds of elaborate rituals for auxiliary worship to attract people desirous of being entertained.

The reciters and the audience in such a passionate recitation participate for some fruitive reward. They tend to rush through the reading of Srimad-Bhagavatam hoping to enjoy the result.

In the mode of goodness, Bhagavatam recitation lasts for one or two months, involves few, if any, rituals or festivities, is enacted without much strain, and pleases everyone involved.

Motivated by a desire for self-purification and spiritual advancement, the speakers and audience in a Bhagavatam recitation in the mode of goodness try to extract the essential teachings from the text.

The Bhagavatam recitation in the mode of ignorance is protracted, often taking a year to complete. What’s more, the reading is carelessly done with no faith in the process or the outcome.

Those who hear this ignorant recitation generally forget most of what they hear and remember at best irrelevant details. But they get some benefit simply because they are in contact with Srimad'Bhagavatam.

Srimad'Bhagavatam recited and heard with loving devotion is transcendental (nirguna) and is not restricted to any duration of time, be it a week, month, or year.

Indeed, Srimad'Bhagavatam itself recommends that one hear it daily (nitya) and in a mood of service (seva). Reciters and hearers in this mood prefer glorifying Krsna’s pastimes even to achieving liberation.

Of all the gradations of quality in speaking and hearing Srimad-Bhagavatam, narrations in the transcendental category are supreme. Nonetheless, any recitation is preferable to no recitation at all.

Srimad'Bhagavatam is like a wish-fulfilling tree that bestows upon its participants their coveted desire, be it love for Krsna, liberation, sense gratification, or any material benefit.

Because Vedic sacrifices cannot be observed properly in Kali-yuga, materialistic persons get no result by performing them; but such persons achieve whatever they desire by hearing Srimad-Bhagavatam.

Persons who desire fame, riches, a spouse, a palace, good progeny, or even a kingdom without enemies can realise these opulences simply by hearing or speaking Srimad'Bhagavatam.

And after enjoying such worldly pleasures, by repeated contact with Krsna’s transcendental pastimes they become free from all desires but the desire to love Krsna, and thus quickly attain the Lord’s abode.

Identical to Krsna, Srimad'Bhagavatam is set apart by the same transcendental attributes as He is — existence, knowledge, and bliss — as are all teachings that follow the message of that sacred text.

In other words, the wise know that discussion of Srimad-Bhagavatam by pure devotees, which manifests the joys of devotional service and the sweetness of Krsna’s pastimes, is also Srimad'Bhagavatam.

An authorised commentary on Srimad'Bhagavatam protects both the speaker and the hearers from the influence of the illusory energy and this age, provided that it contains four topics.

These four topics are: transcendental knowledge, practical application of that knowledge, devotion characterised by pr???, and devotional activities like chanting and hearing about Krsna.

The mind of the ideal speaker always dwells in Krsna. The ideal speaker is pure, compassionate, does not expect remuneration, and is expert at helping others understand things in many different ways.

Thus scriptures acknowledge only one kind of qualified speaker of Srimad-Bhagavatam: the pure at heart. But sastra identifies two types of hearers: superior and inferior. Both types are divided into four.

The superior hearers are called pravara, and they are likened to catakas, swans, parrots, and fish; whereas the inferior hearers are called avara because they are like wolves, crows, bulls, and camels.

A devotee who discards all other scriptures and takes a vow to hear only Srimad'Bhagavatam is like the thirsty cataka bird, which drinks only rain as it falls directly from a cloud.

One who listens to a variety of scriptures but has the keen discrimination to select the essence of what he has heard — krsna-kirtana— is like a swan that extracts milk when mixed with water.
