The third kind of superior listener is one who also speaks well and thus delights his guru as well as other listeners. He is likened to a well-trained parrot that greatly pleases its trainer and bystanders.

The last type of pravara is the silent listener, who neither blinks nor speaks but admires with affection the excellence of what he hears. He is like a glossy fish plying the ocean of milk.

The first kind of inferior listener (avara) is the ignorant fool who shouts and inflicts pain upon rasika Vaisnavas and other listeners. He is like a wolf that inflicts pain upon deer listening to flute-song.

The next avara repeats but does not put into practice what he has heard. He is like a crow in the forest that, in senselessly repeating what it has heard, succeeds only in disturbing others.

The inferior listener who is intellectually blind to the distinction between primary and ancillary topics is likened to the bull that indiscriminately swallows tasty grapes along with oil cakes.

And the last avara listener leaves aside the nectarean topics of Krsna’s pastimes to delve only on other topics, like the camel selects only bitter fruit even when mixed with sweet mangoes.

To identify an ideal listener one should look for a devotee who, leaving aside other discussion, first bows down before the speaker and then, with joined palms, sits face to face with him.

Such a favourite of the Lord has an increasing desire to hear about Krsna, is very adept in understanding what he hears, and reflects carefully upon the problem or topic under discussion.

And finally, like a trustworthy disciple, the qualified hearer, with his body, words, and wealth, renders service to the speaker, the listeners, and to the place where Srimad-Bhagavatam is spoken.

Unfortunate souls neglect Krsna in favour of wealth (dhana). Those who seek wealth by speaking or hearing Srimad-Bhagavatam are called dhanarthi, but those who seek Krsna, the wise call krsnarthi.

If the speaker and hearer are of the same category, they will feel happy by serving Srimad-Bhagavatam because they have a common purpose, a purpose they will quickly realise.

If the speaker and hearers are of different categories, they will not feel satisfied by the process because their tastes for Srimad-Bhagavatam conflict and their goals will be postponed.

Whatever one’s motive for hearing or chanting Srimad-Bhagavatam, the following procedure should be observed: After rising, bathing, and performing one’s scheduled duties, one should sip caranamrta.

Thereafter a person should worship the spiritual master and Srimad- Bhagavatam and then hear the recitation of the text with rapt attention and full respect for the speaker and the subject.

If one hears Srimad-Bhagavatam for a set length of time, then one should observe celibacy, take a vow to eat simply, and at the conclusion of the reading, distribute charity profusely.

Whether read daily or only occasionally, Srimad-Bhagavatam’s recitation should be followed by the enthusiastic congregational chanting of Krsna’s holy names—nama-sankirtana.

Dhanarthis can obtain anything they desire from such an observance: wife, wealth, fame, and so on. Devotees, however, are krsnarthis and have no interest in anything other than obtaining Krsna.

The Srimad-Bhagavatam as spoken by Sukadeva Gosvami to Maharaja Parlksit is pure, perfect, and conducive to devotion for Krsna. Even in this Age of Kali it yields the benefit of eternal love and bliss.

Dear devotees! After recounting the glories of all-worshipable Uddhava, I have taken liberty to glorify Srimad-Bhagavatam at this holy place, Uddhava-kunda. Please do the same.

“O nectar from the ocean of all scriptures, singular fruit of all the Vedas, rich mine of the precious gems of all conclusive truths, You are the only giver of sight to all the worlds.

“O life air of all the Supreme Lord’s devotees, o master, Srimad-Bhagavatam! You are the sun risen in the darkness of the Age of Kali. You are the exact image of Sri Krsna.

“I bow down to You, who are supremely blissful to read. Your every syllable pours down a flood of love of God. You can always be served by everyone. You are Sri Krsna Himself.

“O Srimad-Bhagavatam! ? my only friend, my constant companion, my spiritual master, my great wealth. My saviour, my good fortune, my source of ecstasy, I bow down to You.

“O bestower of saintliness to the unsaintly, ? exalter of the most fallen, please never leave me. Please, always appear in my heart and my voice with pure love for the son of Maharaja Nanda.”

**\* \* \* \***

With great reluctance I left Uddhava-kunda for Sakaroya, the last pastime place situated upon Govardhana Hill, a place associated with Indra, linked to Krsna and the gopis, and close to Saunkarai.

After Krsna replaced Giriraja, Indra came here first and realised what terrible damage he could have done, had Krsna’s protection not counteracted the forces he had unleashed upon Vraja.

To appease Krsna, Indra brought from the heavenly planets a snow white surabhi calf, which grazed here as Indra tried to compose himself. Later the calf was given to the Lord as a coronation gift.

Because Indra — also known as Sakra — was overcome by fear (bhaya) in this place, it is known as Sakaroya. By visiting this grove, one becomes freed from all kinds of fear.

At nearby Saunkarai the gopis once cornered Krsna, suspecting that He was on His way to meet Candravali in Sakhivana. They berated Him for infidelity to Sri Radha, the most chaste girl of Vraja.

As usual, Krsna vehemently pleaded His innocence, but the gopis wanted more than denial of guilt. To gain a stronger assurance, they decided to extract from Him an unbreakable oath.

Lalita-devi said, “Can You take an oath that You will never associate with any girl of Vrndavana other than Sri Radha?” Krsna looked at the gopis and replied matter-of-factly, “But of course.” “Good!” snapped Lalita. Then, as Krsna tried to walk away, she grabbed His wrist and said, “Repeat after me: I swear by all the cows of Nanda Maharaja that I will never know any girl but Radharani.”

Krsna swallowed hard upon hearing Lalita’s proposal, and His jolly expression turned sour as He repeated the vow word for word, trying to put as little emphasis as possible on the phrase “all the cows.”

It was a serious vow, especially for a cowherd, and when the gopis released Krsna from their control, they were certain they had bound Him to Sri Radha, and so they cheered “Jaya Radhe!” as He went.

Because at this place the gopis extracted (karsana) an oath (sapatha) from Krsna it is called Sauhkarai. It is a suitable place to extract promises from cowherds, or anyone else for that matter. Jaya Radhe!

Just facing Sauhkarai is the small forest of Nimavana, home to the village Nima-grama. These names are linked to a pastime of Krsna as well as to the great acarya Nimbarka Svami, who once lived here.

After Krsna replaced Govardhana Hill, at this very spot His mother, grandmothers, and the other elderly gopis embraced Him, kissed His face, and repeatedly wiped the perspiration from His brow.

After they had cuddled Him to their hearts’ content and reassured themselves that He was well and sound, the women worshipped Krsna by offering Him arati lamps along with their tears.

Even though their husbands, sons, daughters, and grandchildren had been in danger from Indra’s deluge, because these ladies loved Krsna more than anything they ignored everyone else to fuss over Him.

Because these elderly gopis worshipped (nirmancana) Krsna with their love, this area became known as Nirmancana-grama, and over time that name has changed to what it is today, Nima-grama.

Let us worship the foot dust of these elderly gopis. Krsna is dearer to them than their own lives. In parental affection they worship each drop of perspiration on His feet and repeatedly kiss His curly hair.

Nimavana was a beautiful garden cradled against Giriraja during the time of Krsna’s pastimes, but because the hill has shrunk since then, this forest of nima trees is now some distance from the hill.

After Krsna’s disappearance, while Vajra ruled Mathura-mandala, the acarya Nimbarka lived in Nimavana. There he worshipped Krsna by the methods followed by the sampradaya of the four Kumaras.

Nimbarka was the son of Aruna Muni, who came along with other sages to the bank of the Yamuna when Pariksit Maharaja was preparing to hear Srimad-Bhagavatam from Sukadeva Gosvami.

By the end of Dvapara-yuga people had become distracted from the path of pure devotional service. Lord Visnu then sent an empowered incarnation to take birth from Aruna’s wife, Jayanti.

The boy, Niyamananda, went t? gurukula, where he observed a vow of celibacy and quickly mastered the Vedas, displaying deep spiritual realisation that prompted him to accept the renounced order of life.

With the hope of getting Krsna’s darsana, Niyamananda set out for Nandagrama. From there he moved to Nimavana by Govardhana Hill and lived sometimes in a cottage and sometimes in a cave.

Once a Jain sannyasi came to Mathura to challenge the learned scholars there, intent on showing the futility of the Vedas. He met Niyamananda, however, and was defeated in scriptural debate.

The debate at Govardhana lasted all day. As the sun was setting, Niyamananda offered his guest some prasada to relieve his hunger, after which they would continue their dialogue.

But because Jain sannyasis are forbidden to eat in the evening or at night, the challenger refused to accept the prasada. Niyamananda then thought of a way in which his guest could eat.

Some say that Niyamananda climbed out on the branch of a tall nima tree and held the sun above the horizon until the sannyasi could complete his meal without breaking his religious vow.

Others say that Niyamananda invoked the Sudarsana cakra of Lord Visnu, which blazed in the sky like the sun while the sannyasi continued to eat, thinking he had remained true to his vow.

Because Niyamananda climbed onto a nima tree and manifested the sun (arka or aditya), he is also known as Nimbarka and Nimbaditya. In addition, Narada initiated him and gave him the name Haripriya.

Lord Caitanya’s teachings accepted the essence from each of the four sampradayas. From Nimbarka he adopted the necessity of taking shelter of Radha, and the highest esteem for the gopis’ love of Krsna.

Nimbarka is also accepted as an incarnation of Sudarsana cakra. In New Vraja-dhama a deity of Sudarsana was installed to protect the dhama and its residents. He should therefore be offered respect.

“O favourite of Acyuta with thousands of spokes! ? master of the material world! Destroyer of all weapons! Original vision of the Lord! I offer my respectful obeisances unto you. Kindly give me shelter.

“O Sudarsana wheel! You are religion, you are truth, you are encouraging statements, you are sacrifice, you are the enjoyer of the fruits of sacrifice, and you are the maintainer of the entire universe!

“O Sudarsana with auspicious hub! You are the upholder of all religion, you are just like an inauspicious comet for the irreligious demons, indeed you are the maintainer of the three worlds.

“O master of speech! By your unsurpassable effulgence, full of religious principles, the dense darkness of the world is dissipated, and the knowledge of learned persons or great souls is manifested.

“If I have given charity, performed ritualistic ceremonies, properly carried out occupational duties, I wish, in exchange for these, that you illuminate those souls blinded by the darkness of ignorance.”

**\* \* \* \***

Dear readers! The area of Radha-kunda is actually part of Govardhana. But it includes many pastime places and is also the Yoga-pitha of New Vraja-dhama, so I have glorified it separately.

Nevertheless, when devotees circumambulate Govardhana Hill I highly recommend that they include Radha-kunda in their itinerary and that they also take darsana of beautiful Radha and Syama.

I could have written more about Govardhana Hill, but I must end now and summarise its glories as I have learned them from scriptures, from Radha and Syamasundara, and from living in its midst.

Devotees who hear or chant these glories, lush as they are with the secrets of Krsna’s transcendental pastimes, will become as fortunate as King Indra in this life, and as fortunate as King Nanda in the next.

I have adapted this glorification from Sri Garga-samhita as an address to Giriraja so that devotees may repeat it at the completion of their parikrama, or employ it as a basis for their own superior prayer.

“O noble-hearted Govardhana! You are the crown of Vrndavana forest. You and that forest are worshipped as the best of holy places, sacred abodes, and places of pilgrimage. My obeisances to you!

“? parasol of the Supreme Lord and protector of the cows, gopas, and gopis! Krsna and His relatives worship you. What holy place, then, either here or in Vaikuntha, could possibly he superior to you!

“Even Lord Brahma cannot properly glorify your sacred slopes, valleys, forests, groves, rivers, lakes, and pasturing grounds, all eternal playgrounds for Krsna and His friends the cowherd boys.

“The mere touch of Manasa-gariga, Govinda-kunda, Candra-sarovara, Radha-kunda, Syama-kunda, Lalita-kunda, Gopala-kunda, and Kusuma-sarovara destroys every sin, and they are but your ornaments.

“By the touch of Sri Krsna’s crown, one of your stones was marked with its impression. Anyone fortunate enough to see that mark may themselves become the dazzling crown upon the Supreme Lord’s head.

“O Giriraja! There is another sacred stone upon which Lord Krsna drew pictures, now called the Citra-sila, and yet another upon which He played musical rhythms, the Vadani-sila.

“If simply seeing, touching, or bowing down before these stones destroys the greatest sins, then who can imagine the result of rolling in the Kanduka-ksetra field, where Krsna played ball with His friends.

“Since a person who bows at Indra-pada or Brahma-pada attains the abode of Visnu, is it any surprise that seeing Ausnisa-tirtha, where Krsna stole the gopas’ turbans, removes the greatest of sins?

“O best of Hari’s servants! So that I may never fall away from service to New Vraja-dhama, I shall regularly give yogurt in charity, drink yogurt from a leaf-cup, and bow down at sacred Drona-tirtha.

“One day may I, by your blessing, hide behind a bush and watch as Krsna and His friends enthusiastically play hide-and-seek at the all-purifying, all-pure forest garden than is called Laukika-tirtha.

“And although by seeing Kadambavana I can easily attain a form like that of Lord Narayana, please do not give me such a blessing: I desire only the service of the residents of Vrndavana and nothing more.

“Indeed, I would instantly trade eternal residence in Vaikuntha for a momentary glimpse of the gopis at sacred Srngara-mandala as they prepare Radha for the rasa dance, Krsna eagerly waiting nearby.

“O king of mountains! Although Sri Gopala Nathaji has moved from your crest to another place, I know that He resides eternally in your heart, and from there takes daily excursions to play on your slopes.

“Although made of stone, your body melts due to the great pr??? in your heart. Please bless me with a loving devotion like yours and with a vision of Krsna’s handprint and footprint near Govinda-kunda.

“O king of mountains! Just by seeing you one is freed from taking birth again in this world, but if one knows your personal opulences he becomes the best of persons and qualified for entrance into Goloka.

“The places of Krsna’s transcendental pastimes are not mere groves, lakes, and forests situated upon you-—they define your limbs and qualities which, like Brahman, are all-pervading and endless.

“O protector of Krsna’s cows! If by being touched by a single stone from your slopes a demon attained a spiritual body and entered Goloka Vrndavana, who can understand your full power?

“Because you are directly a form of Sri Krsna, persons attain the supreme goal of life just by seeing you, a result many millions of times greater than the result of a pilgrimage to Mount Gandhamadana.

“The result one attains by performing austerities for five thousand years on Mount Kedara is attained in a single moment as one circumambulates you with faith and knowledge.

“? peerless place of pilgrimage! A sinful person who donates gold in charity on Mount Mangala attains a form like that of the Lord, but a person who simply sees you, achieves the same result.

“Simply coming close to you one attains piety a hundred thousand times greater than worshipping many brahmanas and giving ten million gold-horned cows in charity on mounts Rsabha and Kutaka.

“And those pious souls who set out on pilgrimage to circumambulate you secure piety ten million times greater than they would by going on pilgrimage to Rsyamuka or Devagiri.

“O Giriraja! Persons who bathe every day for ten years in Vidyadhara- kunda on Sri-saila attain the result of a hundred yajnas, whereas one bath in your Puncari-kunda is equal to ten million.

“To become an Indra some kings perform an asvamedha-yajna on either Mount Venkata, Varidhara, Mahendra, or Vindhya, but they could attain Visnuloka by conducting the same sacrifice beside you.

“Similarly, although bathing in sacred rivers, giving charity, and performing austerities at auspicious times in sacred places brings great spiritual merit, just visiting you gives infinitely more benefit.

“Just to hear the names of tirthas like Puskara, Kuruksetra, Kasi, Naimisa, Madhupuri, Prayaga, and Sri Ranga purifies the heart, but such purity pales compared to the bliss of once saying Govardhana.

“O Govardhana! Great devotees like Rupa, Raghunatha, and Visvanatha sing your glories, the Vraja-vasis, including Sri Radha, praise you, and I, your servant, offer my humble obeisance unto you.

“Devotees, or even sinful materialists, who have firm faith in your unsurpassed spiritual potency and who hear the pastimes Krsna performs upon you will never see Yamaraja, even in dreams.

“? hill that became an umbrella with the arm of Krsna as the handle, in whose caves the youthful divine couple enjoy passionate pastimes, upon which Krsna happily plays with Balarama and His friends —

“Who witnesses the dana-keli pastime of Radha and Krsna, brings bliss to the best of they who relish transcendental nectar, and always affectionately embraces the neck of your dear friend Radha-kunda —

“Who nourishes the cows in so many ways, whom Krsna transformed into a palace to protect the Vraja-vasis, and was therefore called ‘the best of Lord Hari’s servants’ by none other than Sri Radha.

“O transcendental philanthropist who bestows intense bliss! Although I am a cheater, remembering Lord Caitanya and ignoring my disqualifications, please grant me eternal residence near you.’’

## Appendix 15

**Apparent Transience in the Realm of Absolute Permanence**

*(This appendix follows from the description of how Govardhana Hill is said to have appeared from Krsna's heart in Goloka Vrndavana, as it is narrated in chapter nine.)*

On occasion, scriptures describe events in the spiritual world in a way that appears to violate the integral properties of that spiritual realm, even when those integral properties are defined by the self-same scriptures. For example, Sri Garga-samhita narrates the pastime of Govardhana Hill’s “birth” in Goloka, only shortly after leading readers to conclude that the spinritual world is a place where birth does not exist. Yet Vedic scriptures emanate from the Lord and are free of any defect, therefore these statements can be neither false, nor contradictory. They are factual statements pertaining to the Absolute Truth. To understand how they are factual, devotees must take help from realised souls. This appendix will cite the statements of Sanatana Gosvami, a supremely realised soul, in order to help readers understand how it is that once in Goloka, Govardhana Hill — Krsna’s eternal associate — took birth from the Lord’s heart.

Before examining Sanatana Gosvami’s enlightening statements on the dilemma just mentioned, it is useful to discuss a few additional statements from the Vedic scriptures. These statements will help to identify the two opposite poles of an intellectual paradox that has been known to perplex conditioned souls.

The Garga-samhita defines Goloka Vrndavana:

**“...The transcendental realm of Goloka, which is worshipped by all the worlds, and which is a realm where time, the conqueror of the proud, does not prevail, and where illusion, the material mind, heart, intelligence, and ego, material transformations, and the mahat-tattva do not enter. How can the modes of nature enter there?”**

Two essential features of Goloka Vrndavana are stressed in these verses: the absence of material time and the absence of material ingredients. The prevalence of these two existential features ensures the temporality and transience of the material world. Their absence from Goloka indicates that, in that transcendental realm, all persons and things are permanent and eternal. This truth was often repeated by Srila Prabhupada:

**“One should try to return home, hack to Godhead, to the Vaikuntha planets — or to the topmost Vaikuntha planet, Goloka Vrndavana — to live eternally in full, blissful knowledge in the company of the Supreme Personality of Godhead.”**

In other words, the creation and destruction of inanimate things, and the birth and rebirth of conditioned souls — which are all characteristic of a universe dominated by time, material nature, and so forth — do not take place in Goloka, where time and material elements are absent. Since the spiritual planets “are full of transcendental bliss, and are without birth, death, old age, and disease,” once a living entity attains his eternal spiritual form (svarupa) he lives in that form “with Krsna eternally in spiritual happiness.” And if fully perfected devotees (sadhana-siddhas), having regained their eternal forms, live eternally with Krsna, it is correlative that Krsna’s eternal associates (nitya-siddhas) must also live eternally with the Lord in their eternal forms. That is to say, all entities in the eternal spiritual world live there eternally in their eternal spiritual forms.

In light of this description — of a timeless spiritual realm wherein no one takes birth or dies, and being eternal, nothing appears or disappears — how are we to understand that one day in Goloka, Giriraja appeared from Krsna’s heart. Especially considering that to say Govardhana Hill “appeared” is to clearly imply that, before that moment, Giri Govardhana did nor exist in Krsna’s abode.

There would seem to he little value in any interpretation of this pastime other than the direct interpretation: at one point in “time,” Giriraja appeared out of Krsna’s heart. To search for an indirect interpretation would be to doubt the narration about Govardhana Hill; indeed, it would be to question the validity of the entire samhita.

Therefore, the key to understanding the pastime, as it is literally presented, is to closely examine — through the eyes of Sanatana Gosvami—what it means that there is no birth in Goloka. Perhaps the concept of birth in Goloka, as defined by scripture and the acaryas, somehow differs from the concept of birth we have come to knowhere on earth. If so, we may be able to find an explanation that is not incompatible with eternal life on the spiritual plane. In pursuance of this possibility, let us compare and contrast our assumptions about eternal life on Goloka with the authoritative statements of Sanatana Gosvami.

The view generally held by devotees (and communicated by them to non-devotees) regarding the eternality of the spiritual world is of an eternal present, in which everyone and everything remains forever the same. Devotees also commonly profess that because there is no past or future in Goloka, there is neither birth, nor aging, nor death for the all-cognisant souls who reside there. In short, eternal life in the eternal world is diametrically opposed to the life we experience here on earth.

Here is one of many scriptural statements in support of this established view:

**“...Where there is eternal existence of transcendental time, who is ever present and without past or future and hence is not subject to the quality of passing away even for the space of half a moment. That realm is known as Goloka only to a very few self-realized souls in this world.”**

Yet in the verses below, Sanatana Gosvami describes Gopa-kumara’s startling discoveries about life in Goloka, depicting a world that appears to closely shadow life in Bhauma Vrndavana:

**“In that realm, beyond the reach of all the demigods, planetary rulers, and personal attendants of the Supreme Lord, I saw that life was going on just as here in this Aryavarta country in Bharata-varsa on earth. The sun was rising and other natural events were taking place every day in the sky, and on the land the people were speaking and behaving the same way as here. I was stunned by astonishment— extreme astonishment — and immersed in an ocean of ecstatic rasa.”**

Sanatana Gosvami does not describe life on Goloka as antipodal to life on earth. In fact, he says that in terms of appearances and events, in terms of interpersonal communication and conduct, life goes on there in much the same way as it does here. This leads us to our first premise regarding the distinction between eternal and earthly life: the distinction lies not so much in what happens, but in how things happen.

This first premise can be verified by attempting to reconcile Sanatana Gosvami’s statement in Brhad-Bhagavatamrta that “the sun was rising” with Krsna’s statement in the Gita that His “supreme abode...is not illumined by the sun.” It is often assumed, due to a loose reading of this verse from the Gita, that there is no sun in Goloka. This is not, however, precisely what Krsna says. The Lord says that in Goloka illumination during the day is not — as it is in this world — dependent upon a sun. It is not dependent upon a sun because, as Srila Prabhupada’s purport explains, Goloka is selfluminous and thus “there is no need of sunshine.” One may well reasonably suppose — allowing for Gopa-kumara’s testimony, “the sun was rising” — that there is a sun in the sky over Goloka, but that the sun serves simply to beautify that abode. Therefore Sanatana Gosvami and Krsna do not disagree that there is a sun in the sky above Goloka, just as there is in the sky above the earth. The difference lies in the purpose for the sun in these two places. In one place it is the source of all illumination, but in the other it is not.

Let’s delve deeper into studying the nature of life on the eternal plane as Sanatana Gosvami describes it. As we have seen, he establishes that life is similar in both places, but he also gives examples that will serve to distinguish the eternal world from the temporary. In our study we will first examine the repeated appearance of demons like Kaliya, Arista, and Kamsa, and then go on to discuss Krsna’s repeated killing or chastisement of those demons.

How do demons appear in Goloka and in what way is their appearance there different from their appearance on earth? The appearance of demons in Goloka is part of spiritual variety, which gives pleasure to Krsna and to His associates. On earth, demons are miscreants who — as a consequence of their envious nature and past sinful activity — assume the bodies and conduct of creatures inimical to the Lord. But in Goloka there is no influence of the modes of nature; hence there is neither envy nor karma. Therefore, by the arrangement of Yogamaya, devotees appear as demons so as to serve Krsna by becoming His adversaries. These devotee-demons display a semblance of enmity towards Krsna, not unlike the enmity displayed by Jaya and Vijaya during their allotted births as “enemies” of Lord Visnu.

Sanatana Gosvami speaks of Krsna’s exploits with demons in Goloka in this way:

**“Again Krsna went to Madhupuri, again killed Kamsa, and again returned to Vraja, where He continued as before to enjoy His pastimes. ... Krsna subdues Kaliya time and again, in the same way and again lifts Govardhana Hill. And yet He performs His many other pastimes. Thus the Lord enchants the hearts of His devotees.”**

Once again, we must resolve the statements of Sanatana Gosvami with those of Srila Prabhupada. According to Prabhupada, “In Krsnaloka there \[are\] no demons.”

The two statements can be resolved by the understanding that Sanatana Gosvami and Srila Prabhupada are referring to different kinds of demons. The demons in Goloka mentioned by Sanatana Gosvami are really devotees, while the demons mentioned by Srila Prabhupada are not. To put it more plainly, Srila Prabhupada’s comment can be taken to mean, “In Krsnaloka there \[are\] no \[non devotee\] demons.” In that context, both acaryas agree that there are demons in Goloka, but they also agree that the demons appearing here on earth and the demons in Goloka are qualitatively different.

From our analysis of the first example — demons appearing in Goloka — we can draw some conclusions about life on the temporary and eternal planes, conclusions that will provide us with a useful understanding of the general distinction between the two. While, by appearances, the transient and eternal life may seem similar, constitutionally speaking, the two are quite different. The constitution of the former is material — or, at best, spiritual tainted by the material — the constitution of the latter is purely spiritual.

Sanatana Gosvami describes how, in the eyes of a transcendentalist, the purely spiritual nature of Goloka is readily distinguishable from its material counterpart. After noting the similarities between Goloka Vrndavana and Bhauma Vrndavana, Gopa-kumara says:

**“And there I saw, by the hundreds and thousands and tens of millions, all sorts of wonders, unseen, unheard of, unimaginable by anyone in this world A person seeing that place might think he was in the material world. But by carefully reflecting on whatever he had seen before, he would understand he was now somewhere above all material planets, all higher non-planetary regions, and all the transcendental realms of the spiritual world.”**

Now let’s go beyond that general, categorical understanding to see how these two realms differ in a more specific sense.

The second example that Sanatana Gosvami gives in order to characterise eternal life in Goloka is the chastisement, and especially the killing, of demons in that transcendental realm. One of the previous quotes from Brhad-Bhagavatamrta includes the statement, “Again Krsna went to Madhupuri, again killed Kamsa...”

Krsna’s repeatedly killing Kamsa implies that in Goloka there is death, and then rebirth — at least for Kamsa. By now it should be clear, however, that the death and rebirth of Kamsa in Goloka is not like the death and rebirth of Kamsa in the material realm. In Goloka, the fight between Krsna and Kamsa, which ends in Kamsa’s death, is like a theatrical performance playing night after night, over and over again, unchanged and without threat of cancellation. In the material world, Kamsa’s death is a one-time show, its details permanently fixed, with no hope of a repeat performance.

Having accepted Kamsa’s perpetual appearance and demise in Goloka, the question naturally arises: What about the other residents of Vrndavana and Mathura? Don’t they remember the previous time that Kamsa appeared and was then killed by Krsna? The answer must be no. If they did — like a perpetual theatregoer at the same perpetual drama — they would lose their spontaneous attraction to Krsna’s pastimes, impairing the exchanges of love between the Vraja-vasis and Krsna.

But if the residents of Goloka do not remember previous incidents, how can they be said to be in “full, blissful knowledge”? Again our present understanding of spiritual life must be freed from the material bias that equates full, blissful knowledge with omniscience. While the residents of Goloka may be omniscient — in the sense that they are full in knowledge — they are not necessarily fully aware of all things, in all places, at all times.

They are, however, blissful, as stated by Srila Prabhupada. Perhaps our next question should he this: What influence, if any, does bliss have on knowledge?

Sanatana Gosvami discusses this topic extensively. He explains that love — the cause of bliss — veils knowledge, and hence love makes the Vraja-vasis forget that a pastime has already occurred, even if it has occurred an unlimited number of times. He writes,

**“But the residents of Vraja, completely bewildered by the kalakuta poison of their supreme love for Sri Krsna, never think that any of these events ever occurred before.”**

Moreover, since love is impartial, it exercises this same influence over Krsna.

**“And, oh, the most glorious thing is that the foremost of enlightened persons, the Lord Himself, while drowning in an ocean of love for His dear devotees, cannot always remember what He has done and what He is going to do.”**

Regarding Krsna’s killing Kamsa in Goloka, the influence of love upon knowledge is such that, although Krsna leaves Vrndavana to kill Mathura’s king, when Krsna returns home both He and the Vraja-vasis forget He had ever left. And when, once again, Kamsa’s atrocities become unbearable and once again Krsna goes to Mathura, kills Kamsa, and then returns to Vraja, it seems as if He is doing these things for the first time. And after having done them, by the influence of divine love Krsna and the Vraja-vasis once again forget these incidents. Their knowledge is completely obscured by prema.

For the sake of clarity, let us summarise the discussion so far.

While the external features of life in Goloka very much resemble life on earth, the very nature of that transcendental plane is purely spiritual — formed of eternity, knowledge, and bliss — and supplies the spiritual entourage and paraphernalia required for each of the Lord’s pastimes. This sublime nature also enables Krsna to enact and re-enact His pastimes unlimitedly, by making both He and His devotees forget the pastimes after they are performed.

Let us now apply the principles outlined in the summary above to help us understand how Govardhana Hill, Krsna’s eternal associate, can make its appearance in Goloka.

Giriraja is eternally present in Goloka. But when Radha requests that Krsna create a special place for their rasa-lila pastime, Yogamaya makes the Vraja-vasis forget that it had ever existed. Then, as if for the first time, Krsna manifests the king of mountains to reign over Goloka until the Lord or His pastime potency, Yogamaya, again desires to enact the pastime of Giriraja’s appearance. In this way there is a perpetually occurring sequence of events in which Krsna desires the pastime, Yogamaya exercises her powers of illusion, Radha petitions Krsna for a secluded place, Krsna manifests Govardhana Hill, and finally Krsna and the gopis enjoy the rasa-lila there.

Because Govardhana becomes visible at some point in eternal time, Goloka seems to share the transience characteristic of the material realm. And while Krsna and the Vraja-vasis accept that transience as actual, in truth it is merely virtual. Giriraja exists eternally, as does everyone and everything in Goloka. However, Yogamaya employs pr??? as an instrument to cover the omniscience of the residents of Goloka, forcing them to perceive transience in the realm of absolute permanence. By so doing, she enables full realisation of the absolute sweetness and variety that are so unique to the topmost Vaikuntha planet, Goloka Vrndavana.

## Appendix 16

**Krsna Convinces the Gopas That He Can Lift Govardhana Hill**

*(This appendix follows from the narration of the events leading up to Krsna’s lifting Govardhana Hill as they are described in chapter nine.)*

Indra was furious that the Vraja-vasis had stopped offering the sacrifice meant for him and offered it instead to Govardhana Hill. He thus sent rains of destruction to punish them. It was in Paitha that Krsna consulted with His cowherd friends what to do.

Rain fell in solid columns, flooding the pastures around Govardhana Hill. Beneath an expansive kadamba tree, Krsna, knee deep in water and shivering from cold, held Sridama’s hand as He addressed His friends.

“O friends! Subala, Madhumangala, and Subhadra! Angry King Indra is determined to destroy the Vraja-vasis.”

As Krsna spoke, a nearby tree, toppled by the wind, crashed to the ground, dramatising Gopala’s statement. Frightened by the crashing tree and pelting rain, little Visala wrapped himself around Krsna’s left arm and buried his face in Krsna’s chest.

With a slight smile, an older gopa said, “Kanai! It was Your suggestion that our people ignore the king of heaven. Now it is Your duty to protect them.”

“Protect them?” exclaimed another older boy, Gobhatta. “How will our butter-soft friend protect the Vraja-vasis against this deluge?”

Krsna replied, as He gently stroked Visala to comfort him, “Friend! I have a plan.”

As if in defiance of Hari, lightning flashed over Giriraja and the gale-force winds gusted even stronger. The air was thick with rain, leaves, and flying debris.

Krsna’s soft radiance enveloped His stricken friends as His soothing voice continued, “The best protection from a rain storm is an umbrella. Therefore I propose to protect the Vraja-vasis...”

“By giving them umbrellas!” exclaimed Subala.

“It will take more than umbrellas to protect us from this torrent,” Vijaya added.

“Maybe you should give them boats,” said another drenched gopa.

Krsna accepted the sarcasm of His friends with a beneficent smile and, with a soaked peacock feather dangling from His turban, replied, “There is one umbrella which will also serve as a boat to carry us over this great danger.”

Raising His voice over the roaring thunder Hari added, “After the Annakuta ceremony, Govardhana Hill assured us of His protection. Therefore He will be our umbrella and our boat.”

The cowherd boys cast incredulous glances at one another. “And who will lift Your gigantic umbrella?” they asked in unison.

The rain, thunder, and lightning let up to give everyone a better chance to hear Krsna vow, “I will! I will lift Giriraja and give shelter to our people and animals.”

None of the boys took their talkative friend seriously. One by one, they simply humoured Him.

“Oh, that’s rich,” replied Sridama. “I have to carry Your lunch pack because it’s too heavy...”

“And you’re going to lift a mountain?” finished Vijaya.

Subala chided, “Friend! This is no time for jokes. Let’s hear a practical proposal.”

But Krsna simply raised His charming eyebrows and smiled.

Still hugging Krsna’s arm, little Visala looked up and cried, “Kanai! I cannot let You do anything dangerous.”

Devaprastha added, “Let’s find Baladeva, He’ll solve this problem. He’s the eldest.”

But Krsna merely turned towards Govardhana Hill and continued, “Empowered by the love of My friends, I can lift the three worlds, what to speak of a hill.”

“Impossible!” said the older boys.

“It’s too dangerous,” added the younger ones.

“Even if it were possible,” Stokakrsna asserted, “I would never allow it!”

But Krsna just kept smiling, as rainwater dripped from His turban onto His face.

The boys, knowing well Krsna’s willful nature, exchanged uneasy glances. Then an older boy spoke up, “Kanai. Don’t bite off more than You can chew. You may have killed some demons, but lifting Govardhana Hill is another thing. If You failed, surely You would sink in a mire of embarrassment.”

“It’s beyond You,” yelled one gopa.

“You’re dreaming,” said another.

Stokakrsna began to think that Krsna might just be able to do it. Hadn’t He already performed miracles? But if Krsna were to suddenly lift Govardhana Hill without warning, some of the boys might die of shock, overwhelmed with anxiety for Krsna’s safety. So to reassure the boys of Krsna’s strength, Stokakrsna thought to test Him.

Stokakrsna said, “Be reasonable Kanai! A little wrestling tires You. How can You lift a hill?”

Just as Krsna was about to reply, Madhumangala, head wrapped in his cadara, stepped forward and said, “Friend! Right as you are about Krsna’s excessive self-esteem, why do you underestimate me?”

Startled, Stokakrsna turned as Madhu announced, “Krsna may be a weakling, and His fickle appetite may be the cause of His frailty, but I am a storehouse of mystic power. I can empower Him to do the impossible.”

Madhu stuck his thumbs in his belt and rolled on his heels, and the gopas laughed hysterically. When their laughter died down, Stokakrsna challenged both Krsna and His friend, “Brother! Let us test Your strength—and Madhu’s mystic power.” Pointing to the tree that sheltered the boys, Stokakrsna said:

“First let us see You twist the trunk of this kadamba tree, like wringing out a wet piece of cloth. When You have done that, then we might believe You can lift Govardhana Hill.”

In the midst of applause, Sridama stepped forward and announced, “Stokakrsna! Even I can twist this puny twig. Let Gopala not only twist the trunk of this tree but also tie its branches together in a knot. Then I will give my consent for a greater undertaking.”

Stokakrsna looked to his friend. What could Krsna not do? Such feats would hardly be a challenge for Him. Yet true to Krsna’s humanlike pastimes, Stokakrsna raised his eyebrows as if to say, “Kanai! Do the needful.”

And Krsna did just that. As easily as Mother Yasoda could twist the churning rod in a pot of butter, He twisted the tree’s trunk between the palms of His hands and then, to the amazement of His friends, gathered the branches of the tree into two enormous handfuls and tied them together in a knot as the gopas clapped and cheered.

Love and wonder are inseparable; the greater the love, the greater is the sense of marvel. And for those who love Krsna, that increasing sense of wonder causes the impossible, the superhuman, to seem merely wonderful. Thus the humanly impossible feats Krsna performed, rather than distancing Him from His friends by awe, simply endeared Him to them all the more.

Tying the branches of a tree into an enormous knot or lifting Govardhana Hill are deeds beyond the scope of great mortals, what to speak of small boys. But the Vraja-vasis loved Krsna so much that they accepted Krsna’s superhuman acts as mere wondrous feats. They could not think of Him as superhuman. They simply thought, “How wonderful is our boy Krsna!”

And with every wonderful deed Krsna performed, the Vraja-vasis’ love for Him increased more and more. They never considered Hari to be anything but a cowherd, one of them, a village acquaintance, the whimsical son of Mother Yasoda.

Having satisfied His friends with His prowess and secured their permission, Krsna hurried off towards Govardhana Hill. The cowherd boys followed in Krsna’s wake, entering the rain-filled darkness, fully confident that once again He would do something truly wonderful.

## Appendix 17

**The Vraja-vasis Question Sri Krsna’s Divinity**

*(This appendix follows from the pastime in which the citizens of Nandagrama question their king about the divinity and origin of his son, as it is narrated in chapter ten.)*

The pastime found in this appendix — recounting the meeting Nanda Maharaja had with the Vraja-vasis after Krsna lifts Govardhana Hill — is a retelling of the version found in Sri Garga-samhita. The accounts of these events found in Garga-samhita depart from Srimad-Bhagavatam in significant ways. The essential difference between the two versions is that in the former Nanda Maharaja establishes Krsna as God Himself, while in the latter as a God-empowered human.

These variant descriptions each portray the devotional mood of the Vraja-vasis in a fundamentally different way, affecting the mood of the reader correspondingly. The Garga-samhita inspires in its audience a mood of awe and reverence towards Krsna, Srimad- Bhagavatam one of spontaneous attachment.

Devotees who doubt the authenticity of the Garga-samhita due to the apparent contradictions in these two texts sometimes go to theextreme of concluding that these two pastimes must have happened in different kalpas.

But the tone and tenor of the Garga-samhita can be understood from multiple angles. If, for instance, a devotee hears the statement of Sri Garga-samhita, “Krsna is the Supreme Lord,” and interprets it to mean, “Krsna, as a human being, has the powers of the Supreme Lord,” then that devotee’s mood is in concert with Srimad- Bhagavatam. In that case this pastime may be taken as ancillary to the perspective of the Bhagavatam.

To a devotee reading in that mood, the issue of whether this pastime took place in one day of Brahma or another is irrelevant. On the other hand, a devotee who accepts literally the Vraja-vasis’ declaration that “Krsna is the Supreme Lord,” might reasonably conclude that the pastime as described in the Garga-samhita took place in a different day of Brahma.

Devotees may accept either scenario because both come from the teachings of authorised scriptures. Still, while recognising that Krsna is in fact the Supreme Personality of Godhead, Lord Caitanya’s followers aspire for the mood that the Vraja-vasis display towards Krsna as described in Srimad-Bhagavatam.

Here, then, is the pastime, as adapted from the version of Sri Garga-samhita:

On the day after Krsna returned Govardhana Hill to its original place, the Vraja-vasis met amongst themselves to discuss a topic that was on everyone’s mind: Who was Krsna that He could lift a mountain? The leaders of Nandagrama and the other villages of Vraja, accompanied by their respected wives, came together in the great hall of Nanda Maharaja’s palace. Finding the king conversing with his wife, they addressed him respectfully, although the doubt and suspense they were feeling was evident in their voices.

One cowherd elder said, “O King! Not one of your ancestors could lift a hill. ? Queen! You could not hold up even a stone for seven days. But your seven-year-old boy playfully held up the tallest mountain in Vrndavana with one hand.”

Another gopa continued, “We cannot even imagine the extent of Krsna’s strength. This superhuman act — surpassing all His other feats, like killing great demons — has made us question who He really is.” A third gopa finally blurted out the doubt they all shared: Was Krsna actually Nanda and Yasoda’s son? “O Yasoda! Your complex' ion is fair, as is that of your great husband. But this boy is very dark; indeed His complexion is unlike that from either side of your family. And Krsna’s behaviour, like Balarama’s, is that of a ksatriya. Now tell us the truth. Was this boy really born in a family of gopas?”

At these words a great commotion erupted in the assembly hall, and one of the more fiery cowherds said, “If you don’t answer our question there will be a quarrel.”

Another gopa added, “Indeed, we will leave this community and go elsewhere, where the words of royalty ring true.”

When Yasoda heard these accusations and felt the rising anger of her subjects, she went pale in fear and held on to Rohini’s hand for support. But her husband, calm and composed as befit his position, replied to the angry gopas just as a father would pacify his rowdy children.

Nanda Maharaja said, “O great gopas! I shall repeat the words of wisdom Garga Muni spoke at the name-giving ceremony of my son. Hearing his words, you will no doubt at once be freed from your anxiety.

“Letter by letter, Garga Muni explained to me the meaning of my son’s name. He said that ? means ‘the lover of the goddess of fortune’; r stands for Lord Ramacandra; s indicates that He is the Lord of six opulences, or the Lord who resides in Svetadvipa; n means ‘Nrsimha’; a denotes ‘the eternal one,’ or ‘He who enjoys offerings in the sacrificial fire’; and h stands for rhe great rsis, Nara and Narayana. The combination of these six letters forms the name of the original Supreme Personality of Godhead, who is perfect and complete in every way. That is why my son is called Krsna.’’

Nandaraja continued to relay the truths about Krsna that Garga Muni had told him years ago. “That omniscient sage said that my son appears in other ages with various complexions like white, red, or yellow. But at the end of this Dvapara-yuga He becomes dark complexioned, and that is also why He is called Krsna.

“This boy has many names like Vasudeva, the Lord of the Vasus, and Radha-pati, the husband of Radha, daughter of Kirtida. He comes whenever there is a need to re-establish religious principles. And so now, to relieve the earth of her burden in the form of demons like Kamsa, my son has descended from His transcendental abode Goloka.

“Finally, Garga Muni revealed the mysterious nature of Krsna’s names, pastimes and identity, which are hidden even from the Vedas. He told me, ‘Do not be surprised by your son’s extraordinary activities.”’

Nanda Maharaja concluded, “The Vedas declare that the words of a pure brahmana are the final testimony of what is truth; thus, since that time, I have never doubted the statements of Garga Muni.”

When Nanda Maharaja stopped speaking, silence filled the hall. Then a quiet discussion began amongst the men that gradually built to a feverish roar. The Vraja-vasis, already in doubt about Krsna’s identity, now doubted the truthfulness of Nanda Maharaja, and they looked for arguments that would discredit what he had said.

One gopa stood up and in a grave voice said, “If the great Garga Muni actually came to your home and performed a name-giving ceremony, then why didn’t you call us, your relatives, to come and witness it?”

Another gopa added, “Why would you keep the name-giving ceremony secret?”

As Nanda was about to tell the assembly that he had kept the ceremony secret out of fear of Kamsa’s atrocities, the Lord’s pastime potency inspired a feeling of outrage within the Vraja-vasis. As if one body, they all rose to their feet and stormed out of the palace, down the winding stairs to the foot of Nandisvara Hill. Thinking that there was no longer any reason to have faith in their king, the gopas headed for Varsana to take shelter of King Vrsabhanu.

Sometimes Yogamaya creates disharmony amongst Krsna’s associates in order to increase the variety in Krsna’s pastimes and to generate secondary ecstasies that nurture and heighten the Vraja-vasis’ spontaneous love for Him. Therefore, such expressions of anger and disagreement amongst the eternally liberated should not be mistaken for the disputes characteristic of conditioned souls.

Hearts burning with indignation, the belligerent gopas mounted their bullock carts, made their way to Varsana, and requested an audience with Nanda’s friend Vrsabhanu. The kindly king could see the anger on the faces of the simple gopas, and so he welcomed them with sweet words, offered them places to sit, and served refreshments. The welcome complete, the gopas then lodged their complaint.

An elder spoke up, “O noble-hearted king! As the Vraja-vasis’ leader, you are the exemplar. It has come to our notice that the king of Nandagrama has betrayed our trust. We request you to sever all ties of friendship with him, as we have.”

King Vrsabhanu was appalled by the Vraja-vasis’ demand. But seeing how distressed they were, he replied to them with sweet words in an accommodating voice, “Please tell me what reprehensible thing that venerable king of the gopas, my dear friend Nanda, has done to deserve my severing all ties with him.”

The spokesman replied, “Nanda has declared his son to be the Supreme Lord and your daughter the Supreme Goddess of fortune. Yet we do not see any evidence to substantiate his claim, even if his son Krsna does act wonderfully.”

Another gopa added, “The brahmanas cursed King Vena to be struck with a lightning bolt for declaring himself the Supreme Lord. And now our king has proclaimed his son to be the same. Such behaviour is unacceptable for followers of the Vedic tradition. For this reason, as a religious man, you must immediately distance yourself from Nanda or we will reject you as well.”

At this pronouncement a roar of agreement, like the sound of a crashing wave, rose from amongst the gopas. Then still another leader introduced a fresh doubt about the character of King Vrsabhanu.

He said, “O King! In your home lives a grown-up daughter of marriageable age. Have you too become so intoxicated by wealth and opulence, mistaking Her for Maha-Laksmi, that you think none of our sons good enough to marry Her?

“O noble one! Your hesitating to reject Nanda and his claims and your sequestering your daughter in this palace makes us doubt your character as well. Please immediately satisfy us with the words and conduct of one worthy of the sun god’s lineage.”

King Vrsabhanu could understand that the cowherd men were under the influence of some extraordinary power and thus he first spoke to them one by one, and when they were somewhat pacified he addressed the accusations they had placed against him.

Sri Vrsabhanu said, “O gopas. I shall answer your doubt by repeating the instructions Garga Muni gave me at the time of my daughter’s birth. By hearing his words, no doubt you will be freed from all your anxieties.

“Garga Muni said, ‘O King! I shall disclose to you the identity of your daughter by first making known the identity of the son who was born to Nanda just a year ago. That baby is the master of Goloka and the creator of countless universes. No one is greater than or equal to Him. On the request of Brahma, He has appeared in the family of cowherds to remove the earth’s burden by killing the demons headed by Kamsa.

‘“Now hear about your daughter, Krsna’s first queen of Goloka, whose glories are beyond your comprehension. You cannot even imagine how exalted She is. Because She is Krsna’s eternal wife, neither you nor I need to get Her married. Rather, Lord Brahma will oversee Their wedding ceremony in a beautiful secluded place by the shore of the Yamuna in Bhandiravana.

‘“O King! Do not doubt what I have revealed about the identity of your daughter. For your benefit I will now divulge another confidential truth — a truth regarding you and your subjects. You, your gopa-subjects, their wives, and even their cows have come to earth from Goloka. This arrangement was made at Radha’s insistence, for the Vraja-vasis are as inseparable from Her as the divine couple are from each other.’”

King Vrsabhanu paused as the cowherd men exchanged looks of amazement. He concluded, “After speaking thus Garga Muni left, and since that time I have never doubted the exalted position of my daughter, and for that reason I leave Her future in the hands of divine fate. O wise men! What else would you have me do when I am instructed by a great soul like Garga Muni?”

The cowherd men consulted amongst themselves. After some time — their faces and demeanour now peaceful and free from doubt — they replied, “O King! We consider that you must be telling the truth.

“Your daughter must be the goddess of fortune. What other explanation could there be for your unparalleled wealth and heavenly opulence? You are the Lord of unlimited numbers of intoxicated elephants, restless horses, golden chariots, and beautiful palanquins. You possess millions of cows, each beautifully decorated with bejewelled ornaments, embroidered blankets, and gold-plated horns. And your pleasure palaces, lit by jewel lamps and decorated with silk lace curtains, shame even King Kamsa’s capital.

“Your wealth is evidence that the goddess of fortune resides in your home. This we accept. But we see no evidence that Nanda’s wealth is equal to yours, and therefore see no reason to believe that Nanda’s son is the goddess of fortune’s husband, Narayana. The farmer King Nanda may have many cows, but compared to you heis poverty stricken. How could that be if his son were the Supreme Lord? This doubt remains. And this doubt fuels our mistrust in Nanda Maharaja and our reluctance to accept him as our king.”

The Vraja-vasis then requested King Vrsabhanu to prove that Nanda Maharaja was telling the truth. “If Nanda’s son is God, then please devise some indisputable way to reveal the truth of His divinity.”

Realising that he would have to do more than talk to convince the cowherd men, King Vrsabhanu thought of a plan to reveal the extent of Nanda Maharaja’s wealth.

As the cowherd men watched, King Vrsabhanu took from his treasury necklace after necklace of giant pearls — each pearl equal in value to a king’s crown — and placed every last one of them in jewelled containers. He then instructed his couriers to carry them to King Nanda, along with a proposal for Krsna’s marriage to Radharani.

When the cowherd men saw Maharaja Vrsabhanu’s gift, they talked amongst themselves, some saying, “Nanda Maharaja’s entire treasury does not amount to the value of one half of these necklaces,” while others said, “I think Nanda’s treasury is worth less than one twelfth the value of this gift.”

Nonetheless, the Vraja-vasis all agreed that if Nandaraja could match King Vrsabhanu’s engagement gift, it would be evidence of a divine arrangement. Krsna’s godhood would then be confirmed. Eager to see how Krsna would meet this test, the cowherd men returned to their homes, agreeing to meet again in Vrsabhanu-pura two days later.

Soon thereafter, accompanied by soldiers carrying the jewelled containers filled with pearls, King Vrsabhanu’s minister entered the assembly hall of Nanda Maharaja, bowed before the king and presented the marriage proposal along with the precious gifts.

The minister said, “Now that his daughter Radha, who is more splendid than the moon and whose eyes are like new lotus flowers, is of marriageable age, King Vrsabhanu has given thought to who should be Her husband. His conclusion is that your son, who is as charming as Kamadeva himself and whose powerful arm lifted Govardhana Hill, is the most qualified boy in the community of cowherds. O King! Please accept this proposal of Vrsabhanu along with this gift of pearls and act according to our respected tradition.”

Nanda Maharaja examined the gifts he had received and was struck with wonder at their great value. He respectfully thanked the minister and entered his inner chambers to see if he had anything to equal Vrsabhanu’s gift. After consulting Yasoda-devi, the king concluded that they had nothing in their possession equal to those pearls.

Nanda Maharaja told his wife, “If we do not reciprocate with a suitable gift, we will be embarrassed before everyone, and our son will lose a most qualified bride. Everyone will mock us. What should we do?”

Krsna overheard the discussion between Nanda and Yasoda and decided to save His parents from the threat of public ridicule. Krsna took one hundred pearl necklaces from King Vrsabhanu’s gift and planted each pearl one by one as a farmer would sow his fields.

Naturally, the missing pearls did not go unnoticed by King Nanda. Upon discovering their absence early the next day, Nanda began to wonder who in his household could have taken them. The king thought, “I’ll ask Krsna or Balarama if They know anything about this theft.”

When questioned Krsna smiled and respectfully answered, “Dear Father! In the way that farmers sow seeds, I planted some pearls in a field yesterday.”

Hearing this, Nanda Maharaja rebuked his son and then took Him to the fields to retrieve the planted pearls. When they arrived at the site, however, Nanda Maharaja saw hundreds of beautiful, tall, green-leaved pearl trees, each bearing hundreds of bunches of pearls. Everywhere Nanda Maharaja looked he saw millions of pearls all shining like so many stars in the sky. And these pearls were even bigger and more beautiful than those sent hy his friend Vrsabhanu. Through the tears welling up in his eyes, Nanda Maharaja gazed at his son and remembered who He actually was — the Supreme Lord — and then embraced Krsna to his chest.

Nandaraja quickly had his men harvest enough pearls to fill many cartloads — considerably more than he had received from Maharaja Vrsabhanu. Then he presented these treasures to the chief minister of Varsana’s kingdom. In this way, Nanda accepted the marriage proposal with dignity and gave gifts to King Vrsabhanu that were perfectly in keeping with what he had first received, and even more importantly, which accurately reflected how much he valued Radha as a prospective daughter-in-law.

The cowherd men from Nandaraja’s kingdom were at that time assembled in the court of King Vrsabhanu. When they saw the pearls sent by Nanda Maharaja, they unanimously accepted them as proof of Krsna’s divinity. With bowed heads they admitted their offence in doubting their king and they hastily returned to Nandagrama to beg forgiveness and to glorify the divine son of Mother Yasoda. The cowherd men could understand that Nanda Maharaja was not exaggerating when he said that his son was directly Lord Narayana. And from that day on they never doubted Krsna’s divinity or the words of His fortunate father.

The place where Krsna planted pearls later became the lake Mukta-sarovara. If in this place one donates a single pearl received in charity, he attains the result of having given one hundred thousand times that number.

## Appendix 18

**The Liberation of Astavakra Muni**

*(This appendix follows from the general description of and the pastimes related to Kusuma-sarovara narrated in chapter eleven.)*

The Brahma-vaivarta Purana describes in detail an incident referred to through oral tradition by the residents of Govardhana — the liberation of Astavakra Muni. Oral tradition says that a shrine for Lord Siva on the western bank of Kusuma-sarovara is the site at which the sage achieved perfection. Scripture narrates the following pastime leading to that event.

One evening in the month of Caitra (March-April) Krsna and the gopis were enjoying a rasa dance hy the hanks of the Yamuna. These festivals include not only the dance itself but also pastimes that precede and follow it, like wandering in the forest and bathing in the river.

On this occasion, Krsna had expanded Himself into as many forms as there were gopis but gave each gopi the impression that He was exclusively by her side. In this way He cleverly avoided the possibility that any of the gopis would become even slightly jealous of one another. The Lord could then enjoy His pastimes without disturbance.

Meanwhile, in His original form, Krsna remained alone with Srimati Radharani in a grove even moonlight could not penetrate. There the divine couple, full of affection, enjoyed dressing and ornamenting each other. Sri Radha, using sandalwood paste, aguru, saffron, and kasturi, painted decorative pictures on Krsna’s glistening body. She then tied His hair in a topknot with a garland of fragrant jasmine flowers.

Krsna reciprocated by painting delicate creepers on Her cheeks, a spot on Her forehead using kasturi, vermilion, and sandalwood. He then arranged Her hair so beautifully that even Her attendants were amazed.

In charming ways like these, Radha and Krsna dressed and ornamented each other, intoxicated to be together. That delightful atmosphere also intoxicated Their gopi attendants, some of whom took up instruments to accompany others who sang indescribably sweet songs.

This same pastime was repeated in as many bowers as there were gopis, and with each gopi Krsna perfectly reciprocated in ways that satisfied her unique mood of loving devotion.

Fully dressed and ornamented, Radha and Krsna stepped out of

Their forest cottage, accompanied by only a few friends, and left the banks of the Yamuna to wander in the direction of Govardhana Hill. They were drawn by Giri Govardhana’s magnetic desire to serve Them. Enchanting mango, asoka, jasmine, silk-fruit, kadamba, bakula and campaka trees delighted the divine couple as They wandered through Vrndavana forest until They arrived at Puspavana.

As always, Puspavana was rich with clusters of pious trees and creepers which were heavily laden with fruits and flowers of all seasons and gave shelter to pious animals and birds. The forest resounded with the cries of overjoyed peacocks, the humming of intoxicated bees, and the calls of jublilant cuckoos. The air was thick with the aroma and pollen of flowers that lazy breezes carried with what seemed like great effort. Only when invigorated by the spray from sacred ponds could the wind gather enough strength to rustle the leaves on the trees.

When the divine couple entered the heady atmosphere of Puspavana, Their already weary limbs refused to move any further, and thus They took refuge in a cluster of malati creepers and lay down to rest.

A short while later, awakened by the sound of lapping waves, Krsna looked out and saw that They had been sleeping by the bank of Kusuma-sarovara. He quickly woke up Radha and convinced Her to join Him in the water to refresh Themselves.

Moonlight played upon the dancing waves as the divine couple stood on a decorated staircase at the water’s edge, exchanging Their ornate clothes and jewellery for simple bathing clothes handed to Them by Their assistants.

The lake was filled with crystal clear water and multi-coloured clusters of lotus flowers surrounded by greedy bees, whose humming created a continuous hissing sound. At first the hissing sound frightened Radha, but when Krsna assured Her that it was not a snake, She joined Him, and hand in hand They stepped down the stairs into the lake’s cool embrace.

Radha and Krsna enjoyed splashing each other, hiding from each other within the lotus clusters, swimming together out into the deepwater, and other pleasing water sports. When Krsna came across some special thousand-petal lotuses, He plucked two, one for His beloved and one for Himself. After some time, fully refreshed, the couple emerged from the water and were quickly dried and dressed by Their attendants, who had enjoyed even more than They had, simply by watching Radha’s and Krsna’s water pastimes.

Thereafter a gopi brought Krsna a tray full of fragrant ointments. He lovingly dabbed a mixture of sandalwood paste, aguru, kasturi, and saffron on Radha’s limbs before They took a stroll on the path encircling the lake. On the western side of Kusuma-sarovara, just behind a beautiful marble staircase ornamented with gems that shone like lamps in the moonlight, was a banyan tree. That banyan stood at the centre of a crescent of jasmine trees, creating an irresistibly aromatic shelter.

Radha and Madhava sat down at the base of the banyan tree and glorified the beauty of the moonlit forest and its precious lake. Suddenly, the nearby sound of a rustling bush startled Them. Peering into the semi-darkness They could see a dwarfish, deformed figure coming towards them, its naked body covered only by a long beard and scraggly hair.

It was the great sage Astavakra.

From long years of fasting, the ascetic’s body was nearly without flesh. His uncut fingernails were long and curly, and due to severe austerities fire was shooting from his mouth. Despite this fearful display, however, Astavakra radiated composure from his eyes and effulgence from his body, exhibiting a transcendental perfection possible only by exalted worship of the Supreme Lord.

When Radha saw the sage’s deformed body She began to chuckle, but Hari stopped Her and said under His breath, “Do not laugh. He is Our great devotee.”

Astavakra Muni had been meditating on Radha and Krsna for sixty thousand years. Now that the objects of his meditation had appeared before him in person, he bowed down before the Lord and His consort, bathing Their feet with tears of ecstasy. After some time, having received the reassuring touch of Krsna’s hand on his back, the sage rose to his knees. Fixing his eyes on the lotus faces of the divine couple, the great sage offered Krsna a prayer he had received long ago from Lord Siva.

The muni prayed, “O Lord! I offer my obeisances unto You, the transcendent shelter of virtuous souls, who is beyond the modes of nature and yet is their cause, foundation, shelter, and master.

“Obeisances unto You, O transcendental Lord of all virtues, O form of perfection, cause of perfection, stages of perfection, O most perfect of the perfect, and their teacher as well.

“Obeisances unto You of eternal form, personality, and pastimes unknown even by the personified Vedas, although You are their knower, professor, goal, and abode.

“Obeisances unto You, the Lord of everyone including Brahma, Ananta Sesa, Indra, and Dharma. Obeisances to you, Lord of everything including the seed of the tree of the universe, the tree itself, its fruits, indeed of all material nature.

“Obeisances unto You, O Lord of the universal form! You are the cause of creation, maintenance, and destruction, the cause of all causes, yet without cause Yourself. O invisible one, beyond reach of the senses! As you move about at will, You are always visible to Radhika and Her friends. My obeisances unto You.”

The instant Astavakra Muni completed his prayer, he collapsed at Sri Krsna’s feet and gave up his life. Sri Radha looked on as a divine light — the soul of Astavakra — emerged from the sage’s body and circumambulated the Lord. Meanwhile the sound of rolling wheels heralded the descent of a gem-encrusted chariot from Goloka. On the chariot stood a number of attendants who resembled Krsna and who immediately descended from the vehicle, bowing to Radha and Krsna with respect.

The soul that was once Astavakra Muni appeared in his original spiritual form and boarded the chariot, which promptly departed for Goloka, leaving Radha astonished at the rapid chain of events.

With the sage’s lifeless body still before Them, Krsna — who is always indebted for the least service rendered to Him by His devotee — immediately began to make arrangements for its cremation. He collected an abundance of wood and then, His tears wetting His own chest and washing the body of His devotee, Krsna placed the body of the sage on the pyre and lit it, watching as the flames gradually consumed both the wood and the body. When all that was left was ashes, Hari fainted out of intense separation from His devotee. After some time, Radha was able to revive Him with affectionate words and caresses.

Once Krsna had regained His composure, Radha, unable to contain Herself any longer, enquired from Him, “Dearest! Why was this sage — obviously illustrious — so deformed and ugly? Why did he give up his body just at that time? And why did You become so emotional by performing his funeral rites? Please tell Me the reasons in detail, for I am fascinated by these events.”

Sri Krsna paused for some time to recall the many lifetimes of His devotee, stretching as they did through previous millennia. He then began:

“The wise have explained the famous and well-studied history of Astavakra.

“At the beginning of creation, Brahma gave birth to many sages from his limbs. Amongst them were the Pracetas, who had two sons, Sriman and Asita. The latter was a great sage who performed austerities with his wife for one thousand celestial years; still, they were unable to beget a single child even as the end of their lives approached.

Feeling compassion, Lord Siva gave them a mantra, and after one hundred years the couple conceived a son whose name was Devala.

“Devala was married, but even as a young and handsome man the desire to renounce worldly life arose in him. One night he disappeared, leaving his family and kingdom and taking up a life of asceticism in a cave of Gandhamadana mountain.

“Once, as Devala was absorbed in meditation, the Apsara Rambha happened by and, being very passionate, she propositioned the self-satisfied sage. He wisely refused her offer, at which she became incensed and cursed him thus: ‘O brahmana, your mind is wicked. May your body reflect your state of being. My curse upon you is that all the limbs of your body become miserably deformed and black as soot.’ “Devala, distressed at this sudden turn of affairs, immediately thought of entering into fire. But I appeared to reassure him that he would ultimately achieve My grace. And since he was bent in all eight places of his body, I playfully gave him the name Astavakra. Coming to this place, Astavakra performed intense meditation, not eating for sixty thousand years. His fire of digestion consumed all the flesh and blood in his body, and he became even more fearsome looking.” Krsna concluded, “By his intense austerities, Astavakra’s spiritual desire was fulfilled, and he attained pure love for Me. I came here to free him from his mortal bondage, and in return for his devotional sen vice, I personally conducted his final rites and saw him off to Goloka.

“O Radhika! This sage was unequalled, and therefore I lamented His passing with tears. Now that I have told You the story of his past life, and now that You have personally witnessed his perfection, please bless him with eternal service in Our eternal Vraja.”

Taking Krsna’s instruction to heart, Radha folded Her hands and bowed Her head in the direction that the soul of Astavakra Muni had departed. She then continued to enquire from Krsna about the ancient histories of other famous persons connected to Astavakra. And when the birds signalled the break of dawn, Radha and Krsna left the banks of Kusuma-sarovara and returned to where the other gopis were waiting by Yamuna’s shore. From there, everyone returned home before their parents or in-laws could notice their absence.

## Appendix 19

**Which Queens of Dvaraka Heard Srimad-Bhagavatam, at Kusuma-sarovara**

*(This appendix follows from the descriptions of Uddhava’s narration of Srimad-Bhagavatam to Lord Krsna's queens at Uddhava-kunda in chapters eleven and thirteen.)*

The pastime in which the queens of Dvaraka listen to Uddhava speak Srimad-Bhagavatam at Kusuma-sarovara raises an obvious question: How did the queens get to Vrndavana?

This question arises due to an apparent contradiction that puts into doubt the queens’ presence in Vrndavana. Srimad-Bhagavatam tells us that, after the disappearance of most of the Yadus and the destruction of Dvaraka orchestrated by Krsna, a gang of cowherds kidnapped Krsna’s wives while Arjuna was escorting them from Dvaraka to Indraprastha. But this being the case, how did the queens later turn up in Vrndavana, under Vajranabha’s protection, and hear Srimad-Bhagavatam from Uddhava?

Let us reconstruct the history surrounding these events by examining Srila Prabhupada’s purport to the Bhagavatam verse just mentioned and consulting other authoritative hooks, to see if this apparent contradiction can be reasonably resolved.

In the Skanda Purana, Uddhava is quoted as saying,

**“The Lord ordered Arjuna to settle them \[the queens\] in Vraja.”**

This statement shows that Krsna wanted Arjuna — who was in Dvaraka at the time of the Lord’s departure from the world — to take the Lord’s wives to Mathura-Vrndavana.

But when Arjuna finally arrived in Indraprastha and delivered the news to his brothers that the Lord had brought His earthly pastimes to a close, he also reported a second calamity. Arjuna said:

“O Emperor, now I am separated from my friend and dearmost well-wisher, the Supreme Personality of Godhead, and therefore my heart appears to be void of everything. In His absence I have been defeated by a number of infidel cowherd men while I was guarding the bodies of all the wives of Krsna."

It seems impossible that Arjuna, who single-handedly could subdue an army, was defeated by a band of cowherds. There are two explanations for this: (1) Krsna had deprived Arjuna of his prowess, and (2) the cowherd men were none other than Krsna Himself.

But the question remains: If Krsna stole the queens, then how did they end up in Vrndavana?

One simple answer is that the narrations in Srimad-Bhagavatam and the Skanda Purana took place at different times, in different days of Brahma.

According to other evidence, however, both narrations could have occurred in this present day of Brahma. Let us examine that evidence beginning with Srila Prabhupada’s purport to the Bhagavatam verse spoken by Arjuna already quoted:

**“The important point in this verse is how it was possible that Arjuna could be defeated by a gang of ignoble cowherd men and how such mundane cowherd men could touch the bodies of the wives of Lord Krsna, who were under the protection of Arjuna. Srila Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura has justified the contradiction by research in the Visnu Purana and Brahma Purana. In these Puranas it is said that once the fair denizens of heaven pleased Astavakra Muni by their service and were blessed by the muni to have the Supreme Lord as their husband. Astavakra Muni was curved in eight joints of his body, and thus he used to move in a peculiar curved manner. The daughters of the demigods could not check their laughter upon seeing the movements of the muni and the muni, being angry at them, cursed them that they would be kidnapped by rogues, even if they would get the Lord as their husband. Later on, the girls again satisfied the muni by their prayers, and the muni blessed them that they would regain their husband even after being robbed by the rogues. So, in order to keep the words of the great muni, the Lord Himself kidnapped His wives from the protection of Arjuna, otherwise they would have at once vanished from the scene as soon as they were touched by the rogues. Besides that, some of the gopis who prayed to become wives of the Lord returned to their respective positions after their desire was fulfilled. After the departure of Lord Krsna, He wanted all His entourage back to Godhead, and they were called back under different conditions only.”**

In this purport, Srila Prabhupada refers to a certain category of queens that were kidnapped: those who were previously the daughters of the demigods. But not all of Krsna’s wives in Dvaraka were demi-goddesses (deva-kanya) in their previous lives. Some were practising devotees who had attained perfection (sadhana-siddha), others were eternal associates (nitya-siddha), and still others — as mentioned by Prabhupada — were"gopis who had prayed to become wives of the Lord.” What happened to these other categories of queens?

Although Srila Prabhupada speaks mostly of the deva-kanya queens, his reference to the gopi queens at the end of the purport is telling.

Srila Prabhupada says,

**“Some of the gopis who prayed to become wives of the Lord returned to their respective positions after their desire was fulfilled.”**

The implication is that the queens who were originally gopis again became gopis in Vrndavana.

But how did they get there?

In the absence of a direct explanation from scripture, we suggest two possibilities. The first scenario is that only the deva-kanya queens were stolen from Arjuna, who managed to deliver the remaining queens to their destination. This explanation is more likely, as we will show.

The second scenario is that after Krsna stole the queens, He made arrangements to take all of them to Vraja. This seems unlikely, however, because Krsna would have had to abandon His wives a second time, which would have certainly been the cause of their death. At the very least, it would have been a topic that the queens would have raised later in the Skanda Purana narration; in their misery of separation, wouldn’t the queens have mentioned that Krsna had abandoned them twice?
