Nonetheless, it is generally accepted that when Krsna stole the queens they returned either to the heavenly planets or went back to Dvaraka in the spiritual sky. This is confirmed by Srila Prabhupada:

**“After the departure of Lord Krsna, He wanted all His entourage back to Godhead, and they were called back under different conditions only.”**

Bur this doesn’t necessarily mean that they all went back at the same rime.

Which leads us back to the first scenario: Krsna kidnapped only the deva-kanya queens, and Arjuna delivered the remaining queens to Vraja.

To support this first alternative, let us study the implication of Srila Prabhupada’s usage of the words “besides that” in the sentence “Besides that, some of the gopis who prayed to become wives of the Lord returned to their respective positions after their desire was fulfilled.”

Here the use of the word “besides” is straightforward, and thus its direct meaning as a preposition should be accepted as “in addition to” or “apart from.” This implies that there were other queens aside from the deva-kanyas, and, as the next sentence declares, “they were called back under different conditions only.”

What were the different conditions? We are not provided details, but the implication is that different queens went back to Krsna in different ways at different times, a conclusion which certainly departs from the possibility of their being kidnapped all at once by the cowherd Krsna.

In further support of this scenario we need only examine the senfence to which Srila Prabhupada’s aside — “besides that” — directly refers: “The Lord Himself kidnapped His wives from the protection of Arjuna, otherwise they would have at once vanished from the scene as soon as they were touched by the rogues.” By his use of the word “otherwise,” Srila Prabhupada clearly suggests that the queens did not “vanish from the scene” because they were touched by “ignoble cowherd men,” they were kidnapped by Krsna.

Another verse from Srimad-Bhagavatam provides additional evidence that not all the queens were taken away from Arjuna, and that he did bring some of them back to Indraprastha: “Arjuna took the survivors of the Yadu dynasty—the women, children, and old men — to Indraprastha, where he installed Vajra as the ruler of the Yadus.”

In this regard, the Skanda Purana — towards the end of the narration under discussion — states that the queens who heard Srimad-Bhagavatam from Uddhava went back to Krsna. In fact, the actual statement is that “the queens who belonged to Krsna’s eternal pastimes suddenly disappeared from sight.” From this statement it seems clear that the queens who heard the Bhagavatam from Uddhava were not the daughters of demigods but Krsna’s eternal associates of whom one, Kalindi, is mentioned by name.

This evidence casts further doubt on the possibility that the two narrations describe two different days of Brahma during which Krsna appeared on earth. Why? Such a conclusion would mean that all different categories of queens were present in Vrndavana, which is clearly not true.

Therefore the preferred explanation for how, after having been kidnapped, the queens could have appeared at Kusuma-sarovara is this:

When Arjuna speaks in Srimad'Bhagavatam about the stolen queens, he does not mean that all the queens were stolen but only those who were daughters of demigods in their previous lives. The other queens he took to Indraprastha, where they were later entrusted to Vajra, when the latter was posted to Mathura: “Then he posted Vajra, the son of Aniruddha \[grandson of Lord Krsna\], at Mathura as the King of Surasena.”

## Appendix 20

**Who Found Gopala—Madhavendra Puri or Vallabhacarya?**

*(This appendix follows from the description of Srngara-sthali in chapter twelve.)*

Controversies between spiritual traditions enter the arena of history as often as they do philosophy, especially when those traditions share common icons, leaders, or places of worship. Such an ongoing dispute exists between the Gaudiya Vaisnava sampradaya and the Pusti-marga sampradaya over who discovered the Deity of Gopala, the Gaudiyas’ Madhavendra Puri or the Pustis’ Vallabhacarya.

Since faith is the foundation of both spiritual traditions, the Gaudiyas and Pustis naturally accept the history passed down to them by their respective authorities. Still, there is merit in examining the details surrounding Gopala’s discovery to determine if historical facts reinforce one version over the other.

The purpose of this appendix is to show that a bulk of the evidence supports the view that it was indeed Madhavendra Puri who found the Deity of Gopala, also known as Sri Nathaji. This evidence should satisfy the inquisitive Gaudiya Vaisnava and will perhaps offer foodfor thought to the critical Pusti-marga follower, whose authorities are the fortunate custodians of Gopala Nathaji.

One may ask: Why nitpick in this way over five-centuries-old history and risk inflaming denominational feelings?

Our considered response is that devotees should be informed that the historical facts surrounding this issue do not warrant acceptance of a scenario in which two opposing versions are both plausible. To do so would challenge the glory of Madhavendra Puri and especially the authenticity of the widely accepted scripture, Sri Caitanya-caritamrta.

Were such a major historical error to have found its way into the Caitanya-caritamrta, then might not other historical events recorded in the text, as well as its divine teachings — not to mention Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s godhood and omnipotence — all be called into question?

The history of Madhavendra Puri found in the Caitanya-caritamrta is a retelling hy Caitanya Mahaprabhu Himself. How could Lord Caitanya be God if His knowledge of those events — indeed of any event — was incorrect? Such a doubt cannot remain unchallenged. It is for this reason that we have rejected the possibility that either alternative could serve the purposes of history equally well.

Let us begin by summarising how these two schools describe Gopala’s appearance.

The main historical record of the Gaudiyas is Sri Caitanya-caritamrta, in which the author, Krsnadasa Kaviraja Gosvami, writes that Caitanya Mahaprabhu narrated the history of the Gopala Deity as He had heard it directly from His spiritual master, Isvara Puri, a direct disciple of Madhavendra Puri.

Madhavendra Puri, in his travels throughout India, came alone to Vraja and, absorbed in great ecstasy, stayed for some days beneath a tree in the area between Govinda-kunda and Indra-tila. Seeing Madhavendra without food, Krsna assumed the form of a local cowherd boy and brought His devotee a pot of milk. The boy’s beauty and sweetness haunted the sage, and that night the same boy came to Madhavendra in dream, led him to Srngara-sthali, and showed him the Deity of Gopala hidden in a thicket. The cowherd boy said, “I have been here, hiding in this bush for many days, suffering from the elements and pained by hunger. Come rescue Me and arrange for My proper worship once again.”

Madhavendra Puri awoke from his dream, called the local villagers, led them to the place Gopala had revealed, and found the Deity. He subsequently installed the Deity in a temple on Govardhana Hill just above Aniyora. The Deity is said to have been installed in 1479 AD. After establishing proper standards of worship for Gopala, Madhavendra Puri put two Bengali sannyasis in charge of the worship and, on the Deity’s order, went to Puri to bring sandalwood. That sandalwood was then offered to another Deity, Gopinatha, at Remuna, Orissa. It is said that Madhavendra Puri left this world without returning to Vrndavana, and thus his tomb is in Remuna.

The book Bhakti-ratnakara states that Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami — who had become the custodian of Gopala — handed over worship of the Deity to Vallabhacarya’s son, Viththalesvara, with whom Dasa Gosvami had a very cordial relationship. The primary reason for the endowment of the Deity to Viththalesvara was the reluctance of the Gaudiyas to walk up Govardhana Hill to worship Gopala. From that time on, the followers of Vallabhacarya have worshipped Gopala. In 1669 Aurangzeh sent his army to raze the temples in Vrndavana, and Gopala was taken to the kingdom of Mewar, Rajasthan, where in 1675 He was installed at his present home in Nathadvara.

The Pusti-marga sampradayas version of how Gopala was discovered is quite different.

According to the followers of Vallabhacarya (born 1479), Gopala first manifest in 1410, when the Deity’s arm, protruding from a stone of Govardhana Hill, became visible to a local brahmana named Sadhu Pande. It is said that by 1479 the Deity’s face had emerged and during this period the locals, including Madhavendra Puri, worshipped the Lord.

Vallabhacarya, at the age of fourteen, received a divine command to go to Govardhana, at which time Gopala literally leapt out of the hill and, in His original form of Krsna, embraced His devotee.

Gopala requested Vallabhacarya to worship Him in His Deity form, which the Lord then again resumed. Gaudiyas and Pustis jointly worshipped the Deity until the Gaudiyas were completely replaced by the Pustis. Then, as in the Gaudiya account, when Aurangzeb’s soldiers instructed Gopala’s servants to remove the Deity from Vraja, Gopala was transported to Mewar.

Now what is that “bulk of evidence” we referred to earlier that favours the Gaudiyas’ version of Gopala’s discovery over that of the Pustis’?

That evidence consists of the testimony of local tradition, logical argument, the statements of various acaryas, the character of the two saints Madhavendra and Vallabha, scriptural prediction, and finally the recorded statements of Gopala Himself. We contend that if an objective observer evaluates this evidence, it will in fact amount to credible proof of the Gaudiya Vaisnava version.

**Srila Madhavendra Puri**

Let us start with the oral tradition of Vrndavana, particularly that of Govardhana. Excluding of course the followers of Vallabha — who live mostly in Yatipura — amongst the locals it is common knowledge that Madhavendra Puri rediscovered Gopala. The local residents and the saints of every religious tradition in Vraja are all of one opinion. Having visited the places in the area of Govardhana and throughout Vraja extensively for more than three decades, the author has met no one, besides the Vallabhas, who contends that Vallabhacarya discovered Gopala.

And there is good reason for them to hold that opinion.

Sri Caitanya-caritamrta describes that the discovery, installation, and worship of Gopala was a very public event. Not only did the local villagers of Aniyora participate in some way, but so did residents of all the villages of Vraja, people from provinces around Vraja, and wealthy capitalists from Mathura. In short, the witnesses to Madhavendra Puri’s greatness were numerous.

Tradition is also very clear about the fact that Gopala was one of the original Deities commissioned and installed by Vajra, Krsna’s great-grandson. By the Deity’s own testimony, His worship had been interrupted, and, having been installed by Vajra, the Deity had to have been already existing before the historical epoch in question. Therefore, there is no question of His having manifested out of Govardhana Hill for Vallabhacarya.

Simple logic dictates that those who accept the divinity of Lord Caitanya also accept His conclusions. Vallabhacarya met Lord Caitanya on many occasions and accepted Him as Krsna Himself, the Supreme Lord. Vallabha, then, must have accepted the Lord’s judgement that Madhavendra Puri discovered Gopala. It would behove Vallabha’s followers to do the same. To do otherwise would he illogical.

The self-realised Gaudiya acaryas give the same conclusion as that given by tradition and logic.

Krsnadasa Kaviraja Gosvami, having lived at Radha-kunda barely thirty years after Gopala’s appearance, and having spent a good portion of his life there, is a reliable witness to the common understanding of the time regarding Gopala’s history. He dedicated two chapters of his magnum opus to the story of Madhavendra Puri. Those chapters reflect the common knowledge of the time, that Madhavendra Puri discovered Gopala. There is little chance, therefore, that Krsnadasa Kaviraja was wrong. Nonetheless, one may argue that his being a Gaudiya Vaisnava could have prejudiced Krsnadasa’s presentation. Such an accusation, however, challenges his qualifications as a widely revered author of transcendental works. Just the opposite! Krsnadasa’s objectivity is revealed in his narrative, which gives credit to Viththalesvara, the son of Vallabha, for hosting Gopala and the Gosvamis of Vrndavana when they all lived at Viththala’s home in Mathura for two months.

In fact, the close friendship enjoyed by the Gaudiya acaryas and the leaders of the Pusti-marga sampradaya suggests that they shared a common view of Gopala’s discovery. In addition to the example just given — how the Gaudiyas were invited to live in Viththalesvara’s home — a second example corroborates this friendship: how Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami granted custodianship of Gopala to Viththalesvara.

It is unimaginable to think that our acaryas would he so favourably disposed to a spiritual lineage that contested the origin of their common Deity, Gopala, or minimised the root of their disciplic succession, Madhavendra Puri. Thus it is highly likely that the understanding of Gopala’s discovery by Madhavendra Puri was common to acaryas of both sampradayas.

Further evidence from the acaryas comes from the great devotee Raghavacarya, who lived by the foot of Govardhana Hill at the same time that Krsnadasa lived at Radha-kunda.

Within the Vaisnava community, Raghavacarya was considered the most knowledgeable regarding the places of Krsna’s pastimes. He knew well both their locations and their supporting traditions and scriptures. Chapter five of Bhakti-ratnakara describes how Raghavacarya took Srinivasa Acarya and Narottama Dasa Thakura on parikrama of Vraja and how, upon coming to Srngara-sthali, he described that Gopala revealed Himself to Madhavendra Puri.

Later, on the opposite side of Govardhana Hill, he explained how Viththalesvara was given the service of worshipping Gopala after Lord Caitanya’s departure from Vraja.

Another acarya to give supporting evidence is Thakura Bhaktivinoda. Srila Prabhupada writes:

**“Bhaktivinoda Thakura annotates that this Gopala Deity was originally installed by Vajra, the great-grandson of Krsna. Madhavendra Puri rediscovered Gopala and established Him on top of Govardhana Hill. This Gopala Deity is still situated at Nathadvara and is under the management of descendants of Vallabhacarya.”**

Finally, as shown in the following quotes, Srila Prabhupada was true to the opinion of his predecessors and consistently related that Madhavendra Puri found Gopala-Nathaji.

**“So Caitanya Mahaprabhu happened to be a Bengali, but He belonged to the Madhva sampradaya. His guru was Isvara Puri, and his guru, Isvara Puri’s guru, was Madhavendra Puri. And Madhavendra Puri belonged to Madhvacarya sampradaya. The Gopala, the Nathadvara Gopala, that belonged to...Originally it was owned by Madhavendra Puri. Then it was delivered, the Deity was delivered, to the Visnu Svami sampradaya”**

**“We also, we have got our Gopala, as Madhavendra Puri installed a Gopala. That Gopala is now worshipped, Nathadvara, by the Vallabha sampradaya. That is Gaudiya Vaisnava Deity. It is admitted in the court.”**

And when Srila Prabhupada was specifically asked about the controversy, he cited an interesting piece of evidence: a court case (just mentioned) in which the decision favoured the Gaudiyas’ version of history.”

**Tamala Krsna**: “That is a big...That’s the reason. I had a discussion with one man. So I was stating that Madhavendra Puri found this Deity, Gopala, but their teaching is that Vallabhacarya found the Deity. I said, ‘No, Madhavendra Puri handed the Deity over later on for worship, but it was he who found the Deity and originated the worship on Govardhana, and all the villagers —  
**Prabhupada**: “It has been confirmed in the court that this Deity belongs to Gaudiya Vaisnavas. There was big case amongst themselves, and the court gave judgement that ‘This Deity belongs to the Gaudiya Vaisnava.’”  
**Acyutananda**: “Oh. When was that court case?”  
**Prabhupada**: “Say, about four, five years ago. No, about ten to fifteen years ago.”  
**Tamala Krsna**: “Recently.”  
**Acyutananda**: “Who represented the Gaudiya—?”  
**Prabhupada**: “Nobody, but from the history it was found that it belonged to Gaudiya Vaisnava. That... In Delhi one lawyer is there. He knows. He told me.”

The next category of evidence is a comparison of the character of the two saints — Vallabhacarya and Madhavendra Puri.

Let us first examine Vallabhacarya’s initial connection with the Gaudiya sampradaya and how he came to disassociate himself from it. From that history we’ll move on to Lord Caitanya’s comments, and Srila Prabhupada’s confirmation of the same, concerning Vallabha’s character.

Sri Caitanya-caritamrta describes two meetings between Lord Caitanya and Vallabha, one at Prayaga and one at Puri At the first meeting, Vallabha had doubts about Lord Caitanya’s divinity but was still drawn to become a follower of the Lord. Later, at the Ratha-yatra festival in Puri, Vallabha Bhatta, as he was called at the time, saw the beauty of Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu and witnessed the ecstatic symptoms of love for Krsna the Lord displayed. Vallabha then concluded, “Here is Lord Krsna, without a doubt.”

After taking initiation from Gadadhara Pandita in Puri, Vallabha had a very significant meeting with Caitanya Mahaprabhu.

At this meeting Vallabha presented his Bhagavatam commentary to Lord Caitanya, claiming that it surpassed the commentary of Sridhara Svami. Lord Caitanya was so displeased by Vallabha’s arrogance that He publicly corrected him, going so far as to humiliate him by calling him a prostitute in jest. Although after that incident Lord Caitanya continued to accept lunch invitations from him, Vallabha could not bring himself to surrender his ego. Eventually, he left the association of Lord Caitanya and His followers and inaugurated his own spiritual movement, the present day Pusti-marga sampradaya.

Srila Prabhupada summarises Vallabha’s connection with, and ultimate disassociation from, the Gaudiya sampradaya:

**“Vallabha Bhatta was a great learned scholar of Vaisnavism. In the beginning he was very much devoted to Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu, but since he thought that he could not receive proper respect from Him, he later joined the Visnu Svami sect and became the acarya of that sect. His sect is celebrated as the Vallabhacarya sampradaya. This sampradaya has had great influence in Vrndavana near Gokula and in Bombay.”**

Srila Prabhupada and his predecessors, including Rupa Gosvami and Lord Caitanya, respected Vallabhacarya as a great devotional scholar, and Caitanya Mahaprabhu and Vallabha enjoyed a very friendly relationship. And although Vallabhacarya’s philosophy differs from that of Lord Caitanya, Srila Prabhupada would always publicly respect Vallabha.

Nonetheless, there were other aspects of Vallabha’s character that Srila Prabhupada critiqued. First was Vallabha’s pride in his own scholarship that led him to criticise Sridhara Svami and thus deviate from the parampara system.

Second was Vallabha’s subsequent rejection of CaitanyaMahaprabhu, the result of which hindered the spiritual realisation that Vallabha and his followers were able to achieve. Those who reject Lord Caitanya cannot enter into the higher rasas, and thus their spiritual attainment is restricted to the mellows of neutrality and servitude. When Srila Prabhupada was asked about the Pustis’ worship in the parental mood, he answered that such a mellow was a “show, formality.”

The third flaw Srila Prabhupada noted in Vallabha was his lack of actual spiritual acumen. Prabhupada’s personal opinion was that Vallabha was learned but “had no realization.” And according to Prabhupada, while Caitanya Mahaprabhu accepted Vallabha as a learned scholar, “He did not accept him as very highly realized soul.”

This assessment of Vallabhacarya’s character — pride, rejection of Lord Caitanya, and limited spiritual advancement — calls into question whether Krsna would actually appear to such a person in His Deity form, what to speak of jump out of the Deity and personally embrace him. Caitanya Mahaprabhu, by Vallabha’s own admission, was Krsna Himself. And the Lord was displeased with Vallabha’s shortcomings. Why then would Krsna submit Himself to Vallabha in the way the Pustis describe in their account of the discovery of Gopala?

One may argue that because Gopala was given to Vallabha’s followers, Krsna must have been satisfied with them. It is difficult to disagree with this argument, but not without qualifying it first. In this connection, Bhakti-ratnakara narrates an interesting pastime:

When Lord Caitanya came to Govardhana Hill, Gopala went to Ganthuli to give the Lord darsana. At that time two brahmana disciples of Madhavendra Puri, both absorbed in love of God, were taking care of the Deity. With the death of Vallabha, the two brahmanas disappeared and, in consultation with Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami, gave Gopala to Viththalesvara, who, according to the story, was very devoted to Lord Caitanya.

Thus it should be noted that Gopala was given to the Pustis: (1) after Vallabha’s departure from the world, (2) through Viththalesvara who seemed to have greater faith in Lord Caitanya than did his father, and (3) by a devotee — Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami — who is more merciful than the Lord. In other words, Gopala did not come directly Himself.

And what about Madhavendra Puri’s character?

There is no mention of him displaying anything but ideal Vaisnava behaviour, and no evidence that he was on any spiritual platform other than that of the highest perfection.

The Deity of Gopinatha at Remuna is known as Ksira-cora-gopinatha because of Madhavendra Puri. In that pastime, the Lord favoured His great devotee by hiding sweet rice from His pujari and then ordering that this ksira be delivered to Madhavendra. This pastime not only confirms Madhavendra Puri’s intimate connection with Krsna, but also authenticates his devotion to Gopala, to whom he wanted to offer such a tasty preparation. Madhavendra Puri then revealed his true humility by leaving the village immediately upon receiving the sweet rice, fearing that people would worship him as a great devotee. And although the saint was very old — one hundred and thirty years old by some accounts — when Gopala asked him to undertake the arduous trip to Jagannatha Puri to bring sandalwood, Madhavendra Puri displayed full submission to the Lord by setting out on a journey from which he would never return.

Both Advaita Acarya and Lord Nityananda accepted Madhavendra Puri as their spiritual master — in itself strong validation of the saint’s flawless character and high spiritual credentials. And Caitanya Mahaprabhu personally glorified Madhavendra Puri as “the root of the tree of devotional service” brought to earth by the Lord.

Both the Caitanya-caritamrta and the Caitanya-bhagavata describe that Madhavendra Puri displayed symptoms of divine ecstasy characteristic of only the most advanced transcendentalists. Moreover, Lord Caitanya repeatedly chanted a verse composed by Madhavendra Puri, who lived in the mood of Radha’s pain of separation from Krsna. This confirms that Madhavendra Puri shared the devotional mood of Lord Caitanya Himself.

Madhavendra Puri’s spiritual credentials are further confirmed in the authoritative book Gaura-ganoddesa-dipika, whereinKavi-karnapura reveals Madhavendra Puri's eternal identity to be that of a desire tree in Goloka Vrndavana:

**“Laksmipati’s disciple was Madhavendra Puri, a great preacher of devotional service. Madhavendra Puri was the incarnation of a kalpa-vrksa tree in the abode of Vraja. This tree bears as its fruits the mellows of servitude to Lord Krsna, friendship with Lord Krsna, parental love for Lord Krsna, and amorous love for Lord Krsna.”**

Thus the historical record of Vaisnava literature reveals a series of character flaws in Vallabhacarya that one would not expect from a highly realised soul, whereas there are no faults to be found in Madhavendra Puri; indeed, his behaviour is reported to be consistently beyond reproach.

The next item of evidence is the prophecy of scripture.

There is a very significant prophecy in the Garga-samhita regarding the appearance of Gopala-Nathaji. While describing Srngara-sthali , Garga Muni says,

**“O King! 4,108 years after the beginning of Kali-yuga, as everyone watches, Lord Krsna will emerge from a cave on Govardhana Hill and reveal His transcendental form at Srngara-mandala. O King! The saintly devotees will call this form of the Lord Srinatha. He will always enjoy pastimes on Govardhana Hill.”**

Although the dating of this prediction is not close enough to verify either version under discussion, one part of the prophecy can help resolve the controversy at hand — where and how the Deity appeared.

The Garga-samhita says that the Deity will emerge on Govardhana Hill. The description of this ancient text fits with the more recent description in Sri Caitanya-caritamrta in that both books describe the Deity as being found whole at one time, rather than manifesting out of the mountain limb by limb over a period of half a century.

The final piece of evidence, which is no less compelling than the others, is the recorded statement of Gopala Himself. If Gopala tells us who discovered Him, what need is there for further discussion? And we are fortunate to have the following testimony of Gopala recorded in Sri Caitanya-caritamrta.

After drinking milk brought to him by an especially beautiful cowherd boy, Madhavendra Puri fell asleep and dreamt that the very same boy took him by the hand to a bush in the jungle and said:

“I reside in this bush, and because of this I suffer very much from severe cold, rain showers, winds and scorching heat. Please bring the people of the village and get them to take Me out of this bush. Then have them situate Me nicely on top of the hill. Please construct a temple on top of that hill and instal Me in that temple. After this, wash Me with large quantities of cold water so that My body may be cleansed.”

The beautiful cowherd boy continued:

“For many days I have been observing you, and I have been wondering, ‘When will Madhavendra Puri come here to serve Me?’ I have accepted your service due to your ecstatic love for Me. Thus I shall appear, and by My audience all fallen souls will be delivered. My name is Gopala. I am the lifter of Govardhana Hill. I was installed by Vajra, and here I am the authority. When the Muslims attacked, the priest who was serving Me hid Me in this bush in the jungle. Then he ran away out of fear of the attack. Since the priest went away, I have been staying in this bush. It is very good that you have come here. Now just remove Me with care.”

After saying this, the boy disappeared. When Madhavendra Puri woke up, he followed the instructions he was given and installed Gopala on Govardhana Hill.

In Gopala’s testimony some interesting points come to light. He confirms that Vajra first installed Him and that later His pujari hid Him in a bush. Thus He didn’t self-manifest from a stone of Govardhana Hill, as claimed by the Pustis. And furthermore, Gopala reveals that He anticipated Madhavendra Puri’s arrival; in fact, He was waiting for His devotee to rescue Him from the uncomfortable circumstances in which Had been placed. Gopala makes no mention of Vallabhacarya.

Having presented the evidence, let us now evaluate it all together. Local tradition, logical arguments, the statements of acaryas, the character reference of the two saints, scriptural prediction, and the recorded statement of Gopala Himself are unanimous in theirtestimony that Madhavendra Puri rediscovered Gopala. Such consistent evidence is strong proof that the historical version of the Pusti-marga sampradaya — that Vallabhacarya discovered Gopala — cannot be correct.

Some will argue that much of the evidence cited above is based on the authenticity of the Caitanya-caritamrta, an authenticity which some critics may not accept. When offered this objection, Srila Prabhupada dismissed it as insubstantial given the overwhelming acceptance of the Caitanya-caritamrta authenticity by both the Vaisnava and the academic communities. Prabhupada said, “They don’t accept, we don’t accept you. The whole world is accepting Caitanya-caritamrta...”

Narottama Dasa Thakura, for example, writes:

**“Amongst the devotees, Krsnadasa Kaviraja is extremely expert at relishing the transcendental nectar of the mellows of love of God. When the stones hear his Caitanya-caritamrta and Govinda'lildmrta, they begin to melt in ecstasy.”**

It should also he noted that the objective evidence of logic, history, tradition, and even a relatively recent court case all support the version of the Caitanya-caritamrta. Only the opinion of the Pusti sampradaya, without objective supporting evidence, gives a contradictory view of Gopala’s discovery, and dares to challenge the authority of Sri Caitanya-caritamrta.

And Madhavendra Puri? Madhavendra Puri is singular in his service to Krsna. In the words of Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura,

**“No one shall ever be the equal of Madhavendra Puri, so why do you wish to compete with him for fame?”**

## Appendix 21

**Spiritual Affinity and How It Applies to the Places of Krsna’s Pastimes**

*(This appendix follows from the description of Gauri-kunda and the pastimes the divine couple enjoys there, as those descriptions appear in chapter twelve.)*

This appendix will attempt to explain how devotees develop transcendental attachment for, and in some cases aversion to, specific places of Krsna’s pastimes according to the maturity of their Krsna consciousness. We will see how this attachment arises in due proportion to the awakening of one’s spontaneous attraction to the Lord. The need for such an analysis became apparent while describing Radha’s and Krsna’s pastime at Gauri-kunda.

Gauri-kunda is associated with Candravali; in fact it is a place she frequents much in the same way Sri Radha frequents Radha-kunda. By definition, Gaudiya Vaisnavas are followers of Radha and Her associates. They therefore favour Radha’s pastime places and avoid those of Her eternal rival, Candravali. However, because the pastime at Gauri-kunda, as narrated in Vidagdha-madhava, has Radharani as its heroine, devotees will visit Gauri-kunda and hear that pastimewith devotion to Radha and respect for Candravali. Parikrama in this mood will surely nurture their bhava.

But devotees of Sri Radha will not go to Gauri-kunda to hear the glories of Candravali-devi or to nourish a mood of devotion to her. Candravali is Radha’s competitor, and Gauri-kunda is the place to which Candravali and her girlfriends try to lure Krsna, much to Sri Radha’s dismay. Gauri-kunda is a place of sorrow for Radharani, and thus devotees of Sri Radha harbour a transcendental aversion to it.

At the same time, they know that Gauri-tirtha is as worshipable as all other places in Vraja. Aversion to it should not be imitated but allowed to spontaneously develop in proportion to the development of spontaneous attachment to Krsna. Once devotees have reached a stage of perfection similar to that of Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami, then if the influence of pure love so dictates, they may become aggressively averse to Gauri-tirtha or Sakhi-sthali in the manner of our revered acarya. But not before then!

The attachment for one place of Krsna’s pastimes over another, which we will call “spiritual affinity,” emerges as one applies the first of the six principles of surrender, “accepting things favourable for one’s Krsna consciousness” (anukulyasya sankalpah). As this discussion progresses we will see that this first principle of surrender goes hand in hand with the second, “rejecting things unfavourable for one’s Krsna consciousness” (pratikulyasya varjanam).

Our analysis of spiritual affinity in Krsna consciousness is based on these two principles of surrender because they are principles that guide devotees from the very beginning of their lives in Krsna consciousness, until the point at which they have achieved perfection, and even beyond.

Before we begin, let us quickly examine the significance of the phrase “spiritual affinity.” Simply put, an affinity is the natural attraction of one thing to another. For our intents and purposes, it is worth noting that in some contexts the word “affinity” already has intended spiritual connotations. Lastly, we would like to carefully note that affinity should not be confused with partiality; we are not referring to an unfair bias, but rather to a special fondness for one thing over and above others. Spiritual affinity is a preference or predilection that is fair because it arises from an individual soul’s constitutional nature, and is therefore inextricably linked to its eternal spiritual identity.

And yet there may still be some danger that readers will misinterpret our use of this phrase as indicative of a callow and one-sided perspective of Krsna consciousness. An example of such a perspective would be to argue that since Lord Caitanya has advised us to make Krsna in Vrndavana the goal of our lives, we should therefore not worship any other form or incarnation of Krsna. The natural spiritual predilection denoted by the phrase “spiritual affinity” is worlds apart from this sort of misguided sectarianism.

Misunderstanding and abuse of teachings will, unfortunately, always go on. But withholding knowledge does not adequately prevent its misuse. It is better to properly educate devotees by instilling a clear understanding of spiritual principles and their proper application. One consequence of improper application, counterproductive to the healthy development of Krsna consciousness, is extremism— going beyond the intended scope of a spiritual principle. This sort of behaviour is described in the following well-known verse,

**“Devotional service of the Lord that ignores the authorised Vedic literatures like the Upanisads, Puranas, and Narada-pancaratra is simply an unnecessary disturbance in society.”**

Srila Prabhupada taught devotees how to avoid bias resulting from this sort of narrow-minded philosophical misinterpretation in this description of the relationship between transcendental mellows:

**“No one is higher or lower than anyone else in transcendental relationships with the Lord, for in the absolute realm everything is equal. But although these relationships are absolute, there are also transcendental differences between them. Thus the transcendental relationship of conjugal love is considered the highest perfection.”**

Here Srila Prabhupada balances three things: (1) the equality amongst all moods of loving devotion in the absolute realm, (2) the constitutional affinity liberated souls show for their own mood of devotion, and (3) the hierarchical relationship of these moods, one to the other.

If we want to follow in the footsteps of Srila Prabhupada, we must train devotees in affinity, free from feelings of superiority or bias, and in intelligent discrimination free from prejudice. In a spiritual culture that has “inconceivably simultaneous oneness and difference” as its philosophical foundation, and “unity in diversity” as its social foundation, individual devotees can advance spiritually and co-exist with one another only if they learn the kind of spiritual balance His Divine Grace perfectly exemplified.

With this in mind we shall continue by describing the transcendental variety found in the absolute realm, the affinity that transcendentalists show for specific elements within that greater variety, and how practising devotees can apply feelings of spiritual affinity in their pursuit of perfection. Our description of this practical application will conclude by recommending how devotees can meditate on, visit, and aspire to attain feelings of affinity for particular pastime places in the holy dhama.

**\* \* \* \***

Perfected devotees are naturally inclined towards a specific mood of devotional service in harmony with their constitutional identity. Krsna therefore shows Himself to His devotees in the specific eternal, transcendental form in which they always think of Him. Thus devotees are connected to Krsna through an attachment that arises from their natural inclination and which defines their unique, individual transcendental service. Srila Prabhupada tells us that

**“Special natural appreciation of the descriptions of a particular pastime of Godhead indicates the constitutional position of a living entity.”**

And Krsnadasa Kaviraja Gosvami adds:

**“Each kind of devotee feels that his sentiment is the most excellent, and thus in that mood he tastes great happiness with Lord Krsna.”**

The most obvious example of spiritual affinity can be seen in the Vraja-vasis’ single-minded devotion to Krsna and their respectful disinterest in Krsna’s other expansions. The gopis clearly illustrate this by their behaviour in the following pastime.

One spring night Krsna hid from the gopis in a secluded forest grove. When He heard the cowherd girls approaching, Krsna playfully disguised Himself in the form of four-armed Lord Narayana. The gopis expected to see Krsna, but found majestic Lord Narayana instead, all but dispelling their ecstatic loving feelings. After offering their respects to the Lord of Vaikuntha, the gopis asked Narayana for His merciful benediction in the form of Krsna’s company.

That the gopis were hardly even pleased at rhe sight of Lord Narayana is testament to the resolute nature of their ecstatic mood. They are, in Rupa Gosvami’s words, “firmly fixed upon the original form of Krsna.” This state of being “firmly fixed” in a particular mood of devotion is what we have called spiritual affinity.

In addition to or as a consequence of their affinity for Krsna, devotees who share the same devotional mood are naturally attracted to one another as well. Rupa Gosvami identifies this as a fundamental principle of devotee association when he says, *sajatiyasaye snigdhe sadhau sangah svato var*e: “One should associate with devotees more advanced than oneself and with those who are endowed with a similar type of affection for the Lord.”

Affection (rati) for Krsna is of five types: neutral, servile, fraternal, parental, or amorous. The subsequent mellows (rasas) tasted in devotional service are of the same five types. The Sanskrit names for these categories are santa, dasya, sakhya, vatsalya, and madhurya respectively.

Love felt under the influence of these mellows is enriched by a particular quality, uniquely characteristic of each mellow. At the stage of neutrality, for instance, love manifests the quality of full attachment to Krsna. At the next successive stage, servitude, the previous quality of full attachment is augmented by the desire to do something practical for the pleasure of Krsna; this is the attitude of service. At the stage of transcendental friendship with the Lord, the previous two qualities are further augmented by feelings of closeness, or confidentiality. The parental mood, along with the previous two qualities, is accompanied by a feeling of responsibility, or the desire to look after Krsna’s maintenance; and the highest platform of devotion, amorous love, has all the above qualities plus the highest degree of intimacy, marked by the desire to offer one’s body in the service of the Lord. Thus, the love the gopis feel for Krsna is replete with all five of these feelings: attachment, service, confidentiality, maintenance, and intimacy. For this reason, although devotees in any of the five mellows are perfectly situated and feel that their unique service to Krsna is more desirable than any other, when judged according to the full variety of qualities the gopis' love displays, their love is found to be superlative.

One may encounter these relative gradations of loving service and by way of material calculation conclude that since the love of the gopis is the highest and most complete, the spiritual world, which is after all the most perfect place, should have only gopis in it.

But that is not the case. Variety is an essential aspect of perfection, so the perfection found in the spiritual world demands that all five varieties of love be present simultaneously. In that way the individual quality of each mellow offers its own unique flavour of love to Krsna. Under Krsna’s direction His internal energy, Yogamaya, acts in such a way that the Lord’s devotees feel not only fully satisfied by their exchange of love with Him, hut convinced that their particular exchange of love with Krsna is the best of all, regardless of their individual mellow. There are no feelings of inferiority or lack in Krsna’s transcendental realm.

This helps to clarify why the Lord’s associates are drawn together under the auspices of a particular service: The satisfaction they feel from their relationship with Krsna arises directly from their mood of service to the Lord, and so they are attracted to one another by commonality in their love for the Lord. This type of spiritual affinity inspires devotees to group themselves into as many groups and sub-groups as there are unique qualities common to their service. These divisions can be general, specific, more specific, and so on, without limit.

Amongst gopis, for example, there are three groups: those partial to Radha, those partial to Krsna, and those equal to both Radha and Krsna. Amongst those partial to Radha, friends (sakhis) serve Krsna directly, while maidservants (manjaris) serve Krsna indirectly by serving

Radha and Her friends, the sakhis. And amongst the manjaris, one group is especially deputed to act as messengers (dutis) between the divine couple. The gopis are grouped in this way as much by similar qualities as by personal preference. This grouping also reflects whom Krsna’s associates prefer to associate with, or in other words, their affinity for one another.

Bhaktivinoda Thakura expresses his personal preference in service to Radha and Krsna:

**“I yearn to serve the divine couple in the way that Sri Rupa-manjari and the other manjaris do, to he counted among Lalita’s fob lowers, the queen of my group being Radha and Her master, Nanda’s son.”**

And Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami expresses a similar affinity:

*sphural-lakṣmī-lakṣmī-vraja-vijayi-lakṣmī-bhara-lasad- vapuḥ-śrī-gāndharvā-smara-nikara-divyad-giribhṛtoḥ*  
*vidhāsye kuñjādau vividha-varivasyāḥ sa-rabhasaṁ rahaḥ śrī-rūpākhya-priyatama-janasyaiva caramaḥ*

**“In a secluded place in Vṛndāvana forest, following my dear friend Śrīla Rūpa Gosvāmī, in many ways I shall earnestly worship Śrī Kṛṣṇa, who is more splendid than many cupids, and Śrīmatī Rādhārāṇī, whose glorious beauty defeats many goddesses of fortune.”**

Raghunātha Dāsa revealed the strength of his affinity not only by his strong desire to serve in Rādhā’s group of gopīs, but by his strong aversion to serving in any other group. This aversion is exhibited in the following well- documented anecdote, which was referred to briefly in chapter 14 of this volume:

In regards to his own bodily maintenance, Raghunātha Dāsa Gosvāmī was quite austere. He would daily consume as much buttermilk as could be contained in one leaf of a palāśa tree, and no more. A well-wisher once tried to increase Dāsa Gosvāmī’s daily intake by bringing buttermilk in one of the larger palāśa leaves found in the vicinity of Sakhī-sthalī village, well-known to be the residence of Candrāvalī-devī, Śrī Rādhā’s arch rival. When Raghunātha Dāsa came to know where the larger leaf-cup had come from, he threw it away along with its contents, admonishing the devotee who had brought it for going near Candrāvalī’s residence and for trying to make Raghunātha a beneficiary of Candrāvalī’s mercy.

This story illustrates the two guiding principles of spiritual affinity mentioned in the beginning of this appendix. By accepting those things favourable, Raghunātha Dāsa Gosvāmī showed his attachment to the associates of Śrī Rādhā, and by rejecting unfavourable things, his aversion to those in competition with his object of worship.

Devotees, please take note! Such aversion is an expression of spiritual ecstasy and cannot be imitated. Dāsa Gosvāmī’s spiritual aversion is not a license for conditioned souls to disrespect associates of the Lord, who are themselves appreciative of one another’s love and service to Kṛṣṇa. However, knowledge of the attachment and aversion exhibited by liberated souls like Raghunātha Dāsa Gosvāmī is a useful compass in the devotional journey of the practising devotee.

Having briefly summarised why and how Kṛṣṇa’s eternal associates show an affinity for and congregate with those devotees whose mood of service is similar to their own, we will now see how the concept of spiritual affinity applies to practising devotees who are still conditioned souls. Careful, balanced implementation of these principles can help give sādhakas a clear picture of Kṛṣṇa consciousness, directing them towards the goal of their practice.

**\* \* \* \***

Some persons may readily admit that for Kṛṣṇa’s eternal associates in Goloka Vṛndāvana, or for the self-realised souls in this world, spiritual affinity is a natural part of transcendental conduct. But these same people may also challenge whether it is natural for practising devotees, while still in the conditioned state, to show any degree of affinity for particular places or personalities in Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes.

Guru (Śrīla Prabhupāda), sādhu (the Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇava ācāryas), and śāstra (the scriptures) are unanimously clear on this point: cultivating feelings of spiritual affinity under the guidance of superior Vaiṣṇavas for the purpose of proper education is a part of utilising intelligent discrimination and is integral to the development of Kṛṣṇa consciousness. As devotees’ hearts become purified, they will follow an increasingly spontaneous affinity for a devotee of their choice, and the desire to accept that devotee as a model for the type of perfection for which they aspire.

Let’s begin by discussing a form of affinity we all adopt at an early stage of Kṛṣṇa consciousness, one upon which all readers of this book can hopefully agree: amongst forms of the Personality of Godhead, Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇavas are partial to Kṛṣṇa in Vṛndāvana.

In his teachings to his followers, Śrīla Prabhupāda made it perfectly clear that while Kṛṣṇa expands Himself into innumerable forms — all of which are no different from nor any less worshipable than He Himself — in their personal bhajana Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇavas should focus their attention on Kṛṣṇa in Vṛndāvana. In short, Prabhupāda taught that “for a devotee no form of Kṛṣṇa is important but the original form, two-handed Śyāmasundara.”

More than just emphasising the importance of the form of Śyāmasundara, Śrīla Prabhupāda instructed that a devotee should “fix his mind on this original form of Godhead, Kṛṣṇa.” And he added to that positive injunction a negative prohibition to “not even divert his attention to other forms of the Lord.”

Raghunātha Dāsa Gosvāmī also emphasises the need for devotees to be partial towards Kṛṣṇa, while de-emphasising attachment to other forms of the Lord:

*asad-vārtā-veṣyā visṛja mati-sarvasva-haraṇīḥ*  
*kathā mukti-vyāghryā na śṛṇu kila sarvātma-gilaṇīḥ*  
*api tyaktvā lakṣmī-pati-ratim ito vyoma-nayanīṁ*  
*vraje rādhā-kṛṣṇau sva-rati-maṇi-dau tvaṁ bhaja manaḥ*

**“Give up attraction for Lord Nārāyaṇa, which leads to the world of Vaikuṇṭha. O mind, in Vraja, worship Śrī Śrī Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa, two philanthropists who give away the jewel of pure love.”**

Kṛṣṇa and His expansions are ontologically identical, but because Kṛṣṇa possesses qualities His expansions do not, He exerts a special attraction that invokes the affection of devotees. Rūpa Gosvāmī explains this principle:

**“According to transcendental realization, there is no difference between the forms of Nārāyaṇa and Kṛṣṇa. Yet in Kṛṣṇa there is a special transcendental attraction due to the conjugal mellow, and consequently He surpasses Nārāyaṇa. This is the conclusion of transcendental mellows.”**

Ṭhākura Bhaktivinoda says that persons who give up contaminated philosophies and in pure Kṛṣṇa consciousness exercise objective judgement will conclude that the form of Kṛṣṇa is superior.

For those who have already reached that conclusion, Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam recommends, “One must consider the form of Kṛṣṇa very seriously.” A recommendation that Caitanya Mahāprabhu raised to the level of practical application by repeating the song of Raghupati Upādhyāya, which says, “Kṛṣṇa’s fresh youth should always be meditated upon.”

From this evidence it is clear that from the outset of their spiritual lives practising devotees are meant to understand the special qualities of Kṛṣṇa, cultivate appreciation for Him, and make Him the focus of their devotional aspirations. As Lord Caitanya instructs Sanātana Gosvāmī, “The sweet, attractive transcendental form of Kṛṣṇa is so nice. Just try to understand it.”

Even from the point of spiritual infancy, neophyte sādhakas follow the affinity born of ecstasy that the gopīs showed towards Kṛṣṇa when they left Lord Nārāyaṇa behind in search of their cowherd boyfriend.

In practical application this means that while engaged in the cultivation of pure devotional service, aspiring devotees (sādhakas) will direct their senses, mind, and intelligence towards Kṛṣṇa in Vṛndāvana. Thus sādhakas will naturally befriend Vaiṣṇavas who increase their devotion to the son of Nanda Mahārāja. While chanting Hare Kṛṣṇa, sādhakas will meditate on the meaning of the mahā-mantra as it relates to Rādhā and Kṛṣṇa. As they hear Śrīmad- Bhāgavatam, sādhakas will apply what they have learned in the context of increasing their surrender and attachment to the Kṛṣṇa of Vraja. When the opportunity comes to visit holy places, sādhakas will select those places dear to Kṛṣṇa, like Vṛndāvana, Māyāpur, or Jagannātha Purī. And while sādhakas worship the Deities of the Lord, they will make Kṛṣṇa the Lord of their hearts, their iṣṭa-deva, and if they worship forms other than Kṛṣṇa, they will do so only to increase their devotion to Him.

This focused practise of regulated devotional service to Kṛṣṇa in Vṛndāvana awakens attachment to Him, eventually strengthening devotees’ attachment to the eternal form of Śyāmasundara until it reaches the same platform as those companions of the Lord who are eternally attached to Him. In that way practising devotees attain mature attachment, called svarūpa-siddhi.

Scriptures like Padma Purāṇa further elaborate on the means by which practitioners can attain perfection. Such scriptures explain that the principles of worship pertaining to the Lord Himself also apply to those things directly related to Lord (tadīyānām). In the context of this appendix, tadīyānām means that sādhakas display the same affection towards, and affinity for, the service of Kṛṣṇa’s devotees, pastime places, and paraphernalia, as they do for Kṛṣṇa Himself.

Śrīla Prabhupāda almost always translates the word tadīyānām as “Vaiṣṇava,” emphasising that the best way to worship Kṛṣṇa is through His devotees. Beginners in Kṛṣṇa consciousness are well aware of this principle and know that just as Kṛṣṇa is pleased when His devotee is pleased (*yasya prasādād bhagavat-prasādo*), He is displeased when His devotee is displeased (*yasyāprasādān na gatiḥ kuto ’pi*).

Although beginners in spiritual life may not be able to judge the advancement of other Vaiṣṇavas, they must be trained to do so. Loving exchange between devotees can only take place when devotees have the proper discrimination with which to select company fitting for their spiritual advancement. Śrīla Rūpa Gosvāmī teaches us how to associate with Vaiṣṇavas according to their individual status:

*kṛṣṇeti yasya giri taṁ manasādriyeta*  
*dīkṣāsti cet praṇatibhiś ca bhajantam īśam*  
*śuśrūṣayā bhajana-vijñam ananyam anya-*  
*nindādi-śūnya-hṛdam īpsita-saṅga-labdhyā*

**“One should mentally honor the devotee who chants the holy name of Lord Kṛṣṇa, one should offer humble obeisances to the devotee who has undergone spiritual initiation \[dīkṣā\] and is engaged in worshipping the Deity, and one should associate with and faithfully serve that pure devotee who is advanced in undeviated devotional service and whose heart is completely devoid of the propensity to criticize others.”**

In commenting on this verse Śrīla Prabhupāda emphasises three principles invaluable to one’s spiritual development: (1) he teaches devotees, by use of their intelligence, how to distinguish between the kaniṣṭha-adhikārī, madhyama- adhikārī, and uttama-adhikārī; (2) he advises them to accept their own position and not imitate those situated on a higher platform; (3) he admonishes devotees to carefully accept an uttama-adhikārī Vaiṣṇava as spiritual master.

Uttama-adhikārī Vaiṣṇavas serve as sources of inspiration for the devotees who are their dependents. Devotees traditionally select one of these elevated Vaiṣṇavas as their “initiating spiritual master,” but it is possible they may also select other similarly advanced Vaiṣṇavas as instructors or guides. These two categories of spiritual mentors are commonly known as dīkṣā- and śikṣā-gurus, respectively. Because of the inspiration they receive from their gurus, practising devotees develop a natural affinity for them above others, even as they maintain their respect for all Vaiṣṇavas.

Practising devotees are inspired by the teachings and exemplary character of their gurus. The scriptures and the previous ācāryas strictly define the teachings and example bona fide gurus are meant to transmit to their disciples, but by Kṛṣṇa’s grace the interpersonal exchange between disciple and guru transmits a special potency along with the guru’s instructions and example. Through the spiritual master, Kṛṣṇa manifests His attractiveness, drawing conditioned souls closer to Him by increasing their attachment to His devotees. We are all unique individual souls, so it is only natural that a devotee may consider a certain Vaiṣṇava to be the most inspiring person in his life, while another devotee is not nearly as inspired by the same great soul. This is an example, and the basic principle, of spiritual affinity.

Aspiring Vaiṣṇavas receive from their spiritual masters transcendental knowledge that comprises three overarching subjects: relationship (sambandha), functional duties (abhidheya), and the goal of life (prayojana). By assimilating this knowledge and then acting upon it, devotees’ hearts gradually become purified of unwanted habits and unnecessary material attachments. As they externally execute regulated devotional service with their bodies and words, within their minds they develop spontaneous interest in the topics that relate to Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes in Vṛndāvana.

In time, these devotees will become attracted to one of the primary relationships in which the Vraja-vāsīs display their love for Kṛṣṇa. Naturally, they will also wonder what steps they can take in their devotional lives in order to eventually serve Kṛṣṇa in that particular devotional mellow within the spiritual world. This attraction and its accompanying motivation mark the dawn of rāgānuga-bhakti.

We have described the progress of the guru-disciple relationship to point out that vaidhī-sādhana-bhakti obliges devotees not only to accept gurus who are advanced devotees, but who are also sajātīya, which in Śrīla Prabhupāda’s words means “people thinking in the same way.” Devotees are thus encouraged to yield to feelings of affinity regarding the pure Vaiṣṇavas they aspire to follow and serve.

As will be seen in the coming pages, this voluntary commitment to a guru is similar to — and is also a precursor to — the process by which a more advanced devotee follows in the footsteps of a particular Vraja-vāsī in the aprākṛta- dhāma.

**\* \* \* \***

Thus far we have discussed how devotees show affinity: (1) for Kṛṣṇa as their worshipable Deity, (2) for pure devotional service as the means to please Him, (3) for Goloka Vṛndāvana as their ultimate destination, (4) for like-minded devotees as their companions, and (5) for their spiritual masters as guides on the path of bhakti. At the end of the last section we introduced another kind of affinity, one that dawns gradually on the spiritual horizon of a more advanced devotee: (6) affinity for serving Kṛṣṇa in the mood of a particular Vraja-vāsī.

Rūpa Gosvāmī describes in Bhakti-rasāmṛta-sindhu that by practising Kṛṣṇa consciousness devotees gradually become purified at heart, eventually reaching the stage where they are freed from bad habits (anartha-nivṛtti). At this stage the effect of hearing scriptures like Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam may awaken attraction to the sweet loving exchanges that go on between the Vraja-vāsīs and Kṛṣṇa. That attraction excites the devotee’s desire to continue hearing about Kṛṣṇa’s Vṛndāvana pastimes and subsequently awakens a longing to personally possess a mood of service similar to one of Kṛṣṇa’s associates. This mood is called vraja-bhāva.

The longing to attain the mood of a Vraja-vāsī arises spontaneously from the process of regulated devotional service and is not a product of logic or the assimilation of scriptural injunctions. It reflects an affinity of the purified soul for a specific flavour of loving devotion. In its maturity this longing is called greed (lobha). This greed makes its appearance at the liberated stage of ecstatic devotion (bhāva).

The effect of hearing with greed is that devotees become attracted to one of the five mellows of vraja-bhakti, first with their intelligence and then with their hearts. Śrīla Prabhupāda calls this heartfelt attraction “covetousness.”

The symptom of covetousness is that a devotee is eager to reside and serve in Vṛndāvana, in mind if not in body. Covetousness also involves absorption in one’s preferred mellow of devotion and in hearing of the Vraja- vāsīs who share that mellow.

In time, general affinity for a category of associates will unveil an additional, specific affinity for a particular associate one desires to emulate. This preference, to serve and love Kṛṣṇa in the manner of a role model, inspires devotees to think of themselves as Vraja-vāsīs.

Such a conception is general and is characterised only by the desire to be a Vraja-vāsī — it does not include the attributes of a spiritual form such as age, dress, and service. These details are inextricable parts of the soul’s eternal identity (siddha-svarūpa) and are gleaned only by revelation, not conceptualisation. At the same time, Śrīla Prabhupāda says that devotees still in material bondage can serve Kṛṣṇa in a mood similar to that of His associates, and they can place themselves under the direct guidance of a particular associate, following in his or her footsteps. The difference between doing these things in the conditioned stage and doing them in the liberated stage is the difference between conceptualising an aspiration and actually realising it. The former is characteristic of an authorised ascending process of devotion (āroha-panthā), and the latter of the descending process (avaroha-panthā).

Let us look at an example of how the process might work — from conceptualising an aspiration while still in the conditioned state to actually realising the aspiration in the liberated state. Take, for example, a devotee attracted to the devotion of the cowherd boys, their confidential friendship with Kṛṣṇa, their sports and games, and their tending cows in the forests of Vraja. Such an aspirant’s attraction inspires him to read books like Preyo- bhakti-rasārṇava, and in doing so he develops a special affection for Śrīdāmā, Śrīmatī Rādhārāṇī’s younger brother.

To mock fight, the cowherd boys would divide into two groups, Kṛṣṇa in one and Śrīdāmā in the other. And when Kṛṣṇa would blow His horn to lead the charge, the hair on Śrīdāmā’s body would stand on end in chivalrous ecstasy. After their playful scuffle, the dust would settle and dark-complexioned Śrīdāmā, dressed in a yellow dhotī and red turban, sporting his own buffalo horn and a special garland, would emerge victorious over Kṛṣṇa. Berating his friend in a friendly joking mood, Śrīdāmā would thus ride triumphantly on Kṛṣṇa’s shoulders.

Still in his physical body, such a devotee serves his gurus and Lord Caitanya’s saṅkīrtana movement, while in his mind he follows Śrīdāmā throughout the day, assisting his mentor in affectionate dealings with Kṛṣṇa. In this way the advanced sādhaka realises the meaning of the words vraja- lokānusārataḥ (“to follow the activities of a particular associate of Kṛṣṇa’s in Vṛndāvana”).

Dear devotees, please note! A discriminating soul does not abandon the practices of regulated devotional service, especially hearing and chanting. He keeps his internal meditations a completely confidential affair, neither disclosing them to others, nor physically enacting them within the material world. Even when fully self-realised, Śrīla Prabhupāda says, a truly advanced devotee remains externally like a neophyte, faithfully executing all the śāstric injunctions. This is the humility and devotional chastity of a pure Vaiṣṇava.

**\* \* \* \***

In this section we will explain (1) how both practising and perfected devotees show affinity for specific places of Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes because those places are favoured by their Vraja-vāsī mentors; (2) how devotees in the realised state show affinity for specific pastime places as a feature of their eternal spiritual identities; and (3) how the laws governing devotional mellows mandate that as places of bhajana, places of residence, and places of eternal service, devotees should select pastime places in line with their spiritual aspirations.

Naturally, devotees who desire to follow in the footsteps of a particular associate of Kṛṣṇa will accept the preferences of that Vraja-vāsī as their own. If one of Kṛṣṇa’s associates is partial to certain places in Vraja where he or she performs service to Kṛṣṇa, then devotees following that Vraja-vāsī will also show an affinity for those places. And if for transcendental reasons an associate of Kṛṣṇa is averse to a certain place in Vraja, then his or her followers will similarly be averse.

In keeping with Śrīla Prabhupāda’s instruction that devotees conduct themselves as neophytes as long as they are in this world, affinity is an internal matter; it is a state of consciousness kept within one’s internal world. For this reason devotees will respectfully visit every pastime place in the dhāma, hearing intently about the pastimes Kṛṣṇa enacted in those places. However, when it comes to selecting places for bhajana, for internal service, and for eternal residence, devotees will show affinity for those places that are sympathetic to their specific mood of vraja-bhakti.

Let us review some examples of this type of affinity, as demonstrated by both Kṛṣṇa and His devotees.

Rūpa Gosvāmī proclaims the transcendental superiority of Rādhā-kuṇḍa over other holy places, noting the special preference that followers of Śrīmatī Rādhārāṇī show for it. Indeed, he explicitly describes the spiritual hierarchy of transcendental abodes beginning with Vaikuṇṭha, and ascending in superiority through Mathurā, Vṛndāvana forest, Govardhana Hill, and culminating finally with Rādhā-kuṇḍa.

Śrī Rūpa’s reasoning for this hierarchy is that increasingly spiritual intimacy is displayed in the pastimes Kṛṣṇa performs in each of these places: namely His appearance, the rāsa dance, the lifting of Girirāja, and intimate amorous affairs, respectively. Rūpa suggests that those who are advanced in Kṛṣṇa consciousness and who recognise the glories of Rādhā-kuṇḍa will rather live there than anywhere else. This confirms that Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇavas have an affinity for Rādhā-kuṇḍa as the holiest of all pilgrimage sites.

Nonetheless, a question remains: Should all Gauḍīya Vaiṣṇavas reside at Rādhā-kuṇḍa, making it the place of their eternal service?

The answer to that question is no. Śrīla Prabhupāda’s comments qualify precisely what sort of advanced devotees will live at Rādhā-kuṇḍa. For instance, he states: “Advanced devotees prefer to reside at Rādhā-kuṇḍa because this place is the site of many memories of the eternal loving affairs between Kṛṣṇa and Rādhārāṇī (rati-vilāsa).” This implies that the devotees who reside at Rādhā-kuṇḍa are specifically attracted to the amorous mellow. Śrīla Prabhupāda also states: “Śrīla Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura writes in this connection that Śrī Rādhā-kuṇḍa is the most select place for those interested in advancing their devotional service in the wake of the lady friends (sakhīs) and confidential serving maids (mañjarīs) of Śrīmatī Rādhārāṇī.”

Śrīla Prabhupāda adds that these devotees possess transcendental intelligence, which indicates that they are able to discriminate amongst pastime places and are careful to select those places sympathetic to their own devotional moods. Very advanced devotees, like those in the mood of parental love, for example, may therefore visit Rādhā-kuṇḍa, but will not live there, because places like Gokula and Nandagrāma are more conducive to their engagement in vātsalya-rasa.

In his purports to these verses from Upadeśāmṛta, Śrīla Prabhupāda repeatedly cites Nārada Muni as an example of a great devotee in regulated devotion who attains Rādhā-kuṇḍa only after great difficulty. These references to Nārada bring to mind his pastimes at Nāradavana.

When Nārada desired to see Rādhā’s and Kṛṣṇa’s amorous pastimes, Vṛndā- devī did not instruct him to perform bhajana at Rādhā-kuṇḍa, or even at Kusuma-sarovara. Rather, she told him to go to a more spiritually neutral place. After much austerity and penance — and with great difficulty — Nārada received the body of a gopī and was granted an audience with the divine couple at Rādhā-kuṇḍa. But even after his ordeal, Nārada was still not given residence there.

To give another example, Kṛṣṇa’s dearest cowherd boyfriends are fully engaged in Kṛṣṇa’s service and yet cannot readily attain Rādhā-kuṇḍa. Eight gopas have their own groves at Śyāma-kuṇḍa — not exactly at Rādhā-kuṇḍa, but nearby — and still the boys can only access this area by the sponsorship of certain gopīs.

We can conclude from these verses and purports from Upadeśāmṛta that devotees in the amorous mellow show an affinity for Rādhā-kuṇḍa.

What follows is a section of examples in which great souls speak about their affinity for Vṛndāvana or for particular places of pastimes or services in the holy dhāma.

Prabodhānanda Sarasvatī reveals the intensity of his determination to live in Vṛndāvana forest, a level of intensity reminiscent of Raghunātha Dāsa Gosvāmī’s aversion to Sakhī-sthalī. Prabodhānanda writes:

*vṛndāraṇyaṁ tyajeti pravadati yadi ko ‘py asya jihvāṁ chinadmi*  
*srīmad-vṛndāvanān māṁ yadi nayati balāt ko ‘pi taṁ hanmy avasyam*  
*kāmaṁ veśyām upeyaṁ na khalu pariṇayāyānyato yāmi kāmaṁ*  
*cauryaṁ kuryāṁ dhanārtham na tu calati padām hanta vṛndāvanān me*

**“If someone tells me, ‘You should leave Vṛndāvana’, I will cut out his tongue. If someone tries to drag me from Vṛndāvana by force, I will kill him. If a beautiful girl begs me to leave Vṛndāvana and marry her, I will not go. If need be I will become a thief and steal the money of others, but I will not take a single step out of Vṛndāvana.”**

In a more moderate tone, Raghunātha Dāsa Gosvāmī expresses his strong desire to live and die at Rādhā-kuṇḍa:

“My food a little of Vraja’s milk, my home a leaf-hut, and my actions honest austerity and scriptural study, I will live at Rādhā-kuṇḍa by the best of mountains, and I will die at that dear lake in the company of Śrīla Jīva Gosvāmī and the other devotees.”

Narottama Dāsa Ṭhākura reveals his affinity for taking birth in Varṣāṇā as a gopī: “When will I take birth as a gopī in the town of Mahārāja Vṛṣabhānu, where many gopas and gopīs live? O Lord Hari! Lord Hari! Will I someday attain this perfection?”

Actually, spiritual affinity for the places of the Lord’s pastimes is not unique to devotees. Even Kṛṣṇa prefers certain pastime places to others, as shown by this well-known verse of Padma Purāṇa:

*yathā rādhā priyā viṣṇos tasyāḥ kuṇḍaṁ priyaṁ tathā*  
*sarva-gopīṣu saivaikā viṣṇor atyanta-vallabhā*

**“Just as Śrīmatī Rādhārāṇī is dear to the Supreme Lord Kṛṣṇa \[Viṣṇu\], so Her bathing place \[Rādhā-kuṇḍa\] is equally dear to Kṛṣṇa. Amongst all the gopīs, She alone stands supreme as the Lord’s most beloved.”**

In summary: sādhakas, siddhas, and even Kṛṣṇa Himself all display spiritual affinity for the different pastime places in Vraja. And for Kṛṣṇa’s devotees, these feelings of affinity naturally arise from the mellow of devotion eternally unique to their individual spiritual identities.

In the case of liberated devotees, spiritual affinity for certain pastime places — sometimes called a vāsa or “residence” — is a part of their constitutional identity (siddha-deha). In the case of a devotee in the amorous mood, Bhaktivinoda Ṭhākura describes vāsa as follows:

**“In your next life you shall take birth as a gopī in a certain village within the land of Vraja. That place of birth is called vāsa. You will then marry a certain gopa and his place of residence will also be called vāsa. But when your heart is stolen by the sound of the flute you will follow a sakhī to her cottage by Rādhā-kuṇḍa and that grove will be your ultimate place of residence. In this way you will have three vāsas.”**

Why is affinity for a particular place in Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes a part of the soul’s constitutional identity?

Pastime places act as an impetus (uddīpana) for ecstatic love. But if the pastime associated with a certain place is not complementary to one’s own devotional mood, one’s ecstasy will be impeded, not excited. This is a cause of rasābhāsa. Because the soul seeks pleasure by nature, liberated devotees intrinsically favour those places that induce ecstasy and avoid places that don’t.

Constitutional affinity for one pastime place over another is only tangibly visible in liberated souls, but even conditioned souls, if they are to advance to the perfectional stage, must develop affinity for the spiritual world through cultivation of spiritual knowledge. The proper understanding of the spiritual science of devotional service makes it possible for beginners still conditioned to material nature to gain affinity for the spiritual world by carefully avoiding things that conflict with their spiritual progress. With such a foundation, they will then be able to carefully avoid conflicting mellows while seeking out harmonious ones in their quest for perfection.

One may argue that attention to minute details such as these is excessive for inexperienced practitioners of Kṛṣṇa consciousness. But Śrīla Prabhupāda does not agree. He regularly taught that attention to detail was a symptom of seriousness. And he warned in his writings that by the influence of Kali-yuga incidences of rasābhāsa arise more regularly, and devotees are thus tempted to overlook rasābhāsa in the name of liberal mindedness. But indiscriminate liberality merely increases the attraction to material acquisitions and conduct. Such indulgent devotees then fall away from the principles of pure devotion and become prākṛta-sahajiyās. Thus attention to detail in the study of the spiritual science is essential from the very beginning of one’s spiritual life.

For this reason, Caitanya Mahāprabhu would not tolerate even a hint of rasābhāsa amongst His followers; indeed He would become very angry if He detected it.

On one such occasion Kamalākānta Viśvāsa petitioned Mahārāja Pratāparudra to liquidate the three-hundred-rupee debt of Advaita Ācārya, but simultaneously praised Advaita as an incarnation of the Supreme Personality of Godhead. But how can the Supreme Lord be in debt? These two contradictory statements angered Lord Caitanya, so much so that the Lord banished Kamalākānta Viśvāsa from His personal company.

Kṛṣṇa’s names are also related to the places of His pastimes. Therefore, addressing Kṛṣṇa by names that, in terms of their devotional mellows, do not correspond to a particular place of Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes is another example of rasābhāsa. Śrīla Prabhupāda writes:

**“Kṛṣṇa’s devotees in Vṛndāvana address Him as Rādhā-ramaṇa, Nanda- nandana, and Yaśodā-nandana, but not as Vasudeva-nandana or Devakī- nandana. Although according to the material conception Nārāyaṇa, Rukmiṇī- ramaṇa, and Kṛṣṇa are one and the same, in the spiritual world one cannot use the name Rukmiṇī-ramaṇa or Nārāyaṇa in place of the name Kṛṣṇa. If one does so out of a poor fund of knowledge, his mellow with the Lord becomes spiritually faulty and is called rasābhāsa, an overlapping of transcendental mellows. The advanced devotee who has actually realized the transcendental features of the Lord will not commit the mistake of creating a rasābhāsa situation by using one name for another.”**

In this way the Lord and His pure representative, Śrīla Prabhupāda, have carefully emphasised the need for practising devotees to avoid rasābhāsa.

**\* \* \* \***

The pure spiritual greed of perfection and the formative stages of greed that arise successively in practice (such as inclination, longing, and covetousness) are both born from faithful hearing of Kṛṣṇa’s Vṛndāvana pastimes from scripture and advanced devotees. The sweetness of those pastimes, in any of the four mellows, like the sweetness in any confectionary, is the primary ingredient that attracts devotees’ hearts.

Sometimes neophyte devotees make the mistake of rejecting the authority of their superiors and of scripture under the mistaken assumption that one cannot attain spontaneous love by following rules and regulations. But the truth is both before and after the inclination to vraja-bhakti has been awakened — even though the awakening of greed is not dependent on the reasoning of the scriptures or the ruling of their superiors — devotees are dependent on both these authorities for guidance in achieving their spiritual aspirations.

Viśvanātha Cakravartī Ṭhākura has extracted from Śrīmad-Bhāgavatam five essential factors that assist in cultivating an attraction to vraja-bhakti. Proper assimilation of these factors will support devotees not only in their pursuit of vraja-bhakti but also in the way they relate to the pastime places of the holy dhāmas.

We should not try to artificially impose upon ourselves a devotional mellow of our choosing. It simply won’t work. Either an inclination for vraja- bhakti has spontaneously awakened by purification of the heart, or it hasn’t. And if it hasn’t, then we should continue to practise regulated devotional service with enthusiasm, patience, and determination. Greed for vraja-bhakti will naturally arise in due time.

How can one be sure if genuine greed has awakened in any form? When it has, the mind will naturally contemplate (manasā) topics of vraja-bhakti, without separate endeavour. But when it has not, contemplation of this sort cannot be sustained, even by the strength of one’s intelligence. However, always remembering that Lord Caitanya’s gift to the world was vraja-bhakti and heeding Śrīla Prabhupāda’s warnings against rasābhāsa, one can practise vaidhī- bhakti with knowledge of the five factors that assist in cultivating transcendental greed.

The five factors are as follows: (1) those things composed of bhāva (bhāvamaya); (2) those things related to bhāva (bhāva-sambandhī); (3) those things favourable to bhāva (bhāva-anukūla); (4) those things compatible with bhāva (bhāva-aviruddha); (5) and those things opposed to bhāva (bhāva- pratikūla).

Composed of bhāva (bhāvamaya) are the four primary mellows: servitude, friendship, parenthood, and amorous love. Knowledge of these mellows gives practising devotees at least opportunities — as they are being purified by their spiritual practices — to be drawn to one of these moods of service. And when they are actually drawn to one of the primary mellows, every activity they perform is saturated with the flavour of that rasa.

Related to bhāva (bhāva-sambandhī) are the primary limbs of devotion, like hearing, chanting, and remembering Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes, and serving the spiritual master. This also includes living in the dhāma or visiting the places of Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes, preferably Vṛndāvana or Māyāpur. All of these practices are indispensable, because when properly done they saturate the consciousness in topics of Kṛṣṇa, which in time naturally awakens a devotee’s mellow without him having to artificially choose one. Such hearing and chanting and serving helps to cultivate his spiritual disposition.

Then there are related practices, not indispensable, but favourable to bhāva (bhāva-anukūla). Although not essential, these practices should be eagerly accepted because they complement those that are. Some examples of the practices in this category are observance of Ekādaśī, celebration of Janmāṣṭamī, acceptance of vows during the month of Kārttika, renunciation of sense pleasures for Kṛṣṇa’s satisfaction, and worship of trees like tulasī and banyan.

There are also practices that, while not essential or as overtly favourable as the first two factors, are still compatible with cultivating greed or bhāva (bhāva-aviruddha). These practices are optional or neutral, for example: wearing the flower garland worn by the Deity, and decorating the body with the names of the Lord.

And finally, opposed to the cultivation of vraja-bhāva (bhāva-pratikūla) are practices such as meditating on the service of Kṛṣṇa’s queens in Dvārakā, or decorating one’s body with mantras related to the demigods or the deities of Vaikuṇṭha (nyāsa). Practices such as these should be avoided.

These five factors, assimilated generally into one’s life, help cultivate attraction to and greed for vraja-bhakti, and more specific aspects of these five factors can help nourish an affinity for particular pastime places. The application for a devotee on parikrama, for example, might go something like this:
