The very essence of attachment (bhāvamaya) applied to holy places is the deep conviction that Vṛndāvana, and nowhere else, is our eternal home. Upon that foundation, with further purification of the heart, a devotee’s attention becomes fixed upon particular places in Vṛndāvana as possible places of residence (vāsa), places that may be most conducive to following in the footsteps of his favourite Vraja-vāsī. The devotee’s acts and meditations are then related to specific places and favourite devotees within Vraja. Śrīla Prabhupāda describes this bhāvamaya: “The devotee ... should mentally live there.”

Imperative and in all ways related (bhāva-sambandhī) to cultivating such greed are hearing, chanting, and remembering the pastimes Kṛṣṇa enacted throughout Vraja. And applied to parikrama, the devotee regularly hears and chants about the pastimes and persons particular to the places he visits, gradually culminating in specific absorption in those places related to his favourite Vraja-vāsī. Devotees should live in Vṛndāvana, but if they cannot, then they should make their homes like Vṛndāvana by inviting Kṛṣṇa to take up residence there. From there they can mentally perform parikrama in the mood just mentioned.

And wherever one lives, Caitanya Mahāprabhu has ordered that everyone preach Kṛṣṇa’s message, thus giving conditioned souls an opportunity to also aspire for vraja-bhakti. That is also mandatory:

*ataeva āmi ājñā diluṅ sabākāre*  
*yāhāṅ tāhāṅ prema-phala deha’ yāre tāre*

**“Therefore I order every man within this universe to accept this Kṛṣṇa consciousness movement and distribute it everywhere.”**

There are other practices that are favourable (bhāva-anukūla), although not indispensable, like observing festivals or dramatic performances that glorify Kṛṣṇa at His pastime places. Devotees can also worship the deities residing there, bathe in the kuṇḍas or rivers there, bow down to those places, perform parikrama of those places barefoot, and show veneration for relics from Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes. Accepting vows like chanting three lakhs of Kṛṣṇa’s names or performing daṇḍavat-parikrama at a particular place are also favourable. In addition, if appropriate to the pastime place being visited, devotees may meditate on and pray to Vraja-vāsīs who may be of a different mellow to their own, but who are not antagonistic to the associate whom they primarily follow. In other words, they may chant, hear, and remember any pastime in any place so long as there is no overlapping of contrary mellows (rasābhāsa).

In the category of practices neither incompatible nor favourable to bhava (bhāva-aviruddha) relating to parikrama are items such as wearing on one’s body a small container (kavaca) with dust from a holy place, decorating the body with tilaka from a local kuṇḍa, or giving donations to or sponsoring a feast for local brāhmaṇas while visiting the holy places.

And finally, while performing parikrama, devotees must gradually identify and then reject both practices and moods opposed to one’s own mood (bhāva-pratikūla) and the mood of the associate whom one follows. In other words, the avoidance of conflicting moods (rasābhāsa) is an ongoing process that becomes more refined as one advances. Beginners, for instance, may have desired to live in Dvārakā, aspired to go to Vaikuṇṭha, or perhaps embraced any or all mellows of vraja-bhakti as their own. Perhaps they recited mantras to Nṛsiṁhadeva, revered Candrāvalī on an equal level with Śrī Rādhā, or lived in Rādhā-kuṇḍa while cultivating a parental mood towards Kṛṣṇa. Although these practices may be favourable to the attainment of pure devotion in general, they generate moods that may be unfavourable later in one’s spiritual journey as one’s identification with a specific rasa begins to unfold. At such a time such practices are discarded.

The five factors of devotional aspiration and their effects on the cultivation of vraja-bhakti have been applied specifically to parikrama in order to highlight the mechanics of how and why devotees show affinity for places of pastimes.

**\* \* \* \***

Devotees who perform parikrama of New Vraja-dhāma or Bhauma Vṛndāvana must understand the principles of spiritual affinity and how those principles relate to the places of Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes.

Until faith in the dhāma has naturally awakened within devotees’ hearts, they will not feel affinity for one pastime place over another, neither should they artificially try to show feelings they do not genuinely possess. In the meantime, devotees should visit all of the places of Kṛṣṇa’s pastimes without discrimination. By hearing the glories of the Lord in those places, their faith in the transcendental nature of the original holy dhāma and in the New Vraja- dhāma of Kṛṣṇa Valley will grow.

Devotees who have developed faith in the dhāma and who aspire for vraja- bhakti in general will be greatly helped by the factors of spiritual affinity. Assimilating these factors will one day make them more favourably inclined to some places than they are to others. These things will become self-evident as they faithfully visit the pastime places of Rādhā and Śyāma.

As devotees’ real affinity arises, first towards a specific mood of devotion and then to a specific associate of Kṛṣṇa, they will apply the five factors for cultivating bhāva, beginning with bhāvamaya, while externally showing equal respect to all pastime places on parikrama. Now situated in spontaneous devotion, these devotees will encourage others to act from the platform of their own realisations, knowing that Kṛṣṇa does not approve of forcing others to accept a mood or position for which they are unqualified. But in their personal prayer, meditation, and bhajana, advancing devotees will appeal to those Vraja-vāsīs in a similar mood as their own (sajātīya) or who are at least not transcendentally opposed to the associate whom they follow.

There are many details related to the five factors that help develop the natural affinity we have already discussed. The offenceless chanting of Kṛṣṇa’s names naturally reveals those details within devotees’ purified hearts, but Narottama Dāsa Ṭhākura warns serious devotees to keep their internal realisations to themselves rather than risk criticism from others:

*āpana bhajana-kathā na kahiba jathā tathā*  
*ihāte haio sābadhana*  
*nā kariha keha roṣa na laiha mora doṣa*  
*pranamaha bhaktera caraṇa*

**“In the matter of bhajana, devotees should not speak of their internal realisations freely. This is a principle to which everyone should be attentive. Otherwise, some people will become angry, while others will find fault. Better to keep everyone other than confidential friends distant from the contents of your heart, while offering them all respect.”**

When devotees become fully realised in pure greed for vraja-bhakti, the five factors of bhāva which they learned to apply through effort as sādhakas become naturally integrated into their perfected conduct. Those principles are at that point part of their constitutional nature.

Perfected devotees are drawn spontaneously to those pastime places that provide a favourable impetus for their mood of devotion. They are similarly averse to those places unfavourable for their mood, even to the point of mentally avoiding them, although they may still visit those places to guide and instruct others.

Amongst the many pastime places of Bhauma Vṛndāvana or New Vraja- dhāma, perfected devotees will recognise their eternal place of service (vāsa) and will go there to chant the Hare Kṛṣṇa mahā-mantra in solitude, happily absorbed in assisting their favourite Vraja-vāsī in his or her service to Kṛṣṇa’s associates. And when devotees cannot physically go to their vāsa, they will go there within their minds. Thus at the time of leaving their body, the pastime place that was the object of their constant remembrance will be their ultimate destination in the aprakaṭa-dhāma.

## Appendix 22

**The History of the Deity of Lord Ālālanātha at Brahmagiri**

*(This appendix follows from the narration of Lord Caitanya’s visit to Ālālanātha in chapter thirteen, and how His spiritual emotions mirrored Rādhārāṇī’s ecstasy at seeing Kṛṣṇa’s four-armed form at Paīṭhā.)*

The following history of the Deity named Ālālanātha — presently known by local residents as Ālārnāth — was passed down through oral tradition and later confirmed by Śrīla Bhaktisiddhānta Sarasvatī Ṭhākura.

Once, in Satya-yuga, Lord Brahmā came to a hill about twelve miles west of Jagannātha Purī to worship Lord Nārāyaṇa. (The Lord would later name the hill Brahmagiri after the creator.) After some time, Lord Nārāyaṇa instructed Brahmā to make a Deity of Him. The Lord gave a detailed description of the Deity to be fashioned, requesting that deities of Lalitā and Candrāvalī be made as well. This Deity of Lord Nārāyaṇa has been worshipped there ever since.

The present temple of Ālālanātha was constructed a few hundred years before the existing temple of Lord Jagannātha. Brāhmaṇas from the Śrī sampradāya came to be custodians of the Deity. Because these brāhmaṇas were followers of the Ālvārs, the Deity became known as Ālvār-nātha, the Lord of the Ālvārs. Eventually the name changed to Ālārnāth, by which He is known to date. Lands donated to the Deity by the kings of Orissa provided income to maintain these brāhmaṇas.

As the priestly families increased in size, however, the land grants no longer provided sufficient income to maintain the brāhmaṇa servants of Ālālanātha, forcing the priests to resort to begging. And because Brahmagiri was nothing but jungle at that time, begging meant travelling to Purī, which required a full day’s journey.

A brāhmaṇa called Śrī Ketana was once in charge of making an offering of sweet rice to Lord Ālālanātha. One day, when his storeroom was empty, he too was obliged to make the long trip to Purī in order to beg.

Before leaving, Śrī Ketana put his young untrained son, Madhu, in charge of making the daily offering, instructing him how to place the offering before the Deity and to ask the Lord to accept the sweet rice.

When the time came to make the offering, Madhu’s mother gave him the sweet rice she had cooked and repeated her husband’s instructions. The boy went before the Deity and prayed, “Dear Lord! I do not know the proper mantras with which to offer this food, but please accept it.” Then Madhu went outside and waited for the allotted time the Deity required to accept the offering.

Upon re-entering the temple, Madhu was disappointed to see that the sweet rice was still on the offering plate, untouched. He did not understand that the Lord is able to eat simply through His glance. Madhu expected the Deity to eat the sweet rice in the same way a boy would eat, leaving nothing on the plate.

Pressing his palms together, he eagerly prayed, “O Lord of the Ālvārs! Due to some offence I must have committed, You have rejected this offering. But if You do not eat, my father will be angry with me. So please overlook my faults and eat.”

Once again Madhu left the temple, closing the door behind him, and waited for the Lord to eat. And again he returned, only to find the sweet rice still on the plate. Ālārnāth had not eaten. Little Madhu began to cry and, while sobbing, entreated the Lord for the third time. Feeling hopeless, he again went outside and stood by the temple door crying, unable to understand why the Lord would not accept his offering.

A Deity of the Lord generally accepts His meals by glancing over them, but He can also eat in the conventional way. And because Madhu, imbued with spontaneous devotion, was so insistent that the Lord eat in the way that he ate, when the boy re-entered the temple he found the plates empty.

“Finally the Lord has eaten!” thought Madhu. Thanking the Deity again and again, he brought the empty plate back to his mother.

Surprised, she asked, “Son! Where are the Lord’s remnants?”

Madhu replied, “Ālārnāth ate everything!”

“He ate everything?”

“Yes!” Madhu replied. Elated from having successfully fed the Lord, Madhu then went out to play with his friends.

Lord Ālārnāth continued to eat Madhu’s offerings, and since there was no other food in the house, Madhu and his mother fasted on the first day and for the next two days as well. Finally Śrī Ketana returned with the alms he had collected. When he asked his wife for some of the Lord’s remnants, she informed him that there were none. “For three days your son has made offerings to the Lord, and for three days we have seen no remnants. According to Madhu, Ālārnāth ate everything.”

Śrī Ketana was upset. He asked Madhu for an explanation, but the boy stuck to his story. “I offered the sweet rice just as you asked, and the Lord ate it.”

The brāhmaṇas of Brahmagiri were smārtas, materialists who made their living by worshipping the Deity. Śrī Ketana thought that the Deity was a stone statue, not the living form of the Supreme Lord. Thus when Madhu replied that Ālārnāth had eaten the sweet rice, Śrī Ketana took it to be childish prattle, a cover-up for the boy having eaten the offering himself or shared it with his playmates.

Angry Śrī Ketana replied, “Deities are made of stone. They do not eat offerings. Offerings are rituals we undertake for self-purification.”

“Father!” replied Madhu. “Other Deities may not eat, but our Ālārnāth does. I saw Him!”

Sri Ketana wanted to get to the bottom of this mystery. He also wanted to correct his son for apparent misbehaviour. Barely controlling his anger, Sri Ketana said, “Stones do not eat! I will prove it to you. You make another offering and I will hide in the temple to find out what really happens to the sweet rice.”

Sri Ketana then proceeded to hide behind a pillar while Madhu made another impassioned plea to Alarnath: “O Lord! My father is angry because he believes that I ate the offerings, and not You. Please eat this sweet rice again to protect my word of honour.”

Pleased with Madhu’s pure faith, Lord Alarnath happily reciprocated Madhu’s devotion. As the Deity reached down and lifted the sweet rice to eat it, Sri Ketana jumped out from his hiding place and caught the Lord’s hand. The hot sweet rice spilled everywhere, scalding Alarnath in the process.

Bewildered by anger that drove him briefly to madness, Sri Ketana censured the Lord. “Stop what You are doing. If You eat the sweet rice there will be nothing left for us. This is not proper conduct for a Deity!”

Such is the power of the material energy. Even after witnessing the divinity of the Deity, a person may still refuse to surrender to the Lord. Duryodhana, for example, continued to plot against Krsna even after the Lord revealed to him His universal form.

But Alarnath was not to be intimidated by such a foolish person. The Lord replied, “By first acting as if I were mere stone and then by commanding Me so, you have committed serious offences. These abominable acts are the mature fruit of your offensive mentality. As punishment, you and your entire community of atheists will all perish - all except Madhu.”

Then the Lord again became silent and immobile. And sure enough, just as the Lord had promised, some days later a tidal wave came in from the ocean, drowning all the brahmanas of Brahmagiri - except for Madhu. As a testimony to His divinity, and to Madhu’s devotion, the parts of the Lord’s body upon which the hot sweet rice had landed show burn marks to this day.

In his commentary on Sri Caitanya'caritamrta (Madhya-lila 7.59), Bhaktivinoda Thakura writes:

**“Travelling along the coast from Puri about six krosas \[fourteen miles\] is a village known as Alalanatha. There one will find the fourarmed Deity of Vasudeva known as Alalanatha. The Lord’s temple is within a forest in this small village. The most excellent sweet rice is offered to the Deity. Even nowadays the pujaris show the mark on the Deity where He was burnt by hot sweet rice.”**

After this incident the standard of worship at Alalanatha gradually deteriorated. Finally, the famous king of Orissa, Maharaja Purusottamadeva, again raised the standard by incorporating the worship at Brahmagiri into the management of the Jagannatha temple at Puri. The Puri brahmanas are responsible for the Deity service to this day.

There are many references in the Caitanya-caritamrta to Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s visiting Alalanatha; especially during the periods when Lord Jagannatha was not on His altar in Puri. Lord Caitanya’s mood during His visits to Alalanatha is described in chapter thirteen of this book.

Lord Caitanya first visited the temple of Alalanatha in 1510. Upon seeing the Deity, Lord Caitanya fell down like a rod. His ecstasy was so intense that the stone beneath His body melted. That stone is said to be all that remains of Brahmagiri and still bears the clear imprint of Lord Caitanya’s body. Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura says that Brahmagiri is Govardhana Hill and the area surrounding Alalanatha is the forest of Vrndavana.

The imprint of Lord Caitanya’s body in the stone of Brahmagiri has additional esoteric significance, which can be understood by comparing His pastimes in Alalanatha to those in Puri. By such comparison, one can understand both the identity of Lord Alalanatha and the reason why Lord Caitanya felt such profound ecstasy in the presence of this Deity.

In Puri, on the stone beside the Garuda-stambha in Lord Jagannatha’s temple, are the imprints of Lord Caitanya’s feet. These imprints were the result of the stone melting from the influence of Caitanya Mahaprabhu’s intense love for Lord Jagannatha. His finger marks can also be seen on the pillar of Garuda. According to the Caitanya-caritamrta, Lord Caitanya’s ecstasy upon gazing at Lord Jagannatha was due to His seeing Lord Jagannatha as two-handed Syamasundara playing His flute.

**“Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu stayed behind the huge column called the Garuda-stambha and looked upon Lord Jagannatha, but as He looked He saw that Lord Jagannatha had become Lord Krsna, with His flute to His mouth.”**

As previously described, when Lord Caitanya went to Alalanatha, the stone beneath Him also melted. Such a display of ecstasy could only have been caused by Radha’s love for Krsna. In other words, had Lord Caitanya been seeing Lord Narayana when He looked at Alalanatha He would not have felt such intense love. He must have been seeing Syamasundara in Alalanatha as well. Thus the vision of the Gaudiya Vaisnavas is that Alalanatha is not Narayana hut the four-handed Krsna of Paitha.

Moreover, the fact that Lord Caitanya’s entire body left an imprint at Alalanatha indicates that He felt a greater degree of love there than He felt in Puri, where only His feet and hands were imprinted. In fact, it is said that Lord Caitanya’s bliss in Alalanatha was twice that in Puri.

The reasoning of the Gaudiya Vaisnava authorities is that Lord Caitanya was accustomed to seeing Lord Jagannatha at Puri daily. But when He arrived at Alalanatha - especially the first time, and in the pain of separation from Jagannatha - Caitanya Mahaprahhu was startled when the Deity responded to His love by displaying the two-armed form of Krsna. This surprise at unexpectedly finding Krsna in the forest of Brahmagiri caused an unprecedented upsurge of love in Lord Caitanya.

Most priests of Alalanatha will not agree that their Deity is that of four-handed Krsna. For them He is none other than Lord Narayana. But they identify the goddesses worshipped with the Lord as Candravali and Lalita, neither of which are found in Vaikuntha, but rather in Vraja with Krsna. And even more telling are the two mantras the brahmanas themselves chant in their worship of the supposed Visnu, mantras in which they address Alarnath as Krsna and Gopi-jana-vallabha.

Srila Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura visited the temple of Alalanatha in 1918; in 1922 he constructed a Gaudiya Matha temple on the property adjacent to the Alalanatha temple. In 1929 he renovated the temple of Alalanatha and was presented with a small Deity of Alalanatha that had been discovered during construction. The Deity instructed the priest that He should be given to Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura. In 1932 Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati installed the Deities of Radha and Gopinatha in the Gaudiya Matha temple, thus declaring, “Alalanatha is Goplnatha.” During the Thakura’s last visit in 1934, he made this prophetic and revolutionary statement:

**“We must take Lord Jagannatha in an airplane chariot to Eastbourne, London. There Alarnath, Gaudiyanatha, and Gopinatha will appear. We must instal the Deity of Sri Caitanya Mahaprabhu everywhere in the world, otherwise there will be no auspiciousness for the human race.”**

In 1996 ISKCON further renovated Alalanatha’s temple and the temple compound.

Devotees who come to the small forest named Paitha in New Vraja-dhama and recall the four-armed form that Krsna revealed to the gopis may also think of the form of Lord Alalanatha revealed to Caitanya Mahaprabhu. The two are not different, in the same way that Radha and Krsna are not different from Lord Caitanya. By praying to Caitanya Mahaprabhu at Paitha, devotees can more easily enter into the pastime Krsna enacted there with Sri Radha and the gopis. Bhaktisiddhanta Sarasvati Thakura has explained that Brahmagiri is a place where the predominant mood of worship is in separation. Devotees, then, should also adopt that same mood when performing bhajana at Paitha.

## Appendix 23

**The Purifying Effect of the Dust of Devotees' Feet on Sacred Waters**

*(This appendix follows from the description of Airavata-kunda in chapter thirteen.)*

What constitutes spiritual purity? How do persons or things, both material and spiritual, become purified? More importantly, what does it mean that souls, who are pure by nature, become purified? And most specifically, how does the essence of this topic - purity - relate to the effect that the dust of devotees’ feet has on waters already sacred? All of these questions will he addressed in this appendix. The basic principles of purification outlined herein will also be used to explain how the level of purity in a given soul or object can sometimes be further elevated, even though already pure.

The immediate purpose of this study is to shed light on the subject of how waters like that of the Ganges in Airavata-kunda can become even more sacred by contact with the dust from the lotus feet of the Vraja-vasis, especially the gopis.

Let me begin this analysis by defining purity and the process of purification as they relate to Krsna’s internal potency.

Krsna’s internal potency (antaranga-sakti) is fully spiritual. It is one of His three principal potencies. The second, sometimes called the marginal potency, is the living entity (ksetra-jna, or jiva). The third is the external potency, more generally identified as ignorance (avidya-sakti), whose influence causes the living entity to forget God (to degrade himself into godlessness). It is also avidya-sakti that fills the jiva with the desire to enjoy the material world by exploiting its resources.

The internal potency comprises the spiritual world in its entirety, including Krsna’s eternal associates and an unlimited number of conscious but seemingly inanimate objects like stones, rivers, and mountains. The external potency consists of the three modes of material nature and comprises the whole of the material world, conditioned souls plus an uncountable variety of inanimate material objects not possessed of consciousness.

Although the living entity is spiritual by constitution, he has the prerogative to reside in either the material or spiritual worlds. Under the influence of ignorance his constitutional position is covered and he chooses the material world, wherein he is forced to suffer the threefold miseries. Due to karma and material conditioning the living entity is forced to take on different material bodies which act as coverings for his eternal spiritual identity, and by which he is proportionately influenced by ignorance to a greater or lesser degree. In species below humans - animals and plants - spiritual consciousness is almost nonexistent, in most humans it is relatively awakened, and in humans who understand the Absolute Truth and who are trying to become free from ignorance, spiritual consciousness can be fully awakened.

Because Krsna is the source and thus the controller of these three potencies, and because He is always wholly spiritual, He never comes under the influence of His external potency, the material energy and its modes. Souls who understand this truth about Krsna also become free from the entanglement of the material modes. This state of being—complete spiritual existence uncontaminated by matter—is what constitutes “purity” in the context of this appendix. In short, souls or objects are considered “pure” to the degree that they are free from the influence of the material energy. The process by which souls or objects come to that state of being “in complete spiritual existence uncontaminated by matter” we will call “purification.”

The living entities and conscious, non-moving objects in the spiritual world are, like Krsna, also pure. The living entities in the material world, however, although constitutionally pure, identify with their material covering and in such a state are conditioned by their environment and are thus considered impure. Like Krsna, all souls are composed of knowledge, bliss, and eternal existence, regardless of whether they are in the spiritual or material worlds. This truth is supported by the Vedic statement asango hy ayam purusah: “The living entity and the Supreme Personality of Godhead have nothing to do with the material world.” These souls can remain pure - and thus directly aware of their constitution as spiritual sparks, possessed of knowledge, bliss, and eternal existence - as long as they have no contact with the inferior, material energy.

Sukadeva Gosvami explains how souls become connected to matter by the bewildering potency of Krsna (atma-maya), and hence become impure:

*sri-suka uvaca*  
*atma-mayam rte rajan parasyanubhavatmanah*  
*na ghatetartha-sambandhah svapna-drastur ivanjasa*

**“Sri Sukadeva Gosvami said: ‘O King, unless one is influenced by the bewildering energy of the Supreme Personality of Godhead, there is no meaning to the relationship of the pure soul in pure consciousness with the material body. That relationship is just like a dreamer’s seeing his own body working.’”**

It should be mentioned that the purity of a soul, even one free of material taint, has achieved a state of complete perfection only when that individual soul serves the Supreme Soul. Just as knowledge, bliss, and eternal existence are the elements from which souls are constituted, service to the Supreme Soul is the constitutional activity, or purpose, of such souls.

Up until this point in our discussion, the purity or impurity of a soul has largely been determined by the realm in which that soul exists: pure souls reside in the spiritual world, impure souls in the material. This generalisation, however, is not fully accurate. Pure souls also exist in the material world. Numerous scriptural statements support this truth, for instance:

*iha yasya harer dasye karmana manasa gira*  
*nikhilasv apy avasthasu jwan-muktah sa ucyate*

**“Anyone who, by his actions, mind and words, lives only for the transcendental loving service of the Lord, is certainly a liberated soul, even though he may appear to be in a condition of material existence.”**

The most accurate criterion for determining purity, therefore, is not the location of a given soul, but its state of consciousness. Hence, from this point on, the purity of living entities will be analysed in terms of the state of their consciousness.

Since it is the presence or absence of consciousness that differentiates spirit from matter, the spiritual purity of any given soul is established when the consciousness of that soul is freed of material identification. Souls fully aware of their spiritual identity display pure consciousness, and subsequently their conduct is also pure. But when souls identify in any way, and to any degree, with matter, their consciousness is impure, as is their conduct.

The state of pure (suddha) consciousness (sattva) is called, alternately, suddha-sattva, visuddha-sattva, and vasudeva-sattva. As described in the following verse, this consciousness manifests when the unfettered soul is fully absorbed in service to Vasudeva, Krsna.

*sattvam visuddham vasudeva-sabditam*  
*yad iyate tatra puman apavrtah*  
*sattve ca tasmin bhagavan vasudevo*  
*hy adhoksajo me namasa vidhiyate*

**“I am always engaged in offering obeisances to Lord Vasudeva in pure Krsna consciousness. Krsna consciousness is always pure consciousness, in which the Supreme Personality of Godhead, known as Vasudeva, is revealed without any covering.”**

Everything that exists in the spiritual realm - whether it be gopi, cow, tree, stone, water, or hill - is fully conscious and completely pure. Lord Brahma provides us with a vision of this all-conscious, all-pure realm:

**“I worship that transcendental realm known as Svetadvipa, where as loving consorts the Laksmis in their unalloyed spiritual essence practice the amorous service of the Supreme Lord, Krsna, their only lover, where every tree fulfils all desires, where the soil is made of purpose gems, and the water is nectar, and every word a song, every step a dance, and the flute is the favourite attendant. Effulgence in that realm is full of transcendental bliss, and the supreme spiritual entities are all to be tasted and enjoyed. There, numberless cows always give transcendental oceans of milk, and transcendental time, ever present, without past or future, eternally exists, not subject to passing away even for the space of half a moment. That realm is known as Goloka only to a very few self-realized souls in this world.”**

**\* \* \* \***

In addition to conditioned souls, liberated souls, and spiritual objects, there is also matter - already mentioned as the energy that influences the soul to forget God - which by definition is neither conscious nor pure. Matter can, however, be purified, and in principle, it can also be transformed into spiritual substance.

The question now arises: How does such purification take place? Whether going from a greater degree of impurity to a lesser degree of impurity, from a state of impurity to a state of purity, or from a lower stage of purity to a higher stage of purity, what are the processes by which purification takes place?

The answer: by the influence of something pure on something impure, or less pure. By the potency of association - the action of uniting or conjoining two separate entities, either in close physical proximity or in some other way—one object becomes influenced by the state of purity of the other. The scriptures give two examples to explain the way in which this association works: the crystal and the philosophers’ stone.

Regarding the former example, Hiranyakasipu tells his son Prahlada, “My dear son! Association is very important. It acts just like a crystal stone, which will reflect anything that is put before it.”

The crystal here exemplifies the power of association. If that stone is “crystal clear” then it will reflect the qualities of whatever is brought into its association. If those qualities are impure, the crystal then reflects, or assumes, those impure qualities. If, however, those qualities are pure, then the crystal will be purified in that association. Naturally, this process applies just as readily if that crystal stone happens to be the spirit soul.

The latter example is that of a philosophers’ stone, which by mere contact is able to turn iron into gold or is able to create valuable gems from more ordinary stones and yet remain unchanged. Krsnadasa Kaviraja Gosvami says,

**“Although a touchstone produces many varieties of valuable jewels, it nevertheless remains the same. It does not change its original form.”**

In this example the touchstone represents something with a very high degree of purity. Any person or object that comes in contact with such an object automatically takes on its pure qualities. The opposite reaction can take place if something very impure also acts like a touchstone, coming in contact with someone or something and thereby decreasing its purity.

To summarise both examples: pure things have the potency to purify impure things they come in contact with.

Before continuing further, a point of clarification should be made. Unconscious, non-moving objects and conscious beings with lesser degrees of free will than humans, such as animals, lack the selfvolition by which to accept or reject the association of pure things. Therefore, if they somehow come in contact with something pure, they are automatically purified by that association. Conscious beings endowed with free will, however, unknowingly become purified generally only until their desire for purification is awakened after which they are purified in proportion to their ability and willingness to accept the process of purification.

Now, by giving examples of each, we will describe the two categories of purification: attaining a higher state of either material or of spiritual purity.

One example of purification to a higher state of material purity can be seen when a person in the mode of passion takes up the practice of devotional service, becomes purified by the effect of chanting and hearing, and is elevated to the mode of goodness. Krsna explains that for a person seeking purification, by the prominence of the mode of goodness the modes of passion and ignorance are defeated.

Krsna also explains that the soul becomes conditioned by coming in contact with any one of the modes. But because the mode of good-ness is purer than the others, contact with goodness is illuminating, and it frees one from all sinful reactions.

Thus, although still within the realm of material contamination, a conditioned soul in the relatively more impure state of passion, can, by devotional practice and association with the higher mode of goodness, be purified and come to a state of higher material purity.

An example of how one attains a state of spiritual purity is seen when a devotee becomes free of all material contamination and is elevated to the spiritual platform by the practice of devotional service in pure goodness. Such a devotee comes in contact with a ray (suryamsu) of spiritual love (prema). He then attains the primary stage of spiritual purity called bhava, which is followed by still further increments of spiritual purity, indicative of the variety that exists on the spiritual platform.

This process takes place as follows:

*suddha-sattva-visesatma prema-suryamsu-samya-bhak*  
*rucibhis citta-masrnya-krd asau bhava ucyate*

**“When devotional service is executed on the transcendental platform of pure goodness, it is like a sun-ray of love for Krsna. At such a time, devotional service causes the heart to be softened by various tastes, and one is then situated in bhava \[emotion\].”**

When material objects are purified these objects remain material but at the same time exhibit certain spiritual qualities, including the power to purify. For example, food offered to Krsna (prasada) becomes spiritually transformed through Krsna’s grace, and although it appears to act in the way that matter does - it decomposes, for instance - it is actually spiritual and its power to purify those who eat it is celebrated throughout scripture. This is an example of matter that has been sublimated, or “spiritualised.” Because the potency of pure spiritual goodness is unlimited, material substance in constant touch with it is transformed into spiritual substance.

Another example relevant to our study is when ordinary plain water is mixed with the pure water of the Ganges. It is well known that any water, when mixed with even the smallest quantity of Ganges water, inherits the healing and other holy properties of the Ganges.

It is said,

**“Just as unmentionable waters entering into the Ganga attain the state of Ganga (gangatvam) so similarly everything that is offered to God becomes perfectly divine.”**

**\* \* \* \***

We have so far described some general principles underlying the process of purification. To assist us in understanding these principles more deeply, we will now discuss specific knowledge of the purifying agents. As these agents are numerous, we will list only the most prominent amongst them.

Obviously, Krsna, being “the purest of the pure,” is the topmost purifying agent. In one verse from Bhakti-rasamrta-sindhu, already quoted here, He is called pavanam pavananam, the “purifier of all purifiers.” Krsna is also referred to by the compound word visuddha-sattva. The use of the intensifying prefix vi emphasises His absolutely untainted nature, suddha-sattva. Krsna purifies and delivers the living entities, especially His devotees, in the natural course of His pastimes. Haridasa Thakura asserts that when Lord Krsna performed His manifest pastimes in Goloka Vrndavana, He freed all living entities in the universe from material existence:

*na caivam vismayah karyo bhavata bhagavaty aje*  
*yogesvaresvare krsne yata etad vimucyate*

**“Krsna, the unborn Supreme Personality of Godhead, master of all masters of mystic power, delivers all living entities, moving and non-moving. Nothing is astonishing in the activities of the Lord.”**

Krsna’s name, the dust from Krsna’s lotus feet, the water that washes His feet, Krsna’s Deity form, Krsna’s abode, Krsna’s associates, as well as the dust from their feet or the water that has washed their feet, Srimad-Bhagavatam - anything, in fact, that is either a transformation of or is imbued with suddha-sattva, has the power to purify.

But the focus of this appendix is the purifying effect of the dust of devotees’ feet on sacred waters, so that will be our concentration from this point forward. Recall that the context for this discussion is the statement that the pure Ganges water in Airavata-kunda was made more pure by the dust of Krsna’s lotus feet, and still more pure by contact with the dust from the lotus feet of the gopis. To clearly understand this phenomenon, let us start by more closely studying the purity of the Ganga.

The Garuda Purana tells us that

**“Ganges water is the best of all and it removes all sins committed till death.”**

The Padma Purana adds,

**“Especially in this age of Kali, Ganga is the bestower of liberation on mankind. Those lacking spiritual strength can obtain unlimited results of pious activities by practising penances on the banks of the Ganges.”**

And Srimad-Bhagavatam states,

**“The lotus feet of the Lord are by themselves the shelter of all places of pilgrimage. The great clear-minded sages, carried by the wings of the Vedas, always search after the nest of Your lotus-like face. Some of them surrender to Your lotus feet at every step by taking shelter of the best of rivers \[the Ganges\], which can deliver one from all sinful reactions.”**

From these quotes it is clear that the touch of Ganga-devi’s waters can purify persons of all sin, bestow devotional merit, and grant liberation. But as pure and celebrated as the waters of Ganga-devi are, scriptures say that other rivers - Yamuna-devi being the most notable amongst them - are purer still.

Since it is the dust of the Lord’s lotus feet that sanctifies both transcendental rivers, one may well wonder how the Yamuna could be purer than the Ganga.

In pursuit of the answer, let us consult Srimad-Bhagavatam:

*yad-visrutih sruti-nutedam alam punati*  
*padavanejana-payas ca vacas ca sastram*  
*bhuh kala-bharjita-bhagapi yad-anghri-padma-*  
*sparsottha-saktir abhivarsati no ’khilarthan*  
*tad-darsana-sparsananupatha-prajalpa-*  
*sayyasanasana-sayauna-sapinda-bandhah*  
*yesam grhe niraya-vartmani vartatam vah*  
*svargapavarga-viramah svayam asa visnuh*

**“When Lord Visnu appeared before Bali Maharaja to beg three steps of land, the Lord’s second step pierced the shells of the universe. The water of the transcendental river Viraja, lying just outside the universal egg, thus seeped inside, washing Lord Vamana’s foot and flowing down to become the Ganges River. Because of the sanctity of its origin, the Ganges is generally considered the most holy of rivers. But even more potent is the water of the Yamuna, where Lord Visnu in His original form of Govinda played with His intimate companions.”**

One point to consider is the relative potency of two different forms of the Lord, particularly Vamana and Krsna.

Vamanadeva is a lila-avatara who manifests just a portion of Krsna’s transcendental qualities. In the same way that the transcendental name Vamana only manifests one thousandth the potency of the name Krsna, the dust from Vamana’s lotus feet possesses only a fraction of the potency of the dust from Krsna’s feet. Thus, although the dust of the Lord’s feet is mixed with both rivers, the dust mixed in the Yamuna displays greater potency than the dust mixed in the Ganges. Consequently, the waters of the Yamuna are elevated to a higher state of purity.

Srila Prabhupada gives another reason why the Yamuna is more exalted than the Ganga: Regardless of Krsna’s supremacy over Vamanadeva, the Lord spent more time in the waters of the Yamuna than He did in the Ganga.

Vamanadeva’s connection with the Ganges is through a singular pastime in which the Lord pokes His toe through the covering of the universe, briefly coming in contact with Mother Ganga. On the other hand, in the many years that Krsna lived in Vrndavana, hardly a day went by that He did not bathe in the Yamuna with His friends during the daytime or at night with the gopis. Many pastimes, like the chastisement of Kaliya, the killing of Bakasura, and of course the rasa dance, took place near or in the Yamuna. In fact, the Lord sanctified the Yamuna from the very beginning of His transcendental pastimes in this world. Almost immediately after Krsna’s birth in Mathura, Vasudeva took his son to Gokula in order to protect the infant from Kamsa. While crossing the Yamuna, Vasudeva dropped Krsna in her waters, and by the dust of His lotus feet the river was at once further sanctified.

Therefore, although both rivers are spiritually pure, and in that sense there is no difference between them, a transcendental analysis reveals that the water of the Yamuna is still more pure than the water of the Ganga. Jiva Gosvami says, quoting the Varaha Purana, “When the water of the Ganges is sanctified one hundred times, it is called the Yamuna.”

This glory of the Yamuna is the first of five special excellences of Sri Krsna mentioned by Sukadeva Gosvami in summing up the Tenth Canto of Srimad-Bhagavatam. The sage says, tirtham cakre nrponam yad ajani yadusu svah-sarit pada-saucam, “The heavenly Ganges is a holy place of pilgrimage because her waters wash Lord Krsna’s feet. But when the Lord descended amongst the Yadus, His glories eclipsed the Ganges as a holy place.” Commenting on this excellence, Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura points out that the Yamuna became even greater than the Ganges by coming in contact with the dust from Sri Krsna’s feet. In support of his claim, the acarya quotes this verse:

*ganga-sata-guna prayo mathure mama mandale*  
*yamuna visruta devi natra karya vicarana*

**“The renowned Yamuna in My domain of Mathura is hundreds of times greater than the Ganges. About this there can be no dispute, O goddess.”**

This transcendental distinction is similar to the relative potency of Krsna’s different names, as already mentioned. Coincidentally, this is the fourth of the five excellences unique to Krsna’s appearance, as glorified by Sukadeva Gosvami in the section of the Bhagavatam aforementioned. The sage says, *yan-namamangala-ghnam srutam atha gaditam*: “His \[Krsna’s\] name destroys all inauspiciousness when heard or chanted.”

Vedic literature states that chanting one thousand names of Lord Visnu bestows the same benefit as chanting the holy name of Lord Rama three times, or the name of Krsna once. This means that of all the holy names of the Lord, the holy name of Krsna is the most powerful.

Understanding why this is so is instrumental to understanding how things associated (tadiyanam) with Krsna have greater purifying power than those things associated with any one of Krsna’s expansions.

As svayam-bhagavan (the original Supreme Personality of Godhead), Krsna displays more excellences than any of His other incarnations, although they are all equally the Supreme Personality of Godhead. For instance, Krsna has four excellences of sweetness not manifested even in the personality of Narayana: lila-madhurya, Krsna is like an ocean, filled with waves of pastimes that evoke wonder within the residents of all three worlds; prema-madhurya, in His activities of amorous love, He is always surrounded by His dear devotees who possess unequalled love for Him; venu-madhurya, He attracts the minds of everyone in all three worlds with the melodious vibration of His flute; and rupa-madhurya, His personal beauty and opulence are beyond compare.

Similarly, anything connected to Krsna (tadiyanam) displays more excellences, and thus greater power to purify, than those things con-nected to His other incarnations. Therefore connection with the dust of Krsna’s lotus feet makes the waters of the Yamuna one hundred times purer than the waters of the Ganga.

**\* \* \* \***

Of the many things connected to Krsna, the most prominent, in the interests of this appendix, are Krsna’s devotees and the dust of their lotus feet.

Krsna’s pure devotees and the mercy of Krsna’s pure devotees are endlessly glorified throughout Vedic literature as the only media through which conditioned souls are able to approach the Lord. Srimad-Bhagavatam explains that in order to know Krsna, the greatest philosophers may endlessly speculate, studying the Vedas for many years, but all of their attempts will fail unless they are favoured by at least a slight trace of Krsna’s mercy. And Lord Caitanya specifies that Krsna’s mercy takes tangible form in the association of saintly devotees, whose instructions open the gateway to liberation (*guru-krsna-prasade paya bhakti-lata-bija*). For ordinary souls, bereft of the company of great devotees, it is so difficult to understand Krsna as to be virtually impossible. The Garuda Purana says,

**“One cannot be liberated without association with a pure devotee of the Lord.”**

Krsna Himself declares that the devotional process, which begins by surrendering to a devotee, is the only way to understand Him, the otherwise incomprehensible enigma (*rahasyam hy etad uttamam*). And in Srila Prabhupada’s own words,

**“The conclusion is that we have to understand the Supreme Personality of Godhead from the devotees.”**

Pure devotees are so powerful that simply seeing them, touching them, hearing from them, or serving them purifies the conditioned souls. Moreover, they are so merciful that - unlike Krsna, who will directly associate only with those already pure - devotees seek out the most sinful souls in order to grant them their purifying association, by which Krsna can be known and loved. As Srila Prabhupada has said, “A devotee is more merciful than God Himself.”

The association of Krsna’s servants is in many ways more beneficial than bathing in the Ganga. For instance, the Padma Purana asserts that the water that has washed the feet of the devotee spiritual master is equally as powerful as the water of ten million places of pilgrimage and gives the merit of regularly bathing in the Ganga. And Srimad-Bhagavatam says that great devotees “can at once sanctify those who take shelter of them, whereas the waters of the Ganges can sanctify only after prolonged use.” The purifying effect of devotees is both greater and more expedient than that of the Ganga; indeed, Narottama Dasa Thakura says that just once seeing a great devotee is more effective than repeatedly bathing in the Ganges.

The truth is that great devotees can purify even Ganga-devi. This declaration is not just an abstract philosophical conclusion, rather its veracity is borne out by the historical events surrounding Mother Ganga’s descent to earth.

After having performed severe austerities to invoke the descent of the Ganges, King Bhagiratha’s desire was fulfilled when the goddess Ganga-devi personally appeared before him. She was satisfied with his austerities and was prepared to give the king the benediction he desired: to deliver his forefathers, burnt to ashes by the glance of Lord Kapila. However, Mother Ganges had an objection to her presence on earth, which she requested Bhagiratha to resolve.

Ganga said, “O King! I do not wish to go down to the planet earth, for there the people in general will bathe in my water to cleanse themselves of the reactions of their sinful deeds. When all these sinful reactions accumulate in me, how shall I become free from them?”

The king’s illuminating reply was, “Those who are saintly because of devotional service and are therefore in the renounced order, free from material desires, and who are pure devotees, expert in following the regulative principles mentioned in the Vedas, are always glorious and pure in behaviour and are able to deliver all fallen souls. When such pure devotees bathe in your water, the sinful reactions accumulated from other people will certainly be counteracted, for such devotees always keep in the core of their hearts the Supreme Personality of Godhead, who can vanquish all sinful reactions.”

Pure devotees are so powerful that they not only purify those who take shelter of them, they also purify the river Ganges. In the words of Srila Prabhupada, “Devotees and saintly persons advanced in the renounced order can deliver even the Ganges.”

Many sinful people bathe in the waters of holy places and are purified, but those holy lakes and rivers then become contaminated by the residue of sinful reactions these pilgrims leave behind. However, when pure devotees bathe in those same holy places, they neutralise the sins left by sinful men, because the great devotees always carry Krsna, the greatest purifying agent, within their hearts. Thus scrip-tures glorify pure devotees as places of pilgrimage personified, a truth spoken by Yudhisthira Maharaja about Mahatma Vidura:

*bhavad-vidha bhagavatas tirtha-bhutah svayam vibho*  
*tirthi-kurvanti tirthani svantah-sthena gadabhrta*

**“My lord, devotees like your good self are verily holy places personified. Because you carry the Personality of Godhead within your heart, you turn all places into places of pilgrimage.”**

How do pure devotees become so empowered?

The answer is in their exclusive attachment to the shelter of Krsna’s lotus feet, which are themselves the destroyers of all kinds of contamination and the cause of all auspicious things. Pure devotees always keep Krsna’s lotus feet planted upon their hearts. They remain well sheltered beneath the shade of Krsna’s pinkish soles, worshipping them constantly. In this way, devotees become so completely pure and auspicious that even Lord Siva desires to associate with them.

Pure devotees maintain direct contact with Lord Krsna’s lotus feet through constant absorption in hearing and chanting His glories. The transcendental pastimes and holy names of Krsna are identical to the Lord, possessing all His transcendental potencies, and by chanting and hearing these names and pastimes devotees are fully in contact with Krsna. Thus, the power of pure spiritual goodness present in Krsna’s lotus feet is transmitted to devotees through the sound vibration of His glories, which also saturates them with His potencies. In fact, pure devotees become so powerful by this process that each one of them is able to deliver a universe - what to speak of delivering the Ganges - by elevating a multitude of conditioned souls to the blessed stage of suddha-sattva.

Let us disclose another secret of the purifying power of great devotees: wherever devotees engage in the sankirtana of the Lord’s names, Krsna personally comes. Krsna directly revealed this wonder to Narada:

*naham tisthami vaikunthe yoginam hrdayesu va*  
*tatra tisthami narada yatra gayanti mad-bhaktah*

**“My dear Narada, actually I do not reside in My abode, Vaikuntha, nor do I reside within the hearts of the yogis, but I reside in that place where My pure devotees chant My holy name and discuss My form, pastimes, and qualities.”**

During pure sankirtana, Krsna appears in both the sound vibration of His holy names as well as in His personal presence, thereby doubling the spiritual power His devotees are able to deliver.

For instance, when Bilvamangala Thakura sang Krsna-karnamrta, Krsna personally appeared to listen to His devotee’s composition. He was equally present in the sound vibration of the songs. Similarly, when the gopis made Vrndavana forest into their sankirtana arena and sang Krsna’s glories, Krsna hid nearby to listen, unable to resist the attraction of the gopis’ beautiful songs. In these examples Krsna is doubly invoked through sankirtana and by the devotion of His pure worshippers. Such is the power of Krsna’s pure devotees.

Visvanatha Cakravarti Thakura explains that because such great souls are so dear to Krsna they are directly empowered with quali-ties of Krsna (saksad-dharitvena) and are thus as worshipable and as powerful as He. Therefore, Srila Prabhupada says that these great devotees are honoured as the representatives of Krsna’s lotus feet by the names Prabhupada and Visnupada.

The dust of the lotus feet of such great devotees, like the dust of the lotus feet of Krsna, is saturated with transcendental opulences and is the source of all perfections; it is like cintamani, a wish-fulfilling gem, and transforms whatever it comes in contact with. In the words of Krsnadasa Kaviraja Gosvami, this dust is “very powerful” (maha-bala), a phrase significant enough that the Gosvami elsewhere uses it to describe the physical strength of Lord Balarama, as well as the mercy of Lord Caitanya. Unless human society accepts the dust of the lotus feet of these devotees, who have nothing to do with material attachment, it cannot turn its attention to the lotus feet of Sri Krsna, which can vanquish all the unwanted, miserable conditions of material life. Without this benediction, what hope is there for the deliverance of mankind? Sukadeva Gosvami says that the body of a person who has not at any time received the dust of the feet of a pure devotee upon its head is certainly a dead body.

But this wonderful dust is not reserved solely for the materialistically inclined. Practitioners and perfected devotees alike hanker for the dust of mahatmas’ feet as the most desirable ornament for their bodies and their topmost spiritual asset. Jada Bharata explained to King Rahugana that trying to realise the Absolute Truth by observing celibacy, strictly following the rules of household life, leaving home for the forest, accepting the renounced order, or undergoing strict penances, are all utterly futile unless one has smeared his entire body with the dust of the lotus feet of great devotees. Without attaining this mercy, perfection remains a far distant dream.

Narottama Dasa Thakura has therefore instructed that sadhakas and siddhas alike should consider the dust of the feet of the prede-cessor acaryas, especially of the six Gosvamis of Vrndavana, their very means of sustenance. When the self-realised devotee-demon Vrtrasura saw that his death was inevitable he prayed for the dust of the lotus feet (pada-rajah) of devotees who always think of Krsna (manasvinam), like Narada Muni, knowing that it is impossible to return to the spiritual world without the favour of a Vaisnava.

Scriptural evidence shows that great devotees are willing to accept even birth in very low species if by so doing they can get the dust of the feet of Krsna’s associates. The creator of the universe, Lord Brahma, is revered by everyone, even the Vraja-vasis, who think of themselves as ordinary cowherd men and women. The Vraja-vasis would never knowingly permit Brahma to take the dust from their feet. Nevertheless, he tried to overcome this obstacle and attain the “greatest possible good fortune” by praying to Krsna that he “may take any birth whatever in this forest of Gokula and have \[his\] head bathed by the dust falling from the lotus feet of any of its residents.” Uddhava had a similar aspiration: he took birth as the creepers nearby Kusuma-sarovara in order to be repeatedly blessed by the dust of the lotus feet of the gopis when they come there daily to pick flowers.

Having heard the glories of the dust of the lotus feet of pure devotees one may well conclude that this dust is just as powerful as the dust of the lotus feet of Sri Krsna. While such a conclusion is not incorrect, it does not reveal the whole truth: the dust from the lotus feet of pure devotees possesses benefits and unique transcendental qualities that even Krsna does not possess. It may therefore be said that the dust from devotees’ feet surpasses the dust from the lotus feet of Krsna.

**\* \* \* \***

The spiritual power of the dust of devotees’ feet to give perfection is reflected in the following pastime, told by Srila Prabhupada to exemplify the absolute selfless nature of pure devotional service, especially the devotional service of the gopis.

One day in Dvaraka, Krsna fell ill. All the physicians of the city were consulted, but they could find neither cause nor cure for the Lord’s ailment. Finally, Krsna Himself suggested a remedy: the dust of a devotee’s feet should be rubbed on His head. That would surely cure Him.

Many devotees were approached with Krsna’s request, but they all declined, even Narada Muni, and they all gave the same explanation for their reluctance. Krsna is the Supreme Personality of Godhead. To place the dust of one’s feet on Krsna’s head would be an offence of the highest order. Such a gesture would symbolise one’s attitude of supremacy over Krsna, since spiritual etiquette dictates that a devotee place his feet only on the head of a subordinate. This would be an offence for which the perpetrator would surely live forever in hell. “No! No! It is not possible!” came the answer, from one devotee after another.

When Krsna heard the results of their search He suggested that the gopis in Vrndavana be approached. A messenger was dispatched and, in Srila Prabhupada’s words, the following exchange transpired, demonstrating a very powerful truth regarding the gopis’ pure devotion:

**“The messenger said, ‘Krsna is sick. So He wanted the dust of feet of the devotee to be applied on His head. Nobody gave it. So we have come last to you.’ So immediately \[the gopis said\], ‘Yes! Yes! Take it! Take it!’ Immediately, so much dust was collected. And Krsna was cured. So this is gopis. Others thought that, ‘If I give the dust of my feet to Krsna, I may go to hell,’ but the gopis thought, ‘Let us go to hell, but let Krsna be cured.’ Therefore the gopis are the first-class devotee. They do not care for themselves.”**

The essential point to be gleaned from this pastime is that the nature of the gopis’ pure devotion is completely selfless. But the pastime also reveals that the dust of devotees’ feet has extraordinary powers, from which even Krsna can draw benefit. The two benefits Krsna enjoys are the purification of the material worlds, and the ability for Him to taste love of God - love for Himself. The first of these benefits is extolled in the proceeding verse, which also shows that Krsna takes the dust of devotees’ feet as a regular habit. Krsna says to Uddhava:

*nirapeksam munim santam nirvairam sama-darsanam*  
*anuvrajamy aham nityam puyeyety anghri-renubhih*

**“With the dust of My devotees’ lotus feet I desire to purify the material worlds, which are situated within Me. Thus, I always follow the footsteps of My pure devotees, who are free from all personal desire, rapt in thought of My pastimes, peaceful, without any feelings of enmity, and of equal disposition everywhere.”**

By His own admission, Krsna, the devotee of His devotees, always follows in the footsteps of His devotees so that the dust from their feet will touch His body. He does this because all the material universes are situated within Him, and by the power of that dust the conditioned souls residing in those universes are eligible for pure devotional service. Otherwise, without the dust of the lotus feet of the devotees, no one can engage in pure devotional service, let alone taste transcendental bliss.

Nonetheless, the second benefit that Krsna draws from this practice is still more confidential, and more wonderful.

As the object of love, Krsna tastes the bliss of being loved by His devotees. But the taste Krsna’s devotees enjoy through loving Him is a privilege obtainable only by His devotees’ grace. Because Krsna also wants to taste that bliss, He eagerly follows the rules of loving devotion and accepts the dust of His devotees’ feet.

This is the special feature of the dust of devotees’ feet: it bestows the quality of bliss available only in the abodes of love, the devotees themselves. That bliss is not merely different from the bliss that Krsna enjoys, it is much more intense. Sometimes Krsna is curious to taste the nectar of His lotus feet, for which His devotees hanker, and so while playing as a baby He places a toe of His own foot into His lotus mouth. But, once again, the pleasure Krsna is able to taste directly is neither the same as, nor as intense as, the pleasure He tastes indirectly by the grace of His devotees. This is the taste obtainable through the conduit of Vaisnava parampara (disciplic succession). For the reasons above we have said that the dust from Krsna’s lotus feet is not as powerful as the dust from the feet of His devotees.

Nevertheless, to allay any lingering doubt that such things are possible, let us consider the remnants of devotees’ food, glorified in the Caitanya-caritamrta with the epithet maha-bala, the same words used to describe the extraordinary power attributed to the dust of devotees’ feet.

In describing how the devotee Kalidasa attained perfection by eating the remnants of devotees’ food, Krsnadasa Kaviraja Gosvami writes:

*krsnera ucchista haya ‘maha-prasada’ nama*  
*‘bhakta-sesa’ haile ‘maha-maha-prasadakhyana’*

**“The remnants of food offered to Lord Krsna are called maha-prasada. After this same maha-prasada has been taken by a devotee, the remnants are elevated to maha-maha-prasada.”**

This verse makes it is clear that something that has come in contact with devotees may have greater spiritual power than something that has only come in contact with Krsna. At the end of this narration Krsnadasa Kaviraja insists that one “keep faith” in the great potency of the devotees’ food remnants and foot dust. He also gives a hint as to how such wonders are possible, and how these things can be so powerful: by “the greatest mercy of Lord Krsna.”

According to transcendental principles no one is equal to or greater than Krsna. This is one of Krsna’s qualities, called asama-urdhva. But by Krsna’s grace His devotees exhibit qualities that even He does not. Krsna manifests this sort of empowering grace out of love for His devotees because He wants them to be glorified even more than He is.

Srila Madhvacarya attests that Lord Krsna follows the footsteps of His devotees not only for the reasons the Lord gives in the verse, but also to bestow upon them His purifying potency. The acarya explains that as the Lord walks along behind His pure devotees, a wind blows the dust of the Lord’s feet in front of His devotees, who become purified by contact with that transcendental substance. Thus there is a sublimely intimate and inconceivable exchange of influences between the dust from the devotees’ feet and how it affects Krsna and the dust from Krsna’s feet and how it affects His devotees.

**\* \* \* \***

This fact, that the wonders of the dust of the pure devotees’ feet may very well excel those of Krsna’s foot dust, does not mean that the dust from the feet of all devotees has that same potency, because not all devotees have attained the same level of purity.

Let us again recall the story of Krsna’s headache. In that narration many pure devotees, Narada included, were not willing to alleviate Krsna’s suffering if it meant going to hell as a result. Srila Prabhupada emphasised that only the gopis accepted that risk in order to serve their Lord. This proves that even amongst pure devotees there are relative gradations of purity, and since the potency of the dust from devotees’ feet is proportional to the selfless purity of their devotion, there is certainly a gradation in the purifying power of that dust as well.

Both Sanatana Gosvami and Rupa Gosvami have delineated these different grades of devotees. However, before comparing great transcendentalists, we must make it clear that although one devotee may be considered superlative in relation to another, no devotee should be maligned or neglected. Furthermore, all devotees should be properly respected. Krsna makes this point to Narada Muni:

“O sage, each of the devotees you saw and heard about in your wanderings here and there, from Prayaga-tirtha to Dvaraka, is perfeet in all respects. Each of them can deliver the entire world, and each has truly received My mercy. There exist between them only degrees of perfection.”

Thus it is incumbent upon devotees to unreservedly worship all great Vaisnavas like Markandeya Rsi, Maharaja Ambarisa, and Srila Vyasadeva, amongst countless others. Failure to do so is a grave offence that no amount of service to Krsna can undo. Indeed, persons who worship the Lord but fail to worship His devotees are not even recognised as devotees, but are instead labeled dhambikah, “proud hypocrites.” In describing the path of developing loving devotion to the Lord, Krsna Himself tells Uddhava that worship of the devotees is “pre-eminent” (mad-bhakta-pujabhyadhika), surpassing all other methods. Therefore, dear Vaisnavas, always remember to worship the devotees along with their Lord.

Amongst pure devotees, Prahlada Maharaja is the best. After Lord Nrsimhadeva killed Hiranyakasipu, the Lord’s anger pacified, He sat Prahlada on His lap and affectionately placed His lotus hand upon the boy’s head. Because this favour was never shown to Brahma, Siva, or Laksmi, Prahlada Maharaja is considered superior. In the Skanda Purana Lord Siva confirms,

**“A devotee is one who understands Lord Krsna in truth. I am not amongst them. Amongst all Lord Krsna’s devotees Prahlada Maharaja is the best.”**

But even greater than Prahlada Maharaja are the Pandavas. Sri Krsna was intimately related to the Pandavas as the son of their maternal uncle, Vasudeva, and as such He was their most dear friend and constant well-wisher. Actually, Krsna is the supreme worshipable Lord, hut He acted as the Pandavas’ servant and sometimes as their spiritual master. Srila Sridhara Svami contrasts the relative positions of the Pandavas and Prahlada Maharaja:

**“The Supreme Personality of Godhead did not personally stay in Prahlada Maharaja’s home (although He stayed in the Pandavas’ home). The great sages did not travel to Prahlada’s home in order to see the Supreme Lord (although they did visit the Pandavas’ home for this purpose). The Supreme Lord did not become the intimate relative of Prahlada Maharaja (as He became the maternal cousin of the Pandavas). The Supreme Lord also did not personally express great pleasure in the daily activities of Prahlada Maharaja (as He did with the Pandavas). For all these reasons Narada said that the Pandavas were more fortunate than either himself or Prahlada.”**

But some members of the Yadu dynasty are still more exalted than the Pandavas. Because of their close family ties with Krsna, both in Mathura and Dvaraka, the Yadavas were in constant touch with the Lord. They could easily see Him at any time. Lord Krsna moved, talked, sat, rested, and dined with the Yadavas, and thus they may have appeared to be always engaged in nothing but ordinary worldly affairs with the Lord. But factually speaking, they were relieved from all material contamination, situated in transcendental existence. And as a result of this constant and intimate exchange, the Yadavas displayed states of absorption in Krsna not shown even by Prahlada Maharaja or the Pandavas. Sukadeva Gosvami says,

**“The Vrsnis \[Yadavas\] were so absorbed in Krsna consciousness that they forgot their own bodies while sleeping, sitting, walking, conversing, playing, bathing, and so on.”**

Furthermore, it is well known that amongst the Yadavas, Uddhava is the best. Uddhava was a great devotee from childhood who, like Krsna, was never troubled by the modes of nature. By the age of five he was so absorbed in service to the Deity of Krsna that when his mother called him for morning breakfast, he did not wish to have it. Krsna personally glorifies Uddhava’s exalted stature in the following famous verse:

*na tatha me priyatama atma-yonir na sankarah*  
*na ca sankarsano na srir naivatma ca yatha bhavan*

**“My dear Uddhava, neither Lord Brahma, Lord Siva, Lord Sankarsana, the goddess of fortune, nor indeed My own self are as dear to Me as you are.”**

Nonetheless, Uddhava begs to possess love for Krsna on the order of the sweetness of the gopis’ love for Krsna, and therefore the milkmaids of Vraja are even more exalted than Uddhava. The gopis’ all-absorbing love for Krsna is so deep, elevated, and spontaneous that even Lord Brahma’s six thousand years of austerities could not invoke it. Uddhava therefore concluded that the gopis were superior to any brahmana. And because they had given up the association of their husbands, sons, and other family members, forsaking the path of chastity just to take shelter of Krsna, Uddhava prayed to be blessed with the dust of the gopis’ lotus feet by taking his birth as a bush, creeper, or herb in Vrndavana.

Arjuna once asked Krsna three questions: In the three worlds, which devotees know Krsna most intimately, with whom is He always pleased, and for whom does He bear love without equal?

Krsna smiled at His dear friend, very pleased to reveal the truth of the matter, and replied, “O King! Neither Brahma, Siva, Laksmi, nor My own self, are as dear to Me as the gopis. How many devotees do I love in this world? Still, the gopis are dearer to Me than My own life-breath. O mighty Arjuna! Neither the great sages, yogis, nor demigods headed by Siva understand Me as well as do the gopis of Vraja. Neither by austerities, Vedic study, saintly conduct, nor knowledge am I conquered. I am conquered only by love. The gopis are proof of that. The gopis understand My glories, service to Me, faith in Me, and meditation upon Me. O son of Prtha! Others do not understand these things intimately. The gopis worship My body. No one else worships Me with more intimate love.”

Rupa Gosvami thus instructs us that after worshipping Lord Krsna devotees should serve the beautiful-eyebrowed girls of Vraja with offerings of prasada flowers and other things.

Finally, even amongst the gopis, some excel others in their love for Krsna. In their transcendental competition, Srimati Radharani is ever the victor and paragon. This conclusion is secretly sung in the pages of the Puranas and other Vedic literatures, but it is openly broadcast throughout the works of Jayadeva Gosvami and acaryas like Srila Rupa GosvamI.

For instance, the Padma Purana says:

*yatha radha priya visnos tasyah kundam priyam tatha*  
*sarva-gopisu saivaika visnor atyanta-vallabha*

**“Just as Srimati Radharani is most dear to Sri Krsna, Her bathing place known as Radha-kunda is also dear to Him. Among all the gopis, Srimati Radharani is supermost and very dear to Lord Krsna.”**

Srila Rupa GosvamI explains that amongst the gopis of Vrndavana, Sri Radha and Candravali are considered chief. However, when we compare the two, Radha is supreme because She manifests the highest ecstasy. The loving ecstasy of the other gopis cannot compare to Hers.

Krsna concludes His conversation with Arjuna in the Adi Purana by saying, “O son of Prtha! In the three worlds the earth planet, where the town of Vrndavana is situated, is most fortunate. There the gopis stay and there My beloved, who is named Radha, stays.”

**\* \* \* \***

Coming back to our discussion of Airavata-kunda, we now have the necessary perspective to explain how the Ganga water Airavata brought to fill the kunda could have been further purified.

Indra had ordered his carrier elephant to bring the purest water for bathing Krsna. In the mind of most demigods, such water came from the celestial Ganges flowing near their residences. Whatever water was leftover from the abhiseka remained in Airavata-kunda and became another feature of Krsna’s Govardhana playground.

Thereafter, Krsna would regularly herd His cows in the shade of Kadambavana and play there with His friends. When winds or rainwater carried the dust of Krsna’s lotus feet to Airavata-kunda, the Ganges water there became further purified. And when Krsna bathed in that lake its waters became as sacred as the abhiseka water that flowed into Manasa-ganga.

Of course, the dust from Krsna’s feet is not the only auspicious dust mixed with the soil of Kadambavana. Krsna’s cows, the cowherd boys, and the gopis contribute to this area’s dust a variety of spiritual flavours, dust the touch of which further elevates the purity of Airavata-kunda. This means that the water of Airavata-kunda, like the water of many lakes in Vraja, is liquid cintamani. Whether bathed in, sprinkled on the head, or tasted by drinking, its touch can impart whatever spiritual perfection a pure-hearted pilgrim desires.

Waters sanctified by the dust of the Vraja-vasis’ feet are empow-ered to bestow whatever service devotees aspire for in Krsna’s abode, whether it be in the mood of the gopas or the gopis. If the birds and other animals that drink these waters simply to quench their thirst attain all transcendental virtues, what can sincere devotees, who desire love for Krsna and His associates, not attain?

Material piety, good progeny, freedom from disease, freedom from sinful reaction, elevation to heavenly planets, and even liberation are merely incidental benefits derived from Vraja’s waters. As drunkards are interested only in the taste of liquor and its intoxicating effects, caring little for its ability to ease the discomforts of cold weather, devotees with a taste for loving devotion desire nothing more than that taste from Vraja’s waters, paying no mind to its myriad auspicious side-effects. They accept these incidental benefits only if they facilitate their quest for prema.

The Yamuna gives Vaisnavas all protection and blesses them with the most confidential kind of pure devotion. To devotees with suitable spiritual credentials, Dana-nivartana-kunda reveals Krsna’s toll-taking pastimes on Govardhana Hill. To qualified devotees who pray for such things, Syama-kunda grants residence by its shores and spiritual attraction to the divine couple. By residing at and serving Radha-kunda, devotees can get the opportunity to become an eternal assistant of Srimati RadharanI under the guidance of the gopis, particularly a manjari. In fact, even Krsna, when all efforts to win Radha’s favour fail, resorts at last to the shelter of Radha-kunda, as Raghunatha Dasa Gosvami explains:

*agha-ripur api yatnad atra devyah prasada-*  
*prasara-krta-kataksa-prapti-kamah prakamam*  
*anusarati yad ucaaih snana-sevanubandhais*  
*tad ati-surabhi radha-kundam evasrayo me*

**“May very dear and fragrant Radha-kunda, where Krsna, the enemy of Agha, yearning to attain the merciful sidelong glance of Sri Radha, eagerly and diligently bathes and renders service, be my shelter.”**

This verse refers to the following pastime. Once, Radha and Krsna were conversing on the bank of Radha-kunda when, for some mysterious reason known only to Her, Radha wrongly suspected that Krsna had been unfaithful. Krsna had, in fact, met Syamala on the way to Radha-kunda, hut Candravali’s friend could not distract Him. Actually, He had rushed to meet RadharanI. Despite His innocence, prema inspired Radha to think that Krsna had cheated on Her, and so She abruptly went to another part of Her lake, leaving Krsna alone.

Sri Radha’s friends tried to reassure Her of Krsna’s fidelity, hut She turned a deaf ear to them. And when Krsna, in spite of His innocence, came to apologise, She covered Her face with Her veil and insisted that He leave the grove.

Beside himself with sorrow, Krsna sat down in a remote place by the lake and stared blankly over its tranquil waters. Because even the aquatics, birds, and animals of the area were silently lamenting over this lovers’ quarrel, the only sound that could be heard was Radha’s occasional and insistent, “No!”

As Krsna watched the waves of Radha-kunda, He got an idea. He thought, “If this lake is pleased with me, then Radha will also be pleased.” Krsna, who personally established the rules of worshipping holy lakes and rivers, now followed those procedures Himself. First He offered respects to Radha-kunda and then reverentially bathed in its waters. Thereafter He decorated His body with tilaka marks made from its mud, dressed in dry clothes, and collected many items in preparation for worship. One by one, Krsna offered incense, flowers, and fruit. Finally, lying prostrate on the dry stairs of a ghata, He prayed, “O dear lake! By your grace, may your presiding goddess grant me Her merciful glance.”

As Krsna solemnly observed these acts of sacrament, a gopi dis-closed His activities to Radha. Peering through the foliage of Her private grove, Radha watched as Krsna worshipped Her lake, and Her eyes soon welled with tears. Those tears washed away the black mascara on Radha’s eyes and flushed out Her stubborn anger as well. Thus, by the grace of Radha-kunda, Krsna was able to pacify Radharani and regain Her audience, things that even influential gopis like Lalita had been unable to do.

Just as no expansion of Narayana is equal to Krsna, no names of Narayana are equal to Krsna’s names; and just as no devotee of Narayana is equal to a devotee of Krsna, no waters equal the waters of Vraja’s lakes, kundas, and rivers. These waters are the liquid form of spontaneous love for Krsna and they have attained their superla-tive qualities through contact with the lotus feet of Krsna and the lotus feet of His associates.

Dear Vaisnavas! Radha-Syamasundara, Their associates, Their servants, and other advanced devotees tread throughout this dhama, sanctifying it with the dust from their lotus feet, and that hallowed substance inevitably reaches the lakes and rivers of New Vraja-dhama. Therefore, dear readers, respect the waters of this holy place just as Krsna respects the waters of His dear Radha’s own lake, and you will also receive the merciful sidelong glance of recognition from the queen of New Vraja-dhama, beyond which there can be no higher blessing.

## Appendix 24

**Tava Kathamrtam**
