*(This appendix follows from the pastime of Uddhava’s narrating Srimad-Bhagavatam at Kusuma-sarovara in chapter fourteen and elaborates on one of the most well-known verses spoken by the gopis.)*

Krsna’s queens surrounded Uddhava like a garland of golden lotus flowers. They sat in rapt attention as the illuminating sun of the Bhagavatam rose upon his tongue. Completely forgetting food, drink, and other bodily demands, Uddhava and the queens were immersed in the nectar of hari-katha and knew nothing else.

Two weeks into his narration, Uddhava finally began to speak about Krsna’s Vrndavana pastimes. At this point the queens had heard about the Pandavas, the primary and secondary processes of creation, and the entanglement that results from fruitive activities.

The queens were enthralled by the pastimes of Lord Kapila, Lord Varaha, Lord Nrsimha, and Sri Rama, all of whom Uddhava had glorified as incarnations of Krsna. The damsels of Dvaraka had been fascinated by Uddhava’s description of the vast cosmos, had revelled in the innumerable glories of the holy name, and had delighted in the great fortune of the elephant Gajendra, who had become an object of Lord Narayana’s mercy.

To Uddhava’s amazement, his audience had relished every topic, every verse, indeed, every word of the Bhagavatam. Whatever he had described, the queens of Dvaraka had considered to be the glory of Krsna, their beloved. By the strength of their incomparable love, they delighted equally in how Krsna creates universes, protects His devotees, kills demons, and sports in Vrndavana. These were, after all, Krsna’s pastimes, albeit in different forms.

Knowledge of the Lord’s innumerable opulences never caused the queens’ ecstatic love to wane or falter. Rather, the Dvaraka-devis’ love for Krsna simply grew more and more. They thought, “How wonderful is our Lord.” Krsna’s grandeur, like camphor submerged in sweet rice, sank into the ocean of the queens’ love, adding unmistakable taste and fragrance to their ever-sweet affection.

The effects of living in Mathura, of now residing in Vrndavana, and of hearing Uddhava’s teachings on vraja-bhakti had transformed the queens’ love to a quality closer to that of the gopis. The queens’ affection for Krsna was originally mixed with awe of the Lord’s divinity as well as selfish desires for pleasure, but their affection became more spontaneous and more selfless by association with Uddhava and by the purifying effects of the dhama. Therefore the queens’ love was now similar to, although not identical with, the love of the gopis. The constitutional identity of the queens had not changed; they were still the wedded consorts of Dvaraka’s Lord. As such, their love for Krsna would always differ from the gopis’ paramour affection for the cowherd prince of Vraja. However, these influences temporarily altered the mood of the queens so that it resembled gopi-bhava. In other words, as Uddhava spoke the Bhagavatam, the queens identified with the gopis and felt their mood.

Thus when Uddhava described Krsna’s pastimes with the gopis, high waves arose in the ocean of prema, which ebbed and flowed throughout the audience. And as those waves crashed against the shores of the queens’ hearts, tears flowed from their eyes and drenched their beautiful cheeks.

Intently following the path of Uddhava’s recitation, the queens relived every moment of Sarat’s full moon night: the irresistible call of the flute, the frenzied rush into the dark of night, the pain of Krsna’s initial indifference, the joy of His eventual embrace and the pleasure of youthful love, the torture of feminine pride, the desolation of separation, the desperate search in the forest, and the lament by the banks of the Yamuna.

When Uddhava repeated the very words the gopis sang in separa-tion from their beloved, the queens were struck with wonder. How could he have known the gopis’ secrets? Like the gopis, the queens found themselves beneath the autumn moon, sheltered only by their love for Krsna. Like the gopis, they found themselves clapping hands, singing lamentably, and hoping that Krsna would appear in response to their prayers.

In a voice like the king of cuckoos, Uddhava sang:

*tava kathamrtam tapta-jivanam*  
*kavibhir iditam kalmasapaham*  
*sravana-mangalam srimad-atatam*  
*bhuvi grnanti ye bhuri-da janah*

**“My Lord, the nectar of Your words and the descriptions of Your activities are the life and soul of those who are always aggrieved in this material world. These narrations are transmitted by exalted personalities, and they eradicate all sinful reactions. Whoever hears these narrations attains all good fortune. These narrations are broadcast all over the world and are filled with spiritual power. Those who spread the message of Godhead are certainly the most munificent welfare workers.’’**

Prolonged hearing of the Bhagavatam had completely absorbed the queens’ minds in Krsna’s pastimes. So much so that the entire scene of the gopis’ doleful kirtana, saturated with feelings of separation, now appeared before the assembled audience.

There they were, the damsels of Vraja, seated together on the bank of the Kalindi, singing one beautiful song after the other, deeply meditating on Krsna and eagerly hoping that He would return to them. As invisible onlookers, the queens first took in the beauty of Vrndavana, surpassed only by the exquisite beauty of the gopis. The queens also saw Krsna, hiding sometimes behind a bush, sometimes in the branches of a tree. He was eager to hear every word the gopis spoke and to see every loving expression on their faces.

So powerful was the queens’ response to the words of the Bhagavatam that Uddhava and Vajra could no longer remain impartial spectators. As driftwood is bandied about upon strong ocean waves, the two men were carried away by the tidal waves of the queens’ ecstasy and thus they saw and heard everything just as the queens did.

Upon hearing the gopis repeat the verse beginning with tava Kathamrtam, Krsna desired to explore the inner meaning of their words and hence He could no longer remain hidden. Krsna appeared amongst the gopis, conversing with them at times and remaining silent at others. To overhear their secrets, He sometimes hid Himself, but at other times He sat amidst the gopis, and like a symphony conductor, directed a concert of loving exchanges. Dressed in shimmering clothes and nicely ornamented with pearls and forest flowers, Krsna was the collyrium around the gopis’ eyes, the flower ornaments on their ears, the pearl necklaces upon their hearts.

To the gopis, Krsna was the physical form of their words. He was an actor in a play and their speech was His director. Because they were intoxicated by loving devotion born of separation from Krsna, they did not really believe that He was in their midst. When they saw Krsna or heard His voice, they thought He was merely a vision (sphurti). The form they were seeing was nothing more than the product of their great desire to be with Him again.

To Uddhava, Vajra, and the queens, who had been temporarily blessed with feelings of gopi-bhava, Krsna was standing there before them. However, unlike the gopis, who were oblivious to the presence of anyone but themselves, Vajra and the queens were fully aware of Krsna’s presence and of His sublime conversation with the gopis.

Krsna’s disembodied voice echoed within the hearts of the gopis as He questioned them. “O gopikas! How do you continue to maintain your bodies in My absence? How do you live?”

One gopi replied, “Dearly beloved, know for certain that we live only by hearing narrations of Your pastimes and glories. Our direct experience, full of love for You, is evidence in itself that we sustain ourselves only by hari-katha

Again Krsna spoke from within the hearts of the gopis. “How is it possible to live simply by hearing words spoken about someone else?” Another gopi responded, “It is possible when those words are like nectar. Just as the devas become immortal by drinking heavenly nectar, we live by drinking the nectar of hari-katha.”

“So, this katha is like heavenly nectar?” Krsna enquired.

“Oh, no! No! No!” exclaimed the first gopi.

Waving her right hand in protest, the second gopi added, “Don’t confuse hari-kathamrtam with ordinary nectar.”

Krsna’s silence indicated to the gopis that He was puzzled by their explanation. A third beautiful maiden therefore said, “There are two types of worldly ambrosia: the nectar of the demigods and the nectar of liberation.” She paused briefly and then continued, “But neither of these can be equated with the elixir of topics about You. Compared to that divine substance, all other types of nectar are wholly insignificant.”

Aware of a question that had arisen in Vajra’s mind, Krsna placed it before the love-inebriated gopis, “Please be specific. How do these three types of nectar differ?”

The third gopi opened her eyes wide and exuberantly replied, “Oh, there is a great deal of difference! Hari-kathamrtam purifies one of all fault and contamination and frees one of every sin. It even eradicates sinful reactions that have already begun to bear their bitter, rotten fruit.

“As for heavenly nectar, it merely agitates the heart, increases lust, and induces one to sin again. Even the nectar of liberation, attractive as it may be for jnanis, fails to destroy ignorance, the root cause of future sinful reactions. Thus both forms of worldly nectar are inferior to the sublime nectar of hari-katha."

That beautiful, dark-eyed gopi looked to her friends for support, which they readily gave in nods, gestures, and affirmations of, “It’s true.”

But must hari-katha come from the mouths of the gopis to be fully potent? In order to address this question, Krsna asked, “I have full faith that the hari-katha spoken by the gopis eclipses the potency of every kind of nectar, simply by the unparalleled force of the Vraja gopis’ love. But is the hari-katha chanted by other, lesser beings just as powerful?”

A gopi who had not yet spoken joined her palms in a gesture of humility and said, “Syama! Have no doubt. Topics about You, spoken by any sincere soul, surpass all other kinds of nectar.”

Eager to hear more, Krsna requested, “Please, tell Me in detail the effects of this nectar.”

That same humble gopi replied, "Hari-kathamrtam, the greatest friend of all beings, relieves the distress of the souls in this world. Hari! If You were to ask, ‘Of which distressed souls do you speak?’ we would reply, ‘We speak of the souls who suffer from the pangs of repeated birth and death, and those afflicted by the pain of being separated from You.’”

The mere mention of the word “separated” caused that gopi great anxiety. Indeed, her voice faltered and her eyes welled with tears. To mitigate her sorrow, Krsna enquired only about the first category of distressed souls. He said, “I know of the latter category, just tell Me about those souls who suffer under the dominion of Mother Durga.”

The gopi replied, “Tormented by disease, drought, and famine, people of this world cry for relief only to receive further punishment. But hari-katha destroys disease, hunger, and thirst. It gives strength, nourishment, and satisfaction. And unlike other medicine, it bestows the sweetest possible taste of divine love.”

Krsna was about to speak when that same gopi continued, “Not only do the topics related to You cure the disease of the distressed, but they also act as preventative medicine, which protects the hearer from the future effects of both material suffering and the pangs of separation from You.”

Again, just as Hari opened His lotus mouth to speak, the gopi continued, “And krsna-katha gives further protection by simultaneously pacifying the heart and eradicating selfish desires, which are the root cause of fruitive work and karmic bondage.” Clapping her hands in appreciation of the very idea she had just expressed, she concluded by exclaiming, “How wonderful!”

A demure gopi softly added, “But the nectar of heaven simply intoxicates, inspires pride, impels one to struggle for material gain, and in the end results in material entanglement.”

Hari looked at Uddhava and Vajra. Uddhava was happily absorbed in hearing directly from the gopis, and Vajra was astounded at the glories of hari-kathamrtam.

Krsna wanted to increase the queens’ attachment to Him by amplifying their appreciation for hari-katha, so He continued to enquire from the gopis, “Devis! If there is anything else to be known about the soothing effects of this divine nectar, please tell Me.”

A wide-eyed gopi dressed in pink said, “The additional effects of these talks come both before and after the effects we have already described.”

The gopis turned to face this new speaker as she continued, “There are always obstacles to receiving something valuable, especially if that something is pure love for You. Those obstacles, born of either sinful or pious deeds, are all destroyed by hari-kathamrtam. This is the prior effect.

“Then there is the after effect. If one is found to be discussing topics of Hari, that great fortune is the result of untold piety, but those discussions are also seeds that blossom into the unlimited fruits of future hearing.” After a pause, she continued, “In other words, hari-kathamrtam is complete in itself. It is its own means and its own end. It alone creates the qualifications necessary to enjoy it. Moreover, it simultaneously destroys samsara, the cycle of birth and death. What more can I say?”

Another gopi felt that there was more to say and interjected, “The fact that hari-katha destroys the root cause of material existence is constantly glorified by spiritual authorities, although this is but a secondary effect. Long ago, Dhruva Maharaja said:

*ya nirvrtis tanu-bhrtam tava pada-padma-*  
*dhyanad bhavaj-jana-katha-sravanena va syat*  
*sa brahmani sva-mahimany api natha ma bhut*  
*kim tv antakasi-lulitat patatam vimanat*

**‘“My Lord, the transcendental bliss derived from meditating upon Your lotus feet or hearing about Your glories from pure devotees is so unlimited that it is far beyond the stage of brahmananda, wherein one thinks himself merged in the impersonal Brahman as one with the Supreme. Since brahmananda is also defeated by the transcendental bliss derived from devotional service, then what to speak of the temporary blissfulness of elevating oneself to the heavenly planets, which is ended by the separating sword of time? Although one may be elevated to the heavenly planets, he falls down in due course of time.’**

**“In spiritual matters, the opinion of liberated souls like Dhruva is the ultimate source of knowledge and the final authority. And Dhruva also praises the power of hearing Your glories. Both heavenly nectar and impersonal Brahman realization are scorned by poets and scholars who know the Absolute Truth. But those same self-realized scholars praise hari-kathamrtam. Thus it is superior to the amrta of the gods.”**

Within their hearts, the gopis heard a voice ask, “Is Dhruva the only authority who agrees with you?”

To this, a blissful gopi mentally replied, “Brahma, Siva, the four Sanas, Vaisnava acaryas, and great atmaramas all share our experience and agree with our conclusions.”

That reply echoed within the hearts of everyone present, includ-ing Vajra and the queens, who were now even more astonished. Never before had the king encountered a discourse in which the ideas of the participants were communicated not just verbally, but mentally and spiritually as well. Never before had he heard such deep, realised analysis of profound spiritual topics. Never before had he been in such august company: the Lord, the Lord’s associates, and the Lord’s dear servant. Within his heart, Vajra thanked his father, grandfather, and Maharaja Yudhisthira, by whose grace he was now witnessing the Bhagavatam come to life.

Within his mind, Vajra enquired of the Lord, “When the kavis hear and chant Your glories do they really perceive what the gopis perceive?” And Krsna, within the hearts of both the gopis and their audience, repeated the king’s question, to which one gopi replied, “The hearers and speakers of hari-katha realise its secrets in proportion to the degree of prema they possess. Although great poets may not know all the secrets of hari-katha, in time they will receive the same benefit that the gopis receive from their absorption in Krsna’s glories and pastimes.”

Vajra was shocked! Everyone receives the same benefit! How was that possible? That gopi automatically clarified, “Such things are possible by the mercy of hari-katha.”

Now even the great bhagavata scholar, Uddhava, was amazed. He silently resolved, “Surely hari-katha is praised by poets as allauspicious because of all the benefits you have already mentioned, but especially the last one! I only thought I had understood the glories of hari-katha, but now I truly do.”

Again Krsna transmitted the message, and an eager gopi responded, “Only the secondary effects of hari-kathamrtam have been praised thus far. Its full influence has yet to be revealed by us.”

Krsna smiled, His eyes sparkling with pride at the eloquence of His beloveds. Even the Vedas were only able to understand the mysteries hidden within their own pages by worshipping the dust of the gopis’ feet. By that same dust, Uddhava was now relishing those secrets directly from the lips of the gopis. Such is the benefit of living in Vrndavana.

Krsna raised His eyebrows as if to say, “Uddhava! Just wait! Hear what else they have to say.” Then Krsna openly enquired, “What is the primary effect of hari-katha? Please let us know.”

For a moment the gopis were silent, but they appeared to be conversing amongst themselves in a language incomprehensible to men. The queens however, who were women of like minds, could intuit the gopis' mood and they stirred silently in their seats. With great feeling a grave and sober gopi spoke up, at long last, “Simply by being heard, topics of Hari bestow all rewards - up to and including the ultimate reward, prema-bhakti, pure ecstatic love for Krsna. The word srimat indicates that such topics are saturated with every possible excellence. In short, they are the ultimate means and the ultimate end. It is for that reason they are praised by poets, saints, and devas alike.”

Prema, the goal of introspective yogis and elevated Vaisnavas is readily accessible to those who hear hari-katha with even the least bit of effort, not to mention those great souls who liberally distribute that divine treasure.

To that end, Krsna enquired, “Tell Me of those who distribute the nectar of hari-katha. Who are they? What are their qualifications? When and where do they speak?”

A leader of gopis, Her breath like camphor, Her eyes like sapphires, replied: “The qualification Your devotees possess, which allows them to distribute the nectar of hari-katha, is their liberal disposition. By their very nature they are compelled to dispense this nectar everywhere - from Patala to Goloka. And not only are these ambrosial talks heard everywhere, their divine dispensation goes on without end.”

In her enthusiasm, another gopi spoke up, “Such munificent souls are the greatest beneficiaries of mankind, for they bestow life to those nearly dead from the torments of maya.”

Glancing lovingly at Her impetuous friend, the foremost gopi continued, “O Hari! You asked how such souls become agents in the service of hari-katha. Innumerable pious acts, performed in innumerable lives, are the only means to acquire such power of attorney. These foremost of Your servants - even if offered everything, includ-ing one’s very life and soul - can never be repaid for the love they give, for that gift is impossible for anyone else to bestow.”

Once again Her impulsive friend interjected, “Hari-kathamritam can appear in either intimate dialogue, or in assemblies of the masses. Whenever and wherever it is heard, it refreshes like moonshine, tastes like nectar, and soothes the heart as only love can. That is to say, talks about You are not different from You.”

The gopis giggled to hear their overeager friend. In the midst of that jovial mood, Krsna enquired, “O gopis! How does the nectar of hari-katha affect you?”

The leader of the gopis answered, “When we are burning in the slow fire of separation, hearing Your glories gives us reason to live. In fact, it gives us life itself. Persons who hear Your glories are just as fortunate as those who describe those glories.”

The interest Krsna now showed in the gopis’ feelings induced them to recall the unhappiness they felt whenever He was absent. As Krsna beheld His beloveds, the leader of the gopis blushed, quivered, and perspired. The other gopis displayed similar transcendental ecstasies.

In that state, that golden-complexioned damsel of Vraja, Her lips quivering, Her voice unsteady, said, “Yes! Hari-kirtana, songs about You, are sweet only if accompanied by Your darsana. Otherwise they are bitter. Without Your presence, even hari-katha is like a desert full of parched earth, cacti, and scorpions.”

Eyes moist, eyelashes aflutter, She begged, “Therefore we pray, please show Yourself to us, just one more time.”

Vajra wondered, “Why did Krsna light the fire of the gopis’ anguish? Was it intentional or accidental?” Whatever the case may have been, as they recalled the pain of separation, the dry tinder of the gopis’ hearts at once burst into flame.

Krsna said, “My dear gopis! Now that we are together, let Me feed you this nectar of topics about Me.”

To rescue Her gopi friends from the forest fire of misery, the eldest amongst them endeavoured to refute the praise of hari-katha that they had all just spoken, thus surrounding them with the fireproof fortress of indifference.

“No!” snapped that leading gopi.

Not only Krsna was taken aback, the entire assembly was shocked by Her emphatic response. The queens shifted uncomfortably. They would never have dared to speak to their husband in that way. Krsna protested, “But you yourselves declared that there could be no better way to spend our time than in hearing about Me. Why not share a few moments together in such bliss?”

The gopis contemplated Krsna’s proposal, and with one mind they reacted in fear of its certain consequences. Like stalks of wheat swaying in the wind, each gopi veiled her face and turned away from Krsna. They appeared to be rejecting all their previous glorifications of hari-kathamrtam.

The mere thought of hari-katha without Krsna’s company caused the gopis intolerable pain. But that pain was only apparent. In truth it was ecstasy, and addictive as it was, it caused the gopis to now present a very different perspective of hari-katha, one that censured its speakers and condemned its effects. Their view, while disguised as denunciation, was actually a type of glorification. And even in Krsna’s presence it caused the pain of love to become even more intense.

The gopis’ hearts oscillated between happiness and pain, misery and ecstasy. But whatever their condition, they were incapable of restraining themselves from glorifying Hari and hari-katha. Such are the inconceivable ways of love in separation, as displayed by the gopis.

Who can understand the wonders of the gopis’ love for Krsna? Vajra, the saintly king and devotee, tried to understand; Uddhava, the authority on vraja-rasa, understood; the queens of Dvaraka, as expansions of the gopis, understood by tasting its shadow; and Krsna, the object and cause of that love, understood more deeply than anyone else, save and except the gopis themselves.

To further protect the assembly of cowherd girls, that senior gopi continued with harsh words: “We do not desire to hear hari-katha, for such hearing will surely be our death.’’

Krsna innocently enquired, “How can hari-katha, mere words, cause Your death? Surely You exaggerate.”

With a flash of Her bluish eyes, the gopi replied, “Because of the desire to avoid feelings of separation from You, we hear the nectar of Your pastimes and as a result become infected by a horrible disease. That disease is hari-katha itself, and it is so contagious that even discussing our feelings of separation from You brings it on.”

Krsna was stunned. He could say nothing, but the gopi continued, “Would You like to know its symptoms?”

Taking Krsna’s silence to be consent, the gopi carried on, “That diseased nectar causes a fever in our bodies, which makes our life airs sputter and burn like drops of water in boiling oil.”

Uddhava and Vajra, who revered the gopis as their gurus, could not protest. Krsna was thus obliged to come to His own defence, “You say topics about Me are the cause of death. But then why would those same topics be praised by poets in the way your friends have already described?”

**Gopi**: “It is the natural tendency of poets to speak flowery words, whether they are true or not. What harm does it do them?”  
**Krsna**: “But the glorification of My pastimes is not found in ordinary  
books, but in the Puranas, which are holy scriptures.”  
**Gopi**: “Vyasa is also a poet. He is forced to act helplessly according to his nature. Let him say that hari-kathamrtam extinguishes sinful reaction if that makes him happy. Perhaps it does for some, but for us it means certain death.”  
**Krsna**: “If that nectar destroys the sins of others, you must admit it is auspicious.”  
**Gopi**: “Auspicious? Maybe for some, but not for all.”  
**Krsna**: “How is that?”  
**Gopi**: “If one simply hears it, there may be some benefit. But if one fully realises its true substance, she will suffer misery and ultimately — death. ”  
**Krsna**: “Why do you differentiate between hearers of hari-katha?" Gopi: “Not all people hear in the same way. And because people hear differently, they feel different results. Should I explain?”

Krsna nodded.

“When ordinary people listen, they give but cursory attention. For them the effect is auspicious and the narration continues without end. However, those who truly hear hari-katha, investing in it their hearts and souls, know its dangers. Because these people can sense that it is inauspicious, they refuse to hear it. Thus their situation becomes doubly inauspicious.”

**Krsna**: “Why doubly?”  
**Gopi**: “First, hari-kathamrtam brings disease and death. Then, when the nectar stops, even more inauspiciousness comes.”

Krsna shook His head in disbelief. He had to decipher the logic of the gopi's words. At first She said that hari-kathamrtam is inauspicious because it is poison, but then She said it was doubly inauspicious when that poison is stopped. Govinda was used to hearing the convoluted arguments of philosophers, but what He had just heard was incomprehensible.

Krsna continued, “Great devotees go everywhere to speak hari-katha and thereby do the greatest good. How can You fault them?”

“Great devotees, ha!” She huffed. “Intoxicated by beauty, wealth, and pride, kings and professionals wander everywhere like a scourge, destroying life wherever they go.

“Go ahead! Say that poets praise the potency of Your pastimes to destroy sin. But whose sin? Those proud, intoxicated rascals — ”

“Please!” Krsna interjected. “Hari-katha is spoken by great men —"

“No!” the gopi interrupted. “They are evil men. Blinded by wealth they think that the whole world should die, and for that end they travel at great expense from country to country. Behaving like peaceful, gentle souls, they commit genocide and pave their own way to Yama’s kingdom. How can they be called great?”

That gopi paused for a breath of air. She could sense the electrified atmosphere around Her. Gazing at the beautiful princesses of Vraja, Her voice quivering with emotion, She continued. “Thus, even more than they would avoid a contagious disease, the intelligent should stay far away from hari-katha of any sort. As My girlfriend once said:

*yad-anucarita-lila-karna-piyusa-viprut-*  
*sakrd-adana-vidhuta-dvandva-dharma vinastah*  
*sapadi grha-kutumbam dinam utsrjya dina*  
*bahava iha vihanga bhiksu-caryam caranti*

**“To hear about the pastimes that Krsna regularly performs is nectar for the ears. For those who relish just a single drop of that nectar, even once, their dedication to material duality is ruined. Many such persons have suddenly given up their wretched homes and families and, themselves becoming wretched, travelled here to Vrndavana to wander about like birds, begging for their living.”**

Krsna had opened the sacred treasury of the gopis’ hearts to examine the contents there, but He was taken aback by what He had found. Being soft-hearted by nature, Krsna felt the gopis’ pain as His own. His eyes were brimming with tears as He softly said, “Devi! What You say is very painful to hear.”

That foremost amongst the gopis replied, “Syama! This discussion is caused by supreme pain, and it is the cause of supreme pain. When that pain reaches its summit, our mortal frames will no longer be able to bear the agony. Giving up these bodies of twenty-four elements, we shall ascend to the world of spirit, and there engage in Your service eternally.”

Incredulous, Krsna asked, “Please! How can it be so?”

She answered, “Only out of a desire to serve You were we deter-mined to live. Hearing about You has increased our desire to serve You. If You do not stay with us, the pain of Your absence, combined with the pain of our frustrated desires, will be altogether too difficult to bear. We live only for You, and this hari-kathamrtam was the last straw. We can take no more.”

Seeing how the narrations of His nectarean pastimes had intensi-fied the gopis' plight, Krsna finally acquiesced. They were right. For everyone else hari-katha was nectar, but for the gopis it meant certain death. It was better that they never hear of Him.

As the full weight of this dismal conclusion came to rest on Govinda’s already heavy heart, another young gopi blurted out, “Anyway, we have died long, long ago. What is life or death for those already dead?”

Krsna was afraid to hear the answer to His next question, but He enquired anyway, “Whatever do you mean?”

“Again and again,” the gopi replied, “we have been cheated out of hari-kathamrtam by the pious souls who drink it unceasingly. Day and night they hear about You, leaving us without even a drop of nectar. In the absence of such life-giving succour, in the absence of Your company, out of fear of losing Your darsana, we abandoned our bodies long ago. What You see before You are but ghosts lingering in our old bodies, hoping to taste a final drop of nectar. That is all.” Krsna was speechless. His lotus mouth hung open. He was stunned by the gopis’ transcendental madness. And Uddhava, Vajra, and the queens were almost as disturbed. The only difference was that they were merely spectators, whereas Krsna was the very object of the gopis eternal, and eternally confounding, quest for love.

For the gopis, happiness and distress, life and death, all merged with their love for Hari. Whether physically with Him or without Him, by hari-kathamrtam they were always with Him. And so too were His innumerable companions and devotees.

But by the gopis’ own testimony, hari-katha was better in Krsna’s presence than it was in His absence. The taste of hari-kathamrtam in the company of Krsna was honey-sweet, but the taste without His company was bittersweet. Then again, each scenario had its advantages and disadvantages. The disadvantage of the former was in the constant awareness of Krsna’s imminent departure; the advantage of the latter was the anticipation of His future appearance.

This discussion on hari-kathamrtam would go on for a long time, although Krsna and the gopis had delved into this subject many times before. The conclusion of these talks would vary according to the mood of the gopis at the time, while Krsna was a puppet dancing to the dynamic dictates of their love.

One thing was certain: neither the gopis nor Krsna could ever give up hari-katha. It was too nectarean.

**\* \* \* \***

And so, verse by verse, Uddhava recited Srimad-Bhagavatam for the pleasure of Krsna, the queens, and the king. Vajranabha sat spellbound, enchanted by the gopis’ expla-nations of hari-katha. Krsna, the son of Maharaja Nanda, occasionally appeared, attentive to the narration and keen to converse with the gopis. The creatures of Vrndavana, whether native to land, air, or water, were part of the eager audience, and no less so were the trees, bushes, creepers, and flowers.

In the same way, the sun, moon, sky, and air bore witness to Uddhava’s recitation of Srimad-Bhagavatam on the banks of Kusuma-sarovara. Scripture and oral tradition both say that this narration occurred for the benefit of the queens. By the taste of this hari-kathamrtam they were liberated from this world and returned to Krsna’s aprakata-lila.

Moreover, Vaisnavas know that the liberation of the queens is part of Lord Krsna’s lila. The Dvaraka-devis are His eternal associates, upon whom even the goddess of liberation waits hand and foot. To glorify the greatness of Srimad-Bhagavatam and testify to the importance of hari-katha, the wives of Krsna set the highest example for all to follow.

The queens’ transition from prakata-lila to aprakata-lila was instantaneous, not unlike the liberation of Narada Muni. Fully absorbed in thoughts of Krsna, free from mundane attachments, they exited one realm and instantly entered the other, as lightning and illumination occur simultaneously.

At the end of the month, his recitation complete, Uddhava bade farewell to Vajra and the queens, and once again he assumed his form as the creepers around Kusuma-sarovara. Vajranabha resumed his place as the lightning bolt marking Krsna’s left sole. The queens returned to celestial Dvaraka. And Krsna set out in search of devotees engaged in hari-katha, eager to hear what they had to say.

When everyone had departed, Kusuma-sarovara became quiet once more. Birds skimmed across its mirror surface, turtles poked their noses above the water for air, and jewelfish dove in its nectarean depths. Flowering trees showered the lake with their pollen, and greedy bees hummed through its groves in search of honey.

In the prakata-dhama here on earth, pure Vaisnavas can still hear the echo of the songs the gopis sing in the aprakata-dhama in the spiritual world. Uddhava, in the form of grass and shrubs, is one such Vaisnava who is constantly nourished by the gopis’ songs, like tava kathamrtam tapta-jivanam.

## About the Author

Sivarama Swami was born in Budapest, Hungary in 1949, and his family emigrated to Canada during the failed 1956 Hungarian revolution. He became a disciple of A.C. Bhaktivedanta Swami Prabhupada, the founder-acarya of ISKCON, in 1973 and entered the renounced order of life (sannyasa) in 1979. Sivarama Swami is well known for his deep knowledge of Vaisnava literature and has written many books about Gaudiya Vaisnavism, including the “Krsna in Vrndavana” series. He is a member of iskcon’s Governing Body Commission and is responsible for projects in Hungary, Turkey, and Romania. Although he travels and speaks internationally, he spends as much time as possible at New Vraja-dhama (Krishna Valley), a replica of Vrndavana and self-sufficient farming project in Hungary’s countryside.
